More than three and a half centuries ago, in the soft mid-spring of 1492, the dawn angel on his slow, broad flight would have looked down on a world far more familiar to us than we might guess. Travelling from the sun-warmed shores of the Levant to the Pillars of Hercules, and across the snow-capped peaks of the Caucasus over the snowy Alpine ridges all the way to the bare, dark coasts of the western isles, he would have traced nearly the same outlines of firm land and restless sea we know today: the same great mountain shadows sloping over the same valleys, olive-cloaked hills, dark pine forests, broad plains lush with young corn and rain-freshened grass, and the domes and spires of cities huddled beside rivers or rising above the sedge-thick masts of curved coastal harbors, sitting in the exact spots they still occupy now. As his faint first light seeped through the cracks of cottage windows and palace doors alike, it fell just as it does now: on the warm, pink faces of sleeping children curled in their nests of blankets, on the haggard wake of the sick and grieving, on the calloused hands of laborers rising before the sun, and on the pale, bleary eyes of night-students hunched over manuscripts, questioning the stars, the ancient sages, and the depths of their own souls for the hidden truth that would break the narrow limits of a human lifespan and turn what seemed a dark, aimless path into one small arc of an immeasurable circle of light and glory.

The great rivers that have shaped human lives have barely shifted their courses in all that time, and the deeper currents of the human heart ebb and flow in the same rhythm, answering to the same hungers, the same loves, the same terrors. Following in the wake of that slow dawn, we cannot escape the overwhelming truth that the core of the human lot never changes: hunger and labor, seedtime and harvest, love and loss.

If our imagination pauses to linger over one world-famous city that has barely altered its outline since Columbus set sail, we find a living symbol standing against all the chaos of human change, reminding us that we share far more with the people of the past than separates us. The same principles of engineering that raised its domes and towers created a likeness in human building far deeper and more lasting than any fashion or technological shift. We can easily imagine that the shade of a Florentine citizen, who drew his last breath in 1492 while Columbus was still haggling for his three tiny, leaky ships in Palos harbor, could return to walk the golden morning light once more, and find fellowship and understanding among the inheritors of his city.

Let us imagine that very shade, permitted to revisit the waking world, standing now on the hill of San Miniato, looking south over Florence. He wears the clothes he lived in: the long, unbroken folds of his heavy black silk lucco falling straight from his neck to his ankles, his plain cloth cap draped with a long soft becchetto strip he can wrap around his neck as a scarf if the wind turns cold. His face is sharp and firm, not classically handsome, but with a mouth cut clear and determined, softened to warm humanity by the clean-shaven curve of his lip and chin. It is a face lined with a lifetime of sharp, varied memories made on the banks of the gleaming Arno, and as he stares out at the view, the sense of familiarity overwhelms any small changes he spots. He is convinced he could climb down the hill back into the city streets and pick up his busy, full life exactly where he left off.

He does not just recognize the dark mass of Mount Morello rising opposite him, the long Arno valley stretching its grey green luxuriance all the way to the distant marble ridges of Carrara, and the steep hill of Fiesole topped with monastic walls and dark cypresses, or all the scattered green slopes dotted with villas he can name without thinking. He spots far closer, more intimate landmarks of his daily walks. He misses the seventy or more tall, fortified medieval towers that once ringed the city walls like a king’s jeweled diadem, but he does not linger on that blank space for long. His eyes are drawn irresistibly to the famous tower of the Palazzo Vecchio, rising like a tall flower stem leaning toward the sun, from the great square turreted mass of the old seat of government at the very heart of Florence, looking none the worse for the four hundred years that have passed since he walked beneath it as a young man. There, too, is Brunelleschi’s great dome, the largest in the world, which was only a wild, daring dream in the mind of that small, quick-eyed architect when our shade was a small boy, its great curved mass rising high enough to eclipse the surrounding hills. Giotto’s bell tower, with its distant glint of rich colored marble, the graceful spire of the Badia, and all the other familiar belltowers he first looked at balanced on his nurse’s shoulder, are all still there.

“Surely,” he thinks, “Florence can still ring her bells with that deep, solemn hammer clang that used to beat on the hearts of every citizen and strike fire into their souls. There on the right stands the long, dark bulk of Santa Croce, where we laid our greatest dead to rest, pressing laurel to their cold brows and fanning their graves with the banners of their victories. But Santa Croce never had a spire when I lived: we Florentines were so full of grand building plans we could never finish all the facades and spires we dreamed of. We had frescoes to paint and shrines to pay for, not to mention greedy mercenary captains, bribed kings, and territories we had to buy at exorbitant prices, so that spire must have waited all these centuries. What architect could the Franciscan friars have hired to build it? If it had been raised in my day, Brunelleschi or Michelozzo would have designed something far finer, something worthy to crown the great old church Arnolfo laid out.”

He sighs, and lets his gaze drift over the city walls, staring in confused wonder at the changes of this strange new era. Why have five of the eleven convenient old gates been bricked up? Worse than that, why are all the tall defensive towers he once took such pride in levelled flat? Has the world grown so peaceful, then, that Florentines live in such perfect harmony there are no more conspiracies to smuggle ambitious exiles back into the city at the head of armed bands? These are hard questions to untangle, and it is far easier, far pleasanter, to linger on the familiar sights than to puzzle out the new ones.

And there is the Arno, all its bridges standing exactly where he remembers them: the odd, one-of-a-kind Ponte Vecchio, crammed full of the same tiny, quaint little shops where he used to linger for a minute on his walk, either to check on the construction of the massive new palace Messer Luca Pitti was building with stone quarried out of the hill behind his house, or to transact small business with the cloth dyers across the river in Oltrarno. The oversize, sprawling roofline of the Pitti Palace is hidden from this vantage point on San Miniato, but the old Florentine has no wish to see that overly ambitious private palace. He only aches to climb down into those narrow, winding streets, the humming, crowded piazzas, to step back into the eager, communal life he inherited from his fathers.

He wonders if the old system of voting with black and white beans still goes on down there. Who are the current Priori, eating their strictly regulated official dinners in the Palazzo Vecchio, with courses of tripe and boiled partridge, enlivened by practical jokes played on the most awkward, unlucky man among the ruling signors? Do they still hang the bright, heavy banners from every window, and parade them with proper pomp beneath Orcagna’s Loggia every two months?

Life had a sharp, sweet zest for him when he trod those marble steps and held those public honors himself. His trade stretched from Syria to Britain, so his politics spanned the whole of the known world, but they had the fierce, intimate intensity only possible in a small, tight-knit community: a city ringed by six miles of walls and surrounded by hills, where every man knew every other by sight as they passed in the street, where every glance fell on the memorials of the commonwealth every day, and every citizen knew not just that he had the right to vote, but the chance of being voted into the highest offices. He loved his honors, his profits, the work of his counting house, his guild, and the public council chamber. He loved his enmities too, and he used to finger the white bean that would keep a hated rival’s name out of the election bag with far more satisfaction than if it had been a golden florin. He took pride in arranging good, advantageous marriages for his sons and daughters, and walked home under his favorite cool loggia in the evenings with triumphant light in his eyes to toast the match. He loved to play chess there with his friends, to trade sharp jests and even rough, coarse jokes, for he knew a man eligible for the highest magistracy did not need to be above that kind of simple pleasure.

He knew every corner of domestic and foreign affairs: he had served on the Council of Ten that ran the war department, the Council of Eight that managed domestic law and order, and the body of Priori that formed the Republic’s executive government. He had even risen to the supreme office of Gonfaloniere, led embassies to the Pope and the Venetian Republic, and served as commissary to Florence’s hired mercenary army, directing the inglorious, almost bloodless skirmishes where no man died of a brave, clean breast wound, only from accidental falls and being trampled by panicking horses. In that long life, he learned to distrust men without bitterness, seeing most of life as a fair game of skill, but never losing his respect for old tales of heroism and clean, uncorrupted honor. The human soul is hospitable, after all, and can hold conflicting sentiments and contradictory opinions side by side with perfect impartiality.

He was proud, too, to have soaked up all the learning of his age, to judge the world not by the crude opinions of the vulgar, but in harmony with the ancient Greek and Roman authors. In the prime of his life he had chased after the most perfectly corrected classical manuscripts, paid dozens of florins for antique vases and unearthed busts of the ancient immortals, even if some of them were truncis naribus, missing their noses, he still considered them perfectly authentic. In his old age, he had rushed to buy one of the very first copies of the fine printed edition of Homer that became one of the early glories of the Florentine press.

But for all that, he had never forgotten to hang a wax votive effigy of himself beneath the protection of the Madonna Annunziata, or give large gifts to the shrines of saints whose lives had nothing to do with the classics. He had even made generous bequests to build new chapels for the Franciscan friars, the same friars he had mocked with a hundred sharp jokes over dinner. The Unseen Powers were far too mighty to trifle with. Who could be absolutely sure there was no angry force to appease, no intercessory pity to win, hiding behind any one of the thousands of names men called on for help? Weren’t healing gems known to work their magic even if they only pressed against the tip of your finger? Weren’t all things in the world steeped in hidden occult virtues? Lucretius the pagan philosopher might be right, the great poet who argued there were no gods meddling in human affairs. Luigi Pulci, the Florentine poet suspected of believing nothing at all beyond the roof of his own house, had sounded very right over the supper table when the wine and jests flowed fast, even if he only wrote in the crude vulgar tongue. Even learned scholars argued that Aristotle, the wisest of all men (unless Plato was wiser, of course), was a thorough irreligious thinker, and a liberal educated man had to entertain every possible speculation. But all these negations might still turn out to be wrong. They felt very wrong, as the years rolled past and took on graver and graver faces. The world was Christian, after all. He had been baptized in the great baptistery of San Giovanni, where the dome was covered in terrible frescoes of the last judgment, and the altar bore a crucified Christ that forever destroyed any chance he might have felt perfectly complacent about his own sins and the state of the world.

He was no pagan philosopher, no mocking pagan poet. He was a man of the fifteenth century, heir to that strange tangled web of belief and unbelief: of Epicurean lightheartedness and superstitious dread, of pedantic, impossible ethical maxims he recited by rote and crude, unruly passions he acted on with childlike impulsiveness, of a pull toward self-indulgent pagan ease and an unavoidable submission to the human conscience that, in the ferment of that new age, filled the air with strange prophecies and dark presentiments. He had smiled and shaken his head dubiously when he heard simple folk talk of the coming “Angelic Pope,” who would purify the Church of simony and clear the lives of the clergy of all their scandal, a state of affairs so far from the corruption of Pope Innocent VIII’s court that no shrewd merchant and politician like him could ever have counted on it coming to pass. But he had felt the weight of all the evils of his age anyway, for he was a man of public spirit, and public spirit can never be wholly immoral, for its very essence is care for the common good. That very Lent of 1492, the last before he died still hale and upright in his old age, he had sat in the church of San Lorenzo and listened, half reluctant, half moved, to the preaching of the fierce Dominican friar Girolamo Savonarola. The friar denounced the worldliness and vice of the higher clergy with unmatched boldness, and insisted that Christian men had no right to live only for their own ease when wrongdoing ran rampant in high places, no right to spend their wealth on grand pomp and decoration even in churches while their fellow citizens starved and sickened in the streets. The frate pushed his doctrine far too far for the taste of elderly, settled men, but it was unforgettable to see him move his audience so deeply that even the noblewomen took off all their gold and jewels and handed them over to be sold for the benefit of the poor.

“He was a remarkable man, that Prior of San Marco,” our shade thinks. “Arrogant and extreme, perhaps, especially in his fiery denunciations of swift, certain divine vengeance. Ah, Iddio non paga il Sabato—God does not pay his wages on a Saturday. The wages of sin are often delayed for years, I saw decades of prosperous, unpunished wickedness in my own time. But a preaching friar who wanted to move the whole people to action could never be moderate. He might have been a little less defiant and curt to Lorenzo de’ Medici, after all, Lorenzo’s family had funded and built most of San Marco monastery. Did the two of them ever make up their quarrel? And Lorenzo himself, with his weak outward eyes and his sharp, subtle inward vision, did he ever recover from that last illness at his villa in Careggi? He left the world with a sad, uneasy face, and everyone suspected his handsome, hot-tempered son Piero would turn out to be a tyrant like Rehoboam of old. How did it all turn out? Which party is currently in danger of being exiled, their houses sacked by the mob? Is there any successor to the incomparable Lorenzo now, to whom the Great Turk will send gifts of rare wild animals, holy relics, priceless manuscripts, and fugitive political enemies, all perfectly suited to the tastes of a Christian Magnifico who is both learned and devout—and just a little bit vindictive? Which famous scholar writes the Republic’s official Latin letters these days? What fiery philosopher is lecturing on Dante in the Duomo, then going home to write bitter, scathing rants against the parents of any fool who dares criticize his classical spelling? Are the wise heads in Florence leaning toward an alliance with the Pope and the Kingdom of Naples, or are they listening instead to the ambassadors from France and Milan?”

All that news is waiting down in the streets, on the familiar broad marble paving stones in front of the churches, under the sheltered loggias where Florentines still gather to gossip and debate, to trade bitter jests and merry jokes just as they did in his day. All the old buildings are still standing, after all. The changes can’t be that big. He will go down and hear, he will step onto the familiar pavement, and hear the old speech of Florentines once more.

Do not go down, good shade. The changes are greater than you dream, and the speech of modern Florentines would sound like a meaningless riddle to your ears. If you must go, do not stop to chat with politicians loitering on the marble pavements, do not ask questions about the old cloth trade in the Calimara district, do not puzzle over the new currents of scholarship, secular or monastic. Only look at the sunlight and shadows falling on the grand, solid old walls that were built to last, and have endured in all their majesty. Look at the faces of the little children playing in the squares, making their own bright sunlight amid the shadows of age. Look into the churches if you will, hear the same ancient chants, see the same images you remember: the images of willing suffering endured for a great good, of boundless benevolent love, of souls ascending to glory. See the upturned faces of the living, their lips moving in the same old prayers for help. These things have never changed. Sunlight and shadow wake the same old beauty and stir the same old chords in the human heart at dawn, noon, and dusk. Little children remain the eternal symbol of the unbreakable marriage between love and duty. And all across the centuries, men still yearn for a reign of peace and righteousness, still know that the highest life of all is the one lived in conscious, willing sacrifice for others. The Angelic Pope, that old promise of a world made new, has still not come. That age-old yearning burns as bright in the human heart today as it did when you drew your last breath in that spring of 1492.

The Loggia de’ Cerchi stood tucked in the beating heart of old Florence, tangled in a maze of narrow lanes behind the Badia, paths modern strangers rarely tread unless they are hunting for one stark, unadorned doorway carved with the words: *Qui Nacque Il Divino Poeta* — Here the Divine Poet was Born. The same cobblestones would once have rung for Dante with the shouts and clashing steel of warring noble clans; but by the fifteenth century, the streets hummed only with the rough, unrecorded quarrels and raucous jests of wool-carders plying their trade in the clothmaking quarters of San Martino and Garbo.

Under the loggia’s stone arch at dawn on April 9th, 1492, two men stared each other down: one stooped slightly, leaning in with sharp, curious scrutiny, while the other lay splayed on the cold pavement, gazing up with the dazed, startled stare of a man woken mid-dream.

The standing man spoke first. Grey-haired, broad-shouldered, he was exactly the sort of fellow Tuscan folk liked to describe as moulded with a fist and polished with a pickaxe — coarse, unrefined, built for hard labor. But the deep, furrowed lines carved across his brow and around his mouth spoke of a pompous, self-important gravity, clearly meant to dissuade anyone from dismissing him out of hand for his rough exterior. A large, well-stuffed leather sack sat at his feet, and a pedlar’s basket hung slung over his shoulder, strung with pins and spools of thread for ladies’ small wares, and shards of colored glass he’d bartered for across half the city.

“Young man,” he said, pointing a gnarled finger at the gold ring on the other’s hand, “when you’ve got a tougher stubble on that chin, you’ll know better than to nap on street corners with a trinket like that on your forefinger. By the holy gospels! If it’d been any rascal but me standing over you two minutes ago, that ring’d be gone for good. But Bratti Ferravecchi isn’t the sort to steal. A cat can’t enjoy a mouse unless she catches it fair and alive, and Bratti can’t stomach gain that doesn’t come from a proper bargain. Why, three years ago on San Giovanni’s feast, the saint put a dead body right in my path — a blind beggar, his cap stuffed full of silver coins. But would you believe me? My stomach turned against that unearned money, till I convinced myself San Giovanni owed me every penny for what I spend every year at the festa. I buried the old man decent, even paid for a mass for his soul, and then I knew it was a fair trade. But how does a young lad with the face of Messer San Michele end up sleeping on a stone bed with the wind for a blanket?”

The stranger, still groggy from sleep, barely made out Bratti’s guttural Tuscan, but he understood the warning gesture well enough. Glancing down at his ring, he half-automatically slipped it off and tucked it deep inside his doublet, pushing himself upright to stretch.

“Your tunic and hose don’t half match that jewel, lad,” Bratti said slowly, nodding at his travel-stained clothes. “Anybody’d think the saints sent *you* a dead body to rob. If you took his jewels, I hope you buried him proper — can certainly afford to pay for a mass or two for the poor soul.”

A sharp, painful thrill shot through the stranger, freezing his half-raised arms mid-stretch. For a split second he turned on Bratti with a sharp, angry frown, but he schooled his face back to easy indifference fast. He pulled off his red Levantine cap, which hung like a plump purse over his left ear, pushed back his long dark brown curls, and smiled as he glanced down at his sea-spotted clothes.

“You speak truth, friend. My garments are stained as badly as an old sail, and they’re not even old — it’s just they’ve been splashed with sea water and rain alike. Fact is, I’m a stranger to Florence. I stumbled in last night footsore, and decided to curl up in this hospitable porch rather than trawl the streets any longer for a random inn that’d turn out to be a den of more than one kind of bloodsucker.”

“A stranger, right enough,” Bratti huffed. “Your words all melt soft out of your throat, so no proper Florentine can tell a hook from a hanger. You’re not from Genoa? Cut of your clothes says you’re more like to be from Venice.”

“Right now,” the stranger laughed, “it matters far less where I came from than where I can go to get a mouthful of breakfast. This city of yours is turning a pretty grim face on me right here — can you show me the way to a busier quarter where I can find a meal and a bed?”

“That I can,” Bratti said, hefting his leather sack onto his back. “You’re lucky, lad, that I was walking in from Rovezzano this morning, and detoured out of my way to Mercato Vecchio to say an Ave at the Badia. That’s your good fortune, sure. But I need my profit for the favor, mind. Nothing for nothing. If I lead you to the market, you swear by your patron saint to give me the first bid for that stained suit of yours, once you buy yourself something better — which you clearly will, with that pretty ring you’re hiding.”

“Agreed, by San Niccolò,” the stranger laughed. “Now let’s get moving to this market, because this doublet you’re so eager to buy could do with a hot meal inside it a lot more than new lining.”

“Coveting? Pfft,” Bratti snorted, setting off down the lane. He cut himself off mid-sentence, and began to shout in a loud, harsh tone that creaked like a ungreased cart wheel: *Chi abbaratta — baratta — b’ratta — chi abbaratta cenci e vetri — b’ratta ferri vecchi?* Who will barter for rags, broken glass, old iron?

“Your whole suit’s not worth much, truth be told,” he went on, dropping back to conversational tone after a moment. “Hose and all, it’s barely worth a handful of pennies. Still, if you want to buy yourself a lute that plays better than any new one, or a sword once worn by a Ridolfi, or the finest new prayer beads, I can cut you a grand bargain if I set the suit’s value against the price. Simple as I look, I’ve got the best stocked shop of any rag-and-bone man in the district, right near the market. Praise the Virgin, I’m not hauling a useless pumpkin on my shoulders. I don’t stay caged in the shop all day, neither — my wife and a pet raven stay home to mind the stock. *Chi abbaratta — baratta — b’ratta?*... Now, lad, tell me where you’re from, and what business you’ve got in Florence?”

“I thought you never liked getting anything without a proper bargain,” the stranger grinned. “You’ve offered me nothing in exchange for that information yet.”

“Well, well — a Florentine doesn’t mind paying a fair price for news. It fills the stomach a little even if you don’t walk away with a new pair of hose. I’ll take you to the prettiest lass in the market to get a cup of milk — that’s a fair trade, isn’t it?”

“Nay, I can find her myself if she’s that pretty,” the stranger said, shaking his head. “Pretty heads always lean out of doors and windows to see the passersby. No, no — besides, a sharp trader like you ought to know that any man who bids hard for nuts and news might well end up finding both are hollow.”

“Ah, lad,” Bratti said, glancing at him sideways with open admiration, “you weren’t born on a Sunday — the salt shops were wide open the day you came into the world. You’re not a Hebrew, eh? Come from Spain or Naples, right? Let me tell you, the Friars Minor are working hard to make Florence as hot as Spain for those hell’s dogs that want all the profit of usury for themselves and leave none for good Christians. If you wander the Calimara quarter with a scrap of yellow cloth sewn in your cap, it’ll spoil that smooth olive cheek of yours far worse than a sword cut would. — *Abbaratta, baratta — chi abbaratta?* Grey cloth is pitted against yellow cloth now, see? There’s enough grey cloth in Florence to make a gown and cowl for the whole Duomo, and not enough yellow cloth to make hose for Saint Christopher — bless his name, and let me catch sight of him today! — *Abbaratta, baratta, b’ratta — chi abbaratta?*”

“All that very entertaining information you’re giving away for nothing,” the stranger said, a little scornfully, “just happens not to concern me. I’m no Hebrew.”

“See there now!” Bratti crowed triumphantly. “I just made a fine bargain out of thin air. Got you to tell me something, even though you’re as slippery as a lamprey. Praise San Giovanni, a blind Florentine is a match for two one-eyed strangers. And here we are, in the Mercato.”

They stepped out of the narrow warren of lanes into the broad sunlit piazza known to every old Florentine writer as Mercato Vecchio, the Old Market. The space had been a provision market since time out of mind — the old tales liked to say it was the very spot the first Fiesole-dwelling ancestors of the Florentines had climbed down from their hill forts to trade with the valley villagers. Even wealthy noble families hadn’t shunned living there; in the early decades of the 15th century, the Medici and other powerful *popolani grassi* commercial nobility kept their palaces facing the square, never mind the roar of a dozen dialects and the sight of butcher stalls the poet Antonio Pucci had called the market’s chief glory, making it better than any other market on earth. But the butchers’ stalls were empty today: Lent was still in full swing, so the great butchers’ guild had suspended its work, leaving the greengrocers, cheesemongers, macaroni sellers, egg vendors, and dried fruit merchants to reign — a change that made women’s voices ring loudest over the square’s hum. Even in off-seasons, the air hummed with the clink of money changers’ coins, people tapping pots and pans to test their sturdiness, haggling over old clothes, dice players’ loud taunts, vendors shouting the praises of new linen, woodenware, iron kettles and frying pans. Narrow entrances choked with mules and carts echoed with the very same sharp, colorful insults modern women still hurl when they’re jostled and overheated in a crowd. Well-off ladies and gentlemen out to do their marketing would regularly stumble across impromptu street brawls, see rags and beggary and petty crime far worse than anything modern city dwellers could imagine. As the day wore on, the open air audience could watch the ugly drama of gambling unfold right in the square: the trembling eagerness, the blank despair, the sobs, the blasphemies, the flying punches — just as the old verse described:

But there were kinder, sweeter sights too: plump brood rabbits just as innocent and wide-eyed as any today, doves and songbirds to buy as presents for children, even kittens for sale, here and there a fine handsome tomcat boasting a perfect mousing record. Best of all were the young women’s soft, round cheeks and bright eyes, fresh from the pre-dawn walk in from their home villages far outside the city walls, and older faces glowing with the steady warmth of honest goodwill, the sort of faces never absent from scenes of busy, ordinary human work. High on an ancient pillar in the square’s centre — a pillar hauled from the old church of San Giovanni — stood Donatello’s stone statue of Plenty, with a fountain at its foot. The old poet Pucci wrote that the market housewives would stop there to rinse out their utensils and take a sip of cool water, not because they couldn’t afford wine, but so they could save every penny of their wine money for their husbands.

But this particular morning, the whole market had fallen prey to a strange, shared distraction. The stalls were half set up with their wares, and early buyers were already hovering to snap up the freshest vegetables and finest butter — but almost no one was tending to their trade. Every vendor had turned their back on their goods, clustering in tight knots all across the piazza to talk. A secondhand clothes seller who’d been about to hang a pair of long hose out on display had distractedly slung them around his own neck so he could rush over to the nearest gossip group. A cheesemonger with a block of marzolino in one hand and a knife in the other was absentmindedly stabbing the soft cheese to emphasize his points. Elderly greengrocers stood holding their egg baskets tilted so far sideways they looked ready to spill, wailing invocations to the saints in a ragged, overlapping chorus.

Florence’s street boys — a particularly mischievous, sour-crab lot — saw their perfect chance. Some made a dash for piles of nuts and dried figs, others swarmed the stalls selling hot Lenten dishes, followed closely by stray dogs who’d learned to love fasting food too, who’d grab a mouthful and dart under the nearest stall to eat it. Mules jostled and kicked their way through scattered baskets, stretching their muzzles out to nibble bunches of fragrant leafy greens.

“Diavolo!” Bratti swore, as he and his companion wandered unnoticed through the chaos. “The whole market’s gone mad, like the Holy Father just excommunicated the lot of us. I’ve got to find out what’s going on. But don’t you worry — you might think it’s a thousand years till you get that cup of milk from the pretty lass, but stick close to me and I’ll keep my end of the bargain. And remember, I get first bid on that suit of yours — especially the hose. Spotted and stained as they are, that’s fine *panno di garbo* cloth, almost ruined by mud and sea spray, but still worth something.”

“Ola, Monna Trecca!” Bratti shouted, turning to the old greengrocer woman hovering just outside the nearest group, who’d paused her wailing to listen. “Your mules are upsetting all your bunches of parsley! Has the whole world come to an end?”

Monna Trecca — Dame Greengrocer — spun around at the unexpected yell right in her ear, half furious, half bewildered, staring first at Bratti, then at her ransacked stall, then back at him again.

“A bad Easter and a bad year to you, may you die by the sword!” she shrieked, rushing towards her produce stall, the whole of her anger landing first on Bratti. Bratti didn’t even flinch at the curse. He slipped neatly into the empty spot she’d left behind, close enough to hear the story everyone was crowding to listen to, and gestured for the young stranger to stay right next to him.

“I saw it myself,” a fat man said, waving a bunch of newly bought leeks in his hand. “I was at Santa Maria Novella, and I saw it all. The woman jumped up, threw her arms out, screaming that she saw a great bull with horns of fire charging down to crush the church. I saw it myself.”

“Saw what, Goro?” a slim, roguish man with a glint in his eye asked. He wore a tight jerkin, a skullcap slid carelessly over his left ear, a fine linen apron tucked up at one side, and a razor stuck in his belt. “Did you see the bull, or only the woman?”

“Well, the woman, of course!” Goro huffed. “But it’s all the same, isn’t it? Doesn’t change the meaning at all!”

“Meaning’s clear enough!” a dozen voices cried out at once, and then a storm of overlapping chatter broke out: “Lights shooting over San Lorenzo three nights in a row!” “Thunder heard when the stars were bright and the sky was clear!” “The lantern on top of the Duomo was struck by Saint Michael’s sword!” “The *Palle* — the Medici arms!” “All smashed to bits!” “Lions in the cage tearing each other apart!” “Ah, and you’d better believe they would!” “The wax votive offering fell down at Santissima Annunziata!” “Died like the best Christian there ever was!” “God will have pardoned his soul!” The phrases shot across each other like hail in a gale, every speaker desperate to be heard, not caring who was listening. The only silent members of the group were Bratti, who was busily piecing all the scattered fragments of gossip together; the barber; and a thin-lipped, sharp-looking man in spectacles, with a pen and ink case hung from his belt.

“Ebbene, Nello,” Bratti murmured, slipping around the edge of the group till he reached the barber. “So the Magnifico Lorenzo is dead, then? Rest his soul! Does that mean the price of wax is going up?”

“Just as you say,” Nello nodded, then added with mock gravity, speaking so fast his words tripped over each other: “And his little wax image in the Annunziata fell off its hook the exact moment he died, or so the Servite friars say, and they’d know better than anyone. Half a dozen cows had stillborn calves this Lent, and no one’s even counted all the bad eggs that got broken since Carnival. Ah, he was a great man — a great politician — a greater poet than Dante himself. And yet the whole cupola didn’t fall, only the lantern on top. Che miracolo! What a miracle!”

A sharp, long hissing *Pst!* cut through the gale of guttural voices. It came from the pale man in spectacles, and it worked instantly: the noise died down, every eye in the group fixed on him, waiting to hear what he had to say.

“It’s well said that you Florentines are blind,” he began, in a sharp, high, incisive voice. “You’d need nothing more than a

After Bratti the peddler wove his way into the huddled, chattering knot of Florentine townsfolk, the young foreign stranger quickly gave up on making sense of the fevered public agitation rippling through the piazza. He had no stake in whatever local scandal or political upheaval was sending every bystander craning their neck to shout over their neighbors, and he soon grew tired of lingering to wait for Bratti to escort him onward. He set off to stroll the sun-warmed flagstones of the market square, keeping an eye out for any food vendor who might not be half-distracted chasing the latest gossip.

A sudden thought struck him, and he fumbled at the leather wallet slung from his waist, rummaging through its pockets again and again until his face fell in clear frustration. “Not an obolus left, by Jupiter,” he muttered under his breath, in a language that was neither Tuscan nor any dialect of Italian the local folk would recognize. “I swore I had one poor copper piece left. Looks like I’ll have to earn my breakfast for the kindness of strangers, then—for love, not coin.”

He had barely taken a dozen more steps when it seemed he had stumbled on just the quiet corner of the market where that odd form of payment might not be turned away. Tucked out of earshot of every gossiping group stood two mules, draped with bright red wool tassels and stitched leather collars. One carried a stack of hewn wooden milk vessels, the other straddled by woven panniers heaped high with fresh wild herbs and crisp, dewy salad greens. Leaning her elbow on the neck of the milk-carrying mule was a young girl, no more than sixteen, a soft red hood pulled up to frame her face so that not a strand of her hair showed. Her features were round and baby-soft, prettier than any market vendor he had seen so far. Likely worn out from tramping three or four miles from her family’s small hill farm before dawn to get to the square, she had drifted asleep half-standing, half-propped against the mule’s warm neck.

The stranger felt no guilt at rousing her, for he chose the gentlest possible way: he plucked a tiny sprig of thyme from the top of her herb pannier and brushed it light as a breath across her lips. In her half-dream, it felt like she was still out in the meadows, stooping to pluck herbs for market herself, and the spell broke softly. She blinked open wide, bright blue baby eyes, and jolted upright in startled confusion to find the tall dark stranger standing inches from her. His voice was soft, but lilted with such a strange foreign accent that she would have assumed he was speaking total nonsense if she had been fully awake, and her first instinct was to twist her face away and lift the edge of her heavy green serge mantle to hide behind.

He spoke again, slow and clear, picking out the simplest Tuscan phrase he knew. “Forgive me, pretty one, for waking you. I’m dying with hunger, and the smell of your warm milk makes breakfast feel more necessary than ever.”

The words *muoio di fame* were familiar to her, and the edge of the mantle fell from her face. In seconds, she had dipped a large earthen cup full of frothy, fragrant warm milk and held it out to him. He did not waste time on flowery compliments before lifting it to his lips, and as he drank, the little maiden found the courage to peer up at his long, loose dark curls. He spoke like a beggar, but even though his fine tunic was scuffed and worn at the cuffs, he looked nothing like the ragged street beggars she saw lingering at the church doors. Some quiet, impulsive kindness made her reach into the canvas bag slung at the mule’s side, and when he set down the empty cup, he found a thick hunk of crusty sourdough bread held out to him, her blue eyes peeking up to encourage him to take it.

“But that is your own breakfast, is it not?” he said, softening his tone. “I have no coin to pay you for this, gentle one. Thank you a thousand times over.”

She did not say a word in reply, just pushed the bread a little closer to him, impatient that he would refuse her gift. Their gazes tangled, the stranger’s dark eyes holding hers, and Tessa’s shy little face grew bolder and bolder, no longer rushing to hide behind her mantle.

“Ah, if you insist I take the bread,” he said, laughing softly as he curled his fingers around it, “I shall grow even more bold, and beg a small kiss to make it taste all the sweeter.”

His strange accent was growing far more understandable by the minute, even if it had made her half-think of crossing herself when she first heard it. She blushed bright pink, and lifted the corner of her mantle up to cover her mouth again. But just as he leaned in, his fingers brushing the soft arm that held the mantle edge up between them, a harsh, rough voice boomed right next to his ear.

“Who do you think you are—may the murrain take you? No honest buyer, that’s for certain, just a loiterer hanging around the dice dens, or something worse. Off with you! Dance along to find more suitable company, or I’ll give you a beating that’ll make you move faster than you like.”

The stranger stepped back, no flicker of fear or deference in his gaze, just a faint, amused lift of his eyebrows that bloomed into a bright, warm grin as he looked over the woman who had threatened him. She was broad, brawny, sunburned, wearing a man’s thick leather jerkin slung over her faded green serge gown, an old peaked hood from a worn-out mantle tied tight around her lined, prematurely wrinkled face. Beneath the coarseness and the sharp set of her jaw, you could see the same soft round features that made Tessa’s face so sweet—a maternal resemblance that felt almost jarring, like a grim little foreshadowing of what the soft young girl might look like after decades of backbreaking work.

That bright young smile of his was disarmingly charming, but Monna Ghita was not the sort of woman to let softness slip, and she only grew more exasperated. “Yes, yes, you can grin just like every other capering monkey on the street. You’re some wandering minstrel or mountebank, I’ll swear it—you look just as silly as a tumbler who’s been standing on his head so long he’s forgotten how to walk rightside up.” She rounded on Tessa, her temper snapping at the easier target. “And what fool’s tricks have you been up to, you little stupid thing? Giving away our milk and our food for free? Aye, you’d carry water in your ears for any idle vagabond too lazy to bend over and help himself, you silly wide-eyed rabbit! Turn around now, haul those herbs out of the panniers, or I’ll make you recite a dozen Aves without pausing to count them.”

“Nay, good Madonna,” the stranger said, still smiling that pleading, charming smile. “Don’t be cross with your sweet Tessa for taking pity on a hungry traveller who found himself suddenly without a single copper quattrino. Your fine face looks so striking when you frown, I can only imagine how lovely it would be lit up with a smile.”

“Get along with you!” Monna Ghita snapped. “I know exactly what sort of trickster you are. You can tickle me with that pretty talk all day long, I’m not going to laugh for you. Be off with you, and a bad Easter to you, or I’ll scuff that pretty face of yours up so bad none of your kisses will be welcome anywhere before Advent.”

She lifted her calloused, claw-like hands to follow through on her threat, just as Bratti stepped up beside his friend Nello, the town barber, who had been observing the whole scene for a minute or two. “What do you make of this pretty trickster, eh, Nello?” Bratti murmured. “Doesn’t his lilt sound like he’s fresh from Venice?”

“Nay, Bratti,” Nello the barber whispered back, clapping him on the shoulder. “Your wits are as thick as a donkey’s, like I told you only a minute ago, and not just about people’s ears. That stranger’s Greek, or my name isn’t Nello the barber who’s shaved the great learned Demetrio himself, and pulled more than one rotten aching tooth out of his scholarly jaw. That lad looks like he stepped straight off Mount Olympus—at least he would, once I got my razor to his overgrown curls.”

“Hold on there, Monna Ghita!” Nello called, stepping forward to lean against the mule’s harness, delighted to stir up a little fun. “What’s got you thundering like a summer storm? Has this young gentleman misbehaved himself?”

“By San Giovanni, the patron of our city!” Bratti put in, loud enough for everyone to hear, still wary of the shabbily dressed stranger he had found earlier with a fine gemmed ring on his finger that looked like the sort of thing Bernardo Rucellai wore. “He was right to run off from me if he was planning to get up to mischief! I swear I found him loitering under the Loggia de’ Cerchi earlier, with a ring on his finger finer than any I’ve ever laid eyes on on a common traveller. I don’t know a single other thing about him, not even if his name’s not a lie.”

“The truth of it is,” Nello said, grinning at the stranger as he clapped him on the back, “this handsome young man grew too bold admiring your charms, Monna Ghita, and tried to steal a kiss while Tessa’s back was turned—seeing as she’s far too busy sorting herbs to pay attention, right?” He turned to the stranger, tone polite and teasing. “Isn’t that right, Messer?”

“You’ve guessed my crime perfectly, like a soothsayer,” the stranger laughed, playing along. “Only the misfortune was I didn’t find the lovely Monna Ghita here at first. I only begged a cup of milk from her daughter, accepted this fine loaf of bread, and was offering my humble gratitude before I was lucky enough to catch sight of her far more mature charms—though I daresay I was too bold to admire them out loud.”

“Go on, get out of here, all of you!” Monna Ghita snapped, elbowing Nello out of the way and tugging her mule’s bridle to force the stranger to step back out of the path. “I hope you all rot in purgatory until I scrape together enough coin to buy your way out, you hear? Tessa, you little simpleton, bring your mule forward a pace—there’s a market cart coming right for us, it’ll run us all over.”

As Tessa turned to grab the mule’s bridle, she darted one shy, quick glance back at the stranger, who was moving off beside Nello to get out of the cart’s path. The glance lasted just long enough for her to catch the tiny, secret wave of his hand, a quiet little adieu he’d been waiting for the chance to give her.

“Well then!” Bratti called out, loud enough to carry over the rattle of the approaching cart. “I’m leaving you with Nello, young man, there’s no way I can push my heavy sack and basket any further, I’ve got errands to run back home. But you remember our bargain, all right? It’s not my fault you found that pretty little Tessa without me tagging along. Nello’ll show you my shop down in the Ferravecchi district, I won’t leave you hanging.”

“A thousand thanks, friend!” the stranger laughed, as he followed Nello up the narrow, sun-dappled side street that led straight to the marble facade of the Duomo looming over the city.

Pushing past the last of the tangles of braying mules and overloaded market carts, the young stranger drew a breath of cleaner Florentine air and turned to Nello with a faint, relieved smile. “To tell you the truth, I am not sorry my former guide Bratti the itinerant peddler passed me off to you. His accent is rougher than a ploughboy’s, and his whole business was a riddle I had no wish to solve. Is it common among you Florentines for a man who trades in rags and broken glass to talk as if he keeps a shop stocked with fine lutes and polished swords?”

“Common? No! Our Bratti is no ordinary man,” Nello answered, his loquacity spilling out as freely as wine from an overfilled skin. “He has a whole theory for his life, and he lives up to it—more than I can say for any philosopher I have ever had the honour of shaving. Bratti’s plan is to squeeze every drop of joy out of his years on earth, and joy for him is the thrill of hard bargaining, haggling over every last scrap of old iron or tattered cloth. When his days are done, he’ll leave every last penny he’s won to Holy Church, to bargain for the easiest possible passage through purgatory. He even had his will drawn up for the lowest fee a notary would agree to, to save every coin for that final trade. But I have told him to his face, ‘Bratti, your bargain limps, and you are the lame side of it. Does it not sadden you a little to look at paintings of Paradise? You will never get to chaffer over rags or rusty nails there. The saints and angels want neither pins nor tinder, and except for San Bartolommeo, who carries his flayed skin slung over his arm like an inconvenient old cloak, I see no chance for you to strike a deal for secondhand clothing.’” He crossed himself abruptly, his cheery tone softening to gravity. “But God pardon me for this light talk! It ill befits a morning when Lorenzo lies dead, the whole city’s in an uproar, and the Muses are tearing their hair—an agony any barber who serves our learned men feels deep in his bones. And you yourself, Messere, seem to carry a shadow over you too: a man of your speech and fine bearing would never put up with a shabby roadside inn like the one you left last night if some misfortune had not befallen him.”

“What Lorenzo is that whose death you speak of?” the stranger asked, so fixed on the news that he did not even notice the quiet inquiry into his own circumstances.

“What Lorenzo? There is only one Lorenzo whose death could throw the Mercato into chaos, make the lantern atop the Duomo look like it leaps in despair, and set the Republic’s caged lions to looking as if they might tear each other apart. I mean Lorenzo de’ Medici, the Pericles of our Florentine Athens—if I may dare make that comparison to a Greek.”

“Why not?” the other laughed. “I doubt Athens even in Pericles’ prime ever produced a barber half so learned as you.”

“Ah, I knew I could not be mistaken!” the rapid, grinning Nello clapped his hands lightly. “Else I have shaved the venerable Demetrio Calcondila for forty years to no purpose. But pardon my wonder: your Italian is smoother than his, even smoother than that of the accomplished Marullo, who wedded the Italian Muse in more ways than one when he married our brilliant, lovely Alessandra Scala.”

“It will ease your wonder to know I come of a Greek stock planted in Italian soil longer than the mulberry trees that grow so thick around our lands,” the stranger explained. “I was born at Bari, raised by an Italian guardian, and only reconnected fully to my Greek heritage when I travelled long across the old lands of gods and heroes. My Greek learning, and a handful of ancient gems sewn into the lining of my doublet, are all the fortune shipwreck has left me. And when the great towers fall, you know, it is ill luck for the small nest-builders: your Pericles’ death makes me half wish I had turned my steps to Rome, as I almost did. A fallacious Augustinian monk persuaded me otherwise. ‘At Rome,’ he said, ‘you will be lost in a crowd of hungry scholars begging for scraps of patronage. But at Florence, every corner basks in Lorenzo’s sunshine of favour. Florence is the best market in Italy for a man of your talents.’”

“Gnaffè, and so it will remain, I hope!” Nello waved off his worry. “Lorenzo was not the only patron of learning in our city—heaven forbid! Because he was a great, sweet melon, that does not make every other Florentine a worthless pumpkin. Have we not Bernardo Rucellai, Alamanno Rinuccini, and a dozen other wealthy men who adore wit and letters? And if you want guidance on all such matters, I, Nello, am your man. It feels like a thousand years until I can be of service to a bel erudito like yourself. First, though, we must attend to your hair. That beard, my fine young man, has to go, even if it was as dear to you as the nymph of your dreams. Here at Florence, we do not love to see a man’s nose peeking out over a cascade of unkempt hair. But mark this: once you have been shaven, you have crossed the Rubicon. If you repent later and let your beard grow back after it has fought the razor, your mouth will not long show what Messer Angelo calls the divine prerogative of lips, but will look like a dark cavern fringed with wild, horrent brambles.”

“That is a terrible prophecy,” the Greek laughed, “especially if Florentine maidens are half as pretty as the little contadina Tessa I stole a kiss from this morning.”

“Tessa? She is a rough-handed farm girl who reeks of the mule stables!” Nello scoffed. “You will rise to far better favour with ladies who carry no such scent. But to earn that, you cannot look like a sgherro, a ruffian of ill repute: you must look like a polished courtier-scholar, the sort of man who sins gently among well-bred, well-fed people, not one who guzzles vile sour vino di sotto in a random roadside tavern.”

“With all my heart,” the stranger agreed. “If the Florentine Graces demand it, I will give up my beard. But—”

“Yes, yes!” Nello interrupted, already herding him along. “I know what you mean. You will not part with your hyacinthine curls, and there is no need. Just a little pruning—ecco!—and you will look the very image of the illustrious Pico di Mirandola in his prime. And here we are, just in time, at the edge of Piazza San Giovanni, at the door of my shop. But I see you pausing: naturally, you want to gawk at our wonder of the world, our Duomo, Santa Maria del Fiore. Well, a single glance, but I beg you save the closer survey for after your shave. I am quivering with the inspiration of my art right to the edge of my razor! Come this way.”

The mercurial barber seized his arm and led him to the south side of the piazza, where the full view spread out before them: the huge dark, soaring shell of Brunelleschi’s cupola, the slender, graceful line of Giotto’s campanile piercing the clear April sky, and the quaint octagon of the Baptistery of San Giovanni, its unique bronze doors glowing still with the dimmed glory of their original gilding. The pink, white, and purple inlaid marbles were far fresher then than they would be four centuries later, when long winters had bleached their bright whites to the warm ochre of well-mellowed meerschaum. The Duomo’s façade was not yet a disgrace of faded stucco, but held the magnificent half-finished promise of the marble inlay and statued niches Giotto had designed 150 years before. The campanile, with all its harmonious blend of colour and form, drew the eye upward high into the bright air, seeming a prophetic symbol that human life could some day be shaped to match that pure, aspiring beauty.

But that was not the impression it made on the Greek. His eyes were drawn irresistibly upward, but he stood with his arms folded, curls falling back over his shoulders, a faint hint of scorn tugging at the corner of his lip. When his gaze fell again, he glanced around the piazza with a cool, scanning curiosity that pricked Nello’s Florentine pride.

“Well, my fine young man,” he said, with a touch of impatience, “you look at our Cathedral as if you were the Angel Gabriel fresh straight from Paradise. I would like to know if you have ever seen finer work than Giotto’s tower, or a cupola that would not look like a common mushroom next to Brunelleschi’s there, or marbles more cunningly wrought than these our Signoria paid a fortune for—enough to buy an entire dukedom. Come, now: have you ever seen anything to equal them?”

“If you asked me that question with a scimitar at my throat, Turkish fashion, or even your own razor at my neck,” the young Greek said, smiling gaily as he moved toward the Baptistery gates, “I daresay you could force me to confess whatever faith you wanted. But with my throat safe, I must tell you these buildings smack a little too much of Christian barbarism for my taste. I shudder thinking what hides inside: smoke-stained hideous Madonnas, fleshless mosaic saints staring down idiotic astonishment and rebuke from the apse, skin-clad skeletons nailed to crosses or stuck full of arrows or stretched across gridirons, women and monks with their heads tilted in perpetual lamentation. I have seen enough of those wry-necked favourites of heaven in Constantinople. But what is this bronze door, rough with carved imagery? The women’s figures here are moulded in a different spirit from those gaunt, staring saints. The high relief heads speak of a living human mind behind them, not like signs pointing to perpetual colic and spasms.”

“Yes, yes!” Nello said, triumphant. “I will show you soon enough our Florentine art is no barbarism. These gates were cast half a century ago by Lorenzo Ghiberti, when he was hardly any older than you are now.”

“Ah, I remember,” the stranger said, turning away as if his appetite for contemplation was already sated. “I have heard your Tuscan sculptors and painters have been dabbling a little in antique styles. But with monks for models and the legends of mad hermits and martyrs for subjects, the very vision of Olympus itself would be of little use to them.”

“I understand,” Nello shrugged significantly as they walked toward his shop. “You are of the same mind as Michele Marullo, ay, and Angelo Poliziano himself, for all his canonicate. He slips into my shop after his lectures sometimes, rants about the old pagan gods waking from their long sleep, making the woods and streams alive again. But he rails against the Roman scholars who want to force everyone to speak Latin again: ‘My ears are already flayed raw by the barbarians of the learned world,’ he says. ‘If the vulgar have to speak Latin too, I would as soon be in Florence the day they took to beating every kettle in the city, for fear the bells alone were not enough to appease the wrath of the saints.’ Ah, Messer Greco, if you want to taste the full flavour of our Florentine scholarship, you must frequent my shop. It is the very focus of all our city’s wit, and in that sense the navel of the earth, as my great predecessor Burchiello said of his own shop—though he made the more frivolous claim that his street of Calimara was the centre of all Florence. And here we are, under the sign of ‘Apollo and the Razor.’ Apollo, you see, is bestowing the razor on the Triptolemus of our craft, the first reaper of beards, the sublime Anonimo, whose mysterious identity is marked by a shadowy hand.”

“I see you have had customers already, Sandro,” Nello continued, addressing a solemn, dark-eyed youth who made way for them on the threshold. “Clear a chair for this signor, and prepare your finest scented lather, for he has a learned and a handsome chin.”

“You have a charming little inner sanctum there, I see,” the stranger said, peering through a latticed screen that divided the shop from a room of equal size, which opened onto a tiny walled garden where a few bay and laurel bushes circled a small stone statue of Hermes. “I suppose your conclave of eruditi meets there?”

“There, and no less often right out here in the shop,” Nello said, leading the way in. Benches lined the walls, a table held one open manuscript and one printed book with large capital letters, a lute, a few oil sketches, and a pair of small plaster models of hands and ancient masks. “My shop is no less a haunt of the Muses, as you will agree when you feel the sudden flood of clear understanding and serene inspiration that comes with a newly shaved chin. Ah, I see you can make that lute sing! I have some small skill at it too, though serenatas are useless when daylight shows my face, no fresher than a winter-worn apple. But look at that sketch over there: it is a fancy of Piero di Cosimo’s, that strange, whimsical painter. He says he dreamed the whole thing staring for hours at a mouldy old wall.”

The sketch showed three masks: one a drunken, laughing Satyr, another a grieving Magdalen, and the third, set between them, the rigid, cold face of a Stoic. The three masks rested tilted on the lap of a small child, whose cherubic features lifted above them with that soft, supernal glow painters had just begun to give to depictions of the Divine Infant.

“A symbolic picture, I see,” the young Greek said, plucking lightly at the lute to pull a soft murmur of music from its strings. “The child, perhaps, is the Golden Age, wanting neither forced worship nor harsh philosophy. The Golden Age can always come back, so long as men are born as babies, not popped into the world already wearing a cassock or a furred magistrate’s robe. Or the child may mean the gentle wisdom of Epicurus, equally removed from gross indulgence, bitter sorrow, and rigid severity.”

“Ah! Everyone has their own interpretation for that sketch,” Nello laughed. “If you ask Piero himself what he meant by it, he will tell you his paintings are an appendix the Good Lord was pleased to add to the universe, and if any man is confused about their meaning he had best go ask Holy Church. People have begged him to turn the sketch into a full painting, but he claps his fingers to his ears and shakes his head. The fancy has passed, he says—what a strange creature our Piero is! Now, all is ready for your initiation into the mysteries of the razor.”

“Mysteries they may well be called,” the barber went on, his spirits rising at the prospect of a long monologue, as he tucked the stranger snugly into the shroud-like shaving cloth. “Mysteries of Minerva and the Graces both. I get the very flower of men’s thoughts, because I catch them in the very first moment after their shave, when their heads feel light as air. (Ah, you wince a little at the lather! It tickles the very edge of the nose, I admit.) That is what makes a barber’s shop so perfect for wit and learning. Look at the druggist’s shop over by the sign of the Moor: that dull conclave pretends to rival mine, but what inspiration can you possibly get from the stench of nauseous boiled vegetable decoctions? Not to mention the second you cross their threshold you see a doctor of physic, all wrapped in fur and scarlet like a giant disguised spider, waiting to pounce on his prey—sometimes even blocking the doorway on his bony old nag, inspecting a vial of his patient’s saliva. (Lift your chin a little, if you please. Stare up at that angel blowing a trumpet on the ceiling. I had it painted expressly to keep my clients’ chins in the right position.) Besides, your druggist, who tramps the hills to pick herbs and boils his noxious brews, is a man full of prejudices. He has poisoned people according to his system, so he has to defend that system to justify what he has done. Now a barber can be entirely dispassionate: the only thing he owes loyalty to is his razor, as long as he is not also an author. That was the flaw in my great predecessor Burchiello: he was a poet, and so he was hopelessly biased in favour of his own verses. I escaped that trap. I learned very young that authorship is a narrowing business, that clashes directly with the liberal art of the razor, which demands impartial affection for every man’s chin. Ecco, Messere! The outline of your chin and lip is as clear as a young maiden’s now. Fix your mind on a knotty question: ask yourself whether you are supposed to spell Virgil’s name with an i or an e, and tell me if you do not feel a sudden unwonted clarity on the point. Only, if you decide for the i, keep it to yourself until your fortune is made. The e has the far stronger following in Florence. Ah! I think I see a glint of sharper wit in your eye. I have it on the authority of our young Niccolò Macchiavelli, sharp enough to spot il pelo nell’uovo, the tiniest flaw in an egg, and a great lover of fine shaves even though his beard is barely two years old, that no sooner do the hairs start sprouting on a man’s face than a certain grossness of apprehension creeps over his mind.”

“Suppose you let me look at myself,” the stranger laughed. “The happy effect on my intellect is probably being blocked by a little doubt about how the shave has changed my appearance.”

“Behold yourself then, in this mirror!” Nello turned the fine Venetian glass towards him. “It is a mirror from Murano, the true nosce teipsum—know thyself—far clearer than the finest polished steel or silver mirror, which is nothing but dim darkness by comparison. See how diligent shaving preserves the human outline of your lower face, so you do not end up looking no different from a bearded owl or a Barbary ape. I have seen men whose

The almond-scented lather curled thick in Nello’s barber bowl, and the air of his little shop hummed with the distant clatter of Florence’s streets—all still reeling, that day, from the shock of the great man’s sudden death, the whole city talking in hushed, orphaned tones as if the very pivot of Italy had been wrenched from its place. So the barber’s grin was extra bright when two men stepped over his threshold, the first a familiar, grave, silver-bearded figure: “Good day, Messer Domenico!” he called, nodding quiet greeting to the man trailing at his heels. “You could not have turned up more opportunely than melted cheese on hot macaroni. I see you are in haste—off with your doublet collar, no delay on the shave, ecco! Everyone in the city carries grave matter on his mind this morning, heaven itself half at a loss what to do next, *oimè*! But the sun still drifts steady towards dinner time, and see how fortune serves you: this young stranger was just asking for an honourable, trustworthy trader to advance him a small sum on a fine ring of value, and I could not count every goldsmith and moneylender in Florence from sunrise to sundown and name a better man than Menico Cennini. Even better, he carries the very ware your printing house hungers for: fluent Greek, sharp young eyes to pore over manuscripts, a double treasure you printers would chase across the Apennines for.”

Domenico Cennini, son of the master craftsman Bernardo who had cast Florence’s very first set of printing types twenty years prior, was forced to hold his lathered, passive silence while Nello poured out his stream of chatter, turning only a slow, sidelong gaze on the young stranger lingering by the workbench.
“This fine lad has unlimited Greek, Latin, and Italian at his service,” Nello pressed on, ever fond of amplifying a man’s virtues by a generous half. “He is as much a prodigy of juvenile learning as Francesco Filelfo, or our own incomparable Poliziano. A second Guarino, too, for he has suffered the cruel misfortune of a shipwreck, and doubtless lost whole chests of precious manuscripts that would have corrected even your most precise printed editions, Domenico. The luck of it is, he saved a few rare gems from the waves. Your name, Messer—you said it was—?”

“Tito Melema,” the stranger said, slipping the carved ring from his finger just as Nello whipped the shaving cloth from Cennini’s shoulders, his razor as quick as his tongue.

The second man who had entered at Cennini’s side—tall, near fifty years old, his beard trimmed short, an old faded felt hat pulled low over his eyes, his mantle frayed thin at the cuffs—had not once taken his gaze off Tito. Now he spoke abruptly, no preamble at all:
“Young man, I am painting a canvas of Sinon deceiving old King Priam at the gates of Troy. I would pay you well to sit for me; your face is exactly what I need for my Sinon.”

Tito started as if he had been struck, his face draining pale as if he had just been caught in a hidden wrong, but Nello gave him no time to fumble for a reply.
“Piero! Thou art the most ridiculous jumble of humours and fancies ever stuffed into a single human skin,” the barber squawked. “What trick wilt thou work on this fine young scholar’s face to turn it into a traitor’s? Ask him to tilt his eyes upward and thou couldst paint a Saint Sebastian that would draw whole troops of devout women to worship at it; or if thou art in a classical mood, twist myrtle in his curls and make him a young Bacchus, or better yet Apollo the sun god—his face glows as warm and bright as a summer dawn, it made me his loyal friend in the space of one short credo.”

“Ay, Nello,” the painter replied, his speech coming in sharp, abrupt pauses, “if thy tongue would stop its everlasting chirping long enough for thy wits to catch up, thou wouldst see thou hast just given the very reason his face is perfect for my Sinon. The ideal traitor wears a face no vice can leave a mark on: lips that lie with a soft dimpled smile, eyes so bright and agate-deep no amount of infamy can ever dull them, cheeks that walk straight from a murder and never look haggard. I do not say this young man is a traitor. I say he has the face that would make him a far more perfect traitor if he had the heart of one—which is no more than to say he has a beautiful face, fed full with rich young blood, that will keep its colour on a full stomach without needing much help from virtue. He may have the heart of a hero under it all, for all I know. Ask Domenico there if even the finest lapidary can judge a gem’s true worth by sight alone. Now I am going to stuff tow in my ears, for thy chatter and the church bells clashing outside are more than my wits can bear: say no more, sit thee down and trim my beard.”

Piero—called Piero di Cosimo, after his old master Cosimo Rosselli—pulled two wads of rough tow from his pocket, stuffed them tight in his ears, and dropped heavily into the empty shaving chair. Nello shrugged, pulling a face full of playful meaning at Tito, as plain as saying *A whimsical old fool, is he not? Everyone writes his odd speeches off as mere jokes*.

Tito, who had stood transfixed, his long dark eyes fixed on the strange painter who had read some unnameable, unspoken thing in his face, snapped back to his self-possession at the shift in the room. He turned his full attention back to Cennini, who was now turning the small ring over in his fingers.
“This is a curious, valuable ring, young man,” the printer-goldsmith said. “This intaglio of the fish beneath the crested serpent, carved deep into the black stratum of this onyx—or nicolo, more correctly—stands out beautifully against the pale blue upper layer of the stone. This ring surely has a long history?” he added, lifting his gaze to study Tito keenly.

“It does indeed,” Tito met his scrutiny openly, smiling easy. “It was dug up in Sicily, and the scholars who study old gems and sigils say both the stone and the carving hold a charm to make the wearer fortunate, especially at sea, and restore to him all he has lost. But,” he chuckled, turning his palm up, “though I have worn it every day since I left Greece, it has not made me altogether lucky at sea, as you see—unless escaping drowning counts as sufficient proof of its virtue. It remains to be seen if my lost chests will ever be recovered, but to lose no chance of that, Messer, I beg you will hold this ring only a short while as pledge for a small sum far beneath its true value. I will redeem it as soon as I can sell a few other small gems I sewed into the lining of my doublet, or sooner still if I can earn coin doing scholarly work, if I am lucky enough to find such employment.”

“That can be arranged easily, young man, if you will come with me,” Cennini said, holding the ring back out to Tito. “My brother Pietro is a far better judge of scholarship than I am, and he can surely set you to work transcribing and correcting Greek manuscripts that will test your capabilities. Take your ring back for now, I will bring you the florins you need once we reach the shop, come along with me.”

“Yes, yes!” Nello chimed in, bustling to hold the shop door open for them. “Go with Messer Domenico, you could not ask for better company! He was born under the constellation that grants a man skill, riches, and perfect integrity, whatever that constellation might be—no matter, since no baby gets to choose their own horoscope anyway, and if they did all the mothers would crowd to give birth on the same lucky day. Besides, our incomparable phoenix Pico della Mirandola already proved all horoscopes are nothing but nonsensical dreams, which is a far less troublesome opinion. *Addio*, bel giovane! Do not forget to come back to see me.”

“Never fear that,” Tito laughed, throwing Nello a bright, smiling wave as he stepped over the threshold. “You have done me a great service already: that is the surest possible guarantee you will see me again.”

“Say what thou wilt, Piero,” Nello muttered to the painter, once Tito had vanished down the street, Piero still half deaf with tow stuffed in his ears. “I will never look on a face as sweet and open as that and not take it as a sign of a lovable, honest nature. Next thou wilt be telling me that Leonardo, whom thou art forever raving about, should have painted Judas as beautiful as Saint John! But thou art as deaf as the top of Mount Morello with that cursed tow in thy ears. Well, well. I will get the rest of this young man’s story out of him soon enough, before I bring him to the house of old Bardo Bardi.”

The Via de’ Bardi, a street steeped in Florentine history, winds through the Oltrarno — the stretch of the city that clings to the southern bank of the Arno. It runs from the foot of the Ponte Vecchio all the way to the Piazza de’ Mozzi, where the Ponte alle Grazie begins. The houses and high walls on its right side back into the steep slopes of the old Bogoli hill, the famous 15th-century stone quarry that paved all of Florence, its soft rock notoriously prone to shifting and eroding after heavy rains. The buildings on its left side press right up to the river, their facades pocked with irregular, quaint little openings that cast soft, warm reflections on the water as the sun sinks low behind the western Tuscan hills. For all their charm, many of these surviving structures feel far too modern to anyone who remembers the fate of the original Bardi family palaces, burned to the ground by an enraged rioting crowd in the mid-1300s.

The Bardi were a proud, ferocious clan, first among the nobles who clashed in Florence’s earliest legendary civil wars, when narrow streets were shadowed by their high defensive towers and the old patron god Mars might well have grinned to see gutters running red with neighbours’ blood, after centuries of the city pretending to worship his rival the Baptist. But the Bardi were not only good with a sword: they had the true Florentine gift for spotting where real power lay beyond street brawls. By the 14th century, they had climbed from their roots as common popolani to buy feudal lands, strongholds, and the title of Counts of Vernio, rousing no small jealousy in their republican fellow citizens. Their sudden fortune is no mystery: a few years later, they were at the very peak of European commerce, the medieval equivalent of the Christian Rothschilds, advancing huge sums to King Edward III of England to fund his wars, and taking payment in coveted wool shipments carried by Florentine merchant galleys. But Edward defaulted spectacularly on his debts, and panicking Sicilian creditors demanded all their deposits back at once, sending the Bardi and their allied trading houses crashing in a commercial calamity whose shockwaves rippled across every Mediterranean coast.

For all that, the Bardi never slunk away in shame: they held their heads higher than ever, growing so arrogant in their remaining power that they drew the full fury of the armed citizen militia in the 1343 uprising, recorded in unflinching detail by the chronicler Giovanni Villani. Barricaded in their narrow street between the two bridges, they fought like cornered panthers to hold the militia back, only falling when rioters stormed down at them from the hill behind their homes. Twenty-two of their grand palaces along the river were sacked and burned, and most of the leading Bardi were exiled from Florence. But old Florentine families had deep, tangled roots: for generations afterwards, the Bardi kept bobbing back to the surface of public life, through fortunes of disgrace and renewal, of wealth and ruin, so common that Archbishop Antonine of San Marco founded the society of Buonuomini di San Martino explicitly to help the poveri vergognosi — “ashamed paupers,” fallen nobles of good family who would starve rather than beg publicly. By 1492, though, the Bardi no longer owned their old riverfront street, now mostly belonging to the wealthy Neri family, who held a long stretch of houses along the hill side of the road.

In one of those Neri homes lived a Bardi descendant, from the very line that had earned the Counts of Vernio title 150 years before. He carried all the old family pride, drive, and hunger to leave a lasting mark on the world — but under very different circumstances. This Bardo de’ Bardi was no street brawler, no feudal lord fortifying strongholds, no daring merchant plotting cross-continental trade schemes. His deep-veined hands were cramped and gnarled from decades of copying manuscripts, he ate meagre little dinners and wore threadbare clothes, first by choice and then by necessity. He spent all his days surrounded by books and fragments of ancient marble, seeing nothing of the present world, living entirely in the glowing memory of his long-past youth. He was penniless, blind, and it was to him that the barber Nello had promised to introduce the young Greek stranger Tito Melema.

Bardo’s home, on the hill side of the street, was one of those sombre, massive stone palaces dotted all over old Florence, pierced with tiny windows and topped with an open-air roof loggia. Its heavy oak doors, fitted with great curled iron hinges, led into a vaulted entrance courtyard, empty save for a single giant iron lamp hanging from the centre of the arched ceiling. A smaller side staircase door led up past the ground and first floors, both of which the merchant owner used as vast warehouses piled high with goods bound for ports as far away as the Scheldt river, the North African coast, the Aegean islands, and the shores of the Black Sea. Old Maso, Bardo’s slow-moving long-time servant, would trudge up all the way to the third floor after his trips to the market for cheap vegetables, to reach his master’s living quarters, where we arrive on a quiet morning not long after Nello made his promise to Tito.

Maso leads us through a small antechamber and opens the inner door, peeking in with a nod. A clear, soft young voice calls out, “Ah, you are back, Maso. All is well, we have wanted for nothing while you were gone.”

The voice comes from the far end of a long, spacious study lined with shelves, every book and antiquity arranged in scrupulous, exact order. Small stands tucked in front of the shelves hold a half-feminine marble torso, a headless Roman warrior statue with one muscular arm raised to swing a now-missing sword, rounded dimpled fragments of carved infant limbs that look soft enough to kiss, well-preserved imperial busts, and two or three elegant painted vases from Magna Grecia. A great heavy table in the centre of the room is heaped with antique bronze oil lamps and dark rough pottery vessels. Almost every surface is pale or muted: the deep ridged vellum bindings of the old manuscripts blend into the cool, livid marble that has spent centuries buried under Italian soil, the once-rich carpet at the far end of the room is worn almost completely dull, the dark bronzes have no sunlight to bring out their faint green patina — and the sun is not yet high enough to send bright slants of light through the narrow windows facing the Via de’ Bardi.

The only splash of vivid colour in the whole room is the hair of a tall seventeen or eighteen-year-old maiden, standing before a carved wooden reading desk of the kind usually found in church choirs. Her hair is a rippling reddish gold, like the colour of sunset wisping across vast autumn skies, held back from her small ears by a thin black fillet, the soft curls falling down to frame her neck above a plain square-cut gown of rough black serge. Her eyes are fixed on the great heavy volume open on the desk before her, one long white hand resting on its edge, the other curled gently around the back of her father’s chair.

The blind old Bardo sits with his head tilted up, turned a little towards his daughter as if he could see her. His fine pale face, framed by drooping white hair under a soft black velvet cap, shares every feature with the young girl beside him: neither of them has a trace of rose flush in their cheeks, both have the same refined high brow and delicate nostril, balanced by a full, firm mouth and strong, determined jaw that gives their faces an air of proud stubbornness and hidden, smouldering passion.
