It is a face that leaves you unsure if it would draw love, or only a kind of awed, reluctant admiration — the kind of question only the eyes can answer. But Bardo’s eyes have long been dark and silent, and Romola’s gaze is fixed on the Latin pages of Politian’s *Miscellanea*, reading aloud the eightieth chapter, telling the tale of the nymph Chariclo, beloved of Pallas Athene, mother of the prophet Tiresias. The story goes that Tiresias stumbled upon Athene bathing naked in the waters of the Hippocrene spring on Mount Helicon, and the goddess blinded him for seeing her against her will. But when Chariclo begged for mercy, Athene gave him the gifts of prophecy, extended life, a guiding staff to keep him from stumbling, and the ability to hold the perfect memory of her unveiled face in his mind forever, even in death.

At that line, Romola’s hand slips from the back of her father’s chair and meets his, as he lifts his own to find her. She does not look around, her voice trembling a little with unspoken feeling as she prepares to read the Greek verse quotation from Nonnus that follows, but Bardo stops her.

“Wait, Romola. Fetch my own copy of Nonnus. It is far more accurate than any Politian owns — I finished adding my own emendations to it in 1477, the very last year I could still see clearly, and I have never shown my notes to another soul.”

Romola crosses the room to the far shelf with a natural, unselfconscious, almost queenly grace, the easy bearing of her tall, well-built frame with no hint of affected posturing.

“Is it still where I left it, Romola?” Bardo asks, perpetual uncertainty pricking at him, always needing confirmation that the physical world still matches the perfect, minute map of it held in his memory.

“Yes, father. At the west end of the room, third shelf up from the floor, behind the bust of Hadrian, right between Apollonius Rhodius and Callimachus, below Lucan and Silius Italicus.”

A fine ear might catch a faint edge of quiet weariness under her soft, distinct tone, but when she sees Bardo stretch out his arms, trembling with nervous excitement to seize the book, her hazel eyes fill with pity. She hurries to lay the great volume in his lap, then kneels beside him, looking up at him as if the love shining out of her face could somehow pierce the dark wall of his blindness. For a moment, the ambiguous, proud tension in her features softens completely, transfigured by that warm, tender mingling of pity and affection that makes her face glow with the most gentle womanliness — the deepest fountain of her feelings has not yet etched itself into her steady features, and only pours out through her eyes.

Bardo, unaware of that soft radiance, is flushed and agitated, his fingers tracing the edges and spine of the great book. “The vellum has yellowed these past thirteen years, Romola.”

“Yes, father,” she says gently. “But your Roman ink lettering at the back is still dark and sharp, clear as can be — your Greek script is more beautiful than any of the handwritten manuscripts you ever bought from hired scribes.”

“Of course it is, child,” Bardo says, his finger brushing across the page as if he can still make out the lines. “What paid scribe could ever match a man who loves the words he copies, to whom a typo or smudge in a text feels worse than stumbling over a hidden obstacle in the dark? Even these new mechanical printing presses, that threaten to drag all great learning down into cheap vulgarity — they still have to copy their editions from the manuscripts we scholars have pored over, our insight into the poet’s meaning almost equal to the divine spark that made the poem in the first place. If they do not, they will flood the world with garbage, turn the very fountain of the Muses into a flood of poison mud. Now find that passage in Book Five, the one Politian cites — I know it by heart.”

Romola pulls a low stool close to his knee, takes the book in her lap, and reads the four lines of Nonnus where Actaeon cries out that Tiresias was the luckiest man alive: he did not have to die to see the gods unveiled, losing only his physical sight to carry the perfect memory of Athene in his soul forever.

“It is true, Romola,” Bardo says when she finishes. “That trivial, narrow sight that lets men see only the petty scenes right in front of their faces is nothing next to the wide, endless light of ancient wisdom, that stretches across centuries of thought, across the lives of entire nations, letting us walk and talk with the great immortal minds of the past. Even when I could see, I only ever truly lived with those dead great ones. The living always felt like faint, empty ghosts to me. Unlike those Florentines Politian compares to the Lamiae, who took off their eyes when they got home so they could pretend to have no knowledge of the world outside, I would walk home from the streets like I was waking up from a forgotten dream, sit down among my books, and quote Petrarch — the only modern man worthy to be named beside the ancients — ‘Books delight us to the marrow, they speak to us, they give us counsel, and wrap us in a bright, living friendship.’”

“And you are luckier than Petrarch, father,” Romola says, humouring his fond tangents with gentle affection. “He pored over his copy of Homer and grieved that he could never read Greek — he carried that inner blindness, far worse than your loss of sight.”

“True, child. I learned Greek under the great teachers Crisolora the younger, Filelfo, Argiropulo, and all that hard study bore fruit in my mind. But the great magnum opus I spent my whole life planning — the work that would weave every thread of my decades of research into a single definitive, masterful volume — was cut short by my failing sight, because I never had a fitting young assistant to help me. The sustained zeal and endless patience you need to blaze new paths in knowledge is even more impossible to reconcile with the wandering, flighty nature of the feminine mind than with their weaker physical bodies.”

Romola flushes bright red, stung and hurt. “Father! I read every page you ask me to, I hunt down every reference you name, I write every note you dictate. I will do anything for your work.”

Bardo shakes his head, smiling a bitter, pitying little smile. “You might as well try to compete in the five events of the pentathlon with the limbs of a delicate nymph. Have I forgotten how you fainted from exhaustion just searching for references to explain a single obscure line of Callimachus?”

“That was only because the great folios were too heavy,” Romola protests. “Maso could have carried them for me. It was not for lack of attention or patience.”

Bardo shakes his head again. “It is not just strong arms I need. I need the sharp, fresh edge of a young mind to cut new paths through the thickets of research, when my own faculties have gone blunt with age. Blindness acts like a dam, it sends all the streams of thought back down the channels I have already walked, and stops me moving forward. If only my son had never left me, lured away by debasing fanatical visions, the kind that only a madman who lives among tombs would chase. He was such a promising boy, I could have kept going, and my path would have broadened right up to the end of my days. Now the road has closed in around me. I have nothing left but a narrow little track to tread, alone in the dark.”

Romola leaps to her feet, stung too sharply by his words to stay seated and silent. She carries the great Nonnus folio back to its shelf, and stands a little way off, stretching her arms downwards and lacing her fingers tight, her young face heavy with quiet dreariness as she stares at the unmoving parchment spines, the mutilated marble fragments, the obsolete bronze and clay trinkets that line the room. Bardo, usually so attuned to every small movement she makes, is too lost in the bitter pain of old memories to notice she has moved away from his side.

“Yes,” he rants on, “if my son had stayed with me, the names of Bardi father and son would be spoken with reverence by scholars for centuries to come. Not for frivolous verses or presumptuous fake philosophy like all the vain men like Panhormita churn out, even Poggio was not free of that nonsense. No — we would have left a lamp for all future generations to read the greatest works of classical antiquity by. Why should a boy like Politian, who was not even born when I was already old enough to debate with Thomas of Sarzana, be remembered forever as a great commentator? Why should Ficino, whose Latin makes my flesh crawl, who stumbles blindly through the superstitious nonsense that marked the fall of art and literature and philosophy, be worshipped as the high priest of Platonism? Why, when I am far their equal? All because my son, whom I raised to bring my ripe old learning new young energy, abandoned me and all liberal pursuits to lash himself and howl at midnight alongside brainless friars, to wander off on stupid pilgrimages for men who can imagine no history older than the words in a missal. Left me just as the night began to fall over my eyes.”

His voice, which had risen high in indignant protest, drops into a tremulous, plaintive note of grief. Romola turns back to look at his blind, aged face, and her heart swells with forgiving pity. She sits back down beside him, laying her hand softly on his knee — too proud to blurt out empty consolations that would sound like she was trying to prove her own worth, but wanting him to feel she is there.

“Yes, Romola,” Bardo says, his heavy rings falling a little too hard on the back of her delicate blue-veined hand, making her bite her lip to keep from wincing. “If Florence remembers nothing else of me, it will remember me as they remember Niccolò Niccoli — a man who gave up chasing vulgar wealth in commerce to dedicate his life to collecting the precious remains of ancient art and wisdom, to leave them as an eternal gift to his fellow citizens, as the old munificent Romans did. Why stop at Florence? If Florence remembers me, the whole world will remember me... But Lorenzo de Medici’s untimely death has thrown everything into chaos. He promised me, he was going to give me a formal bond that my entire collection would keep my name forever, would never be sold off piecemeal to greedy harpies. But there is nothing to stop them now — they will take everything. And for you too, Romola, you will have nothing if we do not make plans. Bernardo del Nero keeps reproaching me for not finding you a suitable husband, a good family match. We will delay no longer. We will think of it now.”

“No, no,

When the household doorman Maso pulled open the heavy oak door and ushered in their two unexpected visitors, Nello the barber first sank into a deep, exaggerated reverence before Romola, then gave the young man beside him a gentle nudge forward, steering Tito Melema across the shadowed, book-lined room to stand before her blind father.

“Messer Bardo,” he said, dropping his usual chattering, streetwise tone for something measured and respectful, “I have the honour to bring you the Greek scholar you have long heard me speak of. He has burned to meet you no less for my accounts of your unrivalled learning and your irreplaceable collection of ancient texts, than out of hope you might patronize him through the hard straits that shipwreck has laid him low in. His name is Tito Melema, and he is at your full service.”

Romola’s astonishment could scarcely have been greater if the stranger had stepped through the door clad in a panther skin and carrying the ivy-wreathed thyrsus of a Greek bacchanal. The crafty barber had never once mentioned the Greek’s age or looks, and the scholarly visitors who came to call on her father were almost all middle-aged, their heads frosted with grey. Only one young, beautiful male face had ever stuck fast in her memory: that of her little brother, who years before had sat her on his knee, kissed her forehead, and vanished from Florence never to return, fair-haired and sun-kissed, just like her own. Her usual quiet, proud self-reliance, hardened by years of listening to her father’s bitter laments over the world’s injustice, was like a thick snowbank holding back a rush of unlooked-for admiration. Tito’s bright face glowed with rich, warm colour above his plain black knee-length tunic, no other ornament to compete with it: he looked like a wreath of spring wildflowers that had dropped straight into the cold, book-bound winter of Romola’s young life, where all she had ever inherited was memories—of a dead mother, a lost brother, the happy days before her father went blind, faint glimmers of old light and love and beauty buried deep in the dark mines of leather-bound tomes, that could never wake to brightness again until some new joy kindled them for her. Still, she returned Tito’s polite bow at the door with her usual pale, proud composure. But as he drew nearer, the snowbank softened, and when he dared glance her way again while Nello spoke, a soft pink flush spread over her cheeks, only to vanish a heartbeat later, as if her own stern will had called it back. Tito’s gaze, though, held a gentle, almost beseeching warmth of admiration that was the kindest possible appeal to a proud, shy woman—almost the only apology a handsome man can ever make for being too handsome. The easy charm of his air came entirely from the fact that he made no demands, no assumptions: he moved like a fleet, soft-coated dark-eyed creature that does not bound away from you in indifference, but unexpectedly rests its chin on your palm and looks up asking to be petted, as if it already loves you.

“Messere, you are welcome,” Bardo said, a faint gracious condescension in his tone. “Misfortune wedded to learning, and most of all to Greek learning, is a letter of credit that ought to win the ear of every educated Florentine. You know well, I am sure, that since your countryman Manuelo Crisolora spread the light of his teaching across the great cities of Italy nearly a hundred years ago, no man can call himself a true scholar if he only knows the second-hand, derivative literature of the Latins. Those lazy students we rightly dismiss as *opici*—barbarians, as the old Romans called those who drew water not from the original fountainhead, but from other men’s urns. And as you can see for yourself, and Nello has surely warned you, I am completely blind: a calamity we Florentines are said to be peculiarly prone to, whether from the sharp spring winds that rush down on us through the Apennine passes, or the sudden jolt from the cool dark of our palazzo interiors to the blazing glare of our summer sun, the same shift they say made so many of the ancient Romans go short-sighted—or some hidden cause we cannot easily guess. But sit, I beg you. Nello, my friend, sit too.”

Bardo waited until his sharp, practiced ear confirmed both visitors had settled into chairs, and Romola had slipped into her usual place at his right hand, then spoke again.

“From what part of Greece do you hail, Messere? I had thought your unhappy country had almost run out of sons who still held any memory of its old glory, though lately the barbarian Sultans have shown they are not unwilling to graft the precious old vine of Greek learning onto the wild stock of their own realm, the very same vine their own raiders once cut down and trampled underfoot. Where in Greece are you from?”

“I sailed last from Nauplia,” Tito answered, “but I have lived in Constantinople and Thessalonica both, and travelled stretches of the mainland almost no Western Christians have visited since the Turks took control. I ought to tell you, though, Messere, I was not born in Greece at all. I was born in Bari, and spent the first sixteen years of my life in Southern Italy and Sicily.”

As Tito spoke, a soft emotion rippled across Bardo’s delicate, lined features like a breeze skimming over still water. He leaned forward, stretched his right hand out toward Romola, and turned his head as if to speak to her, before correcting himself and turning back, his voice soft and shaken.

“Forgive me… but you are young, are you not?”

“I am three and twenty,” Tito said.

“Ah,” Bardo breathed, still quiet, his tone thrumming with subdued excitement. “And you had a father, I am sure, who tended to your earliest lessons—who was a scholar himself, perhaps?”

There was a small pause before Tito answered, and both Romola and Nello saw a faint tremor run through his frame, his lips twitching for a fleeting moment, as if the question had stirred a sharp, unhealed memory.

“Yes,” he said at last, “a father by adoption. He was a Neapolitan, and a man of great accomplishment in both Latin and Greek. But…” he paused again, “he is lost to me now. He died on a voyage he took too rashly to the island of Delos.”

Bardo sank back in his chair at once, too sensitive to press for more details of a grief he could see was still fresh. Romola, who knew every deep old wound her father carried, felt this new acquaintance had somehow slipped past the high walls that had long separated their quiet, book-bound household from the rest of the world, in the space of a few short minutes.

Nello, seeing the conversation had hit a gentle lull that gave him a perfect graceful out, spoke up at once.

“By your leave, it is as clear as the finest Venetian glass that this fine young man has had the very best training! The two Cennini brothers have already set him to work at their Greek printing sheets, and those men are not the sort to start cutting paper before they test the edge of their new tools. They vetted him thoroughly first, I can swear to it. They say there are two things you can never hide: love, and a cough. I say there is a third, and that is ignorance, the moment a man has to do something more useful than just nod his head in agreement. The half-educated barber cannot hide his flaws the second he picks up the shears, is that not right, Messer Bardo? I speak as a man of my trade, but as Luigi Pulci wrote—
‘Pardon me if I err: let he who hears
Translate my common Tuscan to his Latin ears.’”

“Nay, my good Nello,” Bardo said, his tone half friendly half severe, “you are not entirely unlettered, and you might have made far better progress in scholarship if you spent less time lingering at street corners swapping gossip, which all Florentines are far too fond of doing—and more importantly, if you had not stuffed your memory full of that frivolous trash Luigi Pulci writes, the very worst example of all the absurd, incoherent rhymes that have nothing to do with the pure great models of classical antiquity. They are more like those silly *grylli* conceits of old, where men thought hiding a mystic meaning in a work excused making it an ugly, misshapen mess—except Pulci keeps the ugliness, and leaves out all the actual meaning. It is pitiful next to the great epic of Virgil, who as I long argued before Landino ever claimed the idea, wove all the deepest philosophical lessons into a graceful, tightly constructed fable. And I cannot help but think the spread of these babbling, lawless rhymes—even patronized and half enjoyed by Lorenzo himself, who is otherwise a true friend to learning—is a sign that the great bright hopes of this century are going to be snuffed out. That all this is just the misleading prologue to an age worse than the iron age: an age of tinsel and gossamer, where no thought has enough weight to be shaped into something consistent, lasting, and true.”

“Again, I beg your pardon,” Nello said, holding his palms out and shrugging his shoulders. “I find so many good Tuscan phrases popping out of my mouth before I have time to think up their Latin equivalents! Messer Luigi’s rhymes are always slipping off the lips of my customers, that is what corrupts me. And speaking of customers, I have left my shop and my reputation far too long in the hands of my slow apprentice Sandro, who is not even good enough to be called a half-skilled barber—he is just a plain bungler. So by your leave, Messer Bardo, I will take my leave, on the understanding I am at your service any time Maso calls for me. It feels like a thousand years until I get to dress and perfume the damigella’s hair, which deserves to shine in the heavens as a constellation—though it would be a terrible pity to send it so far out of reach.”

Three voices called out friendly farewells to Nello, who retreated with a final bow to Romola and a little beckoning nod to Tito. The sharp-witted barber could see his young favourite had already won Bardo’s full goodwill, and satisfied his introduction had gone off without a hitch, he knew it was time to leave them to talk.

The small burst of irritation Nello’s unlucky quotation had stirred up had cleared Bardo’s mind of the soft, tangled feelings that had paused their conversation earlier, and he spoke to Tito again in his usual calm, steady tone.

“Ah, young man, you are fortunate to have combined all the advantages of travel with those of study. We welcome you here as a man who can bring us fresh news from a land that has become shockingly strange to us, known only through the accounts of restricted trade agents and hasty pilgrims. The days I remember so well, when men like Aurispa and Guarino travelled to Greece as if it were a vast storehouse of treasure, and came back loaded down with manuscripts every scholar was desperate to borrow—though, I am ashamed to say, not always willing to return—those days are long gone. Even the days when learned Greeks fled to our shores for refuge seem farther away than the day I first lost my sight. But surely, young man, your travels were not for nothing, you went searching for the old treasures of antiquity along the way?”

“Most assuredly not,” Tito answered. “On the contrary, my companion—my father—was willing to risk his very life out of sheer zeal for discovering ancient inscriptions and other traces of long lost civilizations.”

“And I trust there is a full record of his research, and all he found,” Bardo said, leaning forward eagerly. “They must be even more precious than the notes of Ciriaco, which I have pored over diligently, even if they are not always as well informed as they might be.”

“There was such a record,” Tito said, “but it was lost, along with every other thing I owned, in the shipwreck I suffered off the coast of Ancona. The only record left is what remains in—my own memory.”

“You must waste no time writing every last bit of it down, young man,” Bardo said, his interest growing by the moment. “I am sure you remember a great deal, if you helped him transcribe it. When I was your age, words etched themselves into my mind as if they were carved there with a graver’s tool. It still astonishes me how capricious my own daughter’s memory is, clinging tight to some things and letting fall all the tiny little details that are the very soul of accuracy, the soul of all true scholarship. But I have no such fears for you, young man, if your training was backed up by the will to learn.”

When Bardo mentioned his daughter’s memory, Tito dared glance over at her, and at the gentle joke of her supposed flaw, his face broke into its brightest, warmest smile—one that Romola’s own face reflected back as surely as sunlight reflects off still water. That soft, shared smile was the most soothing, delightful thing Romola had ever experienced. She had never once dreamed there existed a scholar anywhere in the world who would smile at the flaw she was constantly scolded for, that made her feel like a disappointment to her father. It was like the dawn of a new sense in her: the sense of comradeship. Neither of them looked away at once, as if the smile was something stolen. They held each other’s gaze for a moment, smiling with unguarded, open enjoyment.

She is not nearly as cold and proud as she pretends, Tito thought to himself.

Does he forget little details too, sometimes? Romola wondered. But I hope not, or he will frustrate my father.

But Tito had to turn back to Bardo to answer his question.

“I have had a great deal of practice transcribing texts,” he said, “but in the case of inscriptions copied on those memorable, dangerous journeys, my memory of the exact written characters might have slipped a little. On the plains of the Eurotas, or among the giant stones of Mycenae and Tiryns—especially when the fear of Turkish patrols hangs over you like a hovering vulture—your mind does wander, even if your hand writes down exactly what your eyes see. But I do remember a great deal,” he added, with a modesty that was not entirely false, even if he knew it was perfectly politic, “things that might be of some use, if they were illustrated and corrected by a wider, deeper learning than my own.”

“That is very well spoken, young man,” Bardo said, delighted. “And I will not hold back any of the help I can offer you, if you will share your memories with me. I can see now a work taking shape that will be a perfect, useful supplement to Christoforo Buondelmonte’s *Isolario*, that will stand alongside the *Itineraria* of Ciriaco and the great Ambrogio Traversari. But we must prepare ourselves for slander, young man,” Bardo went on with sudden energy, as if the book was already half written and the enemies were already at the gate. “If your book contains new findings, people will accuse you of forgery. If my explanations clash with some other scholar’s pet interpretation, they will attack our very characters, spread lies about terrible things we did. They will say your mother was a fishmonger, your father was a renegade priest or a hanged criminal. I myself, for making a single mistake in one tiny preposition, once had a whole vicious invective written against me, where I was accused of treachery, fraud, indecency, and even the most hideous crimes. That, my young friend, is what the path of scholarship is strewn with: those are the flowers of learning. Now tell me, I learned much about Byzantium and Thessalonica long ago from Demetrio Calcondila, who only left Florence recently. But you visited far less familiar places, did you not?”

“Yes,” Tito said, eager to steer the conversation away from the personal questions about his adoptive father. “We went on what I can only call a pilgrimage full of danger, to visit places that the West has almost completely forgotten, so far off the usual path of pilgrims that most scholars think they exist only in books. My father always said a new, glorious era for learning would begin when people started looking for commentaries on the ancient writers not in other men’s books, but in the ruins of old cities and temples, in the paths of rivers, on the faces of valleys and mountains.”

“Ah!” Bardo breathed, fervid with excitement. “Your father was no ordinary man. Was he happy? Did he have many friends?” The last words were heavy with unspoken meaning, the pain of Bardo’s own long years of petty quarrels with other scholars.

“No, he made enemies, mostly, I think, because he was impetuously honest. They blocked his advancement, so he lived his whole life in obscurity. And he would never stoop to flatter anyone to get ahead. He could never forgive an injury.”

“Ah,” Bardo said again, the sound a long, deep, resonating note.

“On one of our most dangerous expeditions,” Tito continued, “I remember a hasty, snatched visit to Athens more vividly than any other. We were in such a rush, caught between the double risk of being seized as spies by the Turks, and our galley weighing anchor and sailing off without us before we could get back, that the whole place felt like a fevered dream. The wide plain, the ring of distant mountains, the ruined porticos and columns standing far off as if they were receding from our hurrying footsteps, jammed all tangled together between the tiny homes of Christians reduced to serfdom, and the forts and towers of their Moslem conquerors, who have their stronghold right on the Acropolis.”

“You astonish me,” Bardo said. “I had imagined Athens had been completely destroyed, swept off the face of the earth.”

“No wonder you think that,” Tito said. “Even most Greeks who live outside the borders of Attica know nothing of what Athens looks like now, or *Setine* as the sailors call it. I remember when we rounded the promontory of Sunium, our Greek pilot on the Venetian galley pointed to the mighty columns standing on the summit of the rock—the remains, as you well know, of the great temple to Athena, who once looked down from that high shrine in triumph over her defeated rival Poseidon. That same pilot pointed to those columns and told me ‘that is the school where the great philosopher Aristotle taught.’ And inside Athens itself, the monk who acted as our guide on

Bartolommeo Scala, Secretary of the Florentine Republic — the very man Tito Melema had staked his most urgent hopes on winning over — lived in a grand palazzo steps from the Porta Pinti, the same house later known as the Casa Gherardesca. His coat of arms, emblazoned above the entrance, showed a transverse azure ladder against a gilded field, the word *Gradatim* (Step by Step) curling beneath it: a bold, unashamed proclamation that this former miller’s son had climbed every rung to high honours by his own effort, and saw no reason to pretend otherwise.

The secretary was vain and pompous, yes, but he was also unfailingly honest: he sincerely believed in his own merit, and never bothered with performative modesty. By this May afternoon, he had already reached the topmost rung of his azure ladder. He had held his post as state secretary for twenty years, delivering the great public orations from the ringhiera platform outside the Old Palace as custom dictated, in front of visiting princes and dignitaries, while the republican lion Marzocco was crowned in gold for the occasion and crowds roared *“Viva Messer Bartolommeo!”* He had travelled on embassy to Rome, where he was named titular Senator, Apostolical Secretary, and Knight of the Golden Spur. Eight years prior he had even been elected Gonfaloniere, the highest office any Florentine citizen could hope to attain. As his honours grew, so did his fortune — and so did his gout, that familiar curse of successful, sedentary middle age. The Knight of the Golden Spur now often sat propped on cushions with his aching heel elevated, sheltered in the beautiful loggia he had built overlooking the wide lawns and blooming gardens at the back of his palace. That was exactly where he sat the day he had granted Tito Melema his long-awaited audience.

The golden afternoon sun spilled over the flowers and grass just beyond the loggia’s shade, and Scala had cast aside his heavy, formal silk lucco robe for a loose, light mantle draped over his plain tunic. His lovely daughter Alessandra and her husband, the Greek soldier-poet Marullo, sat on one side of his cushioned chair; two quiet, unpretentious friends sat on the other, the perfect unpressurised audience for his favourite stories. But for all the pleasant surroundings, Scala’s peace was soured by the stack of papers laid out on the table before him: the full, lengthy correspondence exchanged between himself and the great scholar Politian.

It was a common, if odd, human quirk of 15th century Florence to revisit old scholarly quarrels, and share every tedious letter of the back-and-forth with visiting learned friends. Scala had already asked for impartial judgment on this particular exchange half a dozen times before, all of it spurred by a handful of lines written in the most lighthearted, playful tone imaginable. It was a perfectly typical petty Renaissance intellectual feud, and one that mattered greatly right now: as the barber Nello had once joked, every slow-moving friendship needed that sharp little thistle of shared hatred to prod it into action.

The root of the fight was simple enough: Politian, widely hailed as the greatest classical scholar of his age, had once sought Alessandra’s hand in marriage, and been rejected. He had nursed a sharp, well-honed grudge ever since against the over-prosperous, presumptuous secretary who had turned him away as unworthy of his daughter. Scala was a dedicated public servant and a lucky man, the kind of success that always rankles with a snubbed scholar — but the fates had balanced the scales perfectly. He had an unacknowledged itch to be a celebrated author, and he was bad at it: one of those earnest men who penned little “poetical trifles” in their leisure hours purely for their own amusement, then posted them off to every friend they knew, letters being the 15th century’s version of shared literary periodicals. His close circle of friends — men like Marsilio Ficino and Cristoforo Landino, his fellow regulars in the Medici gardens — all praised his Latin prose as elegant and masculine, and the brutal, hyper-critical Joseph Scaliger who would later dismiss his Latin as entirely illiterate was still a full century away, across the Alps.

But no amount of praise from close friends could ever satisfy that quiet coquetry inherent to amateur writing. Scala was desperate for the critical, supercilious Politian — that fellow Medici garden regular who was far from amiable — to acknowledge that a busy statesman could turn out verses of such natural fertility that he would surely rival the best men of letters, if he did not have republic business to attend to.

That fatal moment came. The man you secretly loathe sends you a Latin epigram where he misgenders *culex* — the tiny gnat that buzzes everywhere along the banks of the Arno — and twists his hendecasyllable metre so badly one line has a beat too many, and his poetic conceits are obvious solecisms. Politian pounced the second Scala poked his soft amateur head out of his official state shell. What looked like a friendly correction, wrapped in a playful epigram mocking his grammatical error, turned sharp when Scala fired back a bad joke in Latin verse, referencing Politian’s rejected suit for his daughter.

Politian replied that the verses were delightfully facetious, a pity they were riddled with obvious metrical mistakes. Since Scala had claimed he had written the lines in imitation of a Greek epigram, Politian wrote, he would do him the favour of enclosing his own Greek epigram about the very same gnat — not out of any desire to humble the secretary, of course, purely to instruct him. The little verse contained the fashionable, whimsical conceit common to the era, drawing on the old myth that the gnat, like Venus, was born sprung fully formed from the waters.

Scala’s reply was masterfully passive-aggressive. He protested that his own silly verses were never meant for a man of Politian’s impossibly fastidious taste, the living scholar closest to the perfect grace of the ancient Romans, so refined even sturgeon must taste bland to him. He defended his own lines, of course — though they had been written hastily, no revision, as a silly summer distraction for himself and friends who were happy with mediocre verse, unlike other people who chased fame by pestering booksellers to publish their work. He claimed he barely had enough Greek to parse the literal meaning of Politian’s epigram, let alone appreciate its grace — but it was Politian’s work, so of course it was brilliant. Then, as a petty postscript, he added that the comparison of the gnat to Venus, born from the waters, was a very shaky conceit: Venus might well take offence, and besides, gnats loved warm air, not cold damp water, a fact that ran counter to the old pre-Socratic Thales’ theory that all life sprang from moisture. A fish would have made far more sense as a water-born comparison, he wrote, or an eagle if you referenced flight, or an owl or bat if you wanted a creature that moved in darkness.

Politian’s response was merciless. He wrote back that he had never expected to find frogs and minnows, not sturgeon, served up to him in verse from a high state official, tore every last thread of Scala’s defence of his bad Latin to shreds, and mocked every last one of his stupid, ill-informed criticisms of the Greek epigram as laughable cavils. Virgil himself, he snapped, had explicitly called the gnat the “nurseling of the waters”, and all his silly alternative comparisons were *nihil ad rem* — entirely irrelevant. Just because an eagle could fly higher did not mean a gnat could not fly at all, he jeered. He claimed he was ashamed to waste time on such trivialities, blowing a gnat up to the size of an elephant, but added a sharp parting shot that he never pretended to be a friend to men who flattered him to his face then slandered him behind his back, supporting the quip with a pithy Greek line from Homer that served as gunpowder for his final insult.

The quarrel only escalated from there. The petty logic got no more coherent, but the secretary grew ever more pompous and defensive, and the scholar’s temper grew more venomous. Politian protested that he had only ever tried to hold up a mirror to the overinflated secretary, to make him stop defending his bad Latin against centuries of accepted ancient authority — unless Scala deliberately intended to sink lower and lower in literary reputation the higher he rose in public honours, to give all men of letters a satisfying chance to feel his equal. Scala replied by begging Politian to read his full body of work: he was the most devout admirer of classical antiquity alive, he said, he was positively ashamed of his own century, blushed for its bad taste. It was other men, not him, who chased fame by praising their own trashy work as equal to the divine monuments of the ancients. And as for his public honours that made envious men seethe? He had earned every single one, every step of the way since he arrived in Florence as a penniless boy.

Politian’s final reply was brutal. He was not surprised that Scala found the entire age distasteful, he wrote, because the entire age found Scala distasteful. He had every right to call the ex-miller a branny monster, born in the filth and refuse of the mill-house, perfectly suited to spend his rest of his days turning the family millstones, not writing poetry.

Scala had brought this whole stack of letters out specifically for Tito’s visit. Here was a new young Greek scholar, untainted by any old loyalties to either man, whose opinion he could trust to be fully dispassionate — and there was nothing he wanted more than a neutral, expert verdict on Politian’s overpraised Greek gnat epigram.

After the usual pleasantries about Tito’s years of travelling the Mediterranean, a long shared look at his collection of ancient carved gems, and a natural drift from a discussion of the late Lorenzo de’ Medici’s famous obsession with collecting such ancient art to the general state of classical study in modern Florence, the talk of Politian was inevitable. Yes, the company agreed, he was a man of eminent ability — but absurdly arrogant. He styled himself a new Hercules, whose whole job was to go around smiting every silly literary “monstrosity” of the age, and sent unsigned letters to his elders as if they were miraculous divine revelations that could not possibly have any human source. For all his mockery of lesser men who thought they wrote like Cicero just because they ended every sentence with *esse videtur*, he had fallen straight into the opposite trap, chasing obscure, weird words and over-affected phrases. Even his much-celebrated *Miscellanea* did not hold up to close scrutiny.

Tito had flipped through the *Miscellanea* only days before, and he was happy to say exactly what the secretary wanted to hear, purely out of his natural instinct to please even before he considered what he stood to gain. He proved himself more than worthy of being named judge over the great gnat quarrel. He picked apart Politian’s vaunted Greek epigram line by line, found every weak point, every overstretched conceit, proving beyond a doubt that Politian had been lying when he pretended the “transmarini”, the native Greeks across the sea, would think nothing of it — he had accidentally been telling the truth in that false show of modesty.

Scala was delighted. This wise young judge, he was sure, could appreciate sharp satire even in the vulgar Florentine tongue, and Scala — that excellent man who never sought publicity through booksellers, who always kept a stock of “hasty uncorrected trifles” on hand to serve to visitors like iced sherbet on a hot day or spiced cordial on a cold one — pulled out a handful of his own private sonnets, gentle satires poking fun at Politian’s well-known foibles, that he did not want shared anywhere else, but which would certainly amuse the whole party.

By the time Tito took his leave, Scala had pressed him to come back again soon, urgently. His gems were fascinating: the agate with its natural *lusus naturae* marking that looked exactly like Cupid riding a lion, and the “Jew’s stone” carved with a lion-headed serpent, both of which the secretary agreed to buy immediately. The serpent stone, Scala explained, would make an excellent amulet to tuck beneath his tunic against his right breast, a proven protection against the savage gout pains that were growing worse and worse, making it obvious he would not survive the attacks long if he did not have a stock of magically potent rings and charms on hand. But Tito himself, he assured the young Greek, was far more interesting than any trinket. He had won his initial introduction through Bardo de’ Bardi, that old worthy scholar and long-time acquaintance of Scala’s (a little too fond of overestimating his own importance, as so many of the secretary’s friends were), but now he was invited back on the strength of his own obvious brilliance.

The interview could not have gone more perfectly for Tito. He wandered out through the Porta Pinti to laugh freely to himself at the sheer absurdity of the great gnat quarrel, and felt the sun warm on his shoulders. Fortune had taken him firmly by the hand, at last. There was no way she would turn her back on him now.

Northern folk think nothing of rising at dawn on Midsummer morning, tracing dew on the grassy edges of dusty lanes, spotting fresh green shoots among the dark mature foliage of oak and fir in the coppice, and leaning over a gate to gaze out at the newly shorn meadow, without once pausing to mark that the day is the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist.

That could never be true of a Florentine—least of all a 15th-century Florentine. For him, that sunrise gilding the Arno held a peculiar, unmistakeable warmth; the peal of the city’s bells was no empty clanging, but a voice proclaiming the greatest summer festival of Florence, the feast day of San Giovanni.

San Giovanni had been Florence’s patron saint for at least eight hundred years, ever since Lombard Queen Theodolinda commanded her subjects to venerate him above all others. Old chronicler Villani swore he could trace that honour back even further, to the reign of Constantine the Great and Pope Sylvester: the Florentines of that era cast down their old idol Mars, but dared not treat him with disrespect. They consecrated their grand, noble temple to God and the “Blessed Lord Saint John,” yet respectfully placed the old war god on a high tower overlooking the Arno, having found ancient records stating he had been chosen as the city’s tutelar deity under such powerful astral influence that breaking or insulting him would bring unthinkable ruin and chaos down on Florence. By the 15th century, that old cautious regard for the pagan god of spear and shield had long faded: no frowning Mars loomed over the Arno’s banks anymore, and the Republic’s defences were held to lie not in weapons, but in its craft and its coffers. Mercenaries could be hired for gold florins, and every single gold florin bore the image of San Giovanni.

Florence had won no small measure of wealth and glory in the long centuries between that old religious shift and the present day. There had been strife and bloodshed, of course: fighting between Guelf and Ghibelline, Black and White factions, orthodox sons of the Church and heretic Paterini, not to mention floods, famine, and plague. But the city had also conquered walled cities once far mightier than itself, most of all its hated rival Pisa, whose marble palaces were too tall and beautiful, whose merchant ships were too well known across the Greek and Italian coasts. Florence’s name had grown prouder by the year, recognised across every court in Europe and even deep into Africa, famed for its purest gold coinage, its finest dyes and textiles, its peerless scholarship and poetic genius, and its sharp, practical wits for statesmanship and banking. A Pope with a taste for epigrams had once dubbed Florentines “the fifth element,” so omnipresent was their influence. All that high destiny, even if it owed something to the stars and the Madonna dell’Impruneta, was widely credited to San Giovanni, whose face was stamped on every one of those fair gold florins.

It was only fitting, then, that the feast of San Giovanni—an ancient Church holiday already venerable in the days of Saint Augustine—should be Florence’s most beloved celebration. The vigil the night before was kept in strict old Florentine fashion, full of street dancing, raucous jesting, a little harmless stone-throwing and broken window panes, and spectacular street pageants only a city that employed the ingenious engineer and architect Cecca could produce. Cecca designed floats that looked exactly like the saintly figures seen in Perugino’s paintings: surrounded by their almond-shaped halos, floating on clouds of woven cloth, flanked by winged cherubs, they seemed to bring a piece of heaven itself down into the narrow streets of Florence. Even more marvellous were the giant saint figures, moving slowly and mysteriously through the crowds like colossi stepped down from the city’s domes and tribunes: men on stilts, steady of foot, enlarged by huge sacred masks and stuffed shoulders, escorted by ranks of attendant angels. To think only of the mechanics behind the magic was miserably unimaginative, even impious—for was there not a spark of sacred virtue in the images of holy things? Once the saints had processed past, honouring the patron saint outside his baptistery gates, came a troupe of playful devils in black costumes, fitted out with claws and whips, putting on impromptu farces of mock beatings and torn clothing, to keep the vigil in their own raucous way. The Tuscan mind slipped as easily from devout reverence to burlesque fun as water slips around a sharp corner.

On the feast day itself, all the tributary tributes from Florence’s dependent cities, districts, and villages—conquered, protected, or long subject to her rule—were brought in grand procession to the shrine of San Giovanni in the old octagonal baptistery, where every Florentine had once been marked with the sign of the cross in holy chrism on his brow. The whole city, from white-haired elders to lisping toddlers, dressed in their finest to see the great sights, and when the sun slanted low enough to cool the streets, the famous Corso race began: unshod horses draped in rich trappings thundered all the way across the city from the Porta al Prato in the northwest, through the Mercato Vecchio, to the Porta Santa Croce in the southeast, where the most magnificent palio, a banner of velvet and brocade lined with silk and trimmed with gold fringe fit for the city that half-clothed the whole wealthy world, waited on a triumphal car for the winning horse’s owner. The rest of the day was given over to more dancing, feasting, and celebration; an early 15th century chronicler wrote that there were so many weddings, gatherings, piping, song, and fine ornaments that the day made earth seem indistinguishable from Paradise.

In the year 1492, that illusion was a little harder to pull off. Lorenzo the Magnificent, subtle and beloved, was dead, and his arrogant, reckless son Piero had taken his place. It was an ill turn for Florence, unless one believed that a bad rider is easier to throw from a horse, and the public regret for Lorenzo was already fading beneath murmurings of desire for broader, fairer government that could stamp out corruption and revive the good old quarrelsome, striving days when Florence built its great palaces, fielded its own citizen soldiers, and drove out would-be tyrants at sword point, proud to keep its word even at its own loss. Lorenzo was dead, Pope Innocent was dying, and a messy Neapolitan succession dispute paired with the schemes of an ambitious, intriguing Milan was set to set all of Italy at each other’s throats before long. The times were growing difficult—but that was all the more reason the Republic should keep its traditional religious festivals alive.

That Midsummer morning in 1492 was no less bright than any other year. Long before the sun climbed high, all the symbolic tribute offerings gathered for the grand procession at their starting point in the Piazza della Signoria, that famous public square where the massive, turreted Palazzo Vecchio (the ancient Palace of the People) still stands, alongside the broad open Loggia built by Orcagna, the stage for every grand state ceremony. The sky stretched over them like the fairest blue tent, and the bells swung so vigorously that any evil spirit sensible enough to fear their noise must long since have fled the city. Windows and terraced roofs brimmed over with faces, the sombre stone houses hung draped in bright textiles, the soaring tower of the Palazzo Vecchio, the older square tower of the Bargello, and the spire of the nearby Badia all stood sentinel over the scene. Down below, on the broad polygonal stone flags of the piazza, the procession was arrayed: banners flying, horses draped in rich trappings, and gigantic ceri—great tapers so tall they were widely nicknamed “towers,” the grand descendants of the small torches the early Church used to worship by in the Roman catacombs. The procession had set out at dawn, so they could traverse the wide, sunbaked squares before the summer heat grew too fierce, before the long pomp completed its pilgrimage to the Piazza di San Giovanni.

In that final piazza, the city and its ingenious engineer Cecca had built a second shelter above the crowds: the whole Piazza del Duomo, stretching from the central octagonal baptistery to the cathedral façade and the surrounding house walls, was draped at a height of forty feet in rich blue cloth, stitched through with neatly sewn yellow lilies and familiar family and guild coats of arms. Bunches of colourful banners hung at graceful angles under this manmade blue sky, dappling the waiting spectators in rainbow light. They leaned from the windows, lined the narrow edge of the pavement, and craned their necks for the procession to arrive.

One of those spectators was Tito Melema. Bright and at ease amid all the surrounding celebration, he sat at the window of the room above Nello the barber’s shop, his right elbow propped on the red drapery hanging from the window sill, his head tilted back against his right hand, his curls pushed back against his ear. His face wore that soft, lively blandness that made him equally popular among men and women: no excitability, no gloom, no trace of the unease that comes with vain insecurity or excessive high spirits, no furrow of resentment or anger marring his brow. The only change in him from the two months since he first stumbled into the city in his weather-stained old tunic and hose was that soft new glow of good fortune, like a flower that has just drunk in a whole morning of warm sun. Nello, dressed in his holiday best, stood tucked in the narrow angle between Tito’s chair and the window frame, and at his elbow was young Pietro Cennini, the scholarly proofreader, not his practical builder brother Domenico. Tito glanced down at the crowds below, then up at the cluster of friends gathered around him in the room, most of whom had come straight from watching the procession’s start in the Piazza della Signoria. The painter Piero di Cosimo had the whole group laughing at his over-dramatic complaints about the bells’ noise: no amount of stuffing in his ears could block the vibration that hummed in his skull, he swore, and he would hide in the most remote valley in Valdarno during feast days if he were not a painter, forced to lie in wait for the rare, perfect shades of colour that only floated through the air when processions of banners wound through a gathered crowd.

Tito had just turned his laughing face away from the whimsical painter to watch the small, lively dramas playing out among the spectators lining the pavement, when at the corner of the marble Duomo steps almost directly opposite Nello’s shop, he spotted a man’s face tilted up towards him, staring with a gaze far more deliberate and loaded with meaning than the casual glance of a passing stranger. It was the tonsured head of a Dominican friar, rising above the order’s standard black mantle and white tunic, a sight common enough in Florence—but the look struck Tito as strangely familiar. There was a faint, warm echo of some memory attached to it, not at all unpleasant, but he could not for the life of him recall any pleasant association he had ever had with a monk. The glance and that flicker of recognition lasted no longer than a flash of lightning.

“Nello!” Tito called out hastily, only to add a moment later in disappointment, “Ah, he’s turned away. That tall, thin friar heading up the steps. I wanted you to tell me if you know anything of him.”

“One of the Preaching Friars,” Nello said carelessly. “You don’t expect me to know the private life of every black crow that flies past, do you?”

“I swear I recognise his face,” Tito said. “It’s an unusual one.”

“What, did you think it was our Fra Girolamo? He’s far too tall. Savonarola never shows himself off like that by random chance.”

“Besides, that loud-barking ‘hound of the Lord’ isn’t even in Florence right now,” added Francesco Cei, the popular local poet. “Piero de’ Medici dropped him a hint to take his ranting prophecies on a little trip out of town for a while.” The reference was a pun on the Dominican order’s official name, Domini Canes, the Hounds of the Lord, even used by the order itself in its own frescoes.

“The friar doesn’t rant or prophesy against any man,” a middle-aged man seated at the other corner of the window put in. “He only preaches against vice. If you take that as an attack on your poems, Francesco, that’s hardly Fra Girolamo’s fault.”

“Ah, he’s gone into the Duomo now,” Tito said, his eyes fixed eagerly on the figure. “No, I wasn’t making that mistake, Nello. Your Fra Girolamo has a high nose and a heavy lower lip. I saw him once—he’s not handsome, but this man…”

“Enough of your descriptions!” Cennini cut in. “Hark! Look! The horsemen and the banners are here. That standard there, carried on the horse draped in white trappings—the one with the red eagle clutching a green dragon in its talons, and the red lily over the eagle’s head—that’s the Gonfalon of the Guelf party, and the cavaliers riding close around it are the party’s chief officers. It’s one of our proudest banners, grumble as we will. It means the triumph of the Guelfs, which means the triumph of Florentine will, which means the triumph of the common popolani.”

“Nay, go on, Cennini,” the middle-aged man at the window laughed. “Which means the triumph of the fat popolani over the lean, which in turn means the triumph of the very fattest popolano over everyone less fat than him.”

“Cronaca, you’re getting sententious,” the printer said. “Fra Girolamo’s preaching will spoil you, make you take life by the wrong handle. Trust me, your architectural cornices will lose half their beauty if you start mixing bitterness into them. That’s the harsh German maniera style you used to rail at when you came back from Rome. The next palace you build, we’ll find you trying to carve the frate’s doctrines straight into the stonework.”

“That’s a fine show of cavaliers,” Tito said, already having learned the best way to endear himself to Florentines. “But are there not strangers riding among them? I see foreign costumes.”

“Assuredly,” Cennini said. “You’re looking at ambassadors from France, Milan, and Venice, and behind them are English and German nobles. It’s tradition for all distinguished foreign visitors to pay their respects to San Giovanni by riding in the train of that gonfalon. For my part, I think our Florentine cavaliers sit their horses as well as any of those rough northern cut-and-thrust men, whose wits live in their heels and their saddles. That Venetian rider over there, I’d wager he’d feel far more comfortable perched on the back of a dolphin. We know a thing or two about horsemanship here, we outshine all of Italy in the Giostra jousting games, and the fortunes we pour into them. But you’ll see a far finer show of our leading citizens soon, Melema. My own brother is marching with the officers of the Zecca, the Mint.”

“The banners are the far better sight,” Piero di Cosimo said, forgetting the bells entirely in his delight at the winding river of colour from the approaching tributary standards. “Florentine men are nothing special; from this distance they just look like a sorry patch of sallow flesh above black clothing. But those banners, with their velvet, satin, minever, and brocade, the endless play of soft light and shadow shifting over their surfaces? Pah! All human talk and doings are a tame joke. The only true passionate life lives in form and colour.”

“Ay Piero, if Satan himself could paint, you’d sell your soul to learn his secrets,” Nello teased. “Though there’s little chance of that, seeing even the blessed angels are terrible at chiaroscuro, if we go by their masterpiece, the Madonna Nunziata.”

“There go the banners of Pisa and Arezzo,” Cennini said. “Look at that sullen-faced man carrying the Pisa standard! You might as well bring your banner to our San Giovanni with good grace, Messer Pisano. The old proverb says ‘Pisans false, Florentines blind’—the second half of that no longer holds true. Behind them come the ensigns of all our subject towns and signories, Melema. They’ll all hang in the baptistery until this day next year, when they’ll be replaced with new ones.”

“They are a beautiful sight,” Tito agreed. “San Giovanni must be as pleased with that fine Italian loom work as Minerva was with her sacred peplos, especially since he’s always content to wear so little drapery himself. But those whirling tower-like ceri would soon make me fall out this window in sympathetic vertigo.”

The “towers” Tito spoke of were the most beloved part of the procession for the Florentine populace. These giant ceri, some so large they had to be mounted on wheels, were not solid wax, but hollow frames of wood and pasteboard, gilded, carved, and painted with successive circles of figures: knights on horseback, foot soldiers with lance and shield, dancing maidens, animals, trees, and fruit, “all things that could delight the eye and the heart,” as one old chronicler put it. Men stood inside their hollow frames to whirl them continuously as they moved, creating a dizzying phantasmagoric effect that made the whole line of spinning painted circles blur into a wonderful, disorienting spectacle.

“Pestilenza!” Piero di Cosimo said, stepping back from the window. “Those whirling circles stacked one on top of the other are worse than all the bells jangling put together. Let me know when the last taper has passed.”

“Nay, you’ll want us to call you when the country folk come carrying their torches, surely,” Nello said. “You wouldn’t miss the peasants from the Mugello and the Casentino, the ones your favourite Leonardo da Vinci could draw a hundred grotesque sketches of.”

“No,” Piero said firmly. “I won’t look at a thing until the car of the Zecca comes past. I’ve seen more than enough clowns carrying lopsided tapers, cowled and un-cowled, to last me a lifetime.”

“Here it comes, then, Piero—the car of the Zecca,” Nello called out after a short interval, as the smaller, trailing ceri trundled slowly past the window.

“Fediddio!”

Tito stepped down into the heaving, sun-warmed crowd of the piazza, and even though he’d brushed off Nello’s teasing question about his chance acquaintance earlier, he found himself nursing a faint, passing wish as he rounded the square toward the Corso degli Adimari. He hoped he might spot the pair of bright blue eyes that had looked up at him from beneath the simple square of white linen that served peasant girls as their holiday hood on feast days. He knew full well the face belonged to Tessa, but he’d never had any reason to tell Nello that. What business of the barber’s was it, anyway? Tito had a natural, uncalculating gift for reticence—an instinct that made him hoard tiny, trivial bits of unshared experience the way others might hide a pretty pebble they’d found on a walk, even when there was no actual secret to guard.

That soft half-wish for a glimpse of Tessa vanished almost at once, replaced by the niggling, unplaceable memory of the gaunt friar who had stared at him so intently in the crowd. Why would that sickly, fasting fanatic single him out of all the hundreds of people in the square? Where across all his years of wandering could he have seen that face before? Foolishness, Tito told himself, swatting the thought away like a cobweb tickling his cheek. Vague half-memories were just annoyances, and he had far pleasanter things to occupy his mind.

By the time he turned off the Corso degli Adimari down a narrow side street, he was only impatient that the sun had climbed so high, that the festa procession had kept him far longer than he’d meant to stay from the rooms in the Via de’ Bardi, where he knew he was anxiously expected. He could picture the scene before he even stepped through the door: the quiet, diffused joy that would light up blind Bardo’s face like glow seeping through alabaster, the faint pink flush that would rise on Romola’s cheeks and throat—no lessening of her quiet, regal dignity, only the soft, exquisite spark of womanly sensitivity, sharpened by that strange, paradoxical mix of gravity and boyish unguarded frankness that made her smile feel like a gift. They were the dearest of comrades when they sat around Bardo’s chair, she turning to him constantly for the Greek and classical references her father could no longer chase down, but Tito always felt a quiet, unnameable awe in her presence, a reverence he had no words for, the kind of worship old pagans might have offered a great nature goddess: not all-knowing, but rooted in a depth of life and primordial goodness far older and more profound than any book learning. They had never been alone together, and he could not even imagine the usual silly, stilted scenes of courtship between them. He only dared to half-dream the impossible: that one day Romola would be the first to tell him she loved him.

He remembered once in Greece, leaning over a sun-warmed stone wall, a little black-eyed peasant girl who had set down her water pot had crept shyly closer, and held up her round olive cheek for him to kiss, no guile, no pretense. Tito was used to affection that came unbidden like that. But Romola’s love would never come that way. Would it ever come at all? She was the highest, ripest apple on the topmost bough, and he had set his whole heart on reaching it. He was young, fresh, impressible, not fiery or passionate, but drawn to her as naturally as white irises reflected in a sunlit clear stream. He had no coxcomb’s false pride; he knew full well she was far above him, the way any grown man might feel small standing before a large-eyed, earnest child.

Even so, the run of extraordinary good luck he’d had in Florence would have been enough to turn a less steady man’s head. Bartolommeo Scala, the city’s sharp, powerful secretary, had taken a marked liking to him after their first auspicious meeting, and a circle of learned patrons had agreed to found a dedicated Greek chair for him, just as Lorenzo de’ Medici had once installed the great refugee scholar Demetrio Calcondila in the post, to give the arrogant, over-confident young humanist Politian a much-needed dose of humility and proper competition. It was all perfectly respectable, no slight to Politian, just a testament to Florence’s zeal for true learning for its youth. Tito was sailing under the fairest wind imaginable. Everyone who met him liked his easy, unpretentious bearing; no one resented his rapid rise. He was handsome, he could play the lute and troll a gay drinking song, make even the driest Greek epics feel like a holiday pastime for the sons of Florence’s richest noble houses. He would soon be welcome in the very highest circles, the ones where the family silver stacked higher than the pattern on the centre of your dinner plates, where the Signory had not yet forbidden the wearing of richest imported brocade.

And that was not all his good fortune. He had sold every last jewel he had carried with him from the Aegean wreck, save for the single plain ring he would never part with, and he now held five hundred full gold florins in his possession.

That moment when the sum was handed over to him was the first serious crisis of his easy, good-natured life. A thought he had been trying to ignore, chasing him for weeks like a shadow, finally rushed at him and seized him. He had to stop, and choose: obey that thought, or turn away, and take an irrevocable step he could never undo.

He was in his small rented lodging above Nello’s shop, when old Bernardo Cennini the printer and goldsmith handed him the last quota of the payment, on behalf of Bernardo Rucellai who had bought his two most valuable gems.
“Ecco, giovane mio!” the old man said, nodding, “You’ve got a tidy little fortune here. Take my advice, let me place these florins somewhere safe, where they can earn interest and multiply, instead of slipping through your fingers on banquets and the silly follies every Florentine youth chases. Too many scholars, the likes of our Pietro Crinito who thinks his learning has to smell of perfumes and be stitched with brocade, squander every coin with one hand till they’re forced to beg with the other. I’ve brought you the money, and you get to choose wisely or foolishly. We Florentines don’t count a man part of any guild till he’s proven his skill and been matriculated—and no man matriculates in the art of living till he’s been well tempted. If you decide to put your florins out at interest, send word to me tomorrow. A scholar may well marry, and ought to have something set aside for the morgengabe, the gift he gives his bride the morning after their wedding. Addio.”

When Cennini closed the door behind him, Tito’s smile died right off his face. He stared down at the pile of glowing florins on the table, his thumbs tucked into his belt, completely still, his entire consciousness fixed on the rising image in his mind.

A man’s ransom. He had heard that saying once, that five hundred florins was more than enough to buy a man’s freedom from Turkish slavery. What if that man—Baldassarre Calvo, the father who had pulled a seven-year-old Tito out of the Naples gutters, saved him from blows and beggary, raised him tenderly, taught him everything he knew—was at this very moment toiling under the hot sun on some distant Ottoman coast, hewing wood and drawing water, getting struck and buffeted for being old and slow and not quick enough? What if he was telling himself, Tito will find me. He only had to take our manuscripts and jewels to Venice, sell them, and he will never rest till he finds me.

If he knew for certain that was true, could he look at the five hundred florins on the table and say, I will stay here in Florence, where the soft airs of promised love and distinction blow around me. I will not risk everything for his sake. No, he could not—if it were certain. But nothing was less certain. The companion galley that had escaped the Turkish raiders had reported the pirate attack on their ship bound for Delos, said there had been bloodshed, that a man had been seen falling overboard. But no one knew who survived, no one knew what had become of any of the passengers amid a thousand possible misfortunes. Tito himself had barely escaped drowning in the wreck that had washed him up on the Italian coast; he knew better than most how easily all plans could be snuffed out by random disaster.

If he left Florence now, went to Venice, secured letters of passage from the authorities, set sail for the Archipelago, what would most likely happen? He would be captured himself, spend every last florin on permits and bribes and fruitless searches, and be a destitute wanderer again—with no more jewels left to sell to buy his own freedom. He was not a boy anymore, he was in the prime of his youth, after years of scraping by as a beggar-scholar. Why should he throw away all the bright promise of love and respectability he had built here, for a fool’s chase that might lead nowhere? The promise would never come back again.

“And yet,” he murmured to himself, “if I was certain Baldassarre was alive, and that I could free him, no matter what the exertion or the peril, I would go now—now that I have the money. I would go to Bardo and Scala this very hour, and tell them the whole truth.”

He did not say the rest out loud, not even to himself: that if either of those men knew the whole truth, there would be no choice for him at all. Baldassarre was the rightful owner of every jewel he had sold, and Tito had always spoken of him only as “lost,” equivocating, never admitting the full possibility that he was enslaved. Every Greek of standing he had ever known would give up everything, sail across half the world, beg kings and popes for help to free a relative from Turkish slavery. Public opinion would not see his choice as anything less than cowardly cruelty.

This was the first time he had ever truly talked to himself, confronted his own actions clearly, instead of drifting along after every impulse of the moment. The impulse to hide half the truth had sprung up on its own, and he had never stopped to examine it till now. What was the point of telling the whole story? He had thought more than once, since he left Nauplia, that it was a relief to be free of Baldassarre. He had always been exacting as he aged, prying constantly into Tito’s thoughts to make sure they measured up to his own wild, exaggerated expectations. A heavy-browed, bald old man of sixty, whose whole life of ideas had narrowed down to the same repeated, monotonous obsessions—how could anyone find that charming? The world does not bow down to faded, worn old men, no matter how noble their hearts, unless they have the philosopher’s stone to make them wealthy and powerful. Only the love rooted in shared memories, the slow, sweet balm of fidelity and forbearance, can make a home for them.

And Tito had those memories, too. Far back in his earliest childhood, Baldassarre had pulled him out of the mud, given him warm bread, held him on his knee, been his whole world. Everyone had praised Tito as the perfect reward for that lonely man’s devotion: bright, gentle, quick to learn, no rough edges at all. No one had ever thought that gratitude would fail him, when the great test came.

But it was not certain Baldassarre was enslaved. It was not certain he was even alive.

“Do I not owe something to myself?” Tito shifted his shoulders, the first movement he had made in minutes, staring at the florins. “Before I throw everything away, and run all the risks I have only just escaped, I need at least a reasonable hope. Am I supposed to spend my whole life wandering the Aegean chasing a ghost? I believe he is dead. Cennini was right about my florins. I will give them to him tomorrow to invest.”

When he carried that resolution out the next morning, he had made his first permanent choice in the game of his life. From that moment onward, he could not help but wish it were true that Baldassarre was dead, that there was no man alive who could expose his secret, no one to make him ashamed of what he had chosen. Under every guilty secret a whole brood of guilty wishes is hidden, feeding on the darkness, spreading their unwholesome infection. The damage of a wrong choice lies less in the deed itself than in how it twists your desires, enlists your whole self-interest on the side of falsity. Just as public confession purifies the soul by sweeping away all hope in lies, letting you stand simple and noble again.

All those small, scattered rills of selfishness that Tito had let trickle through his life till now had merged into a deep, permanent channel that no one could dam up anymore. He had never before definitely decided to abandon the search for Baldassarre. Now he had given himself a clear excuse to never go. He had chosen a secret he would be ashamed to admit to anyone else, ashamed of in a heartbeat if Baldassarre ever stood alive before him. The quiet inner shame, the echo of all humanity’s shared moral code, burned hot in his cheeks even as he stubbornly repeated to himself that his old benefactor was dead, that any search for him was utterly hopeless.

Three weeks to the day had passed since Tito handed his bag of gold florins over to Cennini, and as he turned his steps toward the Via de’ Bardi on the feast of San Giovanni, no trace of care furrowed his brow. He moved in easy harmony with everything immediately around him, his nature too bright, too unapprehensive, to let distant, hidden troubles sink into him as a lingering dread. Stepping out of the blistering Tuscan sunshine into the cool shadow of a narrow side lane, he tugged off his simple black berretta, the cap with its upturned lappet perched lightly on his brown curls, tossed his head back to catch the moving air, and pushed his hair loose from his forehead. There was no brand of duplicity on his brow, no overt stamp of candor either: only a smooth, finely shaped, unlined young square of skin. The slow, absent glance he cast over the upper house windows held no more guile than it did forced ingenuousness—no more than belongs to the wide, unclouded gaze of a youthful, open eye, clear as polished glass, set round a rich dark brown iris, the sclera tinted soft cerulean, streaked with the faint shadows of long lashes. His whole bearing radiated one clear, unmistakeable truth: he was free of restless vanity, awkward obligation, or simmering unease, and so the holiday-makers that passed him turned to look and admire, their tribute entirely unforced and willing.

The festa’s stir had seeped even into the narrowest back lanes by that hour. The crowd that had been pressed tight along the procession route was now spilling out in every direction, their loose, unguided holiday energy fraying at the edges, half-ready to pick on a stray passerby for a bit of cheap amusement. Near the hostelry of the *Bertucce*—the Baboons—Tito found the throng thickening, a knot of laughing onlookers clustered around something holding their attention. He was about to slip down an adjoining side street when a thin, trembling childish cry cut through the din of laughter: “Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!”

He pushed straight through the crowd at once. The distressed voice belonged to a young contadina, her white peasant hood fallen off in her struggle to yank her hands free of a man in the bright patchwork motley of a cerretano, a travelling conjuror. The man grinned and cajoled her, trying to soothe her while the spectators chided her for being stubborn, plying every last Florentine dialect term for simpleton they could recall. For a second her face was turned away, and Tito only saw her light brown hair braided tight and fastened with a long silver pin, but the next twist of the struggle brought her face full to his. It was Tessa, her baby features streaked with tears, her underlip quivering. She saw him too, and a sudden blazing hope burst through the mist of her tears, the same look a lost small child gets when it spots a familiar hand reaching for it through a crowd of strangers.

Tito shouldered his way through the circle of gawkers in two strides, grasped Tessa gently by the waist, and said, “Loose this child! What right have you to hold her against her will?”

The conjuror, whose sharp face had eyes, eyebrows, nostrils and jawline all tilted upward in a permanent impish slant, showed his small neat teeth in a grin, not unkind, as he let go her wrists. He shrugged and tilted his shoulders forward in a half-apologetic, half-protesting gesture. “I mean her no harm in the world, Messere, ask all these good folk! I only wanted her to help me show my tricks—her sweet little kitten face would have reassured everyone my game was honest, and I promised her a lapful of sugared confetti for her trouble. But what’s that, now? This fine signor has better confetti at hand, and she knows it.”

A ripple of laughter ran through the onlookers, prompted by the way Tessa clung tight to Tito’s arm as he drew her away from the conjuror. Tito foresaw the crowd would tease them both for ruining their little show, so he hurried her away down the side street to shake off the jests. The conjuror tossed her fallen white hood over her head, calling after her to bear him no malice, and come back when her signor didn’t need her any longer.

Tito pushed quickly toward the next cross street, a little vexed at the delay that was making him late for the Via de’ Bardi, already mentally planning to find a place to leave the little lost girl safe. The passersby in the next lane tossed them merry holiday jests at the unusual pair, and Tito paused to speak to her gently but hurriedly: “Now little one, where were you going? Did you come to the festa alone?”

“Ah no!” Tessa’s face crumpled again, fresh tears welling. “I got lost in the crowd at San Pulinari! My mother and my stepfather were right beside me, then someone pushed me along and I couldn’t stop, and they’re gone. He’ll beat me, he will, I don’t know where they are. Please don’t leave me!” A loud sob burst out before she could stop it.

Tito hurried her on toward the Badia, the old church not far off. The cloister entrance at the back was open, though the church itself was locked for the midday hour, and he paused there, asking her if she had any cousin or acquaintance in Florence she could go to, or if she was too shaken to walk alone after her fright. He told her he was in a great hurry to cross the Arno to Oltrarno, but he could walk her part of the way if she could tell him where she lived. Tessa sobbed that the conjuror was the devil, she was too frightened to walk alone, and she had no one in the city. She wailed that her stepfather was cruel, her mother favored her younger sister, no one was ever kind to her, and she was sure she’d be beaten half to death for straying.

It would have been brutal to leave her there, and Tito’s nature was all soft, gentle aversion to anything painful. He leaned down, brushed the tears off her round cheek with his thumb, and kissed it lightly. “My poor little Tessa, stop crying. Tell me where your home is, and I’ll walk you part of the way back.”

Tessa’s sobs faded almost at once. The cruel conjuror, the beating waiting for her, all seemed far away. “I live past Peretola, by the big pear tree, out the Por del Prato gate,” she whispered, looking up at him with wide blue eyes, her face soft and calm as a cherub’s.
