She held tight to his arm as they set off, and Tito decided to take the main road toward the Prato meadow, hoping they might run into her distracted mother in the holiday crowds. The streets were full of roistering apprentices jealous of his pretty little charge, bold-eyed women in their mandated yellow veils, beggars tipping their caps for alms with grins, and rough loiterers, and Tessa’s fear of being lost was far from unreasonable.

By the time they reached the Piazza d’Ognissanti they were both hot from their fast walk, and they sank down on a weathered stone bench built against the wall of an old palace to catch their breath. “Holy Virgin, I’m glad we got away from those people,” Tessa said. “But I wasn’t scared. I knew you’d keep me safe.” Tito laughed, asking her why she trusted him so fully, and she said he looked exactly like the saints in the frescoes of Paradise, who were all good and never hurt anyone.

Nearby a street stall was piled high with ripe apricots, honey cakes, and colored comfits, and Tito bought a whole armful, tipping them all into the fold of Tessa’s holiday apron. He told her they would walk on to the Prato, and then she could surely go the rest of the way home alone with no danger. Tessa prattled on happily the whole time, telling him about the two cows and the mules her family kept, her little black-faced pet kid goat she loved more than anything, and how only the parish priest spoke softly to her when she went to confession. Tito smiled to himself, thinking this sweet little idyl was lovelier than the pastoral poems Lorenzo de Medici’s circle gushed over, and he was already so late to the Via de’ Bardi that another half hour of delay would hardly matter.

The wide open Prato meadow inside the city walls was empty and quiet at that midday hour, the Florentine youths who usually played their rough calcio football there all gone inside for their festa dinners. Tito paused there, telling Tessa he must turn back now, and she could walk the rest of the way home alone, promising he would meet her at the market the next morning to buy a cup of milk from her and make sure she was safe. But Tessa’s face fell, her mouth turning down in disappointment, and she said she’d thought they could sit under a tree just outside the gate and eat all the treats he’d bought her together.

Tito laughed, kissed both her cheeks, and told her she was a little siren tempting him to ruin. He turned and started walking away, but he looked back after two steps and saw her shoulders heaving with a held-back sob, apricots rolling right out of her loose apron onto the grass. He couldn’t leave her like that. He walked back, picked up the fallen fruit, and told her fine, they would find a tree to sit under for a little while.

They found a great gnarled old plane tree not far outside the Por del Prato gate, and settled down on the soft, cool grass under its shade. Sunlight filtered through the thick leaves in dancing dapples, Tessa leaning her back against the tree trunk, Tito stretching out on the grass beside her, their feast spread across her apron. She pulled a tiny, carefully sewn bag from down the front of her dress, the blessed Breve a wandering preaching father had given her that Easter, that kept the devil away. Tito teased her, asking if she wouldn’t like to leave her cruel home, if she would want to be his little servant where no one would scold her or beat her. Tessa’s face blazed with joy, saying she would, but only if she could come back for her little black-faced kid first.

Tito laughed, it had only been a playful suggestion, but he was tired after a long day of walking in the sun, and the soft shade was so sweet he didn’t want to drag himself all the way back across the Arno to Bardo’s stuffy, book-lined house. He asked if he could rest his head in her lap for a quick nap, and within minutes he was fast asleep. Tessa sat as still as a nesting dove, only daring to brush her fingers against his soft dark curls when he was fully unconscious. She was so happy she couldn’t think ahead to him waking up and leaving her, to the cold empty cottage waiting for her at the end of the road.

He woke at last, the sun slanting low across the meadow, his nap stretching far longer than he’d meant. He jolted upright, suddenly remembering how many hours late he was to his appointment at the Via de’ Bardi, and told Tessa firmly she must go home now, warning her not to cry or he would get cross and not love her anymore. Tessa’s rising tears vanished, her face pale, wide blue eyes trembling as she stared at him. He rummaged in his wallet, pulled out a tiny red coral charm carved into the shape of a pointed little horn, brought all the way from Sicily, and hung it on the cord around her neck right next to her little Breve, telling her it was a good luck charm that would chase away any evil spirits or bad luck when he wasn’t there to protect her.

Tessa leaned in and kissed his cheek softly, just as he told her to, and Tito laughed, pressing her round cheeks together and giving her a playful smooch across both. Then he hurried away, only waving once when he was ten yards off, never looking back. He did not want to see the quiet, trembling sob she let out once he was out of sight, for the softness of his nature hated to let any sadness cling close to him. He ate a quick, hasty meal at Ognissanti, crossed the Arno over the Ponte alla Carraja, and hurried straight for the Via de’ Bardi, already drafting the simple, unremarkable excuse he would give Bardo and Romola: that he had been unexpectedly detained on the festa, and their proud, delicate natures would never press him to ask for further details.

The hum of holiday crowds eddied around Tito like warm surf long after Fra Luca fell silent, but he remained rooted in place beside the friar, caught fast in a tangle of irresolution. It was only when the press of passing pilgrims and pleasure-seekers thinned, and the old Dominican pushed himself stiffly to his feet to shuffle through the open doors of Santa Felicita’s cool, incense-dim nave, that Tito at last turned his steps down the sun-warmed cobblestones of the Via de’ Bardi.

“If this monk is a Florentine, if he means to settle here long,” he muttered under his breath, fingers tightening unconsciously around the crumpled scrap of parchment Fra Luca had pressed into his hand, “I shall have to lay the whole truth bare.”

A new crisis had risen up before him, but he schooled his features so carefully no hint of his inner turmoil showed by the time he reached Bardo’s house. He had already missed his promised visit the evening before, and he slipped through the second-floor door where Romola and her father sat surrounded by scrolls of parchment and half-carved marble fragments, as aloof from the raucous holiday revelry of the streets as they were on the quietest workday. The regret in his face was barely dimmed from its usual bright, easy charm—he even told them he had skipped the famous Corso carnival procession to call on them, a gesture that needed no further proof of his contrition. He threw himself into the evening’s talk with extra animation, laughing at Bardo’s grumbling jests, leaning over to point out a rare phrase in the Greek manuscript spread before them, while deep below the surface of his words his mind churned like a complex, whirring loom, weaving threads of thought that bore no relation to their conversation. By the time he descended the worn stone stairs and stepped out under the frosty starlight, his purpose had already hardened into a definite shape.

The next day, as soon as his lectures at the humanist academy wrapped for the afternoon, he turned his feet down the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco. He meant to find Fra Luca, to learn exactly how much the friar guessed of Baldassarre’s fate, what clues had led him to those guesses, and how long he intended to remain in Florence. With that full knowledge, he would craft a story that would spare him the necessity of fleeing the city, the life of respect and easy pleasure and Romola’s love that he had built for himself.

He had never needed to spin an elaborate, purposeful lie before—but the moment had come, the moment that always rises unbidden to shore up small, unacknowledged falsities, and his ingenuity was sharp and ready. He had already convinced himself he owed no duty to go chasing after Baldassarre. He had once carelessly said that if he got fair proof his adoptive father was alive, he would seek him out unhesitatingly—but when he turned the thought over in his mind, why was he bound to do that? Looked at clearly, what was the point of all human life but to draw out the greatest possible sum of pleasure? His own young, blooming life promised far more joy, for himself and for everyone around him, than the withered, wintry life of an old man long past the time for sharp, keen enjoyment, whose ideas had hardened into stiff, barren rigidity. This was the natural order, he told himself: maturity was nothing but a nest for the new life of youth. Baldassarre had had his turn, drunk his fill of life’s cup. It was Tito’s turn now.

The quest stretched ahead as nothing but a vague, exhausting prospect: a long dangerous voyage to Antioch, months or even years spent searching with no guarantee he would ever find the old man at all. He would have to leave all his new status, all the love that was already close to his lips, to return at last, if he succeeded, to the same exacting, joyless companionship he had known by rote as a boy. The gems Baldassarre had left him, and the florins he had converted them to, were technically Baldassarre’s, by the narrow rules that governed ordinary legal possession—but in the far truer, more natural order of things that gave the world to youth and strength, they belonged to the man who could wring the most joy out of them. He was aware that the rest of Florentine society would expect him to spend every last florin to rescue his benefactor, but what was society’s so-called moral sentiment, anyway? Nothing but a tangled mess of random, illogical traditions and opinions no wise man would follow, unless it suited his own comfort. He did not even care for the gold itself, he told himself; he would give up every florin for Romola’s sake without a second thought. But he would not fling away the sweet, full happiness that was within his reach, to wander the world thirsty for joy. Any rule that demanded a man throw away the good things that made life worth living was nothing but human selfishness turned inside out: made by people who wanted everyone else to sacrifice their own pleasure for their benefit. He did not want Baldassarre to suffer—he hated to see anyone suffer—but no philosophy could prove he was obligated to care more for another man’s pain than his own. That would only be required of him if he had loved Baldassarre devotedly, and he did not love him. Was that his fault? Gratitude, when you broke it down, made no real valid claim: Baldassarre’s life would have been dreary and empty without Tito in it. Did anyone owe a man a debt for the pleasure he got out of doing something for you?

Once he had started picking apart the old obligations that bound him to Baldassarre, his thoughts moved as fast and as corrosively as strong acid, eating clean through every last thread of sentiment that might have held him back. He felt no trace of that vague, unnameable dread of wrong-doing that even pagan worshippers had felt for the Nemesis, the quiet guardian of the soul that the old Greek plays sang of, the fear that forces men to wisdom even when no mortal law is there to punish them. Tito was too cultured, too sceptical for such old superstitions; he had been raised on mockery of priests whose lives were as notorious for their corruption as their fairy tales, and deep study of hundreds of philosophical debates over the Highest Good that he had long ago decided was nothing more than a matter of personal taste. The only fear that stirred in him was the fear of anything that might rob him of his pleasure, and he was very much afraid Fra Luca might be the thing that drove him out of Florence for good.

He stepped through the outer cloister of San Marco, breathing in the sharp scent of laurel and beeswax candles, and asked the porter for Fra Luca. The man who answered him was a lay brother, a raw contadino fresh from the farms outside Florence, his accent thick, his gaze dull and incurious.

“Fra Luca? Oh, he was taken up to the Dominican house at Fiesole at dawn, on a litter. The poor brother is very ill, they say the cool hill air might help him rest. Would you like to leave a message for him?”

“Thank you—my business can wait.” Tito turned away, a flood of relief so heavy it made his shoulders slack rolling through him. “That friar is not long for this world,” he murmured to himself. “I saw he was worn to nothing but skin and bone. No one at Fiesole will be reminded of me anytime soon. And even if he does come back one day, the explanation I have already crafted will serve just as well then as it would now.” One last faint, lingering thought pricked at him, unplaceable: he still could not put his finger on why Fra Luca’s face had seemed so hauntingly familiar, from some half-forgotten moment long ago.

Tito Melema’s sandals tapped light as a sparrow’s step across Florence’s sun-warmed cobblestones, the sharp, cold fear that had coiled in his gut after his recent brush with ruin dissolving like mist off the Arno. His natural easy joy had reasserted itself, and he was hurrying toward the de’ Bardi house on the hill, to see Romola. It was true that Romola’s whole life was stitched through with that tender, self-sacrificing devotion to her blind father, that endless patience with tedious, unglamorous scholarly labor he had always instinctively shrunk from. But he was not yet tired of goodness, not ready to fling himself headlong into vice: he was still young, his blood thrumming with soft, open fondness for all that was beautiful and worthy of admiration, his appetite for ordinary human joy still unspoiled. The poison of his recent secret betrayal of the man who had raised him could only work slowly, after all. He had bound himself to evil, but for now life felt almost exactly as it had before the bargain he had struck, so much so he barely noticed the chain around his neck. He intended everything to go back to how it was: he would win universal praise through diligent work, clever erudition, his own gentle, obliging charm. He had no intention of doing anything that would break the harmony he shared with the people he cared for most in the world. And no one mattered more to him than Romola. There might be wealthier wives he could win one day, if his talents carried him far enough, but there was no other woman in all of Florence like her. When she was near, her sincere hazel eyes fixed on him, he fell under a spell as strong and unshakable as a melody that seizes your whole body, leaving you restless the moment it fades until you hear it again.

By the time he was climbing the worn stone steps to their door, the thrill of just anticipating her nearness had already wrapped around him.
“Welcome, Tito mio,” Bardo’s voice called out before Tito could even knock. There was new fire in the old blind man’s tone, new cheer softening his lined face, ever since that first fateful meeting more than two months prior. “You’ve brought fresh manuscript, no doubt—but last night, after we parted, new ideas came to me. We must broaden our scope. We must go back over the work we’ve already laid out.”

Tito bowed to Romola as he stepped across the threshold, moving straight to Bardo’s chair as was his custom, resting his hand in the old man’s waiting palm before settling onto the low cross-legged leather seat with scrolled carvings pulled up right next to the blind man’s elbow.
“I have the new manuscript,” he said gently, “but it can wait as long as you please. My legs are young, you know—I can walk back up the hill easily whenever you’re ready to see it.”
He didn’t glance at Romola as he spoke, but he knew perfectly well her eyes were fixed on him, bright with unhidden delight.
“That is well said, my son.” Bardo had started calling Tito that, unprompted, only a few weeks prior. “I am glad to see you do not shrink from labor—for the poet is right, when he says life has granted mortals nothing without it. Too many young men chase the palma sine pulvere, the glory of the prize without the dust of the race. But the Greek proverb says it best: in the morning of life, work; in midday, give counsel; in the evening, pray. I might look to all the world as if I have reached that helpless evening, but I have not, not while I carry unspoken counsel in my chest. My mind was once dammed up, you see: all those years of learning, all that thought, lay dark and still behind the wall of my blindness. But you, Tito, you opened a channel for it, and now the waters rush forward with a force that surprises even me. I want us to revisit all our early preliminary ground, build out a far wider scheme of commentary and illustration, else I will lose opportunities I can now see in hindsight that may never come again. You follow my meaning?”

Tito had just stooped to pick up a roll of manuscript that had slipped off Bardo’s lap, and the old man’s sharp, jealous ear caught the tiny movement. Even if he had been allowed to let out a sigh of exasperation at the extra months of tedious work ahead, Tito was not naturally impatient. He had been raised steeped in that painstaking, detailed, copious erudition that was the greatest intellectual labor of his age, and with Romola so near, every little discomfort felt light, buoyed up by waves of soft, pleasant sensation.
“Absolutely,” he said. “You wish to expand your commentary on certain passages we’ve already cited.”
“More than that. I want to add occasional excursuses, deep dives on every author I have spent years studying, for I may die before I can write any separate work of my own. This is no time for careful, well-sifted learning to sit idle: we face not just rash ignorance, but men like Calderino, whom Poliziano rightly exposed for spouting brazen fake citations just to feed his own vanity and win arguments for his wrong ideas. So we cannot let this opportunity slip through our fingers. We will go back to the Thucydides passage we marked earlier, and I want you to walk through all my notes on Lorenzo Valla’s Latin translation—you know, the one the incomparable Pope Nicholas V, whom I met when I was young, back when he was still plain Thomas of Sarzana, paid Valla five hundred gold scudi for, and not a scudi too much. Even with Valla’s other questionable reputation, his scholarship is held in such high regard that the epigrammatist joked that after he descended to the underworld, Pluto himself dared not speak any ancient language for fear of being corrected. That is exactly why we cannot let his name be a rubber stamp for shoddy, false work: this excursus on Thucydides will be the perfect place for my corrections to his text. Romola, my dear—go fetch the volumes we need, you know where they sit, fifth shelf of the cabinet.”

Tito stood up at the exact same moment Romola did. “Let me get them, just point the way,” he said, hurrying after her into the small adjoining room lined floor to ceiling with perfectly ordered books, the air smelling of old vellum and dried lavender. Every book sat exactly where Bardo had placed it, years before he lost his sight. The two of them had never been alone in that little room before.
“I hope,” Romola said, turning her full, grave, trusting gaze on him, “that this extra work won’t weary you. It makes him so happy, you see.”
“And it makes me happy too, Romola—if you will only let me say that I love you. If you will only think me worth loving just a little.”
His voice dropped to the softest murmur, his dark beautiful face closer to hers than it had ever been, his eyes shining with tender pleading.
“I do love you,” Romola whispered back. She carried all her usual quiet, noble dignity, but her voice had never sunk so soft before. For a long, breathless moment they only looked at each other, before she added, “I know now what it is to be truly happy.”
Their faces tilted toward each other, their dark curls tangled for a heartbeat with the rippling gold of hers. Quick as lightning, Tito stepped up onto a projecting shelf edge to reach down the two volumes they needed, and both fell quiet, content to walk back to Bardo without any more words: that first blissful, shared secret was far more precious unbroken by chatter. The moment had passed as irreversibly as two streams flowing into one, and Bardo, blind and jealous of their attention, had not even had time to grow impatient.
“You have the volumes, Romola?” the old man called as they drew near. “Now get your pen ready: Tito will mark out the scholia we decide to extract, and you will copy them straight away, minding the numbers carefully to match the markers Tito will write in the text.”
Romola always had her own part to play in their shared work. Tito took his place at the tall writing desk where he read and transcribed, and she sat at a table just in front of him, ready to pass Bardo anything he reached for, or take a finished book out of his hands. They had sat in those exact positions every day since their work began, but today it felt brand new: every small movement, every time Tito took a book from her hands as she lifted it off Bardo’s knee, carried a new, quiet weight of joy. Romola’s deep, calm happiness wrapped around them like the soft, fading evening light that drives all restlessness away.

They had worked for two full hours, and were just stopping as the last of the sunlight faded through the leaded windows, when the door swung open to admit a figure that could not have been more out of place in their quiet, vellum-scented scholarly bubble. She was a short, stout, black-eyed woman of about fifty, wearing a black velvet pearl-embroidered close cap over massive braids of jet-black hair that bulged over her small forehead, her plump cheeks dusted with a startling bright carmine rouge above a sallow undertone. Three rows of pearls and a lower gold necklace rested on the soft wide cushion of her neck; her trailing black velvet gown and long drooping rose damask sleeves were embroidered around the edges, slightly faded, but any Florentine would recognize instantly that they were six months’ fine work from a master embroiderer. Her rose-colored petticoat, trimmed with dimmed white fringe and seed-pearl arabesques, was on full proud display, and a handsome coral rosary hung from one side of her niello-work silver clasp belt, a large crimson velvet pearl-stitched purse hanging on the other. Her plump little right hand, soft as paste left too long in the oven, held a richly decorated devotional book bound in velvet, silver, and pearls.

Monna Brigida was no stranger to Tito. She was Bardo’s late wife’s cousin, allowed full run of the house even though Bardo ordinarily banned all frivolous feminine visitors, for she had helped raise Romola when she was small. Romola smiled affectionately at her, rising to pull a spare seat far enough from her father’s chair that the gush of incoming gossip would not disturb him too much.
“Cugina?” Bardo asked, catching the short, heavy steps and the swish of heavy velvet.
“Yes, it’s your troublesome cousin,” Monna Brigida said, lifting her fingers playfully at Tito before leaning up to let Romola kiss her cheek. “Always bursting in on all your deep wisdom. Well, if I didn’t bring you bits of news from the outside world now and then, you’d forget there was any life at all beyond these mouldy old ancients of yours, who’d probably need a sprinkling of holy water if half the stories I hear about them are true. Not that the world’s any good these days, of course—scandals pop up everywhere you step, and you can’t go about with your head in a sack. I was just at Dianora Acciajoli’s wedding to that young Albizzi boy everyone’s been talking about, the one everyone expected to happen yesterday. Cieli! That wedding was so grim it might as well have been put off until Lent as a penance. The bride looked like a white nun, not a single pearl on her, and the groom was as solemn as Saint Joseph. Half the guests were Piagnoni, those new saints of Fra Girolamo’s making! Can you imagine two families as grand as the Albizzi and Acciajoli going in for that nonsense, when they can afford the finest clothes in Florence? I couldn’t even stand still in my best gown without giving offence—there was Monna Berta, who’s hidden far worse secrets in her time than I ever told, glowering at me from under her hood like a pinzochera, telling me I ought to read Fra Girolamo’s book for widows for guidance. Holy Madonna! According to them, widows ought to buy their coffins the second their husbands die and wait to rot, instead of enjoying themselves for the first time in their lives. And what did they serve us for entertainment at dinner? A long, rambling sermon from Fra Domenico of San Marco, listing off Fra Girolamo’s three great doctrines, marking them off on his fingers until my flesh crept. The first one says Florence—or the Church, he mixed them up—will be scourged, and he talked so much about plague I half thought the swellings would pop up under my arms right there at the table. Then he said Florence would be regenerated—well, what good will that do if we’re all dead of the plague? The third one, the one he said over and over, is that all of this will happen in our lifetimes. He tapped that one hard on his thumb until I was shaking like the jelly on my plate! The jellies still had both families’ coats of arms molded in colored sugar, so they haven’t turned the whole world upside down quite yet, but all they talked about was going back to the old ways: up stands Francesco Valori, who I used to dance with in the Via Larga when he was a bachelor and loved the Medici as much as anyone, making a whole speech about how we ought to ban fine clothes and fancy feasts and bad books, as if any of that belongs at a wedding!”

“Stop, cousin!” Bardo cut her off in his imperious, sharp tone, desperate to stop her endless rambling long enough to speak. Monna Brigida jumped, pursed her mouth, and stared at him with wide round eyes.
“Francesco Valori is not entirely wrong,” Bardo continued. “Bernardo thinks he only calls private grudges public zeal, but I say the old noble frugality of the ancients, the very mother of true greatness, is slipping away from us. Men now spend more on one transient joust than would be enough to found a whole library, to leave a lasting gift to the world for centuries. But for all the Neapolitan and Milanese courtiers laugh at us Florentines for having so little silver, for borrowing table furniture from each other at feasts, we still carry more of that great sober generosity, that hates trivial waste so it can be grandly lavish on things that matter, than any other city in Italy. The laughter of fools is half applause to wisdom, after all.”
Monna Brigida burst back into speech the second Bardo paused. “Laughter? No one laughed at that wedding! Young Niccolò Machiavelli tried to tell jokes from Franco Sacchetti’s book, about how no sumptuary laws for women ever worked, because we’re cleverer than painters and architects and all the logic doctors, we can make black look white and yellow look pink and give any forbidden thing a new name—but the Piagnoni all looked even more glum, and someone said Sacchetti’s book was wicked! I’ve never been so uncomfortable at a party, we non-Piagnoni all sat there as soggy as wet chickens. The only other time I felt that bad was last Lent, when I went to hear Fra Girolamo preach in San Lorenzo. He called us poor women cows, lumps of flesh, wantons, mischief-makers—and then he described a vision of a pale dead young woman lying in her bier, and all us light-minded widows rushing up to her bareheaded like vultures, cutting off her young dead hair to plait into our own old heads! I had nightmares for weeks! I came home and took off all my jewels, this very clasp, everything, tied them up in a parcel ready to give to the Good Men of Saint Martin for the poor, and then thank God I went first to my confessor Fra Cristoforo at Santa Croce, and he told me it was all the devil’s work, the Dominicans were trying to turn the whole world upside down, I never had to go hear Girolamo preach again. I just wish that poor Dino, Bardo’s son, hadn’t joined the Dominicans all those years ago—then I would have been happy when I heard everyone say he’s come back to Florence now, after all this time—”

“Silenzio!” Bardo roared, his whole frame shaking with passion. Romola half rose out of her chair, clasping her hands and turning to Tito, as if she instinctively wanted his support. Monna Brigida yelped a little, and bit down hard on her lip.
“Woman,” Bardo said, his voice trembling, “hear my will once and for all. Bring no reports of that name to this house. Romola, I forbid you to ever ask after him. My son is dead.”

No one dared break the heavy silence that followed. Monna Brigida lifted her shoulders and her hands in silent dismay, then got up as quietly as she could, waving frantically at Tito and Romola to stay seated, and slipped out of the room like a fat, guilty spaniel who had barked at the wrong time.

Meanwhile, Tito’s mind was racing at lightning speed. So Bardo’s son was not dead, as he had assumed this whole time. He was a Dominican monk. He was “come back.” That Fra Luca he had run into outside the Duomo, the one whose face had seemed half-familiar? Of course he looked like Bardo and Romola. What were the odds that man dropped dead back in Fiesole this very moment? The selfish, desperate thought pushed all other feelings aside. It was true that Bardo’s iron will was strong enough to keep Romola from ever seeking out her brother, but it could not stop Fra Luca from telling other people what he knew—especially if anyone ever connected the monk who was hunting for Baldassarre to Tito Melema, the exact name the description the old man had sewn into his tunic named as the man who had robbed and abandoned him. Nothing but Fra Luca’s death could wipe that threat away. But it was very possible the monk was already dying, after all the stories they had told of his fragile health. Tito breathed slow, and let himself sink back into that quiet, hopeful confidence.

The three of them sat in the deepening twilight for many long minutes, before Romola dared speak.
“Shall I light the lamp, father, and we can go back to work?”
“No, Romola. We will do no more work tonight. Tito, come sit here by me.”
Tito left the writing desk, and settled on the other side of Bardo, close to his left elbow.
“Come nearer to me, figliuola mia,” Bardo added after a long pause. Romola sat on the low stool at his feet, resting her arm on his right knee, so he could lay his hand on her golden hair the way

The heavy, honeyed warmth of a Florentine September afternoon hung thick over the city on the seventh of the month, two full months to the day since Tito and Romola had first spoken their confession of love to one another. When Tito ducked under the low lintel of Nello’s barber shop, he found the owner sprawled across his workbench, cap drawn low over his eyes, one leg bent to his chest and the other half-slid off the cushion, dragging with it a manuscript of verse whose pages were crushed and splayed open across the earthen floor. In the far corner, Sandro sat alone, playing the ancient hand game of mora against himself, his wide solemn eyes fixed on the slow, deliberate movements of his left fingers as if he were solving a mathematical riddle no other man had ever dared tackle.

Tito moved so softly his sandals made no sound against the packed earth. He snatched the lute leaning against the wall, leaned over the sleeping barber, and plucked the strings into a soft lilting tune, singing low:
*“Beauteous is life in blossom!
And it fleeteth—fleeteth ever;
Whoso would be joyful—let him!
There’s no surety for the morrow.”*
It was Lorenzo de Medici’s famous carnival song, the lines every Florentine knew by heart.

Nello woke as easily as a sparrow startled by a falling leaf. His cap was off his eyes in an instant, and he sprang upright, stretching his arms high. “Ah, my little Apollo! I’ve let my siesta run far too long on this sweltering day, it seems. The fault’s not in natural sleep, but in drinking deep of a potion of potent poesy before I laid down. Hear me? I’m already matching every first letter of my words like a wandering troubadour—sure sign the good wine of my wits is about to pour right out the spigot of authorship, leaving me nothing but an empty cask reeking of dregs, like half the so-called incomparable geniuses I rub elbows with. What is it, my Orpheus?” He reached out, tangled his fingers playfully in Tito’s curls, and tugged lightly. “That soft coaxing in your song tells me you want something. Let me peer straight through the needle’s eye of your desire, as the sublime poet says, so I can thread it for you.”

“That tailor’s metaphor is a strange one for a sublime poet to use,” Tito laughed, his fingers still brushing the lute strings in light, quick strokes. “But you’ve guessed it. I came to see you specifically. I don’t want a trim on my chin—what I need is for you to work your art on this zazzera, this tangled mess of curls on my head that’s knotted worse than Florentine political intrigue. No one else can wield a comb so deftly that it strokes the scalp just right to stir a man’s good spirits, and a dusting of your finest orange blossom scent wouldn’t go amiss. I’m bound for the Scala palace today, you see, to move in radiant company. The young Cardinal Giovanni de Medici will be there, and he’s bringing that sharp-witted young Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena. His tongue is so quick I know the only way to outshine him is to carry the scent of orange blossoms in my hair.”

Nello had already seized his comb, flourished it in the air, and pushed Tito gently back into the barber’s chair, tucking a linen cloth around his shoulders. “Never speak of rivalry, my handsome boy. Bernardo Dovizi is a sharp lad, but he’s not one to chase wind with a net. He’s got the same sharp, snappish look as his brother Ser Piero, that weasel Piero de Medici keeps on a leash to slip through every small crack for him. You outdistance all rivals easily, and you’ll soon be able to touch the sky with your forefinger if you keep on as you are. They say you’ve even sweetened the sour old Messer Angelo, that crusty scholar—he’s gone so far as to say you’re less of an ass than he’d expected, for all how close you are to the Chancellor.”

“Between us, Nello mio, Messer Angelo has more genius and erudition in his little finger than all the rest of Florence’s scholars put together,” Tito said, smiling. “They can cry my praises now that Poliziano is laid low with grief or illness or whatever ails him, but I’m only fit to fly against a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito. Poliziano is a great eagle who’d swallow me whole, feathers and all, and never even notice he’d eaten something extra.”

“I won’t contradict your modesty if that’s the way you want it,” Nello chuckled as he combed Tito’s curls into soft, neat arrangement. “But you don’t expect us clever Florentines to repeat the same truths every single day, do we? We’d die of boredom. We cry down Dante and praise the no-named Francesco Cei just for the sport of variety, and if we call you the new Poliziano, heaven knows the lie’s not as big as it could be. And aren’t you a model of virtue in this wicked city, anyway? Ears double-waxed against every siren call that would drag you away from the Via de Bardi, and that great scholarly work of yours that’s meant to astonish all posterity?”

“Posterity will probably be as confused by it as we are by the universe,” Tito said dryly. “They’ll never figure out what its original plan was.”

“Something very like that was said here just the other day,” Nello went on, dabbing a drop of orange blossom oil at Tito’s temples. “Cristoforo Landino said the excellent old Bardo was one of those scholars who gets overthrown by his own learning, like a knight weighed down in full plate armour, then gets angry that people are walking over him. Though that pithy line wasn’t his, mark you—of all men in Florence, Cristoforo Landino is the king of feeding people with an empty spoon, dragging out long useless discourses to leave you gagged on vain expectation. There! You’re perfect now. Not a single grace could be added to you.” He pulled the cloth away. “But love can’t live on scholarship forever, eh? Before long I’ll be dressing this fine head of curls for your betrothal feast, won’t I?”

“Perhaps,” Tito smiled. “Unless Messer Angelo convinces Bardo next that I need to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the Zecca’s cart before I can win my Alcestis. He’s right, though, that I’m unworthy of her. Romola is one of the Pleiades, and she’ll only dim herself if she marries any mortal man.”

“*Gnaffè*, your modesty is well placed there,” Nello agreed. “Yet fate seems to have carved you exactly to fit the empty niche left by Bardo’s long-lost son, you know. By the way, Cronaca told me he’s back at San Marco. Did you hear that?”

A faint electric jolt ran through Tito’s body, but no flicker of it showed on his face. He stooped at once to pick up the crumpled verse manuscript from the floor, busying his fingers flattening its dog-eared pages. “No, I thought he was still at Fiesole. Are you certain he’s come back to San Marco?”

“Cronaca is my source,” Nello shrugged, taking the book from him. “I don’t go poking around that monastery sanctuary myself, but he does. Ah, my poor *Nencia da Barberino*! It jars your scholarly sensibilities to see its pages all crumpled like that, doesn’t it? I fell asleep reading about that charming rustic maiden, prettier than a turnip flower, with cheeks more savoury than fresh cheese. Though to get that kind of romantic idea of peasant girls, you’d have to be napping on velvet cushions in Via Larga, not out watching the Fierucoloni trudge into the Piazza della Nunziata after sundown tonight.”

“And who are the Fierucoloni?” Tito asked, half-distracted as he settled his cap on his head.

“The peasant women coming down from the mountains of Pistoia and the Casentino, all trundling into town to keep their vigil in the Nunziata church for the eve of the Virgin’s Nativity. They sell their spun yarn and dried mushrooms at the little fair we call the Fierucola. They make a queer sight, all carrying paper lanterns, howling their hymns to the Virgin. You’ve got the time to go look, if you wanted. No? Well, I’ve had my fill of it too. The piazza gets wild after dark. You might end up with a random stone bouncing off your ears or your shins if you loiter, and I’ve never been fond of that kind of unwanted attention. Addio, then!”

Tito carried a faint, nagging unease with him as he walked towards the Scala palace, and his visit ended far earlier than he’d planned. The young boy-cardinal Giovanni de Medici, who would one day be known to all posterity as Pope Leo X, had gotten distracted by his favourite pastime of the hunt and never showed up at all. It was still half an hour before sunset when Tito stepped out of the palace doors, intending to head straight for the Via de Bardi, when he stopped short in surprise. There on the Borgo Pinti, walking towards him, was Romola.

She was wrapped in a thick black veil and heavy black mantle, but there was no mistaking her tall, graceful figure and the steady, unhurried way she walked. At her side was the short, stout form of Monna Brigida, her face hidden by an unusually plain, unadorned cloak. Romola had never been raised on devotional rituals, and she almost never left the house save for rare walks on the rooftop loggia—Bardo hated to be left alone, and every outing was planned and discussed for days in advance. Tito knew at once something urgent and unplanned had drawn her out, something she had not mentioned to him the day before. She saw him through her veil and hurried her steps.

“Romola, is anything wrong?” Tito asked, turning to fall into step beside her.

She did not speak at once, and Monna Brigida cut in breathlessly. “Ah, Messer Tito, it’s lucky we met you, for we are in a great hurry. And what a nuisance it is—we’ve had to detour all along the city walls and turn up the Via del Maglio, because the fair crowds have blocked the direct road to the Nunziata that would have gotten us to San Marco in half the time.”

Tito’s heart gave a great, violent thud. “Romola,” he said, his voice dropping low, “you are going to San Marco?”

They had left the Borgo Pinti now, walking along the wide stretch beneath the city walls, with quiet walled gardens on their left and scarcely a soul in sight. Romola lifted her veil to breathe the soft evening air, and he could see the faint, sad agitation on her face.

“Yes, Tito mio,” she said, looking straight at him with her dark, sorrowful eyes. “For the first time, I am doing something behind my father’s back. I am glad I ran into you, for at least I can tell you. But if you go to the house later, do not tell my father you met me. He thinks I have only gone to visit my cousin, who sent for me. My godfather is staying with him, and he knows where I am going, and why. You remember that evening when my brother’s name was spoken, and my father told you the story of him?”

“Yes,” Tito said, his voice low and thick. His mind was a tangled mess. His heart sank at the thought that all his carefully laid plans might crumble to nothing, even as his thoughts raced over a hundred different moves he could make to steer himself through the coming crisis. Even so, he met Romola’s gaze with a hungry, aching feeling that this might be the last time she would ever look at him with that full, unshaken trust.

“The cousin heard he had come back to the city,” Romola went on. “The night of San Giovanni, our servant Maso saw him lingering near our front door. But when Maso went to San Marco to ask after him, they said Dino—my brother, that is, he was christened Bernardino after my godfather, but now he calls himself Fra Luca—had been moved to the monastery at Fiesole, for he was very ill. This morning, though, a messenger came to the house to say he was back at San Marco, and he had sent a desperate adjuration for me to come see him. I cannot refuse him, even though I still hold him guilty of the great wrong he did to my father. I still remember how I loved him when I was a small girl, before I knew he would forsake the father who doted on him. Perhaps he has a word of penitence he wants to send to my father, by me. It cost me so much to go against my father’s wishes, which I have always known are just. I am sure you will think I chose rightly, Tito. Your nature is far less rigid than mine, nothing ever makes you angry, and it would cost you far less to be forgiving. Though even you, Tito, if you had seen your father forsaken by the child he loved most, the child he poured all his labour and all his hopes into, forsaken when his blindness left him needing support most—even you would find it hard to forgive.”

What could he say? He could not bring himself to lie to her, to pretend that such a great wrong could never be forgiven, nor did he have the courage to say a word to dissuade her.

“You are right, my Romola. You are always right—except in thinking far too well of me.”

There was genuine truth in those last words. Tito’s face was unusually pale, and a faint tremor shook his lower lip as he spoke, and he looked so beautiful, so full of quiet feeling, that Romola’s eyes glistened with happy tears. She took it for proof that his gentle soul shared every bit of her own sorrow, that he understood her choice perfectly. “Now, Tito,” she said, not pausing in her walk, “I want you to leave us here. If two veiled women walk into the piazza alone, no one will notice us. If you walk with us, everyone’s eyes will turn.”

“Aye, best you leave us,” Monna Brigida put in. “Truth be told, Messer Tito, every eye in Florence follows you wherever you go, and no matter how much Romola muffle herself, everyone cranes their neck to see what’s under her veil, she walks like she’s leading a religious procession. It’s no fault of hers, but it slows me down. And besides, I’m dressed as plain as one of the Piagnoni, as old as Saint Anne herself, because I don’t want a soul to see us heading to San Marco. It’s only for poor Dino, and if he’s dying he deserves to be forgiven. What’s the point of holding a grudge against a dead man? Make them suffer for their sins while they’re still breathing, that’s what I say—”

No one ever bothered to wait for Monna Brigida to finish her rambling speeches. Tito lifted Romola’s hand to his lips, kissed it gently, and said “I understand. I will obey you,” before he stepped back and lifted his cap to her in a gesture of reverence almost no native Florentine man ever offered a woman. Bernardo del Nero would later sneer at him for that trick, calling the young Greek a fawning foreigner, but to Romola, who loved honour and respect more than anything, it made him seem more noble than any other man in the city.

As he walked away, he felt half relieved to be dismissed, half desperate to cling to her for every last second that she would love him with no shadow of suspicion. It was inevitable, he knew. That dying friar would first demand to know every detail of what had become of his old father and little sister in all the years he had been gone. Romola would pour out everything, tell him all about the young Greek scholar she was soon to marry, the man who was meant to fill the empty place in their household that Dino had left. The moment she spoke Tito’s name, Fra Luca would spill every secret he knew: that the scholar she was betrothed to had abandoned his own adoptive father Baldassarre to rot in the hands of slave traders, that all his carefully crafted stories were lies. Tito could think of no lie, no trick, no dissimulation that could save him now. All his prospects in Florence were gone. Bernardo del Nero would smirk, pointing out he had been right all along to warn against a hasty betrothal to a stranger, and the story of the Greek scholar who had left his benefactor in slavery would be gossiped about under every loggia in the city.

For the first time in his life, he was too fevered, too unmoored to trust in his usual gift for self-control. He gave up all thought of going to Bardo’s house, and paced back and forth along the quiet stretch under the city walls until the last golden glow of the sunset had faded entirely from the western sky. He had no clear plan in his head, when he turned down the first street he came to, the Via San Sebastiano, which led him straight towards the crowded, lantern-lit Piazza dell’Annunziata. He was at that lawless, unguided point every man reaches when all the paths his desires led to are suddenly blocked, and he will follow any small, fleeting call that offers him a purpose, any escape from the ruin he sees waiting for him just over the horizon.

The roar of the moving crowd and the wild jumble of noise bursting off the piazza hit Tito the moment he stepped through the entrance, and he remembered Nello’s offhand story about the Fierucoloni harvest fair. He pushed his way deeper into the throng, even taking a strange quiet pleasure in the good-natured hoots and accidental elbowing, for the chaotic movement filled the empty, gnawing stretches of time that had lately been full of dreary premonitions of wandering alone across unknown lands chasing a vague fortune. He’d even caught himself half considering, in his bleaker moments, giving up and hunting down Baldassarre after all.

At every piazza inlet, crowds of people jostled their way inward, paper lanterns balanced on tall sticks bobbing and weaving uncertainly above their heads. A low, droning, monotonous peasant chant cut a clear line through the din, crisscrossed by high-pitched screams, sharp whistles, piping taunts from gangs of boys, the thud of distant drums, and the tinny jingle of tiny hand bells, all clashing in a glorious, unruly symphony. Every few minutes, one of the dim floating lanterns shattered with a soft thwack, felled by a stray stone thrown in the name of mischief, followed by a girl’s shriek and a fresh wave of whooping. On the edges of the chaos, though, groups of fairgoers were marking the vigil of the Virgin’s Nativity with far more purpose than random stone-throwing. Ragged men with sharp, shifty glances darting between passersby chattered merrily as they lured country folk to their crooked dice games, swearing the terms were fair and generous. Two stilt-walkers in half-baked masquerade costumes had snatched lanterns from the crowd, swinging the lights back and forth like meteors as they strode across the square. A shrewd vendor turned a steady profit at his small covered stall selling hot berlingozzi, the beloved chewy farinaceous street treat. A peddler stood balanced on a barrel, back braced against a pillar of the Spedale degl’ Innocenti loggia, hawking miracle pills invented by a Salerno physician, guaranteed to ward off both toothache and death by drowning. Not far off, leaning against another pillar, a tumbler showed off flips and tricks on a tiny raised platform, while a handful of apprentices, bored by the scattered chaos of random stone fights, held a concentrated private tournament of the favourite Florentine sport at the narrow mouth of the Via de’ Febbrai.

Tito, forced to slip through random gaps in the crowd, found himself for a moment right next to a slow trotting procession of barefoot, thick-calloused peasant women, their sun-bronzed faces lined with years of mountain wind, their clothes stitched from obsolete fabrics and faded old fashions, crusted with layers of inherited grime. To city eyes, they looked like a long-dead generation of ancestors wandering home from a pilgrimage they’d set out on a full century before. These were the hardy, sparsely fed women from the Pistoia mountains, each carrying a year’s worth of hand-spun yarn slung in a modest bundle over their shoulders, their hearts holding only a meagre hope of small good fortune and a wide, unnameable dim fear of harm—all of which they trusted the Virgin to tend to, for that very night, the curtain would be pulled back on her miraculous angel-painted icon, letting her holy power stream out unobstructed over all the faithful.

A moment later the crowd swept Tito toward the piazza edge, where the more stationary peddlers and performers had positioned themselves to keep their backs clear of trouble. Among them, Tito spotted his old acquaintance Bratti, the pedlar, leaning against a pillar, mouth pursed in a disdainful silence, eyeing every passerby with a cold, superior glance, one hand clamped tight over a serge cloth covering the basket slung at his chest. Surprised to see the usually frantic, haggling trader acting so aloof, Tito drew nearer and watched as two women wandered up to Bratti’s basket, curious. The pedlar only pulled the covering tighter, and pointedly looked away. Too intrigued to leave, one of the women asked what wares he was hiding.

“Before I answer that, Monna, I must know if you mean to buy,” Bratti said, snappish. “I can’t show goods as choice as mine at a fair where every fly can land on them for free. I’ve only two left, and I won’t soil them for nothing—with the plague wise men whisper is coming, these will soon be worth their weight in gold. No, no, andate con Dio.”

The two women exchanged a look. “What’s the price, then?” the second asked.

“More than you’re likely to have in your purse, buona donna,” Bratti said, his tone pitying and supercilious. “I’d recommend you put your faith in the Lord and the saints: poor folks can do no better.”

“Not *that* poor!” the woman huffed indignantly, yanking her money bag out of her skirts. “Come now—what do you say to one grosso?”

“I say you can get twenty-one quattrini in change for that,” Bratti replied coolly, “but not from me. I haven’t got that small of change.”

“Two grossi, then?” the woman snapped, exasperated, while her companion eyed her with faint envy. “It can’t possibly be worth more than two.”

After more back and forth haggling, Bratti made a great show of softening into concession. “Since you’ve set your heart on it, I’d hate to do you a mischief,” he said, slowly lifting the serge cover. “Maestro Gabbadeo always said a woman denied the thing she’s set her mind on is more at risk of the plague than ever. Ecco, I’ve only two left, and let me tell you, the matching ring is on Maestro Gabbadeo’s own finger, that wisest of doctors who’s lately gone to Bologna.”

The precious goods were two clunky iron rings, hammered to look like the antique Roman artifacts sometimes dug up from Florentine backyards, their thick rust and vaguely promised magical properties convincing enough that the two women paid the agreed sum without complaint. The first woman, who had no grossi to spare, bartered with a generous handful of her own hand-spun yarn to take the last ring, and wandered off triumphant. Bratti covered his basket—now stuffed full of random odds and ends he’d bartered for on equally favourable terms—and stepped away from his pillar, nearly bumping straight into Tito, who had been about to slip away unrecognised.

“By the head of San Giovanni, this is luck!” Bratti yelped, yanking Tito back behind the pillar. “I was talking about you this morning, Messer Greco! Said you’d climbed up among the signori now, and I’m glad of it, seeing as I was at the start of your good fortune. I never thought I’d catch you loitering on the piazza stones these days! This is your luck, though, not mine—save for a small trifle to compensate me for my trouble in the matter.”

“You speak in riddles, Bratti,” Tito said, smiling faintly. “I don’t sharpen my wits the way you do haggling over fake iron rings. Say plainly what you mean.”

“By the Holy Gospels, that bargain was generous! If a Hebrew can make thirty two per cent, a Christian deserves a little more, right? I could have charged twice the money and twice the yarn if I hadn’t a conscience. But speaking of rings—*that* ring on your finger? I can get you a buyer with a very deep money bag.”

“Truly?” Tito said, glancing down at the plain old ring Baldassarre had slipped onto his finger as soon as his young hand had grown big enough to fit it.

“A Genoese merchant heading straight for Hungary, from what I hear. He rummaged through every shop in Florence looking for old trinkets no one else knew the value of, then wandered into my stall. He had a ring on his finger not unlike yours, and when we got to talking about old rings I said I had a fine young acquaintance of mine with one far finer. He said ‘tell him I’ll meet whatever price he asks.’ I was going to stop by Nello’s shop to find you tomorrow—for I’ve always thought, Messer Greco, that you’re not the sort to stand by and watch the Arno run broth without dipping your finger in.”

Tito had not missed a word, and his mind raced the whole time. Why had he ever kept the ring, anyway? It had only been silly sentiment that had stopped him selling it alongside Baldassarre’s other gems. If he’d been sensible and sold it then, he might never have been recognised by Fra Luca, tied back to a past he was desperate to outrun. Superstitious scruples about an inanimate object were pointless. This was a perfect chance to get rid of it, no extra work required.

“You speak with your usual sharp sense, Bratti,” Tito said. “I’ve no objection to hearing what your Genoese will offer. When and where am I to meet him?”

“Tomorrow, three hours after sunrise, at my stall. If your wits are as sharp as I’ve always known them, you’ll name a heavy price—he doesn’t care about the cost. I swear he’s buying the ring for some great noble, not for himself.”

“Then I will be there, if nothing hinders me,” Tito agreed.

“Doubtless you’ll deal generously by me for old acquaintance sake, so I won’t waste time bickering over the small tip you’ll give me,” Bratti cackled. “I can’t wait to get out of this piazza—fairs are dull, not to say wicked, when you’ve no more goods left to sell!”

Tito waved a quick goodbye, and the crowd swept him away again, zigzagging across the square without him having a say in his path, until he reached the far end of the piazza opposite the church, in a deep recess where the building line jutted inward, and a huge laughing crowd had gathered. Loud bursts of laughter interrupted a monologue that shifted from slow, oratorical grandeur to fast, buffoonish patter. A strong, concentrated light burned in the recess, silhouetting the heads of the spectators as the crowd pushed Tito slowly forward, and above them rose the head of a man wearing a white mitre painted all over with yellow cabalistic symbols.

“Behold, my children!” the man boomed. “Behold your chance! Do not neglect the holy sacrament of matrimony when it costs only one white quattrino—the cheapest marriage ever offered, pre-dissolved by special papal bull at every man’s own will and pleasure! Behold the bull!” He held up a crumpled parchment strung with huge dangling seals. “Behold the indulgence granted by his Holiness Alexander VI, newly elected Pope for his great piety, who means to reform the Church and wisely start by abolishing that priestly abuse that hoards too much of this privileged matrimony for the clergy and leaves none for the laity! Spit once, my sons, pay one white quattrino, and the bargain is done! The quattrino is the only difference the Holy Father allows between us and the clergy, who spit and pay nothing at all!”

Tito recognised that sharp, ringing voice, though the man’s face was hidden in shadow cast by the torches behind him. He pushed closer, and saw that behind the speaker, a mock altar draped in red cloth stitched all over with yellow zodiac signs was raised on a small platform. Half a dozen thin tapers burned at its back, a conjurer’s props scattered across its surface, a large open book propped in the centre, and at one front corner a monkey tied by a cord to a ring, holding a tiny lit taper in one paw. An impish little boy dressed in a miniature white surplice spent most of his time cuffing the monkey and straightening the wobbly taper every time the creature fidgeted. The man in the mitre wore a surplice too, over a chasuble printed with the zodiac signs in black on yellow cloth. Tito knew that sharp, angled face under the mitre: it was Maestro Vaiano, the mountebank he had rescued Tessa from not long before.

“Come, maidens!” Vaiano whooped. “This is the hour for the pretty girls who have many chances, and the plain girls who have few! Matrimony hot, eaten, and disposed of as easily as a berlingozzi! And see!” He held up a cluster of tiny drawstring bags. “To every bride I give a breve with a secret inside—the secret alone is worth the quattrino you pay for the ceremony! The secret to—no, I won’t tell you what it’s about, which makes it double the secret! Hang it round your neck unopened if you like, and you’ll avoid all the mishaps you never expected! Though if you open it you might break your leg, è vero, but you’ll know a secret no one else in Florence knows! And mark you: I give you the breve for free, I don’t sell it, like every other greedy holy man would. The quattrino pays for the marriage, the breve is a gift. Orsù, giovanetti, come be dutiful sons of the Church and buy the indulgence of his Holiness Alexander VI!”

The crowd ate the buffoonery up. The Fierucola fair was a modest occasion, and the Florentine townsfolk were happy to settle for jokes far tamer than the obscenely rhymed carnival fare the Magnifico and his poet friends usually served up. The women, besides enjoying the laughter, soon grew eager to get their hands on the mysterious breves. Several couples had already gone through the silly ceremony, roaring with laughter at Vaiano’s gibberish Latin, his dramatic grimaces over the open book, the monkey’s antics, and the required preliminary spit. Now a merry, bright-eyed seventeen year old boy in a loose tunic and red cap pushed forward, tugging a plump dark-haired girl by the hand, her ragged clothes showing off her round arms and legs charmingly.

“Fetter us right now, Maestro!” he yelled. “I’ve got to take my bride home and paint her portrait by lantern light!”

“Ha, Mariotto, my son, I commend your pious devotion—” Vaiano began, just as the monkey chattered loudly behind him, signaling disaster. The little creature’s patience had frayed thin under the surplice boy’s constant cuffing, and when the boy smacked him hard as his taper tipped horizontal, the monkey leaped back so hard he slipped the loose knot on his cord. He bounded first to the far end of the altar table, chattering furiously, and when the surplice boy picked up a wand to threaten him, he leaped straight onto the head of a tall woman in the front row, dropping his taper as he went, and screeching triumphantly from his new perch. The crowd erupted in screams and chaos, many of them half-convinced the mountebank’s familiar could do far more hidden mischief than just bite or scratch. Vaiano panicked, afraid his monkey would get hurt in the scuffle to grab him, and in the commotion Tito slipped out of the circle, letting the crowd carry him toward the doors of the Church of the Nunziata.

The brilliant golden light inside pressed against his eyes like something tangible after the dim, shadowy chaos of the piazza, and for a minute he could barely make out anything. That warm yellow glow felt supernatural, almost heavenly, to the many mountain peasant women who spent their lives half-shrouded by peaks and went to bed at twilight, and the low uninterrupted chant from the choir felt like soft rest to ears ringing with the piazza’s racket. Slowly the scene cleared, though a thin haze of incense mixed with the breath of the packed crowd hung in the air. In a left nave chapel strung with silver lamps, the miraculous fresco of the Annunciation stood unveiled, glowing so bright from all the surrounding lights it looked dark from where Tito stood on the far right of the nave. The whole of the great church was crammed full of peasant women, some kneeling, some standing: the rough, sun-darkened skin and dingy homespun clothes of the highest mountain dwellers mingled with the softer faces and bright white or red head drapery of wealthier families from the lower valleys. Spread high across the walls and ceiling was another, far older multitude, all pressed close together to be as near as possible to the Virgin’s power. These were hundreds of votive wax effigies: great Florentine statesmen in their black silk lucco council robes, popes, emperors, kings, cardinals, famous condottieri perched on their plumed chargers, even visiting Mohammedan merchants standing in easy, tolerated companionship with Christian cavaliers. Many of the older figures had faces blackened by centuries of incense fumes and robes tattered with age, others were brand new, bright in fresh red mantles or polished steel armour. Wedged between full effigies were severed wax arms, legs, and other fragments of bodies, with only a few empty gaps where a figure had been removed for public disgrace, or fallen ominously—like Lorenzo de Medici’s effigy had done, six months before. It was a teeming resurrection swarm of long dead people and pieces of people, their varying degrees of newness and decay mirroring the dingy, bright-spotted crowd below them.

Tito’s eyes wandered over the wild mass of people, searching. He’d thought of Tessa already, and the sea of white hoods made him hope he might spot her sweet face under one of them. It was a far more welcome thought than the vision of Romola staring at him with cold, changed eyes. He searched for a long time without luck, and was about to leave the church, weary of the unvarying scene, when he caught sight of Tessa no more than two yards away, right by the doorway. She was kneeling with her back propped against the wall, behind a group of standing peasant women who were craning to find a spot closer to the miraculous icon. Her head lolled a little to the side with exhaustion, her blue eyes staring absently at an altarpiece of Archangel Michael in armour, his face young and his hair flowing, surrounded by bearded tonsured saints. Her right hand, clutching a bunch of cocoons, hung limply by her side, her round cheeks pale either from the golden light or the tiredness written all over her. Her lips were pouted softly, and every few seconds her eyelids fluttered half shut, looking like a large, sweet sleepy child. Tito felt an overwhelming urge to go to her, to soak in her trusting, uncomplicated

The sharp, cool air of late Florentine autumn brushed Romola’s cheeks as she reached the stone entrance of San Marco, where one of the white-robed friars waited, clearly expecting her arrival. Monna Brigida, her older companion, slipped away into the dim, incense-draped nave of the adjoining church, for no woman was permitted to step past the outer cloister’s threshold. Romola was led straight to the closed door of the chapter house, where the dying man had been carried to rest.

When the door swung open, soft, muted daylight blended with the glow of two beeswax tapers set behind a low, narrow truckle bed, revealing Fra Luca’s gaunt, emaciated face. A thin crown of golden hair circled his tonsured scalp, and his deep-sunken hazel eyes were fixed unwaveringly on the small wooden crucifix he clutched in bony, trembling fingers. He was propped almost upright against a pile of rough wool blankets, and as Romola set aside her veil, she registered only in passing that a second hooded monk stood by the far side of the bed, gliding toward the door the moment she stepped in. Behind them, the tall pale fresco of the crucified Christ rose over the far wall, a row of sorrowful, pale saint faces gazing down from beneath its feet.

The next instant, her eyes locked with Fra Luca’s as he lifted his gaze from the crucifix, and a sharp, stabbing pang tore through her: this wasted, otherworldly monk was the same fair, bright young brother she had loved through her childhood. “Dino,” she breathed, the sound half a cry of raw pain. She did not step closer. She stood rigid, two yards away from the bed, an unshakable repulsion coiling in her chest. This monk’s coarse habit felt like a brand, marking the dastardly, unfilial choice that had left their blind father isolated and desolate for years, the grovelling superstition that had dressed that desertion up as piety.

Raised by Bardo, a proud, unapologetic humanist who was one of Florence’s few frank, unashamed pagans of the era, Romola had always been taught to see the Church’s claims over cultivated reason as nothing to do with their quiet, studious household, a feature of the messy, uneducated masses they had always kept apart from. She had never thought of her brother’s flight as anything other than a source of indignant, uncurious contempt. Even so, the deep well of love in her soul clung to the old memory of the boy she had known, and her eyes searched his face, hungry for a flicker of the old, familiar Dino that was almost impossible to trace.

No corresponding warmth stirred in the monk’s face. He looked at his little sister, now grown to full, bright womanhood, with the far-off, unblinking gaze of a spirit that has already half left the mortal world. “My sister,” he said, his voice thin and ragged, but shockingly distinct through his ragged breath, “it is well you did not delay. My time is very short, and I have a message I am bound to deliver.”

Romola took one tentative step nearer, her heart softening. The message, she assumed, would be a penitent plea for forgiveness from their father. Nothing could erase the long years of abandonment, but a dying man looking back on the wrong he had done would surely draw pity, bring understanding at the last. Dino would ask after the speed of their father’s fading sight, how he filled his long dark days, how their home felt now without him, and his last words would be full of tenderness and regret.

“Romola,” Fra Luca began, “I have had a vision concerning you. Three times in the last two months, and each time it grew clearer. That is why I travelled from Fiesole: I knew it was a message from heaven, and I had no right to keep it from you. That my breath has been preserved long enough to give it to you is a sign God holds mercy for you—” A fit of laboured, gasping breath cut his words short.

Romola’s heart chilled again. A vision. The very sort of fantastical nonsense her father had always scoffed at with bitter amusement. Indignation rose hot to her lips. “Dino, I thought you had words to send to our father. You forsook him when his sight was failing, you made his life so desolate. Have you never once cared? Never repented? What sort of religion puts these silly visions before the duties you owe your own blood?”

The sunken hazel eyes turned slowly to her, and he held her gaze in silence for long moments, as if weighing whether he should answer her at all. “No,” he said at last, his tone low, passionless, as if a spirit not quite human was speaking through his dying frame. “I have never repented fleeing that stifling poison breath of sin, thick and hot around me, that threatened to steal over my senses like besotting wine. Our father could not hear the voice that called me night and day; he knew nothing of the demon tempters that dragged at my sleeves to pull me back. He has lived all his life in the middle of human sin and misery and never believed they existed, like a man picking up pretty shining pebbles in a mine while the whole world above him dies of plague. I tried to tell him, but he only scorned me. The studies he wanted me to live for were childish, dead toys, or else they were only given life by worldly ambitions and fleshly lusts—the only things that give weight to the old poetry and history he wanted me to pore over forever.”

“Has our father not lived a pure, noble life then?” Romola burst out, her face flushing bright pink, her whole body trembling. “He seeks no honours, he never lies, he denies himself every luxury, he lives like one of the ancient sages. He never wanted you to chase ambition or lust! He wanted you to live as he has done, by the purest maxims of the philosophy he raised us on.” She spoke half out of the rote of the beliefs he had instilled in her, but with the full, burning conviction of every ardent young idealist.

Her brother paused for a long while, staring at her passionate face with his strange, emotionless eyes. “What were those philosophical maxims to me? They told me to be strong, when I felt so weak I would have rolled myself in thorns like Saint Benedict just to smart my body raw and drive temptation away. Divine love found me, penetrated me, planted a great hungry need in me like a seed that demands space to grow. I was raised in ignorance of the true faith, but it flooded over me like a rising tide. I knew there was a life of perfect love and purity for the soul, where there would be no hungry craving for pleasure, no tormenting unanswerable questions, no fear of suffering. Before I had ever read the lives of the saints, I foresaw their ecstasy: even pagan philosophy stumbled on that truth, that man can reach bliss by dying to mortal wants, and living only for the unseen perfect God. But to reach that I had to forsake the world. No affection, no hope could bind me to anything that passes away. I could only live among other people as souls linked to that eternal unseen life. That need drove me constantly. It came to me in visions when my mind grew weary poring over the vain words that record the passions of dead men. It came to me after I had stumbled into sin, and turned away loathing from the stench of the emptied cup. And in those visions, I understood the true meaning of the Crucifix.”

He stopped, gasping hard for a minute or two, and Romola made no move to speak. There was no point trying to reach the mind of this unearthly brother; it was as useless as trying to grasp a shadow with her bare hand. When he spoke again, his heaving chest had grown calmer.

“I knew who I had to follow, but I saw that even among the servants of the Cross who claimed to renounce the world, my soul would have been choked by the fumes of hypocrisy, lust, and pride. God did not choose me, as he chose Saint Dominic and Saint Francis, to wrestle with evil in the Church and the world. He called me to run. I took my vows and I fled—fled to lands where danger, scorn, and want carried me like angels, straight to rest on the bosom of God. I have lived as a hermit, I have ministered to pilgrims, but my task is almost done. The veil between me and my everlasting rest has worn very thin. I came back to Florence so—”

“Dino, you *did* want to know if father is alive,” Romola interrupted, the thought of all his years of wandering suffering softening her, making her crave once more for reconciliation.

“—so that before I died I might urge our brethren to study Eastern tongues, as I never did, and go out to do greater work than I managed. And I found they are already set on that path. But since I came back, Romola, I have known I was sent partly to you—not to renew the bonds of earthly affection, but to deliver the heavenly warning given to me in my vision. For three times I have seen it. All the years since the divine voice first called me, when I was still in the world, I have been guided by visions. In our waking thoughts, we can never be sure we have not mixed our own error in with the light we pray for, but in visions we are passive, our souls are as an instrument in God’s hand. So listen, and do not speak again. My time is too short.”

Romola’s mind recoiled hard from the thought of listening to this vision. Her indignation had faded, but only because she could feel the gulf between her and her brother widening, impossible to cross. But as Fra Luca spoke, the second hooded monk had stepped fully around the bed, and now stood on the side opposite her, cowl drawn low over his face.

“Kneel, my daughter,” said a deep, rich, ringing voice, startlingly different from Fra Luca’s thin, breathless tone. “The Angel of Death is present here, waiting while the heavenly message is delivered. Bend your pride before it is bent for you by an iron yoke.”

The tone was not imperious, but calm, assured, steeped in quiet benignity. Romola, vibrating at the sound, looked up at the figure across the bed. His face was almost hidden in the shadow of his cowl, and her gaze fell first to his hands, folded across his chest, resting on the edge of his black mantle. They were strikingly, beautifully formed, almost transparently delicate, and their grace amplified the strange pull of his voice. Romola’s usual instinct to rebel against monks, whom she had been raised to despise, would have latched onto any repulsive detail to resist him—but his face was hidden, and his hands seemed to hold a gentle plea against all hardness.

The next moment, his right hand reached out to take the crucifix from Fra Luca’s fatigued grasp, and his left lifted a damp sponge to touch the dying man’s parched lips. As he bent, his cowl slipped back, and the full glow of the tapers fell on his features: high arched nose, prominent full lower lip, a thick crown of dark curly hair above his brow, all speaking of fierce energy and passion. His blue-grey eyes shone mild beneath soft auburn lashes, and like his hands, they spoke of sharp, tender sensitiveness. Romola knew at once it was Fra Girolamo Savonarola, the prior of San Marco, a man she had always considered more obnoxious than every other monk in Florence, simply because his preaching was louder and more unyielding. Her rebellion stirred against the strange soft influence that had almost made her want to bend.

“Kneel, my daughter,” the penetrating voice said again. “The pride of the body is a barrier against the gifts that purify the soul.” He looked at her, steady and mild, and Romola felt that subtle, unaccountable pull of a rare personality that few men on earth wield, to move the people around them. Slowly, she sank to her knees. In the very act of abandoning her proud, straight stance, a tremor ran through her, and her whole frame of mind shifted. She fell into a strange state of passive, open receptivity.

Her brother began to speak again, his voice thinner now than ever. “Romola, in the deep of the night, as I lay awake, I saw our father’s room—the library, all the books, the marbles, the reading desk where I used to stand to read. I saw you, your hair long and fair, sitting by our father’s chair. At the desk there stood a man whose face I could not see. I looked and looked, but his face was blank, like a painting scrubbed away. I saw him move, take you by the hand. Then you took our father by the hand, and all three of you walked down the stone steps out into the streets, the faceless man leading the way. You stood at the altar in Santa Croce, and the priest who married you had the face of death. The graves opened, and the dead in their shrouds rose and followed you like a bridal train. You passed through the streets and the gates, out into the valley. The man leading you hurried you faster than you could bear, and the dead grew tired of following, and turned back to their graves. At last you reached a stony waste, no water, no trees, no grass. Instead of water, unrolling parchments spread out everywhere. Instead of trees and grass, men of bronze and marble sprung up, crowding around you. Our father fainted of thirst, and fell to the ground. The faceless man let go of your hand, and fled. As he went, I saw his face. It was the face of the Great Tempter. You wrung your hands, searching for water, but there was none. The bronze and marble figures mocked you, holding out cups of water, but when you grasped them and lifted them to our father’s lips, they turned to parchment. Then the bronze and marble figures turned to demons, and snatched our father’s body out of your arms. The parchments shrivelled up, blood ran everywhere, and fire burned on top of the blood, until they all vanished. The plain was bare and stony again, and you were all alone in the middle of it. Then night fell, and I saw no more. Thrice I have had this vision, Romola. It is a revelation meant for you: a warning against marriage, as a temptation of the enemy. It calls on you to dedicate yourself—”

His pauses had grown longer, more frequent, and now a terrible fit of gasping seized him, his eyes rolling up to fix on the crucifix as if it were a light that was fading fast. Presently he found a little more strength to speak, in a tone so faint it was barely audible. “To renounce the vain philosophy and corrupt thoughts of the heathens: for in the hour of sorrow and death, their pride will turn to mockery, and the unclean gods will—”

The words died away entirely.

Even as a little part of Romola’s mind told her this was nothing but a dream, fed by old childhood memories and his own fervent religious convictions, a strange, unearthly awe had settled over her. She was not prone to sickly fancies; she had a sharp, vivid intellect and healthy human passions, too grounded in the ordinary relations of the world to crave morbid, exceptional events.
