But the images of the vision, which she despised, jarred at her like cruel, piercing cries. This was the first time she had ever stood beside a man in the throes of death: her young life had been sombre, but she had never known acute suffering, never a grief that cut to the bone. This brother, returned to her at the very edge of his final agony, felt like a sudden, awful apparition from a world she had never dared to think about. The pale sorrowful faces on the fresco behind the bed seemed to draw closer, keeping company with the pale face on the bed.

“Frate,” the dying voice whispered. Savonarola leaned down, but no other words came for long, dragging moments. “Romola,” the voice said next. She leaned forward, but again silence fell. The words were struggling in vain, in his closing throat. “Fra Girolamo, give her—”

“The crucifix,” said Savonarola’s steady voice. No other sound came from the dying lips.

“Dino!” Romola cried, the sound low and piercing, as the full realization hit her that all the years of misunderstanding between them would never be mended now.

“Take the crucifix, my daughter,” Savonarola said, after a few moments of quiet. “His eyes behold it no more.”

Romola stretched out her hand and took the small wooden cross, and the action seemed to break the terrible tension that had wrapped around her. A great, ragged sob burst out of her. She bowed her head beside her dead brother, and wept aloud. It felt to her as if this first encounter with death would change the colour of daylight for her forever.

Savonarola moved to the door, and called in a lay brother who had been waiting outside. Then he stepped back to Romola, and spoke in a tone of gentle command. “Rise, my daughter, and be comforted. Our brother is with the blessed now. He has left you the crucifix, in remembrance of the heavenly warning—that it may be a beacon to you in the darkness.”

She rose from her knees, trembling, pulled her veil back over her head, and hid the crucifix under her mantle. Savonarola led the way out into the cloistered courtyard, lit now only by starlight and a single lantern held by a servant near the entrance. A group of San Marco’s regular lay visitors stood clustered there, waiting to see the prior, who they had been told was tending a dying friar in the chapter house. Romola was dimly aware of forms stepping back to make way for her, of Savonarola’s low murmur that “our brother is departed”, of a light, gentle hand resting on her arm. A moment later, the main door swung open, and she was standing in the wide, open Piazza di San Marco, with no one but Monna Brigida and the servant holding the lantern beside her.

The fresh night air revived her, helped her regain her self-control. The scene that had just ended was still horribly clear and vivid in her mind, but as the ordinary, familiar world outside the chapter house pressed back on her, it began to shrink to the size of a terrible, lingering memory. She hurried her steps, nervous, desperate to get back home to her father—and to Tito. They had been together while she was

The pale gold edge of dawn was still gilding the slime-streaked cobbles of the narrow Ferravecchi thoroughfare when Tito stepped out of Bratti’s shop. The Genoese stranger had long since vanished around a corner with Tito’s heirloom onyx ring tucked safe in his pouch, and Tito’s own hand closed around the heavy bulge of fifty florins sewn into the inner lining of his doublet. A faint, fleeting twinge of regret pricked at him: if fortune somehow spared him the need to flee Florence after all, it would look odd to have lost the ring he’d always claimed he wore for the sake of cherished, irreplaceable memories. But he waved the thought away at once. It was a trivial thing, not worth dwelling on, not when his whole world had been tipped on its axis just a night before. The fevered, panicking rush of his first alarm had burned out, leaving nothing but a dull, heavy lassitude in its wake. He cared too deeply for the soft, easy respect and goodwill of his neighbours, and for the pleasures that goodwill bought him, to not now wish he had never gambled his good name by shrinking from the debts other men called sacred.

But deeds are like children born to us, he thought dully as he walked: they live and move apart from our own will. Even a child can be strangled, but a deed cannot. It carries an unkillable life of its own, inside and outside the mind that gave it birth, and that dreadful, unshakable vitality was pressing in on Tito for the very first time.

He was heading back to his lodgings near the Piazza di San Giovanni, but deliberately swerved away from the shortest route through the Mercato Vecchio. He did not want to stumble on Tessa just now. He had no taste for coaxing or small talk, no energy to spare for anything but waiting for the first sign of his shifting, uncertain fate.

The piazza was bathed in that soft, warm autumn morning sunlight that still lingers on leftover dew, that invites lassitude without the drag of tired bones. It was festival day, the Nativity of the Virgin, and the air hummed with the quiet invitation to loiter and stare. Stalls heaped with figs, pomegranates, and armfuls of white lilies were being set out around the octagonal Baptistery. Laden mules stood dozing, munching from their nose bags, their drivers slipped through the heavy sacred doors to kneel before the altar of the Madonna. On the wide, sun-warmed marble steps of the Duomo, groups had scattered: a white-haired old crone held a round-capped toddler up to test his tiny bare feet on the warm stone, while a scruffy street dog sat nearby snuffling at the pair suspiciously; two shaggy-headed boys leaned in to watch a pale, small cripple carving a crude little face into a cherry stone; and above them on the broad raised platform, knots of laughing men broke apart and reformed in desultory chat, or stood huddled in pairs gesturing fiercely over some point of local news.

But the largest, busiest crowd of loungers stood right in Tito’s path, clustered around Nello the barber’s shop. It was the peak hour for his trade, and in the soft warm weather, most men chose to be shaved out under his bright striped red-and-white awning, rather than crammed inside his narrow little shop walls. No man looks particularly dignified sitting lathered with his chin tilted back, having his nose tweaked like a handle, but a close shave was the mark of respectable Florentine manhood, and all the men gathered there carried themselves with perfect, unselfconscious gravity. It was also the hour when yesterday’s gossip was still at its freshest, and Nello’s tongue moved as fast and deftly as his razor, the two seemingly powered by the same hidden spring. Tito knew he could not slip past the crowd without being pulled in. He would have to smile, banter, play the part of the carefree, brilliant young Greek scholar to perfection. It was no worse, he told himself, than swallowing a bread-and-cheese pill. Only a weak man would let the mere fear of exposure choke him before the danger even arrived.

Then a hand closed firm on his shoulder. No amount of prior resolve could stop the sharp, jolting shock that shot through him. His face twisted before he could smooth his features, and the man who had touched him burst into a light, warm laugh. He was a young man near Tito’s own age, with sharp, keen features, a close-clipped head of hair, and a clean-shaven jaw that gave the impression of a mind as uncluttered by unnecessary softness as it was possible for a man to have. His eyes glowed with bright, hopeful friendliness, the same light that would later go out in the bitterness and disappointment that marked his later life; everyone in Florence at that moment still predicted great things for Niccolò Machiavelli, the promising young secretary who was expected to mend his ancient, once-great family’s broken fortunes.

“Why, Melema!” he said, grinning. “What evil nightmare did you have last night, that you took my friendly hand on your shoulder for the grip of a sbirro’s arm?”

“Ah, Messer Niccolò,” Tito said, laughing as he recovered his poise. “It must be that there’s a double measure of slowness in my veins this morning, that I flinched from the sharp edge of your wit. The truth is, I slept very badly.”

“That’s terrible luck,” Machiavelli replied. “You’re supposed to shine today in the Rucellai Gardens, no fog allowed to dim your light. I assume you’re coming?”

“Messer Bernardo was kind enough to invite me,” Tito said, “but I have another engagement I cannot get out of.”

“Ah, of course,” Machiavelli said, shrugging. “You’re in love. No other reason would give a man such inconveniently immovable plans. We’re supposed to feast on peacock and ortolans under the loggia, surrounded by Bernardo Rucellai’s rare fruit trees, with all the cleverest men in Florence and the best vintages the cellars can offer. Though since Piero de’ Medici will be there, all our fine wits are likely to be drowned out by the stupid game of capping impromptu verses. I hate that nonsense. It’s nothing but a trick to let small wits triumph, since they always get most inspired by the pettiest little occasions.”

“What’s that you’re saying about Piero de’ Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?” Nello called, bounding over to them as he skipped around the giant form of Niccolò Caparra. The famous master ironsmith, who Tito had last seen bared-armed in his leather apron at the Mercato Vecchio, was dressed in his holiday best that morning, sitting perfectly still and docile while Nello lathered his chin, tweaked his nose, and scraped his skin clean at impossible speed. He looked for all the world like a lion that had donned linen tunic and fine cuffs, preparing to make its first appearance in polite society.

“Any private secretary will never get ahead if he mixes great and small like that,” Nello continued, wiping the lather off his razor on a cloth. “When great men aren’t allowed to arrange their children’s marriages as they please, small men don’t get to arrange their words as they please. Did you hear the news Domenico Cennini just told us? Pagolantonio Soderini boxed Ser Piero da Bibbiena on the ear for goading Piero de’ Medici into interfering with young Tommaso Soderini’s marriage to Fiammetta Strozzi. And as punishment, they’re sending him off as ambassador to Venice.”

“I don’t know which I envy him more,” Machiavelli said, smiling. “The crime, or the punishment. The punch will make him the most popular man in all Florence, and the punishment gets him sent to the only city in all Italy that’s ever known how to run its own affairs.”

“Aye, and if Soderini stays in Venice long enough,” Cennini put in from the shaving bench, “he might pick up their ways, and bring them back home. The Soderini have always been loyal friends to the Medici, but this little incident will open Pagolantonio’s eyes to the value of that old Florentine trick of slipping into new harness when the old one starts chaffing. We haven’t forgotten that trick in the last fifty years, not entirely.”

“Of course we haven’t,” Caparra rumbled, now free to move his lips again. “Eat eggs in Lent, and the snow will melt. That’s what I tell the lads when they’re drunk and noisy at San Gallo, talking about starting a romor, an insurrection. I tell them, don’t ever plan a romor. That’s as stupid as trying to fill the Arno with buckets. When the water’s high enough, the Arno will fill itself, and that won’t happen until the torrent’s already ready.”

“Caparra, that oracular speech of yours is entirely my doing,” Nello cackled. “You never could have come up with that wisdom with all that dark rust of a beard on your chin. Ecco, Messer Domenico, I’m ready for you now. By the way, my learned Tito,” he called, spotting Tito edging towards the edge of the crowd, “old Maso was looking all over for you earlier. Your lodgings were empty. He looked sad and in a great hurry. I hope nothing’s gone wrong at the house in the Via de’ Bardi.”

“Of course Messer Tito hasn’t heard the news,” Cronaca said, pushing through the crowd just then. “Bardo’s son Dino is dead.”

Tito’s heart gave a violent leap. Had Dino died before Romola reached him?

“No, I had not heard,” he said, his face no more disturbed than was natural for a man hearing sad news about his betrothed’s brother. He turned and leaned against the doorpost, pretending he’d abandoned his plan to leave. “I knew his sister had gone to see him. Did he die before she arrived?”

“No,” Cronaca said. “I was at San Marco at the time. I saw her come out of the chapter house with Fra Girolamo, who told us the dying man’s breath had been preserved almost by miracle, just long enough for him to make a full disclosure to his sister.”

Tito’s fate was sealed. His mind raced back to every moment of his flight from Florence years before, and he hatched a new plan: he would get his money back from Cennini before that disclosure spread to the whole city. Once he had his gold in hand, he would not have to stay here enduring scorching looks and sharp, biting words for long. He stood perfectly still, hands tucked into his belt, eyes fixed absently on the cobblestones at his feet. Nello glanced over at him, and assumed the young scholar was overcome with grief and anxiety for Romola, looking like the very picture of unselfish sadness. He deliberately waggled his razor at Piero di Cosimo, the painter, who he had never forgiven for refusing to see any hint of flaw or hidden motive in Tito’s handsome, amiable face. Piero, leaning against the other doorpost, just shrugged. Nello’s repeated little challenges had long since worn down the painter’s neutrality, and now he found himself actively inclined to suspect the much-praised Greek of hiding some ill secret.

“So you’ve got your Fra Girolamo back, Cronaca?” Nello said, changing the subject. “I suppose he’ll be preaching again this coming Advent.”

“And not a moment too soon,” Cronaca said gravely. “We’ve had the best possible proof of his words since last Lent. Even the wicked now find wickedness a plague. The ripeness of sin has begun to rot, and even sinners can smell it. There’s not been a single change in Rome or Florence since last Lent that hasn’t put a new seal on his words: that the harvest of sin is fully ripe, and God will reap it with a sword.”

“I hope he’s had some new visions, then,” Francesco Cei sneered. “The old ones are getting a bit stale. Can’t your Fra Girolamo hire a poet to liven up his imagination for him?”

“He has no shortage of great poets around him,” Cronaca replied, quiet and unruffled, “but they are poets of real merit, not petty little rhymers. They are content to learn from him. They no more think the truth he speaks stale than they think the light of the moon stale. But perhaps those high prelates and princes who hate his denunciations will be pleased to hear that though Pico della Mirandola, Poliziano, Marsilio Ficino, and every other man of mark in Florence revere Fra Girolamo, Messer Francesco Cei despises him.”

“Poliziano?” Cei laughed, sharp and scornful. “Of course he believes in your new Jonah. Look at the fine orations he wrote for the Sienese envoys, telling Alexander VI that the world and the Church were never better off than since he took the papal throne!”

“Now, Francesco,” Machiavelli said, smiling. “A scholar of such wide reading must have room for many differing opinions. And as for Fra Girolamo, whatever we think of his sainthood, you judge his preaching too narrowly. The secret of great oratory is not saying new things. It is saying old things with a power that moves your audience. Without that, as old Filelfo once said, a man deserves not to be called an orator, but an arator, a ploughman. By that test, Fra Girolamo is a great orator.”

“That’s true, Niccolò,” Cennini said, from the shaving chair. “But part of that secret lies in his prophecies. Our people—no offence, Cronaca—will chase after any prophet, especially if he predicts terrors and disasters.”

“Say rather, Cennini,” Cronaca corrected him, “that the real secret lies in his pure life and unshakable faith, which mark him out as a true messenger of God.”

“I concede it, I concede it,” Cennini said, holding up his palms as he rose from the chair. “His life is spotless. No man has ever dared accuse him of a single vice.”

“He just gets his pleasure from the sweet sin of arrogance,” Cei burst out bitterly. “I can see it in that proud lip of his, that satisfied glint in his eye. He hears the whole city humming his name: Fra Girolamo Savonarola of Ferrara, the prophet, the saint, the mighty preacher, who scares Florentine children so bad they throw away their little wicked toys.”

“Come now, Francesco,” Nello said, stepping forward to smooth things over. “You’re just grumpy waiting for your shave. Let me stuff your mouth full of lather, that’ll shut you up. I won’t have my friend Cronaca getting angry: I’m very fond of his chin, and it hasn’t changed an inch since he turned Piagnone. For my part, I admit I got into the habit of slipping into the Duomo to listen to him last Advent. I might even have turned Piagnone myself, if two things hadn’t stopped me: first, the free, broad nature of my trade, and second, the length of those sermons, which drag on forever before they get to the moving part. But as Messer Niccolò says, the Frate holds people with a power far beyond his visions. Monks and nuns who claim to prophesy are not so rare. What did Luigi Pulci say? Dombruno’s famous scimitar was said to be enchanted, but Luigi said he rather thought it cut so sharp because it was made of perfectly tempered steel. Aye, paternosters might make you feel clean, but you still need a good sharp razor to shave properly.”

“Look, Nello!” Machiavelli laughed, pointing down the street. “What doctor is that riding up on his Bucephalus? I thought your piazza was clear of those furred, scarlet-robed lackeys of death. This man looks like he had the same night adventure as Boccaccio’s Maestro Simone, and got his bonnet and mantle pickled a little in the gutter. Though he himself is as sleek as a miller’s rat.”

“Ahhh,” Nello drawled, looking up at the approaching figure. It was a round, fat little man with a round head, perched on a half-grown, skittish raw horse that held its nose out stubbornly, constantly trying to back up and bolt off at a diagonal, with very advanced ideas about obeying authority that were far ahead of the century.

“I’ve got a few little pranks all ready for him,” Nello whispered to Machiavelli, “that’ll chase his prying nose right out of our part of the city. He’s a quack from Padua. They say he’s been in Prato for three months, and now he’s come to Florence to see what he can net. His favourite trick is roaming the countryside visiting the contadini. See those huge saddle bags he’s got slung over his horse? They’re for all the fat capons, eggs, and meal he extorts from silly country folk who don’t have much coin to pay for his cures. He sells his own secret medicines, so he never goes near the apothecaries, and for the last week he’s been turning up here in my piazza for two or three hours every day, drawing crowds of asthmatics and squalling sick babies to my shop steps. It makes my blood boil to see that toad-faced fraud counting out his greasy quattrini, or taking a fat pigeon in exchange for his worthless pills. But I’ll stick a few thorns in his saddle, or I’m no true Florentine. Laudamus! He’s coming over to get shaved, just like I was hoping. Messer Domenico, don’t leave yet. Stay, you’ll see a rare piece of fooling I dreamed up two days ago. Here, Sandro!”

Nello leaned in and whispered in his assistant Sandro’s ear. Sandro rolled his solemn eyes, nodded, broke into a slow, deliberate smile, and then took off running at a surprising speed.

“How are you today, Maestro Tacco?” Nello called, as the doctor finally wrestled his skittish horse around towards the barber’s shop. “That’s a fine young horse you’ve got there, but he’s a little raw in the mouth, isn’t he?”

“He’s an accursed beast, the vermocane eat him!” the doctor snapped, swearing in irritation as he dismounted, and tied his frayed

The loggia crowning Bardo’s Florentine house rose higher than all the neighbouring rooftops, forming a sun-warmed gallery wrapping around the quadrangular stone walls. On the street-facing side, its sloped roof rested on slim carved columns; the remaining three sides were lined with walls pierced by graceful arched openings, so that the back walk, looking out over a jumble of half-erected, weathered dwellings toward the soft green slope of the hill of Bogoli, gave Romola a secluded, sheltered path where she could wander unseen by passersby.

Close to one of those arched openings, just a few steps from the door that led up from the main house, Tito waited for her. A sick, twisting weight sat in his stomach as slanted golden Tuscan sunlight pooled at his feet, weaving through the shards of all the plans he had thought were broken. He had never for a second foolishly presumed Romola’s love for him would overpower the revulsion she would feel if she learned the truth of his secrets: he knew full well her affection for him was rooted in her unshakable faith in his goodness, a faith he had no right to count on surviving exposure. But as he pictured her walking toward him, luminous and lovely, her face softened by that warm hazel gaze that had first stolen his breath, he found himself aching for something small, something selfish: that she had been less unyieldingly pure, just for a moment, so that he could pull her tight and kiss her deeply before their world crumbled apart. They had never shared more than fleeting, chaste touches: a lingering glance across the library, a quick brush of lips, a squeeze of hands as they parted. He had dreamed for months of being alone with her at last, and now that the moment had come, he had been certain she would stand far off, cold and unforgiving.

His heart jolted so hard it ached when the loggia door creaked open. There she was. It hit him like a flash of summer lightning: he felt rather than saw the glow of her golden hair lit by the sun, the tears swimming in her wide, imploring eyes, he felt rather than heard the soft, breaking cry of his name on her lips: “Tito!” In the same instant she was in his arms, sobbing hard, her face pressed into his shoulder.

Romola had lain awake through every hour of the previous night, yearning for the comfort of his bright, familiar face. The shock of seeing her long-lost brother Dino on his deathbed, the bewildering, unanswerable new questions his strange, fevered testimony had planted in her mind, the haunting vision that felt less like a story she had heard than a nightmare she herself had lived through—all of it had driven home just how completely Tito was the first person who had ever flooded her quiet, library-bound life with hope and gladness, who had softened the sharp old grief of losing her brother years before. She could not tell any other living soul what she had witnessed that day at San Marco, not even her grieving, blind father. She had held her sobs in all morning while tending to Bardo, but now that Tito was here, the pent-up sorrow burst free. Proud and reserved with every other person in Florence, Romola was as unguarded and simple as a child in her love for him. She had been happy for months just sharing quiet looks across Bardo’s shelves, but now that she ached to cling to him, no old shyness held her back.

“My Romola, my golden goddess,” Tito murmured, his voice thick with sudden, fierce fondness as he held her gently, pressing soft kisses to the thick, sun-bleached curls at the nape of her neck. In that moment he was in paradise. Disgrace, shame, the fear of being found out—all of it vanished. This happiness was too bright to be spoiled by the quiet awareness that Romola still did not know his secrets. He found himself rejoicing in her innocence; concealment, he told himself, had only ever been wisdom. The only thing he regretted now was how unnecessary all his prior dread had been, if that dread had not been worth enduring for the sake of this sudden, overwhelming rapture.

The last of her ragged sobs faded, and Romola lifted her head. Neither spoke for a long while. They stood still, staring at each other’s faces with that soft, wondering sweetness only young love can bring: her long, white scholar’s hands tangled in his dark brown curls, his dark, slim fingers sifting through the streams of her loose golden hair. They had never felt so completely seen by another person. The cold, heavy weight of her new grief pressed at Romola’s chest, making her cling all the longer to that quiet, soothing sense of nearness. Tito did not even dare to lean in to kiss her lips, afraid to break the perfect, wordless peace of the moment.

“Tito,” she said at last, “all of it was so terrible, but I have to tell you everything. Your strength will help me shake off these impressions that reason alone cannot chase away.”

“I know, Romola,” Tito said, his dark, lustrous eyes giving away nothing of the hundred sharp wishes he had held for Dino’s death long before that day. “I know he is gone.” Romola saw nothing but her own pure, loving thoughts reflected in their dark depths, the way we read warm, familiar letters in a happy dream.

“He was so changed, Tito! It pierced me to the heart to realize that wasted monk was the brother I remembered. And it was so strange, so hard: he would not say a single word to our father, nothing but a vision he insisted he had to share with me alone. And yet it was so piteous—his gasping, fighting breath, his eyes fixed on the crucifix over the altar, that he could not even see clearly. I will never forget it. It feels like that memory will stand between me and every other thing I ever look at for the rest of my life.”

Romola’s throat swelled with fresh tears, and she had to stop speaking. But the desperate urge to unburden herself to Tito pushed her to push down the rising anguish, and when she spoke again, her thoughts had shifted a little. “It was odd, Tito. That vision he spoke of was about our marriage—and he had never even heard your name.”

“What was it, my love? Sit down, tell me all of it,” Tito said, leading her to the weathered stone bench set near the arch. A sudden sharp fear pricked at him, terrified that the dying man’s vision might somehow reference Baldassarre, the secret he could never let Romola learn, and the unease made him crave the small change of position.

Romola recounted every second of the scene at San Marco, almost word for word, Dino’s lines burned into her memory the second they had left the dying monk’s lips. But when she finished describing the vision, she paused, the rest of the memory rising so vividly before her eyes she could not bring herself to speak it, staring out across the distant Bogoli hill, almost forgetting Tito was there beside her. His mind was completely at ease now: the vague, rambling vision had washed over him like thin white mist, leaving no trace at all. He held his silence, waiting for her to go on.

“I took the crucifix from his hands,” she went on, as if he had been reading her unspoken thoughts. “It is down in my bedroom, on my bedside table.”

“And now, my Romola,” Tito said, his tone soft and pleading, “you must banish all these ghastly fancies from your mind. That vision was nothing but a common monkish hallucination, bred of weeks of fasting and fanatical delusion. It cannot possibly hold any real weight for you.”

“No, Tito, not logically. But poor Dino believed it was a divine message. It is so strange,” she went on, her voice distant and meditative, “this life of men seized by such ferocious, unshakable beliefs that look like madness to everyone around them. Dino was no vulgar, uneducated fanatic. And that Fra Girolamo—his very voice seemed to seep into my bones, convincing me there is some hidden truth to the things that move him, some truth I have never once understood.”

“That was only because you were overwrought, my Romola,” Tito soothed her. “Your brother’s state of mind was no more than that old brand of theosophy that has plagued excitable, dreamy men for centuries—the very same ideas your father’s old rival Marsilio Ficino pores over in the New Platonist texts. The only difference is that Dino’s passionate nature drove him to act out what other men only write about and debate over wine. As for Fra Girolamo, he is nothing but a narrow-minded monk with a gift for preaching and scaring the easily swayed crowds. Any loud, urgent voice would have shaken you, after all you had been through. Once your mind has a little rest, you will judge all these things exactly as you always have.”

“I do not think I will ever be angry with poor Dino again,” Romola said. “I was furious with him when he first started speaking, my heart felt closed off to him. But since then I have thought far less of my own hurt, and far more of what he was suffering. Oh Tito, it was so piteous to watch his young life burn out that way. That yearning, straining look he gave the crucifix as he gasped for his last breath—I will never forget it. Last night I sat staring at that crucifix for hours, trying to imagine it giving him comfort, until by lamplight it felt as if the carved suffering face was actually shining with pity.”

“My Romola, promise me you will resist these dark, lingering thoughts,” Tito said. “They are fancies fit for sickly shut-in nuns, not for my golden-haired Aurora, who looks like she was made to chase away all these twilight nightmares. Try not to think of them now. We will not get many more chances to be alone like this.” He turned her face gently toward him with a light touch of his right hand, his tone soft and beseeching.

Romola had been staring absently through the arched opening, not seeing the distant hill, still stuck back in that cold San Marco chapter house, watching the pale faces of sorrow and death play out before her. Tito’s touch and his warm voice pulled her back to the sunlit present, and suddenly she was looking at his rich, dark, vivid beauty—the kind of beauty that seemed to hold every image of joy the world had to offer: purple grapevines strung between gnarled elms, golden corn ripening under the hot summer sun, bright-winged darting bees and butterflies drifting over clover, dancers’ feet pounding the earth in celebration as they held cymbals aloft, light lilting melodies sung over the thrumming rhythm of stringed lutes. Every sight and sound of Nature revelling in her own strength was wrapped up in him. The transition felt bewildering, dizzying: how could that worn, anguished face of her dying brother, straining for an invisible peace he would never reach, exist in the same world as this bright, satisfied youthfulness, this sun-god who seemed to have never known a single dark night? For a split second, Romola felt that sharp, unanswerable question as a dizzy lurch, a craving for something solid to hold onto. It was over as quickly as a sigh, that seed of all the ages’ great philosophical doubt compressed into a single moment of uncertainty, and it vanished fast, washed away by the rush of her young, bright love for the man before her.

“Your mind is wandering far away from our love, my Romola,” Tito murmured, soft and gently reproachful. “It feels like I am a forgotten thing to you.”

She stared back at his beseeching eyes in silence, until every last trace of sadness melted out of her own. “My joy,” she said, in her full, clear, warm voice.

“Do you really care for me enough to banish all those cold fancies, or are you always going to suspect me of being the Great Tempter?” Tito teased, his bright, familiar smile spreading across his face.

“How could I not care for you more than everything else in the world?” Romola laughed softly. “Everything I ever felt before—my love for my father, my loneliness growing up in that library—all of it was just preparation for loving you. You would laugh at me, Tito, if you knew what kind of husband I once imagined I would have married: some old scholar with deep lines carved into his face, like Alamanno Rinuccini, streaked with grey hair, who would agree with my father and argue for the Aristotelians, and be perfectly happy to live shut up in our library with him. I used to read about grand love in the old poets, but I never once dreamed anything like that could happen to me here, in our dusty old Florentine library. And then you came. You were so kind to my father, and slowly I started to believe that life could be happy for me, too.”

“My goddess—there is no other woman in the world like you,” Tito breathed, equal parts fondness and wonder at the strange, perfect mix of quiet majesty and sweet simplicity that made her Romola. “But dearest,” he went on, a little timidly, “if you cared half as much about our marriage as you ought, you would persuade your father and Messer Bernardo not to put it off any longer. You act like you do not mind the delays at all.”

“I do mind, Tito, more than anything. But I am sure my godfather will insist on even more delay now, because of Dino’s death. He never once agreed with my father disowning Dino, you know that. He has always said we ought to wait until you have spent at least a full year in Florence. Do not judge him too harshly. I know he is narrow and set in his ways, but he is so deeply noble. He often says my father is a fool for wanting to hoard his library to keep it with his name, and yet he would do everything in his power to make sure my father’s wish is carried out. That seems so great and noble to me—the ability to respect a feeling you do not share, or even understand.”

“I bear no grudge against Messer Bernardo for thinking you are far too precious for me, my Romola,” Tito said, and that was entirely true. “But your father knows his son is dead, then?”

“Yes, I told him. I could not keep it from him. I told him where I had been, that I had seen Dino die, but nothing else. He has forbidden me to speak of it ever again. But he has been so quiet all morning, making those restless, fidgety movements that always break my heart—the ones that look like he is trying to claw his way out of the prison of his own blindness. Let us go down to him now. I convinced him to try to rest, because he barely slept a wink last night. Your voice will soothe him, Tito, it always does.”

“Not one kiss? I have not gotten a single one yet,” Tito said, his soft, reproachful tone giving him an air of sweet, vulnerable dependence that felt utterly charming on a man so gifted he usually seemed to have every right to presumption. A bright pink blush spread across Romola’s face and neck as fast as light, as she leaned in toward him. It felt impossible that their kisses could ever grow commonplace.

“Let us walk all the way around the loggia once first,” Romola said, “before we go down.”

“There is always something grim and grave about Florence to me,” Tito said, as they paused at the street-facing edge of the loggia, looking out over the red rooftops to the far bank of the Arno. “Even its merriment feels sharp, shrill and hard—biting, not warm and joyful. I wish we lived down in Southern Italy, where thought does not get worn down by heavy study, but by delicious, drowsy languor that never touches the sharp, fiery Florentine wits. I should love to see you under that soft southern sun, laying among wild flowers, no heavy books or sad memories, nothing but sweet enjoyment, while I lean over you and pluck at the lute, singing you a little tune that feels as much a part of the warm light as the breeze. You have never known that careless, happy life of the nymphs, my Romola.”

“No, but I have dreamed of it ever since you came,” Romola said. “I am so thirsty for that deep draught of joy, for a life as bright and warm as you are. But let us not think of it right now, Tito. It feels to me as if there would still be those pale, sad faces lingering in the flowers, those eyes that strain for something they can never reach. Come. Let us go down to my father.”

The warm Florentine sun gilded the edge of Tito’s crimson tunic as he turned away from the Via de’ Bardi, light and giddy with a relief so complete it felt like he had shed lead shackles he had not even known he carried. For weeks he had carried the dread that his old life, and the enemy he had thought lost forever, would rise up to drag him down from the gilded future he had built beside Romola. Now that peril had vanished, swept away by lucky chance, and his mind, no longer occupied by the immediate presence of Romola and her blind father, turned back on the foolish, panicked choices he had made in those hours of terror.

Chief among them was the ring he had hocked for fifty-five florins to fend off starvation and capture. He would never have parted with it deliberately, he told himself now: the gem was so familiar to Romola and their circle that its absence would spark quiet questions unless he invented a graceful story to explain it, and at the moment he felt sick to death of spinning lies. He was safe now, he reminded himself. No harm had come of that first desperate deception, and Baldassarre, the ragged old man he had once called father, was surely lost somewhere far off, too broken and destitute to ever track him down. What was the harm in that? No one of consequence was hurt. He swore he would not entangle himself in any more hidden shackles, no matter how small, that might chafe at the perfect happiness he had won with Romola. He did not realize that very delight in escaping consequence was already dulling the soft sensibilities that might have stopped him from stumbling into new traps.

The sale of the ring was a small thing, all said and done. But what of little Tessa? The soft, round, innocent country girl was still wrapped up in the silly carnival farce that had led her to believe she was his wife, filling her small head with impossible, doomed hopes. Should he track her down alone and undeceive her at once? Or leave the disillusionment to time? Happy dreams were sweet, he reasoned, and they faded easy enough when the bright light of day and the noise of ordinary life came crashing in. The sweet, pouting little thing! He could not stop thinking of her. For a moment he considered bringing her a pretty gift, checking if her cruel stepfather was treating her any better now her mother was dead. He even toyed with the idea of telling Romola the whole silly story, so they could find the poor child a safe, happy home somewhere far from the abuse of her stepfamily.

No. That absurd farce of the cerretano carnival wedding, the way he had let Tessa drift away believing they were married, could never be allowed to reach Romola’s ears. Romola was too proud, too unyielding in her sense of honour, she would never forgive such a betrayal. And even soft little Tessa might grow bitter, once she realized the man she thought loved her was never coming back. It was better he never saw her again. The whole silly adventure would fade soon enough: some ruddy, broad-shouldered young contadino from the valley would court her, and she would forget the pretty Greek signor who had danced with her at the fair, and realize she was free to marry him. *Dunque*—goodbye, Tessa, he murmured to himself, half smiling. All the best to you. That trivial affair would have no further consequences for him. People were always very sure that resolutions made entirely in their own self-interest would hold fast.

As for the fifty-five florins from the ring, Tito already knew exactly how to spend a portion of them. He had hit on a clever, graceful little scheme that would let him explain the absence of the ring to Romola without a hint of sordidness, and even push back against the grim, monkish visions Savonarola had been filling her head with lately, the fancies that made her frown at their shelves of classical poetry. He would commission a custom painted artifact, something that honoured her father Bardo’s love of ancient fables, and tell Romola he had traded the ring to a master craftsman for the piece as a special gift. With that thought fixed in his mind, he set off for the Via Gualfonda, to find Piero di Cosimo: the wild, eccentric painter famous across Florence for the fantastical mythological scenes that were exactly what Tito needed.

When he stepped into the courtyard outside Piero’s home, he found the heavy iron door knocker thickly wrapped in wool, bound tight with ingenious knots of twine. Everyone in Florence knew Piero stuffed his ears with cotton wadding to block out the clatter of the street and the shouts of passersby, so Tito was not surprised at the door’s defense against the thunder of impatient visitors. He tapped gently at first, then shook the door harder, all to no avail. He was already turning to leave, annoyed at himself for wasting time when he could simply wait to see Piero at Nello’s bookshop later, when a tiny girl trotted into the courtyard with a basket of boiled eggs slung over her arm. She stood on tiptoe, slid open a small iron hatch set into the door, and pressed her mouth to the gap, piping out “Messer Piero!” at the top of her small voice.

In moments the bolts clattered, the door swung open, and Piero stood there in a frayed red nightcap, a shapeless loose brown serge tunic with the sleeves rolled all the way up to his shoulders. He darted a surprised look at Tito, but ignored him entirely, stretching out his hand to take the egg basket from the child, disappearing inside for a moment to return with the empty basket. “How much?” he asked.

“Two grossoni, Messer Piero. My mother says they’re all boiled fresh this morning.”

Piero fished the coins out of the leather purse at his belt, and the little girl trotted away, glancing back over her shoulder a few times in awed admiration at the handsome exotic young signor lingering by the door. Piero’s own glance was far less complimentary. “What do you want hanging around my door, Messer Greco? I saw you at Nello’s this morning. You could have asked then. I don’t let any man step foot in this house unless I know his business and we’ve agreed on it beforehand.”

“Pardon me, Messer Piero,” Tito replied, unshakably good-humoured as ever. “I acted without thinking. I was so caught up in admiring your famous skill for inventing charming, whimsical fancies that I came straight over the moment the idea struck me.”

Piero pulled his habitual grimace, the one he made whenever anyone flattered him: he grinned, stretched the corners of his mouth wide, and pressed his brows down so no one could possibly guess what he thought of the compliment. After a long pause he said, “What exactly do you need?” Secretly, he was already tempted by the chance to exercise his creative fancy on a new commission.

“I want a very delicate miniature design taken from the old poets’ fables, that only you would know how to combine to perfection. It will be painted on a small wooden case, I’ll show you the exact dimensions, in the form of a triptych. The inside can be simple gilding: all the art is on the outside. It’s a subject you Florentines adore—the triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne, but treated in a new way. Ovid’s story will give you the hints you need. Young Bacchus will be seated on the deck of a ship, his head bound with clusters of grapes, a spear twined with vine leaves in his hand. Dark-berried ivy will wind up the masts and over the sails, the oars will be shaped like thyrsus staffs, flowers will wreathe all around the stern. Leopards and tigers will crouch at his feet, dolphins leaping through the waves all around the hull. And I want fair-haired Ariadne right there beside him, made immortal with her starry golden crown—that’s not in Ovid, but no matter, you’ll conceive it perfectly. Overhead there will be your little winged Loves, the kind you paint so beautifully, shooting arrows tipped with rose petals.”

“Say no more!” Piero cut him off. “I’ve got the vernacular Ovid inside. I don’t care to be choked to death by other people’s ideas. Come in.”

Piero led him through the first room, where the basket of eggs sat on the open hearth next to a heap of empty eggshells and a mound of cold ash. Oddly out of place with that sordid litter stood a low carved ebony bed, draped carelessly with a scrap of rich oriental carpet that looked as if it had once covered the steps of a church Madonna’s throne, and a large carved painted cassone chest. There was barely any other furniture, just plaster casts, wooden steps, easels, and rough crates, all draped in fine cobwebs.

The second room was even larger, far more crowded. It looked as if Piero was keeping a festa to himself, for the double doors beneath the skylight that lit the studio were thrown open to a wild tangled garden, half thicket, where fig trees and vines grew trailing wild among nettles and hemlocks, a tall dark cypress rising out of a mass of stifling yellow mulberry leaves. It seemed as if that dank overgrown greenery was already creeping through the walls: tufts of long grass and feathery dark fennel pushed up through cracks in the dirt floor amid a heap of broken marble fragments and rusted armour, a large stone vase tilted on its side poured trailing ivy out across the flagstones. The walls were hung with pen and oil sketches of fantastic sea monsters, dances of satyrs and maenads, Saint Margaret climbing out of the devouring dragon, Madonnas glowing with holy light, studies of wild plants and grotesque, distorted faces. On rough uneven shelves a few books lay scattered among drooping bunches of dried corn, bullock horns, pieces of dried honeycomb, stones patched with rare coloured lichen, skulls and bones, peacock feathers and huge spread bird wings. Propped up out of the messy litter on the floor were lay figures: one dressed in the frock of a Vallombrosan monk, topped absurdly with a barred helmet, another smothered in hastily tossed brocade and animal skins. Pigeons perched and strutted unafraid, too tame to fly at the arrival of visitors, three fat toads crawled slowly near the doorstep, and a fluffy white rabbit, clearly the model for the small creature startling Cupid in the unfinished Mars and Venus on the central easel, twitched its nose contentedly on a crate full of bran.

“All right, Messer Greco,” Piero said, nodding Tito to a low stool by the door, then standing over him with his arms folded. “Don’t try to gawk at everything all at once like some overcurious saint. Tell me how big you want this triptych.” Tito indicated the dimensions, and Piero marked them down on a scrap of paper. “Now for the book,” Piero said, reaching for a handwritten manuscript volume.

“There’s no mention of Ariadne crowned there,” Tito said, pointing out the passage he wanted. “But remember: her likeness, her golden hair, right beside Bacchus.”

“Ha!” Piero said abruptly, pursing his lips. “You want them to be portraits, don’t you?” He stared straight down into Tito’s face.

Tito laughed, a faint flush rising to his cheeks. “I know you are unsurpassed at portraiture, Messer Piero. But I can’t ask Ariadne to sit for you, the painting is a secret gift.”

“Perfect! That works for me. Giovanni Vespucci wants me to paint Oedipus and Antigone at Colonus, just as he’s laid the story out for me. I’ve a great fancy for the subject, and I’ve been dying to get old blind Bardo and his daughter Romola to sit for the two figures. You ask them. If you get them to agree to sit, I’ll put Romola’s likeness straight into Ariadne’s face.”

“Agreed, if I can prevail on them. And what is your price for the Bacchus and Ariadne scene?”

“Bah! If you get them to let me paint them, that’s payment enough. I don’t want your florins. You can just pay for the wooden case itself.”

“When do you want me to sit for Bacchus?” Tito said, smiling. “We can hardly have Ariadne’s perfect likeness without the other half of the pair.”

“I don’t need you to sit,” Piero said. “I already have your likeness done. Only I made you look terrified. I’ve got to rub that fright out for the merry Bacchus.” As he spoke he laid down the book and rummaged through a stack of paintings propped face-first against the wall. He returned holding a small oil sketch. “I call this one of the best portrait bits I’ve ever painted,” he said, holding it out. “Yours is a bright, merry face, so it expresses fear better than any dour man’s. I noticed it the very first day I saw you. The rest of the painting is barely sketched, but I’ve painted you perfectly.”

Tito stared. There he was, his right hand raised holding a wine cup, caught mid gesture of triumphant revelry, but his face was turned away from the cup, his eyes wide and dilated, his lips pale with an expression of raw, unmasked, overwhelming terror. A cold trickle of dread ran down his spine, as if he was suddenly caught in the same paralyzing fear his painted self wore.

“You’re starting to look like the sketch already,” Piero said, with a short dry laugh, pulling the painting away. “That fine young Greek has just seen a ghost. I’ll finish it one day, when I work out which kind of ghost is most terrible—solid, like a dead man come back to drag you off, or half transparent, like a mist.”

Tito laughed, shaking off the strange, unwelcome prick of unease that warred with his usual easy self-possession. “That’s a subject made for you, Messer Piero: a merry revel interrupted by a ghost. You love blending the terrible with the gay. That’s why your shelves are full of death’s heads while you’re painting those roguish little Loves running off with Mars’ armour. I think you’re a secret cynic philosopher, hiding behind the mask of a clever painter.”

“Not at all, Messer Greco. A philosopher is the last sort of fool I’d ever want to be like. It’s enough for me to just live, without spinning lies to make up reasons for all the messy parts of life. Fowls cackle, asses bray, women chatter, philosophers spin false explanations—that’s what the sight of the world makes them do. Me? I’m just an animal that paints, instead of cackling or braying or lying.” He gave Tito a pointed look, nudging him gently toward the door. “Our business is done, then? You’ll keep your end of the bargain about the Oedipus and Antigone sittings?”

“I’ll do my best,” Tito said, getting the clear hint to leave immediately.

“Send word to me via Nello’s. No need to trudge all the way back out here again.”

“Understood,” Tito said, laughing, lifting his hand in a friendly wave as he stepped out into the courtyard, the memory of that painted face of terror lingering, unshakable, at the back of his mind.

Messer Bernardo del Nero held firm, just as Romola had predicted: his advice that the marriage between Tito and Romola ought to be deferred until Easter was unshakable, and on this point the blind scholar Bardo submitted entirely to the sway of his sagacious, practical old friend.

Yet Bernardo, who had never once felt the soft pull of Tito’s personal charm that had wrapped so many others around the young Greek’s finger, could not quite resist the undeniable argument of worldly success that carries such weight with seasoned men of society. Tito was advancing at a remarkable pace through Florence’s highest circles. He had won particular favour with the young Cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici, who had even mentioned naming Tito to his learned retinue for a forthcoming journey to Rome. That bright, quick-witted young Greek, whose tongue was always ready but never quarrelsome, grew more welcome by the day at lavish late-night suppers in the Medici’s Via Larga palace, and at Florentine games where he never pretended to outshine the regular masters, but could praise Piero de’ Medici’s incomparable skill with such effortless grace that every onlooker warmed to him. By that unbroken rule of elite social life, Tito’s reputation as a delightful companion in “magnificent” high society made his existing scholarly talents shine all the brighter; he was accomplished enough that no overinflated praise of his gifts ever exposed him to public embarrassment.

Messer Bernardo’s old loyalties and long-held attachments soon began to wear down that faint, lingering new prejudice against the too-supple young stranger from abroad. For a lifelong Florentine of his standing, it was impossible to dismiss the credential of being fully accepted by the city’s most respected families, the small circle whose shared judgment was the undisputed standard of social worth. It began to feel irrational to pretend there was any flaw in the prospect of such a man as a son-in-law for Bardo, and a husband for Romola. No one could deny that Tito’s arrival had dawned a whole new life for the blind scholar and his daughter, and the reality had outstripped even the brightest early hopes.

Bardo, whose unflinching pride and devotion to truth would never let him take part in the petty game of absurd, performative admiration and equally absurd vicious backbiting that made up almost the whole of learned social intercourse in Florence, had long ago fallen into neglect even among his fellow citizens. For years, common gossip had held that while his blindness and the loss of his son Dino were pitiable misfortunes, he was tedious for harping on the value of his own life’s work, and his discontent was inconsistent for a man who had openly disregarded religious rites, and turned down multiple patronage offers back in the day that asked only that he take holy orders — a small condition, then, since there was scarcely any other easy way for patrons to support a scholar’s work.

But since Tito’s arrival, the narrative tied to Bardo’s name was no longer one of unbroken quiet gloom. Word spread that the old man had stopped his endless plaints, and his fair daughter would no longer be trapped in lonely, dowerless pride waiting for a noble arranged marriage match. Tito’s winning manners and rising favour made that new public interest in the old household warm and genuine, so that visitors to the quiet Via de’ Bardi library were no longer a rare novelty. Elderly acquaintances drifted back, prompted by that vague urge to reconnect with an old friend everyone was suddenly talking about again. Young men Tito had once invited to visit found it easy to drop by unannounced later, when they caught him on his way to the Bardi house, slinging an arm over his shoulder to fall into easy, meandering chat. They came to gaze on Romola’s famed beauty: to see her live up to the old writer Firenzuola’s ideal of womanly majesty, “sitting with a certain grandeur, speaking with gravity, smiling with modesty, and casting around, as it were, an odour of queenliness.” She unfolded there like a strong white lily under the genial breath of admiration and homage, and all this new public warmth felt no different to her than the bright, glowing new life Tito’s love had already given her.

Tito had even stoked the dearest, long-buried hope in Bardo’s breast: that before he died, he might secure his beloved library against being absorbed into some other man’s private collection, against being handed over to a monastery and renamed for the order that housed it, so it would stand forever as the Bardi Library, open for the use of all Florentines. The old generation’s instinct to trust the Medici, whose influence was still the most powerful lever in the Florentine state, ran too deep to fade, and Tito, who already had the ear of Cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici, could do far more even than Bernardo to win that desired patronage: he could stand before a crowd of learned elites and lay out the unique, irreplaceable value of Bardo’s collection for all to see. Tito spoke of this future with bright, sanguine certainty, happy to cheer the old man on, and he knew full well Romola repaid those gentle kindnesses to her father with a kind of adoration no direct compliment to herself could ever win.

The question of the library was the subject of many quiet, private discussions between Bernardo and Tito after Christmas, when the prospect of the fast-approaching marriage drew closer — always well out of Bardo’s hearing. For the old man clung to a vague, unchallenged belief that all his property apart from the library was more than enough to meet every demand on his household. He would never, except in a moment of sharp, angry despair, admit to himself that the will he had written disinheriting Dino had left Romola the heir to nothing but debts, and that all he asked from patronage, in exchange for gifting his library to the Florentine Republic, was a guarantee it would be assigned its own permanent, separate home.

“My judgment is this,” Bernardo said to Romola, during a quiet consultation they held under the household loggia. “Since the marriage is settled, and Messer Tito has a solid, competent income of his own, we ought to begin winding up all your father’s old affairs now, to calculate exactly how much sum is needed to save the library entirely from being touched by creditors, rather than letting the old debts accumulate any longer. Your father needs nothing more than his daily small shred of mutton and his plate of macaroni to live comfortably, and I think Messer Tito has agreed to supply that for whatever years he has left, in place of the traditional morgen-cap wedding gift.”

“Tito has always known my life is bound up with my father’s,” Romola said softly. “And he is better to my father than I am: he delights in making him happy.”

“Ah, he is not made of the same common clay as other men, is he?” Bernardo said, smiling dryly. “Thy father thought to shut all woman’s folly out of thee by cramming thee full of Greek and Latin, but thou wert as ready to believe in the first pair of bright eyes and the first soft words that crossed thy path, as if thou couldst recite nothing but Paternosters like every other ordinary Christian girl.”

“Now, godfather,” Romola said, shaking her head playfully, “as if it were only bright eyes and soft words that made me love Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito too because he is good. I see it, I feel it, in everything he says and does. And if he is handsome, too, why should I not love him the better for that? It seems to me beauty is part of the finished language by which goodness speaks. You know you must have been a very handsome youth, godfather” — she looked up with one of her happy, loving smiles at the stately old man — “you were about as tall as Tito, and you had very fine eyes; only you looked a little sterner and prouder, and —”

“And Romola likes to keep all the pride to herself?” Bernardo cut in, not immune to the pretty coaxing. “Still, it is well that in one way Tito’s demands are far more modest than any proper Florentine husband of fitting rank we should ever have found for you: he wants no dowry at all.”

That was the agreement settled between Messer Bernardo del Nero, Romola, and Tito. Bardo assented with a dismissive wave of the hand when Bernardo told him he thought it would be wise to start selling off small bits of property to clear the old debts; he had always thought of property and debts as a kind of thick tangled wood his blind imagination never once penetrated, let alone navigated. And Tito set about winning Messer Bernardo’s full respect, throwing his quick, ready faculties into mastering all the fine points of Florentine money matters: the inner workings of the public funds known as the Monti, the market value of real estate, the ins and outs of banking profit.

“You will soon forget that Tito is not a native Florentine, godfather,” Romola said brightly. “See how he is learning every last thing about Florence.”

“It seems to me he is one of the demoni, the spirits of no particular country at all, child,” Bernardo said, smiling. “His mind is a little too nimble to be weighted down with all the heavy, messy old loyalties and memories we ordinary men carry tucked in our hearts.” Romola smiled back, wrapped in unshakable, happy confidence.

It was the final week of Florence’s Carnival, and the city’s streets hummed at their fullest, loudest pitch. Masked processions wound past shopfronts, singing the raucous ballads Lorenzo the Magnificent had made a beloved, unshakable tradition decades prior; the crowd’s favourite rigoletto round dance spun across the main piazza, feet stamping on cobblestones beneath a sharp, frosty blue sky. Pranks of every stripe ran riot, from flinging sugared comfits at passersby to hurling stones – stones most of all. The boys and striplings who formed so large a part of Florentine crowds were as loud and unruly as crickets in summer tree boughs at Carnival’s height, and theirs was an ancient, unchallenged privilege: barring all thoroughfares with long poles until every traveller paid a tribute to fund the boys’ suppers and bonfires. The evening’s centrepiece entertainment was the traditional stone-throwing brawl, far from tedious in its chaos: maimings came in all degrees, and not uncommonly more than one soul was left dead in the street. Carnival’s pleasures were thus checkered, stained with grossness and barbarity so stark that a painter rendering it truly would have to hide his canvas face to the wall, save to pull it out for the grave historical work of justifying the reforming zeal later generations would dismiss as narrow-minded, unaware of the cruelty it sought to uproot.

Still, there was no shortage of innocent, picturesque merriment, the natural product of a people quick of wit, warm of spirit, sharp of sense. There was no heavy, sodden drunkenness of the northern bloods, no stealthy, southern penchant for a brawl to end in a hidden dagger thrust.

By high morning, Carnival’s revellers were still lingering over their wine and gossiping over last night’s jests, when Tito Melema strode briskly toward the Via de’ Bardi. At his side walked young Bernardo Dovizi, the sharp-eyed man who would later stare out at the world from Raphael’s portrait as Cardinal da Bibbiena, and the two talked so animatedly as they pushed through the throng along the Por’ Santa Maria that they barely glanced at the costumes and noise around them. Even so, both cast quick, wary glances over their shoulders at regular intervals. When they turned onto the Lung’ Arno leading toward Ponte Rubaconte, one of Tito’s rapid scans picked out a figure not far behind him: someone he ached not to be recognised by, not today.

His time and thoughts were fully claimed by a once-in-a-lifetime event: that evening, he and Romola were to be formally betrothed. The ceremony had been arranged on far shorter notice than originally planned: though they had been slowly readying the rooms in Bardo’s home, furnishing everything but the long neglected library, it had been agreed to wait both the betrothal and the wedding until after Easter, when the year of probation Bernardo del Nero had insisted on for Tito would be over. But then word had come from Cardinal Giovanni, summoning Tito to Rome to help Dovizi organise his new library, leveraging his unmatched fluency in ancient Greek – an opportunity far too good to turn down. Tito had begged the elders to move the betrothal forward, to bind him and Romola to each other before he left: the sooner the wedding could happen when he returned, and the less painful their parting would be, if they had exchanged rings and signed formal contracts that made their bond more than half legal, impossible for Bernardo del Nero or anyone else to nullify. Romola had shared his eagerness, and the elders had consented. Now Tito was snatching a brief morning visit before he turned to the chaos of packing for his departure the next day – and the last thing he could afford was to be detained by the wrong person.

That person was unmistakably Tessa. He caught sight of her timid, forlorn little figure drifting toward the same turn on the Arno bank, and knew in an instant she had seen him, that she would follow. There was no way to slip away from her on this straight road to the bridge, not unless he kept Dovizi at his side the whole way. She would not dare speak or approach him while he had company, he told himself as he quickened his pace, rambling on with new threads of conversation until he walked his companion all the way to Bardo’s front steps, pulled off a hasty “addio” without turning his face anywhere Tessa might be standing. As he climbed the stone steps, he tried to shake the unpleasant thought away, telling himself Tessa might not have seen him after all, or might have lost him in the crowd.

But when his morning visit with Romola was over, he stepped out the door so quickly he barely glanced left or right – and his eyes, without his choosing, picked out the small figure in a white hood lingering right beside the threshold. Before he could move, a hand landed on his arm, not a timid little touch, but a desperate, firm grasp: Tessa had seen him break into a run and lunged forward. When he turned to her, her face was white and frightened, as if she already feared she had angered him.

“Tessa,” Tito said, his voice sharper than she had ever heard it. “Why are you here? You must not follow me, you must not loiter outside doorways waiting for me.”

Her blue eyes flooded with tears, and she said nothing. Tito’s stomach twisted with fear of being seen: this quiet, residential street was no bustling marketplace, and Bernardo del Nero, or any other dangerous onlooker who knew of his betrothal plans, might turn the corner at any moment. It was far worse than awkward, not least on this of all days. But he could not bring himself to drive her off with cruel words, not to this soft, silly little contadina he had stumbled into a foolish, hidden tryst with months ago, never expecting it would sprout into this inconvenient, unseasonable tangle of trouble.

“Tessa, I cannot talk to you here,” he said hurriedly, softening his tone to avoid upsetting her further. “I will walk on to the bridge and wait for you there. Follow me slowly.”

He strode fast to Ponte Rubaconte, leaned against the quaint little stone house built into the bridge’s side, and watched for her coming. Even Tito’s hard heart would have softened at the sight of her, her round face paled and sadder than the day he had last parted with her at the door of the Nunziata. The bridge was nearly empty, the quietest in Florence, no one else nearby to see them.

“Tessa, I have very little time,” he said the moment she drew near. “Do not cry. Why did you follow me? You must never do this again.”

“I thought you would come back to take care of me long before this,” she whispered, fighting back a sob that rose unbidden at the new sharp edge to his voice. “My stepfather beats me. I can’t bear it any more. Every holiday I have, I walk the whole city trying to find you, and I never can. Please don’t send me away again. The days are so long, I don’t care about the goats and the kids any more, I just –”

Her sobs broke loose, great fat tears rolling down her cheeks. Tito pulled her close to soothe her, knowing he had to send her away but not cruel enough to break her heart entirely. He could not weigh distant consequences, not when the moment pressed so sharp.

“My little Tessa,” he said, falling back into the old soft, caressing tone she remembered. “Bear with your stepfather just a little longer. I am going away to Rome, a long journey, but I will be back in a few weeks, and I promise I will come see you. Promise me you will be good, and wait for me.”

The familiar sound of his voice was enough to half soothe her. She looked up at him, tears glistening on her lashes, trying and failing to stop sobbing. “Yes,” she whispered. “But you won’t be long?”

“Not long at all. But I must go now. And remember: no one can ever know you see me, or you will lose me forever. Go straight home when I leave you, and never follow me again. Wait for me to come to you. Goodbye, my little Tessa. I will come back.”

He turned and walked away without looking back, no time to spare to see how she took his departure. When he finally glanced over his shoulder he was already in the Via de’ Benci, far past the bridge, and he knew Tessa, so trusting and obedient, would do exactly as he had been told. For that day, at least, the trouble was over. The faint, distant necessity of hurting Tessa later lingered in his mind no heavier than a far-off cry from a small suffering animal buried in the thicket, unnoticeable to a merry cavalcade trotting across sunlit plains, too busy looking at the bright path ahead.

By the time he was hastening back across Ponte Rubaconte for the second time that day, the memory of Tessa did not dim his happiness at all. He pulled his mantle tight around him, not so much to ward off the cold as to hide his fine, dainty clothes from drawing extra notice, and leapt up Bardo’s stone steps two at a time.

“Where is the damigella?” he asked Maso, the servant who met him at the door.

“In the library,” Maso said. “She’s all ready. Monna Brigida and Messer Bernardo are already there with Ser Braccio, none of the other guests have arrived yet.”

“Ask her to meet me in the salotto for a few minutes alone.”

The room he stepped into was a vivid contrast to the pale, muted half-light of the library. Its walls were frescoed bright with playful scenes of nymphs and cherubs dancing under blue skies, wading through wild flowers and chasing birds. Red leather benches lined the walls, a single great oak table sat in the centre, and the only other furnishings were two tall white ceramic vases and a small marble faun playing the flute, carved by a promising young artist named Michelangelo Buonarotti. It felt like stepping out into open sun. Tito kept his mantle wrapped around him, his eyes fixed on the door, until Romola stepped through.

She looked like a tall lily come to life, all white silk and rippling gold: her dress cinched at the waist with a golden girdle strung with heavy tassels, her long hair spilling in waves down her back beneath a wispy white veil caught at her brow with a band of pearls, Bernardo del Nero’s betrothal gift. The veil fell back from her face, floating behind her.

*Regina mia* – my queen, Tito breathed, taking her hand to kiss it, still wrapped in his mantle. He stepped back to stare at her, and she stood there, calm and glowing, revelling in the warmth of his admiring gaze.

“Romola,” he said, remembering how little time they had, “you said you would show me the next room once you had finished arranging it. Can we go there now?”

She led him without a word into the narrow long room beside the salotto, frescoed as brightly, this time only with birds and wild flowers. The furniture was old, faded relics of feminine use and ornament arranged in an open oak cabinet between the two narrow windows, the portrait of Romola’s mother hanging above it, and the small plain crucifix she had brought home from San Marco sitting on the cabinet’s top edge.

“I have something hidden under my mantle,” Tito said, smiling as he threw the heavy garment off, revealing a small painted triptych tabernacle, the work of Piero di Cosimo. The eccentric painter had carried out Tito’s plan perfectly, making up for all his long delays. “Do you know what this is for, my Romola? It is a little shrine, to hide that sad old remembrancer away from you forever. You are done with sadness now. We will bury every memory of it here, in a tomb of joy.”

A faint, soft tremor crossed Romola’s face as Tito reached for the crucifix, but she did not stop him. She had long wanted to banish the lingering, unquiet shadows of old memories that still flitted across her new happiness, unanswerable questions she did not want to face. He opened the triptych, set the crucifix in its central niche, snapped the little lock shut, pulled the key out, and set the painted tabernacle in the exact spot the crucifix had stood.

“Look,” he said, turning her to face it. “See the portraits old Piero made of us? Am I not the purple-gowned Bacchus, crowning you Ariadne? It is perfect, is it not?”

“Ah, it is you,” Romola said, her eyes glistening with happy tears. “You have crowned my poor quiet life, Tito. That is exactly true.”

Their hands laced together as they stared at the painted scenes: the ship gliding across a calm blue sea, the never-wilting ivy winding around its frame, cherubs who had laid down their sharp arrows to shower soft rose petals over the pair, the once fierce tigers and leopards that represented all her old troubles, now tamed and sleeping at their feet. Even the strange sea monsters with their merry grins, Tito teased, were once the dull, heavy passages in old books that had become fun to read now that they could sit side by side.

“Tito mio,” Romola laughed, half teasing, and held out her hand. “Give me the key, won’t you?”

“Not a chance,” Tito said playfully, tucking the small key into his scarsella pouch at his belt. “I’m going to throw it in the Arno.”

“But what if I ever want to look at the crucifix again?”

“Exactly why we hide it,” he said, pressing a soft kiss to her brow. “Hidden away behind all these images of youth and joy.”

Romola did not argue. Strong souls submitted easily when they felt no real reason to resist. Together they joined the waiting company, forming a small dignified procession walking across Ponte Rubaconte toward Santa Croce. They moved slowly: Bardo, who had barely left his house in years, walked with unsteady, timid steps, and his pace suited the gouty, stately gait of Messer Bartolommeo Scala, who had come to grace the occasion alongside his daughter Alessandra. Once, the sumptuary laws of Florence had been forced to cap betrothal guest lists at four hundred, two hundred per family, because the feasts after both the formal *sposalizio* and the later wedding *nozze* had grown ruinously expensive, even the scholar Leonardo Bruno complaining his own betrothal had nearly beggared him. But Bardo, proud and poor, had refused to invite guests on the strength of name or status, and the small group of twenty following the couple were all close personal friends, no one else. Bernardo del Nero walked ahead as their vanguard, Bardo led by Tito on his right arm and Romola on his left. Bardo had been married at Santa Croce decades before, and had insisted Romola be betrothed and wed there rather than the tiny nearby church of Santa Lucia: he held a perfect mental image of the grand stone basilica, and hoped to one day be buried there, among the Florentines who had served their city well.

The short walk lay far from the noisiest Carnival chaos, and the red glow of the setting sun gilded their path, casting soft warm light over the small, solemn procession with the blind old father at its centre. By the time the betrothal ceremony was complete, the rings glinting gold on Tito and Romola’s fingers, dusk had fallen, dim struggling starlight overhead, and the servants lit their torches to light their way out.

As they stepped onto the broad church steps, a strange, dreary Miserere chant drifted to their ears. Down at the far end of the piazza, a crowd was surging toward them from the Borgo de’ Greci, pushed forward by something coming up the street.

“Only one of their masqued processions,” Tito said, staying close at Romola’s side while Bernardo del Nero shepherded Bardo ahead.

Slowly, the spectacle came into view, towering far above the heads of the onlookers: a huge, ghastly painted effigy of Winged Time, his scythe raised, his hourglass in his hand, surrounded by his winged children the Hours. He rode on a great high cart draped entirely in black, the bullocks pulling it swathed in black cloth too, only their white horns poking up above the gloom, so that to onlookers in the darkening shadow of the houses, it looked as if Time and his ghostly family were floating through the air. Behind the cart, a troop of sheeted, robed figures glided forward, chanting their wailing dirge.

A cold shudder of horror seized Romola. For a split second, it felt as if her dead brother’s old, unshakable apocalyptic vision from San Marco was coming true. She clung tight to Tito’s arm.

“What grim foolishness your Florentines love,” he said, laughing soft to calm her. “This is no doubt Piero di Cosimo’s work, that odd man who delights in this kind of grim merriment.”

“Tito, I wish it had never happened,” Romola whispered. “It deepens those old images from that vision I have tried so hard to forget.”

“Nay, my Ariadne,” Tito said, pressing her gloved hand tight. “You will not look at that ghastly mummery. I locked all sadness away from you, remember? Now you only look forward, to Easter, when you will triumph over every last trace of sorrow, with me.”

The crisp November wind carried the scent of woodsmoke and newly pressed linen over Florence’s terracotta roofscape on the 17th of November, 1494. It had been more than eighteen months since Tito and Romola’s sunlit Easter wedding, when handfuls of rainbow-hued sugar comfits had been tossed over the newlyweds in the old Byzantine custom, a charm that promised their shared life would be stippled with nothing but sweet fortune. But those months had shifted the very ground under the city: just as the root sap feeds every bud and fruit on a thousand-bloom tree, so the great sweeping political tides reshaping all of Italy dictated even the private fortunes of a quiet scholarly couple like Tito and Romola.

Barely seven days before, the ancient fire of republican Florence had blazed back to life. The iron clapper of the Palazzo della Signoria’s great alarm bell boomed out across the piazzas, and citizens flooded the streets armed with rusted halberds, farm tools, and rough-hewn cudgels to chase the arrogant Piero de’ Medici straight out the San Gallo gate. His hired horsemen spooked and galloped at his heels, fleeing toward Bologna, while his sharper, younger brother the cardinal slipped away disguised as a ragged Franciscan friar. The Republic set a price on both their heads. Crowds sacked the homes of Medici loyalists per long-held custom, scrubbed the faded, ignominious portraits of decades-old anti-Medici conspirators off public walls, and sent invitations home to every citizen exiled by the Medici regime. The half-fledged tyrants had been well and truly routed from their gilded nest on the Via Larga, and the Florentine Republic had reclaimed its free will for the first time in a generation.

That grand palace on the Via Larga, the old Medici seat, was now draped, polished, and furbished to receive a new occupant. Street-side tapestries dyed deep crimson and indigo, not seen since the last great Medici feast, hung from window sills; hundreds of banners snapped in the sharp wind; thick woven carpets were spread across every stone step and cobblestone path where the feet of the honored guest might tread. By every outward visible sign of public joy, Florence could not have been more eager to welcome him. That river of vivid color wound all the way from the Via Larga palace, past the marble facade of the Duomo, through the crowded Piazza della Signoria, across the goldsmith-lined arches of the Ponte Vecchio, to the Porta San Frediano: the western gate facing Pisa. A raised platform draped in velvet had been erected there for the Signoria, where Messer Luca Corsini, doctor of laws, felt his pulse thrum fast as he ran a thumb over the parchment of the grand Latin oration he was set to deliver. Every elder statesman of the Republic had trimmed his beard to a smooth sheen, donned his best lined silk lucco robe, and practiced his place in the long public procession.
