The city’s noble young men had spent days preening in their new French-cut velvet tunics, certain their lean, elegant Florentine frames would wear the foreign fashion with a grace no Frenchman could match. The entire body of the city’s clergy, from the archbishop blazing in gold vestments to the long lines of black, white, and grey robed monks, had gathered at dawn to map their procession, arranging sacred relics, embroidered banners, and jeweled consecrated vessels so they would fall into perfect step to meet the illustrious visitor due to arrive at three that afternoon.

This was no ordinary guest. He had crossed the Alpine passes with an army no Italian had ever seen before: thousands of ferocious Swiss mercenaries who fought as fiercely for personal loyalties as for pay, a host of proud noble cavaliers, an unprecedented infantry where one in every hundred men carried an arquebus, and even bronze cannons that fired iron balls instead of stone, pulled by horses not oxen, fast enough to reload and fire a second round before a besieged city could patch the breach their first shot tore open. Some Florentines whispered he was Charlemagne reborn, the legendary restorer of Florence, rightful conqueror of corrupt kings, regulator and savior of the Church. Others hailed him as a new Cyrus, liberator of the chosen people, come to rebuild the Temple in purity. This young king, Charles VIII of France, was the son of that cunning Louis XI who had died ten years prior trembling at the unforeseen consequences of his own political scheming. He had led his armies across the Alps with glittering, grand plans: he would march through Italy cheered by every grateful crowd, resolve every bitter feuding faction in Rome, claim the Kingdom of Naples by right of inheritance and a little well-placed force, then launch from there on a holy crusade to crush the Ottoman Turks, slaying half their armies and converting the rest to the Christian faith. It was a scheme grand enough to make even sober men believe that marble statues might sweat, that phantom fiery armies clashed in the midnight sky, that strange monstrous births among livestock were divine omens: this was no ordinary turn of fortune, most agreed, it was no less than a special work of God.

That widespread conviction did not spring merely from the terrifying size of the French army, but from the fiery preaching of one man: Fra Girolamo Savonarola, prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco. Back in September, when the first news arrived that the French had crossed the Alps into Italy, Savonarola had climbed the Duomo’s pulpit and preached from the Book of Genesis: “Behold, I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth.” Guided by divine hand, he said, he had reached that exact passage in his Lenten exposition months before, and he declared that the flood was no metaphor for rain: it was the French army itself, both a scourge to punish the wicked and a purifying tide that would wash all corruption out of the Church. His audience, many of them the most learned, cultivated men in the most cultivated city in all of Italy, listened with skin prickling in awe. For four full years prior, he had thundered from that same pulpit that a scourge was coming down on Italy, one that would purify the Church of all its rotten corruption. He believed he had a prophetic mission equal to the ancient Hebrew prophets, and that the Florentines who heard his warnings were a new chosen people. In an age where self-proclaimed seers and gossiping fortune-tellers were common even inside cloisters, Savonarola stood apart: their prophecies were tiny, trivial candles lighting up small corners of petty fate, while his was a blazing lighthouse beam, shining across the whole of the known world to warn men of coming judgment.

Only two years prior, in 1492, the year Lorenzo de’ Medici died and the young Greek wanderer Tito Melema first stepped into Florence, Italy had known nothing but unbroken peace and plenty. Harvests had been fat with corn, wine, and oil, new marble palaces rose in every fair city, new villas dotted every sunlit Tuscan hill, and the rich men who owned them never feared the poor, for citizens’ armour had all gone rusted hanging on walls, the populace had grown tame, and tyrants hired mercenary armies the same way they bought imported silk from Smyrna. No one feared the Turks anymore, and the Borgia Pope found it far more profitable to take their gold in exchange for quiet complicity than to waste time planning crusades. The world seemed arranged perfectly for the benefit of the wealthy, powerful, and corrupt: lust, treachery, oppression, even murder were all acceptable as long as you had the money and influence to get away with them. As a pretty garnish for that life of luxury, wealthy patrons poured gold into fine art and humanist learning, so flatterers could pen their praise in the most elegant Ciceronian Latin, and great painters would paint the sacred and the profane with equal, perfect skill. The Church itself had never been more corrupt at its highest ranks, nor more spiritually lifeless among its lower clergy, yet it had never been wealthier. The skies were blue and calm, no one saw a single sign of approaching earthquake. And yet Savonarola, for years already, had been thundering that a sword hung suspended in those very calm blue skies: the sword of God’s justice, about to fall at any moment, to punish the wicked and purify the world. Seventeen years prior, in Ferrara, the unbearable gap between the lives men lived and the faith they claimed to follow had burned so hot in his chest that he had renounced all worldly pleasure at 23 and entered the cloister. He could not believe a just God would let this state of moral rot last forever, and when rumors began to spread in 1493 that Charles VIII was gathering his army to cross the Alps, Savonarola knew his prophecy was coming true. This was the flood he had foretold, this new Cyrus an instrument of divine will: if Florence, the city that had received his special warning, repented of its sins like the ancient city of Nineveh, the storm would pass over it and leave only life-giving rain.

For all Savonarola’s great influence, though, now that the French king had been in Italy for three months and was almost at Florence’s gates, the public mood was a tangled mix of excitement, fear, and deep distrust. He had not redressed a single grievance the Italians had complained of, and Florence had no reason to thank him. Piero de’ Medici, in his cowardice, had handed over all Florence’s key border fortresses to the French king without a single honorable treaty in exchange, and Charles still held them. He had done nothing to crush the revolt in Pisa, which had been emboldened by his arrival to throw off Florentine rule. Even the city’s official orators, including Savonarola himself, had failed to get a concrete promise of good treatment from Charles, who kept saying only that he would “settle all matters” once he was inside Florence’s walls. Still, the citizens had taken great joy in chasing Piero de’ Medici out of the city for his treason, and that act of defiance had stoked the old republican fire in their bones.

Behind all the bright public tapestries and banners meant to signal welcome, the city had made far more secret preparations: hired mercenary soldiers were quartered hidden inside walled palaces, old armour had been polished until it gleamed, cudgels and sharp farm tools were stacked in every street-side shopfront, barricades of heavy planks and stakes were pre-built and ready to drag across alley mouths, and piles of paving stones were stacked on every upper story window sill. The Florentines were in no mood to let a foreign king push them around, not after the sweet taste of chasing the Medici tyrants out just a week prior. Their temper was not improved when small parties of French advance riders rode through the streets ahead of the king, hawks perched on their wrists, swaggering as they chalked the doors of the best houses to mark them as quarters for the French nobles.

That was the sharp, complicated, charged mood that hung over Florence on the morning of November 17th, 1494.

The morning sky hung pale and grey, but the overcast light barely touched the vibrancy of the Piazza del Duomo, strung from spire to arch with a holiday canopy of deep blue drapery, dotted with constellations of golden lilies and blazoned noble coats of arms. sheaves of banners fluttered unfurled from the Baptistery’s corners, though the marble steps of the great cathedral were not yet carpeted for the upcoming royal procession: they bore the tread of hundreds of ordinary citizens hurrying to and fro, no velvet slippers or ceremonial guards among them. It was the hour of the great Advent sermons, and the same political fervor that had draped the streets in festive colour made the preaching inside the Duomo a necessity no devout or wary Florentine would dare skip.

Yet not all feet in the piazza turned toward the cathedral steps. Patricians and artisans alike moved at the brisk, purposeful pace of men on urgent errands, and clusters of talkers dotted the open space, some lingering late for the sermon by choice, others content to miss it entirely. No faces wore the lazy softness of a crowd enjoying an idle holiday. Small groups huddled in tight, fierce discussion; listeners leaned in to catch every word from a single speaker, only to snap their heads up and scan every passing stranger as if half-expected a blow to fall at any moment.

At the corner where the Piazza’s artificial rainbow glow cut off, the raw grey daylight fell full on the sombre stone facades of the Via de’ Cerretani, where a thick cluster of working people had gathered. Most wore the marks of their daily labour on their clothes and skin, and nearly every man carried a tool or weapon he could use at a moment’s notice: bare brawny arms, stained work garments, they stood sharply contrasted against the bright, stagy pomp of the piazza behind them, listening to the thin, sharp-tongued notary Ser Cioni. Barely two and a half years prior, his bitter speeches had been laughed to scorn in the Mercato, but now he spoke with the easy, complacent confidence of a man whose faction held all the power, and who knew his voice carried weight with the crowd.

“Never dare prattle to me about bloodthirsty Swiss pikemen or ferocious French infantry,” he was saying, his incisive voice cutting through the murmur. “They might as well be trapped in a mountain pass as in our Florentine streets. Peasants have cut down the finest condottieri armies in our history once they herded them between precipices. I tell you: Florentines have no reason to fear any foreign army inside their own walls.”

“True enough, Ser Cioni!” called a man whose hands and arms were crusted deep with crimson dye that looked exactly like fresh dried blood, a small hatchet tucked into his belt. “Those French cavaliers that swaggered through the other day, preening in their fancy doublets? I saw the lot of them strutting down Ognissanti, squinting up at the Vespucci and Agli palaces like they thought our homes were not fit lodging for their fine selves, eyeing us Florentines like top-knotted game cocks pitying us for not knowing how to strut. ‘Aye, my fine Galli,’ thinks I, ‘stretch out those bellies all you like. The meat axe in my belt will slip right through them all the easier.’ Then the old cow lowed* — you all know what that means, the great Palazzo Vecchio bell — and I knew trouble was afoot, no matter what. I dumped my armful of dyed cloth in the nearest doorway, grabbed my axe, and chased after those fine cavaliers towards Vigna Nuova. Guccio came running up beside me, said ‘I think Piero de’ Medici is marching back.’ Bembè! I knew it! We threw up a barricade right there, the Frenchmen turned around and realized they were trapped, and a swarm of our Ciompi** came surging up after them. One wool-beater with a scythe in his hand cut the feather clean off the leading cavalier’s hat! It’s no lie! The lasses up in the windows pelted them with pebbles to scare them off. Turned out Piero wasn’t coming after all, and more’s the pity. We’d have left him no legs to run away on, no wings to fly with.”

* The local phrase for the sounding of the great Palazzo Vecchio alarm bell.
** The poorer wool artisans: beaters, carders, washers, the core of the 14th century Ciompi rebellion.

“Well said, Oddo!” a young butcher called out, his own sharp knife sheathed at his belt. “I say Piero will think twice before he dares show his face here again. He looked as scared as a hunted chicken the last time we hustled and pelted him in the piazza. The coward could have made a proper stand with all his horsemen at his back, but he ran. We won’t swallow any more Medici tyrants, no matter what the French king tries to force down our throats.”

“But I don’t like talk of those French cannons they’re hauling south,” whimpered Goro, no less round and jolly for two years of political grievances. “San Giovanni defend us! If the Frate’s right and the Lord loves Florence, why couldn’t He have sent the French a different road to Naples?”

“Thou’ve a better head on thee than I thought, Goro,” the dyer Oddo laughed, clapping him on the back. “Why couldn’t they have gone through Bologna? Or Arezzo? We wouldn’t have minded them tramping through Arezzo a bit.”

“Fools, all of you,” Ser Cioni huffed, about to launch into a speech about how the French presence would bring Florence nothing but glory — when a dozen voices cried out “Look there!” and every head turned down the length of the Via de’ Cerretani.

“It’s Lorenzo Tornabuoni, and one of those French noblemen staying in his household,” Ser Cioni said, with clear contempt at the interruption. “That deep, smooth Tornabuoni pretends to be all loyal to the popular government, but he’s a Medicean at heart. Mark my words.”

The approaching party made a glittering show. There was Tornabuoni, patrician and smooth courtier; the French nobleman in finery stitched with gold thread, his starched white linen peeking out ostentatiously at cuffs and collar; two more wealthy young Florentines in new dresses made for the upcoming royal procession. Walking on the Frenchman’s left, though, was a figure no amount of brocade could outshine: Tito Melema, dressed all in plain black for mourning, soon to don the bright red mantle marking his official post as Latin Secretary to the Ten. His fluency in French and deep knowledge of southern Italian customs had made him indispensable in all dealings with the French forces, and he had already made multiple trips out to the French camp at Signa. Fortune shone bright on him still, he smiled and listened and explained with his usual easy, unassuming grace — only a very keen, watchful eye would have spotted the subtle shift in him, the change that comes when the last trace of moral youth melts away, and a man deliberately, consciously chooses the life he will lead. His face was still soft, his eyes still clear as spring water, but something intangible was gone: the faint uncertainty, the twilight of a man still half undecided.

The French nobleman was collecting last instructions for the royal parade before riding back to Signa, and the whole party had come to survey the Piazza del Duomo, where the king’s procession was meant to pause for a special religious ceremony. Every eye turned to them as they stepped out of the street’s shadow into the piazza’s bright drapery light — but the stares were far from all admiring. Jokes circulated about the Frenchman’s oddly shaped, pointed “hoof-toe” shoes, made to flatter the king’s absurd excessive foot size, and no one bothered to mumble quiet snarls about “Medicean lackeys” out of earshot. But Tornabuoni had perfected the art of hiding irritation to court public favour, and Tito, besides his natural instinct to disarm ill will with easy good humour, carried the detached, unimpassioned air of an alien who could not feel the burning, personal fury such names roused in native Florentines.

They paused where they could get a good clear view of the Duomo’s main doorway, to debate whether the festoons and painted crests hung there suited the royal ceremonial. Tornabuoni waved over Piero di Cosimo the painter, who as usual at that hour was loitering outside Nello the barber’s shop. A lively, funny debate broke out, stoked by the French nobleman’s utterly bewildered reaction to Piero’s dry, sharp observations — which Tito translated word for word, to everyone’s delight. Even the snarling, suspicious onlookers grew curious, their faces softening into that half-amused, half-embarrassed smile of people who can’t quite hear the joke that has everyone else cackling. It was a perfect moment for Tito: he was the only person in the whole party who could have pulled off such a funny, seamless translation, and he basked unselfconsciously in the approving glances, as if he was soaking in warm sun. The piazza’s rainbow light wrapped around the laughing group, and all the solemn sermon-goers had already vanished inside the Duomo’s dark doors. For a minute, it truly felt like the city’s decorations were for a real, uncomplicated Florentine holiday.

But out in the grey, unadorned side streets far from the piazza’s glow, other travellers were making their way towards the centre of the city, in no festive mood. French soldiers in their distinctive uniforms herded three ragged men before them, growing more and more surrounded by a crowd of angry, unadmiring Florentines. The three prisoners wore almost nothing but rags. Their hands were bound tight together with cord, and a thick rope looped around each man’s neck and torso, so the soldier holding the other end could throttle them in an instant if they tried to run.

The soldiers prodded their prisoners forward with the knotted ends of their ropes, barking broken Italian orders to make them beg. Two of the younger, sturdier prisoners obeyed, holding out their bound hands to every Florentine they passed and whimpering piteously: “For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us a coin towards our ransom! We are Tuscans, captured fighting in Lunigiana!”

But the third old man refused to speak a single word, no matter how hard the soldiers struck him. He looked nothing like his two fellow prisoners, who were young, hardy, and looked like ordinary coarse beggars in the rags their captors had left them. He was well over sixty, his long neglected beard and the thick hair falling around his bald crown nearly all white. His broad, thickset frame was still firm and upright even starved thin, radiating stubborn strength well into old age — a quality reflected in his dark eyes and heavy dark eyebrows, whose vivid colour stood out shockingly against his yellow, bloodless, deeply wrinkled face. Yet there was something fitful, wandering in his gaze, even when a flash of sharp ferocity lit up as he scanned every face and window: his lips stayed clamped tight, his bound hands hung resolutely at his sides. He would not beg.

The sight had stoked the watching Florentines into slow, searing exasperation. At first many had pressed coins into the prisoners’ hands half-automatically, moved by the appeal to God, or cowed by rumours of the French soldiery’s brutal campaigns. But the further the sorry procession penetrated into the heart of the city, the thinner that compliance grew, until the soldiers found themselves surrounded by a shouting, jostling throng of men and boys, whose jeers were easy enough for any foreigner to understand without a translator. The soldiers began to fear for their position: they ached to draw their pikes and strike out, but they could not let go of the ropes tied to their prisoners’ necks, and they hurried along as fast as they could, hoping to make it to the nearest friendly hostelry for shelter.

“French dogs!” “Bullock feet!” “Snatch their pikes from them!” “Cut the prisoners’ cords and see how fast those web-footed French curs run!” the crowd yelled. Everyone seemed happy to hurl insults, but no one dared make the first move — until the dyer Oddo spotted the commotion, understood what was happening, and roared “Santiddio! What a shameful sight! And you fools just hoot at them? Come on!” He snatched his axe from his belt and sprinted into the crowd, the butcher and all his fellow artisans at his heels — only Goro turned tail and scurried down a narrow alley to hide.

Oddo, his red-stained arms flying, his axe held high, was like a waving battle standard to the crowd. No sooner had he pushed through to the front of the throng than the impish young apprentice Lollo, who worked for the local conjuror, danced forward before anyone could stop him. He had no particular pity for the prisoners, but he carried a sharp little knife of his own, and it struck him that slicing a prisoner’s rope and darting back before the guarding soldier could react would be the funniest, most dexterous trick he had ever pulled. The crowd was jostling so hard no one noticed him slip right up next to the oldest prisoner — in a single flash, he sliced clean through the neck rope. “Run, old man!” he piped in the prisoner’s ear, then darted off as fast as a spooked fowl.

The old man did not hesitate. The thought of escape had never left his mind, and the crowd’s obvious anger at his captors had given him fresh hope. He bolted at once, and the Florentines surged forward between him and his guard, blocking the soldier from grabbing him. The other two prisoners were freed in the chaos seconds later, and the soldiers stumbled after them, tripping over the cobblestones in their awkward hoof-shaped shoes, slowed by the crowd but not properly attacked. One young prisoner turned up the Borgo di San Lorenzo and drew half the chase off with him, but the main surge ran straight towards the Duomo piazza.

“A prisoner escape!” Lorenzo Tornabuoni exclaimed, as his party turned around just beside the cathedral steps to see the ragged old man dash past. “The mob wasn’t content with emptying the Bargello the other day. If no proper authority is in sight, they’ll attack the guards and free every thief in the city. Ah — there’s a French soldier. That is far more serious.”

The French soldier Tornabuoni spotted was struggling along the north edge of the piazza, but the old prisoner he was chasing had run the other way. Baldassarre Calvo had wheeled around the Baptistery and was running straight for the Duomo steps, determined to claim the sanctuary of the church itself rather than trust his thin legs to outrun the soldiers. But his foot caught on a cracked marble step, and he stumbled, pitching forward straight at the group of signori standing with their backs turned to him. He caught himself only by grabbing tight at the nearest man’s arm.

It was Tito Melema who felt that iron, calloused grip on his velvet sleeve. He turned his head, and the face of his long-lost adoptive father was inches from his own. The two men froze, dead silent. Baldassarre’s dark, haggard face burned with unmistakeable, ferocious rage, his bound hands tightening so hard on Tito’s arm his knuckles went white. Tito’s own face drained of all colour, his lips bloodless, his whole body locked stiff with the kind of terror that paralyses a man where he stands. To them it felt like an eternity — in truth, it was only a single heartbeat.

The first sound Tito heard was Piero di Cosimo’s short, dry laugh, right beside his shoulder. “Ha, ha! Now I know exactly what a ghost is supposed to look like.”

“This is another escaped prisoner,” Lorenzo Tornabuoni said, frowning. “Who is he, I wonder?”

“Some madman, surely,” Tito said.

He hardly knew how the words had escaped his lips. There are moments when our deepest unspoken passions speak for us, when we stand outside ourselves watching, shocked at what we have done. The words carried the sharp, sudden inspiration of a crime he had never dared premeditate, made real in a single instant. For a split second it felt to Tito as if some dark magical poison had leapt straight from Baldassarre’s blazing eyes into his own veins, running hot through his whole body. Then the iron grip on his arm slackened, and Baldassarre stumbled past him, vanishing into the dark, shadowed doors of the Duomo.

“You are far too quick to take fright, you know,” Piero jeered, barking another scornful laugh as he tapped the edge of his half-finished portrait sketch. “My little drawing of that old wild fellow doesn’t do his glare justice, you see. He had the very look of a caged tiger. I must slip into the Duomo later today to stare at him again, just to fix the face properly in my memory.”
“It’s no pleasant thing to be seized by a raving madman, if madman he truly is,” Lorenzo Tornabuoni put in gently, offering Tito a gracious out for his momentary fluster. “Though more like a common ruffian, for all we know. We’ll hear more of the story soon enough. I say, we ought to step over and see if our authority carries enough weight to quiet this brawl between our townsfolk and your countrymen,” he added, turning to the French captain standing nearby.
They advanced toward the crowd with drawn swords, all the quiet onlookers falling in behind them as an informal escort, and Tito trailed along too. He knew he could not afford to miss a single scrap of information the rest of Florence would soon learn about Baldassarre, and the first icy paralysis of terror had already melted away, replaced by the slippery, quick-witted ingenuity mortal danger stirs even in the most timid of men.
The rabble of rough artisans and jeering boys, far keener to hoot at the French soldier and tease him than to land or suffer any serious blow, scattered at the sight of well-known signori marching toward them with naked steel. The Frenchman was soon questioned: he and his men had simply marched their prisoners back into Florence to beg alms for their ransoms. Two of the captives were Tuscan conscripts taken in the Lunigiana hills; the third, the raving old man, had been travelling with a party of Genoese traders the French foragers had clashed with outside Fivizzano. He might be deranged, the soldier shrugged, but he’d done no one any harm—he could not even make out a single word the old man shrieked, let alone follow his speech.
Tito heard every word of this, but tuned out all the rest of the chatter around him until Tornabuoni addressed him directly.
“Will you walk back with us, Melema?” Lorenzo asked. “Or, since our party is riding out to Signa shortly, would you not do the sensible thing, as is all the fashion these days, and head to hear the Frate preach? They say he’s like a swollen torrent come down off the hills this morning, his sermon thundering loud enough to shake the rafters. We all have to make an appearance there sooner or later, you know, if we want to keep our Medicean skins intact. I’d go myself if I had the leisure for it.”
The colour had flooded back into Tito’s cheeks by then, and he forced himself to laugh easily. “I am of course one of the great orator’s many admirers,” he said, smiling bright as ever. “But unfortunately, I am bound to meet the Segretario Scala long before the procession is due to start.”
“I’m heading straight to the Duomo to gawk at that savage old man again,” Piero cut in.
“Then do us all a charity, Piero mio,” Tornabuoni said dryly. “Take him to the travellers’ hospital on your way. The monks there can work out if the poor creature needs to be locked away somewhere safe.”
The party split ways soon after, and Tito turned his steps toward the Palazzo Vecchio, where he was due to meet Bartolommeo Scala. It was no long walk down the sun-warmed stone streets of Florence, but to Tito it stretched endless, each minute dragging slow as the half-asleep stretches of a morning dream. The short spans of cobblestone lane and open piazza were crammed full of half-forgotten memories, dark premonitions, and torturing fears heavy enough to fill the story of months.
He felt as if a cold serpent had begun coiling itself tight around his limbs. Baldassarre alive, here in Florence, was a living, breathing vengeance that would never rest, no more than a winding serpent would pause until it had crushed its prey in its coils. That man was never the sort to overlook a betrayal, to let a great wrong pass unpunished. His love and his hatred burned with the same wild, unquenchable fervour that consumed every other part of his being, that would drive a man to sacrifice his very self to his passion as if it were a god to be worshipped through self-ruin.
Baldassarre had loosened his grip and vanished. Tito knew exactly what that meant: the vengeance was being planned, plotted carefully, to make certain it struck true.
If only he had not blurted out those fateful words—“He is a madman”—if only he had summoned the courage, the clear-headedness, to admit he knew who Baldassarre was, would the risk have been far smaller? He could have sworn he had had every reason to believe Baldassarre had died at sea, back when the pirates attacked their galley. The only people who could have proved that a lie were Fra Luca, who was long dead, and the crew of the companion galley that had brought him the news of the pirate attack. The odds were infinitesimal that Baldassarre had ever run into a single one of those sailors. Tito’s chest ached with bitter regret: a timely, well-spun lie right then could have spared him every fatal consequence. But to tell that lie he would have needed perfect calm at the very moment he was convulsed with shock. The words had leaped out of his mouth unbidden, like a thing born sudden out of the dark, unplanned.
Tito was learning the unforgiving law of the human soul: that every small, repeated choice for good or ill builds the person you become, and you do not get to choose your reactions in a crisis out of nowhere.
There was only one sliver of hope left for him now: that Baldassarre would fail in his quest for vengeance. And then a thought, crueller than any he had ever dared entertain before, flashed bright in his mind. Could the unplanned words he had blurted out earlier hold a grain of truth? Enough truth, at least, to back him up when he denied every wild claim Baldassarre might make against him? The old man looked wild, his eyes blazing, his clothes ragged. With that fierce heart and overworked brain, it was more than possible that years of suffering had unhinged his wits. If that were the case, the vengeance that sought to drag Tito’s name through the mud would fail entirely.
But there was another kind of vengeance that no clever lie could fend off. Baldassarre was of a temperament where a knife-thrust came as naturally as a tiger lashing out with its claws. Tito shuddered, sick with dread of public disgrace—but he also carried that soft, physical fear inherent to a man who loves ease and pleasure, that makes him recoil from wounds and death far more than he could ever welcome them as an escape from shame. His mind darted at once to the idea of hidden defensive armour, something he could wear under his tunic to shield him from a blow his wits could never talk their way out of. He was stunned by the raw, consuming power of the fear that held him. It was as if he had been struck by some blighting illness that had turned all the bright, joyful ease of his young life to ash and pain.
There was still one way out, of course. He could turn back, hunt Baldassarre down, confess everything to him—to Romola—to all the city. But the thought never even crossed his mind. That kind of repentance, that cuts every last tie to old lies and old cowardice, demands far more than selfish fear. Tito had never learned to see truth as a source of strength and safety. The only strength he had ever trusted was his own quick wit, his own talent for dissembling. Now that the first shock that had made his fear so plain for everyone to see had passed, he was certain he could outmaneuver every threat with cool, calculated deception—and a hidden layer of steel to protect his body.
It was entirely typical of Tito’s state of mind in this crisis that no scheme to actively kill or get rid of Baldassarre ever occurred to him. He ran through every other possibility, even the idea of fleeing Florence entirely, but he never once thought of arranging his enemy’s death. His fear did not curdle into active, cruel malice; he would still have been glad not to cause any unnecessary pain to any living soul. He had simply always chosen to make his own life as easy as possible, to carry the weight of his human lot in such a way that no part of it ever pinched or discomforted him. That repeated small choice had landed him in all kinds of unforeseen messes before now. The question he faced now was not whether he could share the common weight of fate with all his suffering fellow men. It was whether all the cleverness and all the lies he had spent years perfecting could save him from being crushed by the consequences of that lifelong habit of choosing the easy way out.

The heavy velvet curtain strung across the Duomo’s side doorway swished shut behind Baldassarre, his wrists raw where the hemp cord binding them rubbed against his skin, a second length of the same rough rope looped tight around his throat and torso. He had stumbled there half-expecting the vast marble nave to be all empty stillness: unlit candles guttering on half-shrouded side altars, pale statues rigid as corpses, a handful of doddering old worshippers far off in the choir chanting in droning, monotonous voices. That was the only sort of church he had ever known, the kind a man with no religious purpose entered only to hide.

But what met his gaze was no dead, silent space. Thousands of warm, breathing faces packed every aisle and cranny, all tilted upward in breathless, dead silence toward the pulpit set at the angle where the nave curved to meet the choir. Every rank of Florentine life was crammed shoulder to shoulder: crimson-robed magistrates near the front, silk-gowned noblewomen with pearl hairpins glinting in the candlelight, dye-streaked artisans still in their work tunics at the back, sun-browned peasants tramping in from the hill villages standing huddled in the side aisles. Up in the pulpit, a Dominican friar with sharp, strong features and dark hair held a plain wooden crucifix clamped tight in his fist as he preached.

For the first long minute, Baldassarre did not hear a single word he said. Even quiet as his entrance had been, a handful of eyes near the door flicked to him, sharp with surprise and suspicion. The rope made plain he was an escaped prisoner – but every man on the run knew the Duomo was a sanctuary he had every right to claim. His grey streaked hair and the wild, haggard misery carved deep into his face stirred pity more than fear, and as he stood motionless, his gaze drifting from the vast throng down to the worn marble pavement under his bare feet, the onlookers who had marked his presence soon turned back, drawn again to the far greater pull of the sermon.

One of those pairs of eyes that had lingered on him was Romola’s. She had slipped in late through a far side door, positioned so she had a full view of the main entryway, and the old man’s grey hair and the raw red line the rope left on his neck tugged at that long-nurtured softness she had always carried for the unspoken sorrows of old age. She thought for a moment their gazes had locked in that first wandering glance of his, but Baldassarre had never noticed her at all. His was only a fleeting, startled awareness of the scene around him, a flicker that did nothing to break the red, raging tumult of emotion left by his brutal run-in with Tito. Old half-mad memories of the man he had raised swirled through his head, tangled with the burning thirst in his throat and the ache of his broken body. No clear plan for the future had yet formed amid the fire of his hatred; the closest he could come to any thought of what came next was the bitter, searing knowledge that his strength was failing him, and a vague, ironclad resolution to trust no living soul ever again.

Then a sound rolled over the crowd, deep enough to vibrate in his very bones: a voice ringing with such absolute, triumphant certainty it felt like the echo of his own rage given flesh. “The day of vengeance is at hand!”

Baldassarre quivered so hard his teeth chattered, and wrenched his gaze upward. He was too far back to make out more than the broad shape of the preacher, one arm outstretched, the crucifix held high above his head – and he found himself panting to hear that threatening voice again, as if it was the promise of water to a man dying of thirst in the desert.

There was a pause. Slowly the preacher lowered his arm, set the crucifix down on the edge of the pulpit, and crossed his arms over his chest, sweeping his gaze over the multitude as if he meant to lock eyes with every single man and woman there.

“All of you in Florence are my witnesses – I did not speak my words in some hidden corner. You saw, four years ago, when no sign of war or tribulation had yet touched our land, that I preached of the scourge that would come. I lifted my voice like a trumpet to the prelates, the princes, the people of all Italy, and said: The cup of your iniquity is full. Behold, the thunder of the Lord is gathering, and it shall fall and shatter that cup, and the sin you have sipped like sweet wine shall pour out over you as molten lead. To you, O priests, who laugh and say ‘There is no Presence in the sanctuary – the Shechinah is nothing, the Mercy-seat is bare: we may sin behind the veil and none shall punish us,’ I said then: the presence of God shall reveal itself in His temple as a consuming fire. Your sacred vestments shall become winding sheets of flame, your sweet choirs shall turn to shrieks and hissing, your soft couches shall sprout thorns, and the breath of the wantons you harbor shall give birth to pestilence.

“Put no trust in your gold and silver, no trust in your high walled fortresses. Though your walls were forged of iron, your towers hewn from adamant, the Most High shall pour terror into your hearts and confusion into your councils, till you turn and flee like frightened women. He shall shatter mighty men beyond number, and raise up others in their place. For God will no longer suffer the pollution of His sanctuary; He will purge His Church entirely clean.

“As it is written, God does nothing without revealing His purpose to His servants the prophets. He has chosen me, His unworthy servant, and made His will burn in my soul through the living word of Scripture and the unmistakeable work of His providence. Through the ministry of angels He has shown me His purpose in visions, so that His word possesses me as the wind of heaven possesses a dry branch in the forest. I cannot keep silent, even when the scorner mocks me. For four years I have preached in obedience to His will, through all the scoffing: that in these times, God will regenerate His Church, that before that regeneration a scourge shall sweep across all Italy, and that these things shall come swiftly upon you.

“But the hypocrites who cloak their hatred of truth in a show of piety say to me, ‘Come now, Frate, lay down your prophesyings. It is enough for you to teach virtue.’ To them I answer: You whisper in your hearts that God lives far away, that His word is no more than parchment written by dead men, that He no longer walks among us to rebuke nations, punish oppressors, and strike down unholy priests as He struck down the sons of Eli. But I cry it again in your ears: God is near, not far off. His judgments never change. He is the God of armies. The strong men who march to battle are His ministers, no less than the storm, the fire, the pestilence. He drives them on by the breath of His angels, and they come upon the chosen land that has forsaken His covenant. And thou, O Italy, art that chosen land. Did not God set His sanctuary within your borders, and have you not polluted it? Behold, the ministers of His wrath are upon you – they are at your very doors!”

Savonarola’s voice had risen, growing more fiery and forceful with every word, until he fell suddenly silent, his hands dropping to clasp loosely before him. His hush was no signal for small stirrings or whispers in the crowd; it wrapped around them like a spell as strong as his words. Men and women across the whole vast cathedral sat motionless, faces upturned like breathing statues, until his voice sounded again, clear and low.

“Yet there is a pause – even as there was a pause before Jerusalem fell, so that the children of God might flee from the coming ruin. There is a stillness before the storm: see the black clouds roiling overhead, and not a single leaf trembles. The winds are stayed, so that the voice of God’s warning may be heard. Hear it now, O Florence, chosen city in the chosen land! Repent, turn away from evil. Do justice. Love mercy. Cast every unclean thing out from among you, that the spirit of truth and holiness may fill your souls and breathe through every street and home, and then the pestilence shall not cross your threshold, and the sword shall pass over you and leave you unhurt.

“For the sword hangs from the sky even now, quivering, ready to fall! The sword of God upon the earth, swift and sudden! Did I not tell you years ago that I had seen the vision, heard the voice? Behold, it is being fulfilled! Is there not a king with his army at your gates? Does not the very earth shake with the thunder of their horses’ hooves and the wheels of their swift cannon? Is there not a fierce multitude that can lay the land bare as a sharp razor? I tell you the French king and his army are the ministers of God. God shall guide him as a man guides a sharp sickle, and the joints of the wicked shall melt before him, and they shall be mown down like stubble. None who flee shall escape, none who run shall be delivered. The tyrants who built their thrones out of the vices of the crowd, the unbelieving priests who traffic in men’s souls and fill the sanctuary itself with fornication, shall be hurled from their soft couches into burning hell. Even the pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand afar off and say, ‘See these men, who brought the stench of a new wickedness into the everlasting fire.’

“But thou, O Florence, take the mercy that is offered you. See! The Cross is held out to you: come, and be healed. What other nation in Italy has been given a sign such as yours? The tyrant has been driven out from among you. The men who held a bribe in their left hand and a rod in their right are gone, and not a single drop of blood was spilled. Now cast every other abomination from among you, and you shall be strong in the strength of the living God. Wash yourselves clean from the black pitch of your vices, that have made you no better than the heathen. Cast out the envy and hatred that have turned your city into a nest of wolves. No harm shall come to you. The passing of armies shall feel no more threatening than a flock of birds flying overhead. Rebellious Pisa shall be returned to you, famine and pestilence shall be kept far from your gates, and you shall stand as a beacon among all nations. But mark this: so long as you suffer the accursed thing to remain in your camp, you shall be tormented and afflicted, even if a small remnant among you is spared.”

His tone had been sharp, authoritative for those warnings and promises, but now his voice softened, melting into raw, tender entreaty.

“Listen, O people, over whom my heart yearns as a mother’s heart yearns for the children she has travailed to bring into the world! God is my witness: if not for your sakes, I would happily live as a turtledove deep in the forest, singing low to my Beloved, who is mine and I am His. For you I toil, for you I languish, for you my nights are spent in watching, and my soul melts away with heaviness. O Lord, Thou knowest I am willing, I am ready. Take me, stretch me on Thy cross. Let the wicked who delight in blood, rob the poor, defile the temple of their own bodies, and harden their hearts against Thy mercy – let them wag their heads and shoot out their lips at me. Let the thorns press into my brow, let my sweat turn to anguish. I long to be made like Thee in that great love. But let me see the fruit of my travail. Let this people be saved. Let me see them clothed in purity. Let me hear their voices rise in concord, like the voices of angels. Let them know no wisdom but in Thy eternal law, no beauty but in holiness. Then they shall lead the way before all nations, and peoples from the four winds shall follow them, and be gathered into the fold of the blessed. For it is Thy will, O God, that the earth shall be converted to Thy law. It is Thy will that wickedness shall cease, and love shall reign. Come, O blessed promise. Behold, I am willing. Lay me on the altar. Let my blood flow, let the fire consume me, so that my witness shall be remembered among men forever: that iniquity shall never prosper for all time.”

As he spoke that last plea, Savonarola stretched his arms wide and lifted his eyes to the vaulted ceiling high above. His strong voice trembled with emotion one moment, then rose again in renewed, blazing energy, until the passion of his vow to offer himself as a victim grew too great for speech, and he broke off in a sob.

Every shifting tone rippled through the crowd, stirring them to answering emotion. Many there had only a lukewarm faith in the Frate’s prophetic mission, and hated him in their cooler, calmer moments. But even they were swept up by the great wave of feeling, rooted in sympathies that ran far deeper than any doctrine. A loud, collective sob rose up from the multitude at once, as Savonarola dropped to his knees and buried his face in the folds of his Dominican mantle. In that moment, he felt the rapture and glory of martyrdom, without the pain.

Baldassarre’s sob tangled right into that great roar of sound. Of all the thousands of souls packed into the Duomo, there was scarcely one whose frame vibrated more fiercely to the preacher’s words than his – but it vibrated like a harp from which every string had been torn away save one. That threat of fiery, unrelenting vengeance, of a future where the hated sinner could be pursued forever, held fast by the avenger in an eternal grapple, had come to him like the promise of an unquenchable spring to a man burning with unquenchable thirst. All his old learning, his old learned contempt for priestly superstitions, fell away from his soul like a forgotten tongue. What use could they ever be to his great, ravenous need, beside the answer this voice of absolute conviction gave him? The thunder of the preacher’s denunciation struck his passion-wrought nerves with all the force of self-evident truth. He never stopped to question it. He was possessed by it, as a war-horse is possessed by the sound of clashing weapons. No word that was not a threat touched his consciousness; he had no fibre left in him to thrill to anything else.

But the fierce, exultant delight that flooded him at the thought of endless vengeance found its climax, its release, in the preacher’s words of self-sacrifice. To Baldassarre, those words brought a vague, triumphant certainty that he too was devoting himself – signing his name in his own blood to the deed that gave himself over to an unending fire, a fire that would feel no hotter than cool water against his burning hatred.

“I rescued him. I cherished him. Let me clutch his heartstrings forever! Come, O blessed promise. Let my blood flow. Let the fire consume me!”

That last remaining string in his soul hummed at its utmost pitch. Baldassarre drove his long, sharp nails deep into the palms of his hands, till blood beaded up under his fingers, and burst into a sob right alongside the rest of the weeping multitude.

Wrapped entirely in the thunderous echo of Savonarola’s voice, Baldassarre did not notice the stranger slip through the great cathedral doorway behind him, or pause a few paces off to watch him. It was Piero di Cosimo, the painter, who had not come for the sermon at all—he had ventured into the Duomo purely to hunt down word of the escaped prisoner the city’s gossips had been chattering about for days. When the preaching fell to a hush, and thousands of listeners surrendered to a wordless, tear-choked swell of shared emotion, Piero stepped forward and tapped Baldassarre lightly on the bound arm.

Baldassarre turned, tears still rolling slow down the deep furrows of his cheeks, and huffed out a sharp, vigorous sigh as if shaking off a heavy, half-dreamed trance. The painter spoke low, so no one else might overhear:
“Shall I cut those cords for you? I heard how the French troops made you a captive.”
Baldassarre stared back suspiciously at this uninvited, helpful stranger, held his silence for a long beat, then said at last, “If you will.”
“Better step outside,” Piero murmured. “The crowd is too thick here.”
Baldassarre’s suspicion lingered, plain in his narrowed gaze, so Piero grinned, pulled a small sharp knife from his belt, and sliced clean through the frayed rawhide bonds. He found himself thinking the rumours of the old man’s partial madness were not far-fetched—there was something wild, unmoored, in the lines of his face. “Well,” he reasoned to himself, “if he does any mischief, they’ll tie him up again soon enough. The poor devil deserves at least that small chance.”
“You are wary of me,” he said again, quiet, as Baldassarre stretched his newly freed arms, relishing the long-forbidden feeling of unbound muscle. “You won’t tell me anything of who you are.”
Baldassarre’s look softened, steadied, no longer edged with distrust. “No. I have nothing to tell.”
“Just as you please,” Piero shrugged. “But I’d guess you’re in want of shelter, and may not know how kindly we Florentines treat ragged travellers with empty bellies and torn doublets. There is a hospital for poor pilgrims outside every one of our city gates. I could lead you straight to one. You’ve no need to fear the French soldier who took you, either—he was marched out of Florence at dawn yesterday.”
Baldassarre nodded, silent in acceptance, and the two men left the cathedral together.
As they tramped down the Via dell’Oriuolo toward the Porta Santa Croce, Piero added, “I am a painter, you see. Would you sit for me to paint your portrait? I would pay you coin, enough to keep you fed and warm for a week.”
Suspicion flared sharp back in Baldassarre’s eyes. He met Piero’s gaze and said firmly, “No.”
“Ah,” the painter said, curt, no offence taken. “Go straight ahead, and you will come to the Porta Santa Croce. The pilgrim hospital is just beyond its walls. You will take no other service from me, then?”
“I give you thanks for what you have already done,” Baldassarre said. “I need no more.”
“Very well,” Piero shrugged, and the two parted ways.
“A mysterious old tiger!” the artist thought, turning back toward the Duomo square. “Well worth putting to canvas. Ugly, lines carved deep as if plough and harrow have passed straight over his heart. What a perfect contrast to my soft, smiling Messer Greco—my *Bacco trionfante*, who has married the fair Antigone against all history and good sense. Aha, that pretty scholar’s blood ran cold at the old fellow’s grip on the steps the other night, didn’t it?”

When Piero stepped back into the Piazza del Duomo, the entire multitude that had come to hear Fra Girolamo was pouring out of every cathedral door, hurrying off to their shops, their mills, their market stalls at a run—proof of how completely the sermon had detained them from all their daily business. The artist leaned against the smooth stone corner of the Baptistery and watched the departing crowd, giddy with delight at the riot of colours in their garb, the sharp, distinct faces: faces exactly like the ones Masaccio had painted more than fifty years prior, the very faces Domenico Ghirlandajo still set into his frescoes today.

This morning was no ordinary occasion. The Frate’s audience, always a perfect cross-section of Florentine life, represented the city’s classes and political factions even more completely than usual. There were men of old noble birth, long accustomed to holding public office at home and representing Florence abroad, who had newly risen to prominence not only as enemies of the Medici and champions of republican rule, but as fervent Piagnoni who embraced every word of the Frate’s teaching and treated San Marco monastery as the seat of a new prophet Samuel. Some were men of commanding, handsome presence, like Francesco Valori, whose hot, arrogant temper was secretly gratified to have immediate divine authority to hand for bringing about freedom in exactly the way he saw fit. Others, like Soderini, had far less of the fiery Piagnone about them, and far more of the shrewd, calculating politician. There was Piero Capponi, plain, brave, no taste for fine doctrinal debate, a man who loved sober republican liberty more than words, who would always rather draw a sword than argue, and who had no quarrel with any idea that kept the Medici out and made space for public spirit.
At their heels were doctors of law, men who had pored over the tomes of Accursius and his fellow jurists for decades, but whose ardour had not been entirely consumed by study: Messer Luca Corsini himself, for example, who would soon raise his learned fists to hurl stones in street fights for the cause of religion, freedom, and the Frate. Sprinkled among these dignitaries, who carried their black lucco robes and furred mantles with the easy air of habitual authority, were crowds of more contemplative, sensitive faces: scholars bearing the great old names of Strozzi and Acciajoli, who had already made up their minds to take the cowl and join the community at San Marco. There were artists, fired to new, higher ambitions by Savonarola’s teachings, like the young painter who had lately surpassed himself in a fresco of the divine Child on the wall of the Frate’s bare cell—unaware that one day he would take the tonsure and the cowl himself, and be known as Fra Bartolommeo. There was the mystic poet Girolamo Benevieni, hurrying no doubt to carry word of the beloved Frate’s latest sermon to his friend Pico della Mirandola, who would never see the light of another morning. There were noblewomen dressed in scrupulous, almost excessive plainness, their refined grace the only mark that set them apart from their working-class sisters.
The great bulk of the crowd was made up of the genuine popolani, the middle classes of both the Major and Minor Arts, all acutely aware their purses were being gnawed thin by war taxes. More striking and varied than all the rest of the Frate’s disciples was the long stream of poorer tradesmen and artisans, whose faith in his divine message ranged from the simple, unthinking trust of the unlettered labourer in the Frate as the friend of the poor and the enemy of greedy oppressive nobles, to the eager hunger for every subtlety of biblical interpretation that takes such a strong hold on the sedentary artisan, illuminating the long dim hours at his workbench with a pale glow that feels to him like the very light of divine science.

But scattered through these ranks of the faithful were many who were no disciples of the Frate at all. Some were Medicean loyalists who had already, out of fear and political calculation, begun to pay feigned deference to the presiding spirit of the popular party. Others were sincere advocates of free government, who regarded Savonarola simply as an ambitious monk—half sagacious, half fanatical—who had made himself a powerful instrument with the people, and must be accepted as an unavoidable fact of social life. There were even some of his bitterest enemies: members of the old aristocratic anti-Medicean party, determined to seize the reins of power once more for their own leading families; or licentious young men who detested him as the great killjoy of Florence, who had turned all their favourite feasts, hunts, and revels into sin. The sermons in the Duomo had long since become political incidents, drawing the ears of curiosity and malice as much as of faith. The men of ideas, like young Niccolò Machiavelli, came to observe and write detailed reports for friends staying in distant country villas. The men of appetites, like Dolfo Spini, who was bent on hunting the Frate down as a public nuisance that had spoiled all his sport, came to feed their hatred and lie in wait for any slip that could be turned into a charge against him.

No preacher ever held a more massive influence than Savonarola, and no preacher ever had more heterogeneous, conflicting human material to work upon. Baldassarre, wrought to an ecstasy of self-martyring revenge by his words, was only an extreme case of the partial, narrow, personal sympathies that ran through every corner of that audience. In Savonarola’s preaching there were strains that appealed to the very finest susceptibilities of human nature, and elements that gratified low egoism, tickled gossiping curiosity, and fascinated timorous superstition. His hunger for personal predominance, his labyrinthine allegorical readings of Scripture, his enigmatic visions, his absolute false certitude about the Divine intentions, never ceased, in his own large soul, to be ennobled by that fervid piety, that passionate sense of the infinite, that active sympathy, that clear-sighted demand for the subjection of selfish interests to the common good, which he shared with all the greatest of mankind. But for the mass of his audience, all the power of his preaching lay in his bold assertion of supernatural claims, his denunciatory visions, and that false certitude that made every sermon feel as urgent and dramatic as a breaking political bulletin. Once he had that audience in his mastery, it was necessary to his nature—and necessary, he believed, for their welfare—that he keep that mastery.
The effect was inevitable. No man ever struggled to retain power over a mixed multitude without suffering some measure of vitiation; his standard must bend to their lower needs, not stay fixed to his own highest insight. The mysteries of human character have seldom been presented in a way more fitted to check the judgments of facile, quick knowingness than in Girolamo Savonarola. But we can give him a reverence that needs no shutting of the eyes to fact, if we regard his life as a great drama in which deep inward modifications accompanied every outward change. Up to this point, when his more direct action on political affairs had only just begun, it is probable his imperious need for ascendancy burned still completely hidden, dissolved in the great bright flame of his zeal for God and man. It was the custom of old, when an ox was led out for sacrifice to Jupiter, to chalk over all its dark spots and give the offering a false show of unblemished whiteness. Let us fling the chalk away, and say boldly: the victim is spotted, but it is not therefore in vain that his mighty heart is laid on the altar of mankind’s highest hopes.

By six o’clock that evening, nearly every man and woman in Florence breathed a quiet sigh of relief: the grand entry of the new “Charlemagne”, King Charles VIII of France, was well and truly over.

When the low roll of drums, blare of trumpets, and steady tramp of thousands of hooves along the Pisan road had first tangled with the wild peal of every city bell, the crowds perched on turreted rooftops had held their breath. Below them, the long, winding, intimidating stretch of royal procession unfurled against a backdrop of soft green hills and the broad Arno valley. No bright November sun glinted off the edges of banners, spear points, plumes, and silken surcoats to blind onlookers, but there was no choking dust cloud either, so every detail of the picked vanguard of troops stood out sharp and clear: tall, weathered Scottish archers, fierce, heavy Swiss halberdiers, quick-footed Gascon skirmishers built to wheel and climb rough terrain, and cavalrymen each sat tall on their warhorses like knights out of old tales, their spears held high and unyielding. The crowd murmured to one another that surely none of these fearsome fighters would raise a hand against Florentine citizens — they would only ever strike down the enemies of God. With that thought settled in their minds, the pageant of splendor felt less menacing, more grand: the gold-worked hilts of nobles’ swords, satin scarfs stitched with pious or chivalric symbolic emblems, heavy gold chains, pearl-set aigrettes, jewel-encrusted horse trappings, and the towering embroidered canopy held aloft by hand-picked noble youths directly over the head of the Most Christian King. As one contemporary diarist put it, in his simple, unpolished phrasing: *fù gran magnificenza* — it was great magnificence.

The ruling Signoria, however, who had waited for hours on their raised platform by the city gates, tasked with marching out at exactly the right moment with their orator at their head to greet the royal guest, had no time to savor the grandeur. When a sudden light rain began to fall, smudging the carefully written pages of Messer Luca Corsini’s flawless Latin welcome speech, the whole moment descended into chaos. Restless horses and jostling crowds drowned out his practiced lines, and the scholarly representatives of the Republic were reduced to cobbling together a messy, impromptu greeting in broken French.

That chaos created a perfect opening for Tito Melema. As one of the junior secretaries, he was positioned just behind the Signoria, and the high dignitaries were pushed back right into his group when the royal retinue pressed too close. “Someone step forward and say a few proper words in French,” cried Soderini, one of the city’s leading statesmen. None of the high-ranking men around him were willing to risk a second embarrassing failure. “You, Francesco Gaddi — you speak French well,” Soderini pressed. But Gaddi, uncertain of his own quick wit, hung back, and shoved Tito forward instead. “You, Melema.”

Tito stepped forward in an instant. The soft air of profound deference he wore so naturally, like a second skin, wrapped around him, and he delivered the short, respectful welcome speech perfectly in the Signoria’s name, before bowing gracefully and stepping back to let the king pass. His wits had failed him that morning, when Baldassarre had stepped out of the crowd and shocked him speechless, but this time, when no obvious, life-or-death threat hung over him, his presence of mind served him perfectly. He laughed off all subsequent praise for his quick thinking, and let Gaddi take full credit for the impromptu speech to everyone who had not witnessed the moment. No wonder Tito was so universally popular: the test men most often use to judge others is how well their vanity is flattered.

The rest of the day’s planned pageantry had fared almost as poorly as the opening oration. The mixed procession of clergy and lay citizens had found their planned route blocked, and were forced to trudge through narrow, muddy back alleys just to meet the king at the Duomo, rather than the grand, planned spot at the city gates. Worse still, the young monarch perched under the great canopy, lance propped on his thigh, looked nothing like the noble, legendary Charlemagne the Florentines had imagined. He had a thin, misshapen leg, fewer toes than a normal man, a wide, reptilian slit of a mouth, and a nose and skull that jutted out at strange, awkward angles. But no one spoke of it: his misshapen leg rested on cloth of gold stitched with pearls, his odd, ugly face was just a tiny patch of skin surrounded by swathes of black velvet, glinting gold thread, blazing rubies, and the brilliant, pearl-stitched embroidery of the canopy over his head. The crowds screamed *Francia! Francia!* so loud their throats went raw, and tore that very canopy to ribbons for souvenirs, per ancient local custom. The royal lips duly kissed the altar at the Duomo, and by the end of the long chaotic day, Charles VIII and his personal retinue were settled in the grand Medici palace on the Via Larga, the rest of the French nobles were scattered through the largest patrician homes across Florence, and the rank-and-file French soldiers were encamped safely out in the Prato and other open fields outside the city walls. The day’s business was done.

The streets that evening, under the pale glow of November stars, looked like nothing Florentines had ever seen before. Where once the dark thoroughfares had only been lit by a guttering little oil lamp hung beside a street corner saint’s statue, or a flicker of firelight spilling out an open doorway, every household had strung lit lamps from their windows, so the whole city was bright as day, safe and easy to walk. *Fù gran magnificenza*, indeed.

At nearly eight o’clock, Tito Melema was walking home along those glowing streets. He had snuck away unnoticed from the after-supper feasting, worn thin by the hidden anxieties he had carried all day. Now that he had a moment to breathe, he told himself he could surely settle his mind, and shake off that foolish, childish fear that had seized him that morning. If only he had not frozen up, had not been so shocked when Baldassarre recognized him out of nowhere — things could have been so much easier. He still winced at the thought that he was deliberately letting his old adoptive father suffer, that he would far rather Baldassarre was far away, prosperous and happy, no trouble to anyone. But there was no going back now. There was no second path left open to him. The only thing he had left to do was look after his own safety.

Lost in these thoughts, he walked from the Piazza di Santa Croce down the Via dei Benci, and just as he neared the corner turning onto Borgo Santa Croce, he heard a sharp, rhythmic clang that was not the sound of evening revelry: it was the sound of an anvil, ringing with the rhythm of hard, vigorous work. Tito jumped a little, then sped up. That sound was exactly what he was looking for. It came from the workshop of Niccolò Caparra, the most famous blacksmith in Florence, the favorite gathering spot for every local who loved fine, masterfully worked iron.

He paused for a moment to admire the scene before the shop. The wide doorway, set into the angled corner of a great block of houses, was topped by a small loggia with a fluted tile roof, supported by rough-hewn stone columns. Against the red-orange glow of the forge fire, Niccolò’s huge, broad-shouldered figure stood out in sharp black silhouette, his massive arms rising and falling in perfect, steady rhythm, half hiding, half revealing the strong line of his firm mouth and broad, powerful forehead as he struck the anvil. The two smaller, slimmer silhouettes of his two apprentices — one holding the iron steady, one working the bellows — only made the smith look even larger, more solid.

Tito stepped into the edge of the doorway, and waited in silence: no one interrupted Niccolò mid-strike, it was useless. He waited until the smith had hammered the head of a new axe to a perfect sharp edge, and set it aside on the cooling rack. Niccolò turned, rested his heavy hammer on his hip, and gave Tito a blunt, good-natured nod. “What is it, Messer Tito? Business?”

“Assuredly, Niccolò,” Tito smiled back. “I would never dare drag you away from your work after hours if it was not urgent — I know well enough that when you stay late at the forge, your work cannot wait.”

“All day I’ve been making axe heads and spear points,” the smith grunted. “Every fool that walked past my shop stuck their pumpkin head in to say ‘Niccolò, aren’t you coming to gawk at the French king and his fancy army?’ I told ’em no. I don’t want to see their faces. I want to see their backs, the day they march out of Florence again.”

“Forging arms for the citizens, then, so if there’s an uproar they’ve got something better than rusty scythes and kitchen spits to defend themselves with?” Tito asked.

“We shall see. Arms never hurt anyone to have, and Florence is going to need all the good steel she can get. The Frate tells us we will take Pisa back, and I hold with the Frate. But how do you think we’ll fulfill that promise if no one forges us good, sharp weapons? The Frate sees further ahead than any of us, that I believe. But he does not think you can catch birds just by winking at them, like some of his daft followers claim. He uses sense, not nonsense. Though you were a Medici man once, weren’t you, Messer Tito? That’s fine, I was too, back before the cask turned sour. What’s your business?”

“I simply wanted to know the price of that fine coat of mail I saw hanging up here the other day,” Tito said. “I need to buy it for a man who needs that kind of protection hidden under his doublet, these days.”

“Then let him come and buy it himself,” Niccolò said bluntly. “I’m fussy about what I sell, and who I sell to. I like to know who my customer is.”

“I know your scruples, Niccolò. But that is only defensive armour — it cannot hurt a soul.”

“True enough. But it might make the man wearing it feel bold enough to hurt someone else, knowing no one can stab him back. No, no, it’s not even my own work, it’s fine craft from Maso of Brescia. I would hate to see it cover the heart of some scoundrel. I need to know who is going to wear it.”

“Well then, to be plain with you, old friend, I want it for myself,” Tito said, knowing persuasion was useless. “The fact is, I am about to set off on a long journey, and you know how dangerous the roads are in these times. You don’t suspect me of treason against the Republic, do you?”

“No, I have heard no harm of you,” Niccolò said, in his usual gruff tone. “But do you have the money to pay for it? You’ve passed my shop often enough to see my sign, the painted burning account books. I trust no one for credit. The price is twenty florins, and that’s only because it’s second hand. You are not likely to be carrying that much on you right now, I’d think. Come back tomorrow.”

“As it happens, I have the money with me,” Tito said, who had won big at gambling the night before and had not emptied his purse. “I will carry the armour home with me now.”

Niccolò reached up and pulled the finely wrought chain coat down from its hook: it was made so light and fine it folded up to barely two handfuls in size. He handed it over once Tito had counted the twenty florins onto his palm. “There, then. It will turn a sword blow, a poniard stab, or an arrow, no trouble. But for my part? I would never put such a thing on. It’s nothing more than carrying your fear around with you, sewn up in steel.”

The words sent an unpleasant cold jolt through Tito, but he only smiled. “Ah, Niccolò, we scholars are all cowards. Pushing a pen around never thickens your arms the way swinging a hammer does. Addio!”

He folded the armour under his mantle, and hurried across the Ponte Rubaconte, the small, heavy weight of steel hidden against his chest as he headed home.

The autumn light was fading fast over Florence that day, the echoes of the French king's grand entry procession still humming in the streets, as Tito Melema hurried across the Ponte Rubaconte, the weight of his newly purchased chain armour tucked hidden beneath his fur-lined mantle. A mile away, in the long shadow-draped old library atop Bardo de' Bardi's palazzo, Romola paced back and forth among the shelves of yellowed codices, her thoughts fixed only on her husband, her ears straining for the sound of his step on the stone stairs.

That day, nearly every window in the city had held a curious face leaning out to gawk at Cristianissimo and his retinue of French nobles. Romola's window was one of the very few that stayed empty. She had not joined a single public feast or celebration since her father's sudden death three months prior, when the blind old scholar had slumped mid-dictation in his oak chair, his pen still resting on the page, after waiting long past the hour Tito usually came to transcribe his commentary on the New Platonists. Even in the first raw burst of her grief, a thought she had been too ashamed to name had risen unbidden in her breast: perhaps now, unshackled from the endless, exacting demands of her father's monotonous days, her life with Tito might finally be the perfect, undivided union she had dreamed of as a girl.

The rainbow glow of her early wedded bliss had not faded, exactly, but it had grown threaded with tiny, unspoken thorns she had spent every month since marrying Tito trying to explain away. She told herself it was no flaw in his love that he had gradually wearied of the long quiet hours of transcribing for Bardo, that the novelty of the shared scholarly work had worn thin. No one but she, who had built her whole young life around tending to her father's needs, could possibly sustain that same unbroken patience year after year. Even she had felt small, secret surges of rebellious frustration, after all. It was only natural that a bright, quick-witted man like Tito, who was welcomed into every noble household, every cardinal's feast, every political gathering across Florence, would crave that vivid, buzzing society far more than the hush of a library full of dead languages. She told herself she would not lay unfair claims on him, that she would soften all her little sharp edges, bend her own will to his, for her love for him was the whole anchor of her world. To ever admit Tito could act unworthily of that love would be to let the ground crumble beneath her feet.

The one sacred duty she clung to, the small atonement she could make to Bardo's memory for that guilty quiet relief at his passing, was to fulfil his lifelong dearest wish: to turn his half-century of toil, his collection of rare manuscripts and ancient artefacts built up through decades of frugality, into a public library for Florence. The precious collection was protected from creditors thanks to her godfather Bernardo del Nero, who had given Romola a thousand florins out of his own modest fortune to settle their outstanding debts, taking only a lien on the manuscripts as security, saying cheerfully that the state would surely repay him once the cardinal found a building to house the library. But ten days prior, all hopes tied to the Medici had collapsed: the Medici family had been driven out of the city, their own famous library was at risk of being seized and sold off by the French agents who swarmed the streets, and the cash-strapped Florentine Republic, facing war to recover Pisa and forced to pay vast tributes to the French king, was far more eager to sell off manuscripts for gold than to honour Bardo's quiet scholarly dream. For Romola, these seismic political shifts had no meaning beyond their threat to her father's legacy. She had grown up in a world of books, far removed from the brawling streets and partisan rivalries of Florentine public life, and now, for the first time, she found herself curious to understand what power the fiery preacher Fra Girolamo Savonarola held over the whole city, that he might turn the tide of events.

That very morning, on a sudden impulse, she had walked from Bardo's tomb in Santa Croce straight to the Duomo to hear his Advent sermon. The memory of that last terrible meeting with her renegade brother Dino had faded into the background of her busy married days, and she had gone half-expecting to find nothing but the ranting of a narrow fanatical monk. But when Savonarola spoke of martyrdom, the whole crowd had dissolved into sobs, and Romola had wept with them, caught in a strange, unnameable thrill, like being held in the swell of actual music rather than just remembering its sound. The feeling had faded as soon as she left the cathedral, though, and all she could think of was waiting for Tito to come home.

It was more than two hours after Tito had crossed the bridge before Romola heard the great iron gate of the courtyard creak open. She flew to the top of the stone stairs, the gilded lamplight over the landing spilling over her flushed cheeks. Eighteen months of marriage had softened her once proud, unyielding face, turning its cold distant dignity into something tender and beseeching; she was far lovelier that night than the day Tito had first laid eyes on her.

"You are tired, my Tito," she said, reaching for him as he climbed the steps. "It has been such a long, fatiguing day, has it not?"

They did not speak of private things until they had shut the library door behind them, the fire crackling bright on the great iron andirons casting warm light over the rows of manuscripts. Tito kissed her perfunctorily when she took his mantle from him, then threw himself heavily into the high-backed chair by the hearth, shivering a little.

"Romola, I wish you would not spend all your time in this drafty old library," he said, not peevish, but worn thin, his usual bright charm fraying at the edges. "Our own private chambers are far cozier on a chill night like this."

Romola felt a small, sharp hurt prickle in her chest. He had never been so distant before, usually so full of quick, solicitous little attentions. "You forget," she said softly, leaning in to read the excuse for his coldness in his tired face, "I am making the new catalogue of the collection exactly as my father wished. You have no spare time to help me with it, so I must work here closely."

Tito closed his eyes, rubbing his hands over his face and hair. The terror of running into his escaped old master Baldassarre in the Duomo that day still gnawed at him, the secret fear that would alienate Romola from him forever if she ever learned the truth of his past, and he could not summon the usual soft affectionate words he had for her. "I am not well," he said. "Do not be surprised if I seem peevish."

"Oh, of course you are worn out after all that you have done today," Romola said, kneeling beside him, her arm resting light on his chest as she brushed the hair back from his face. Then she drew back sharply, her eyes wide with alarm. "What is this under your tunic, Tito? It is hard as iron."

"It is iron," he said calmly. "Chain armour. It is mine now. I will have to wear it constantly, for some time."

"Is there some sudden danger today?" Romola said, clinging to his arm, her face going pale.

"Anyone who is not a rabid enemy of the Medici is at risk right now," he said, smiling as he laid a hand on her neck, the familiar soft habit of his affection breaking through the cold distance of a few moments prior. "This armour will keep me safe from any hidden attacks."

"But my godfather Bernardo is a far more prominent Medici ally than you are," Romola protested. "He has no armour, no precautions. Why is he not in more danger?"

"Men of his standing are protected by their names and their vast family connections that stretch across every party in Florence," Tito said smoothly. "I am a foreign Greek, no one would avenge me if I were knifed in a dark alley. I have had specific threats, Romola. You must promise never to speak of this to Bernardo. It would be a breach of my confidence."

"I will not say a word, if you wish it to be a secret," Romola said, blushing. A small, ugly flicker of a thought that he was acting out of cowardice rose in her mind, but she pushed it away immediately. "But this heavy armour, this constant fear -- it will make you so wretched. It feels like some dark enchantment, as if a fiend has wrapped my bright, light-footed Tito in a cold hard shell."
