"Would you rather I walk the streets unprotected, to be stabbed or shot?" Tito laughed. "If you do not mind me being harmed, why should I mind? I will throw the armour away right now, if you wish."

"No, no," Romola said hurriedly. "I am being fanciful. Do not listen to me. But are such violent attacks really common in Florence now? My father always said they were unheard of here."

"The bitter partisan hatreds that are growing by the day will make them common enough soon," Tito said, shrugging.

Romola forced herself to set the troubling subject aside. "Tell me of the procession today, then," she said, brightening. "Did all go well?"

"Perfectly well," he said, grinning. "The rain came just in time to cut short Luca Corsini's endless tedious oration that no one wanted to sit through, and someone stepped up quick to give the French king the shortest possible welcome in rough, stilted French -- no one is quite sure who it was, it happened so fast."

"It was you, I know it was you," Romola said, laughing and kissing his cheek, proud that he never bothered to claim credit for his clever little acts.

"After that there was nothing but parades, jewels, trappings, feasts, and endless flattery," Tito said. "I was at Tornabuoni's banquet later. I will tell you all the rest tomorrow, I am too tired now."

Romola hesitated a moment, then said cheerfully, "You will never guess where I went today. I went to the Duomo to hear Fra Girolamo's sermon."

Tito started sharply, immediately thinking of Baldassarre stumbling into the cathedral that day, but he relaxed when Romola went on. "I wanted to see for myself what power he has over all the people, what he says about this French invasion. He had such a strange hold over the whole crowd, I found myself sobbing along with everyone else, I cannot quite explain why. I even saw a wild old man there, an escaped prisoner with a rope around his neck, crying as he listened. Piero di Cosimo came and cut the rope off and led him out."

Tito paused for a beat before he answered. "I saw that man. I was outside the Duomo with Lorenzo Tornabuoni when he ran in, escaping from a French soldier." He rose slowly to his feet, the cold heavy secret of Baldassarre's freedom pressing down on his chest like a second sheet of armour, far heavier than the iron hidden under his tunic. "I need to rest, Romola," he said, and the quiet weight of dread hung heavy in the library firelight.

Four days later, Romola made her way along the Via Gualfonda to the home of the eccentric painter Piero di Cosimo, her steps hurried beneath the thrumming, uneasy hum of Florence’s streets. The thoroughfares were lined on all sides by wide-eyed French soldiers gawking at the city’s monuments, and Florentine citizens staring back at the unwelcome guests, neither side’s gaze softening with goodwill. The self-styled greatest nation on the continent, unused to anything but deference outside its own borders, carried itself with a heavy air of unearned superiority, while the Florentines, who had bent over backwards to play gracious hosts through weeks of festivals, elaborate church mystery plays with floating stage clouds and mechanized angels, and royal banquets where the French king had leered openly at the city’s noblewomen, had curdled into sullen, sharp-edged resentment. Once talks of policy began, it became clear the new “Charlemagne” saw Florence as a conquered prize: he had ridden in with his lance at rest, spoken of leaving a viceroy behind, and openly plotted to restore the exiled Medici line. To Florentine ears, this was absurd logic. The only slight the city had ever offered France was the work of the long-disgraced Piero de’ Medici, whom the people themselves had cast out, and the councils meeting day and night in the Palazzo Vecchio had already made clear they would not submit. Everywhere in the public squares, on the broad flagstones of the streets, ordinary citizens took any small chance to snub the insolent French troops.

For a well-born woman to walk abroad unescorted by a retinue was hardly safe in such an atmosphere, but Romola, shrouded head to foot in a thick black veil and mantle, with her loyal old serving man Maso trundling at her heels, faced no risk of unwanted attention. She was desperate to reach Piero, too: he had long before undertaken to paint a copy of her father’s portrait as Oedipus, the only existing likeness of the old scholar in his years of blindness, and it was still unfinished. Piero was famously erratic in his work: when no one pushed him, he would lay a canvas aside for months, shoving it into a dark corner or a cedar chest until he entirely forgot it existed, and Romola could not bear the thought of the portrait slipping away, the fine lines of her father’s face fading from her memory before she could fix it forever in paint. Wracked by quiet, constant guilt that she had not tended to her father as devotedly as he deserved in his final days, she clung to the portrait as a lifeline, staking her own loyalty of heart on preserving his memory.

As a favoured guest, Romola needed no prior announcement to enter Piero’s studio. She lifted the iron sliding panel in his door and called out in a soft, flutelike tone, the exact same cry the little village girl who sold him eggs had used that day Tito had stood beside her in the doorway. Piero wrenched the door open almost instantly, and scowled when he saw her.
“Ah, Madonna Romola? I thought my eggs had come at last. I’ve been waiting for them.”
“I’ve brought you something far better than hard-boiled eggs, Piero,” Romola laughed, easing her veil back from her face and taking a small wicker basket of cakes and coloured confetti from Maso’s hands. “I know you love these treats best when they come to you with no effort on your part. Do not pretend otherwise.”
“Aye, only when they arrive as easily as daylight,” Piero grumbled, folding his arms and staring down at the sweetmeats as she uncovered them, then lifting his gaze to look at her face and unbound gold hair, his painter’s eye lighting up with quiet admiration as her heavy veil dragged her hood slipping back off her head. “But I know well enough these sweetmeats are a bribe to stop me putting off your portrait another month. Go on through to the back room, then. I’ve worked on the Oedipus since last you saw it, though it’s not done. I never promised you a deadline, you know: the old saying holds, he who swears a vow and breaks it never has a whole soul.”

The door leading out to his wild unkempt garden clicked shut behind them, and Piero turned to his workbench. He was not, however, set up at his easel working on Romola’s portrait. That canvas stood propped against the far wall, and Piero stooped to lift it to carry it to the patch of golden afternoon light by the window. But when he moved it, a far smaller oil sketch that had been hidden entirely behind it came fully into view: a half-finished portrait of Tito, to which Piero had added a critical new detail only a few days prior. Piero, peering at a smudge on the edge of the large canvas he held, did not notice what he had uncovered as he carried the Oedipus to his easel.
Romola gasped, her face flushing bright hot with astonishment. “That is Tito!”

Piero turned around, and gave a silent, sheepish shrug, annoyed at his own careless forgetfulness. Romola stared at the sketch, bewildered and uneasy: Tito’s face was twisted with unmistakable, raw terror, and a ragged gaunt man with a thick rope noose knotted around his neck clung to his arm, as if begging for help or clutching him for purchase.
“What a strange, strange picture,” she said, turning to Piero, her voice tight with alarm. “When did you paint it? What does it mean?”
“Nothing but a silly fancy of mine,” Piero mumbled, yanking his skullcap off and scratching at his head, pulling his usual gruff grimace to hide any hint of unshared thought. “I needed a handsome young man’s face for it, and your husband’s was the finest I could find.” He stepped forward, grabbed the sketch, and held its back to Romola, pretending to brush at a stray spot of paint on its surface like it was a worthless throwaway piece, ready to tuck it out of sight.

But Romola’s mind flew straight to the secret chain armour she had found hidden in their home, and the two details clicked sharp and bright in her head, stoking her unease. “Do not put it away. Let me look again. That man with the rope around his neck – I saw him! I saw you tending to him in the Duomo that very morning. What made you paint him clutching at Tito?”
Piero saw no way out but to tell a half-truth. “It was nothing but an accident. That man was running, tripped on the cathedral steps, and grabbed at your husband to keep from falling. I happened to be there, saw the whole thing, thought the wild ragged wretch made a fine, dramatic subject. It’s worthless, just a daub I threw together for fun.” He moved quickly, hoisting the sketch up onto a high, out-of-reach shelf where she could not tug it back down, and gestured impatiently toward the Oedipus portrait. “Come, look at this one, it’s the work you came for.”

His over-eagerness to hide the sketch only confirmed the exact fear he had tried to avoid stoking: Romola was now certain there was some unpleasant, unflattering secret behind the encounter he had depicted. But her pride, and her deep, delicate loyalty to Tito, would not let her press further – to question more would feel like an admission that she could even entertain the smallest suspicion against her husband.
“He looked so piteous, that prisoner,” she said, as evenly as she could. “Do you know anything more of him?”
“Nothing more. I directed him to the city hospital, that is all. Now see here: the blind face of Oedipus is almost complete. Tell me what you think.”

Romola stepped before the portrait and stood silent for a long while, tears pricking at the corners of her eyes. Piero had captured exactly what she had begged him for: that soft, tilted listening look her father always wore, when he would turn his head at the sound of her footsteps, waiting to hear what she had brought him from the library. “You have done perfectly,” she said at last, turning to Piero with bright, wet eyes. “I am so grateful to you, my good Piero.”
“Ugh, that is the thing I cannot stand about you women,” Piero grumbled, turning away to kick a pile of discarded paint rags out of his path. “You gush feelings all over the place where no one asked for them. Why should you thank me for a painting you are paying me for, especially when I made you wait for months for it? A man does not deserve gratitude for not being a rogue or a fool – he only needs to thank God for not making him either. Women act like the whole world runs on honeyed words.”
“You great crusty thing,” Romola laughed, wiping the tears from her cheeks and plucking a crisp, sweet almond biscuit from the basket to tuck into his mouth. Piero accepted it like a gruff old bear accepting a ripe pear he had been craving for weeks: he loved the treat, but he would never let anyone see him show it.
“It is good, Madonna Antigone,” he mumbled around the mouthful, already reaching into the basket for another. He had eaten nothing but hard-boiled eggs for a full fortnight. Romola stood watching him, her sharp new unease held at bay for a little while by the sight of his unguarded, simple pleasure.
“Goodbye, Piero,” she said at last, setting the empty basket down. “I promise I will not thank you at all if you finish the portrait well and soon. I will just tell you you only did what you owed to your own good name.”
“Good,” Piero said shortly, helping her fold her mantle and veil tight around her shoulders to keep her safe from the street wind. When he shut the door behind her, he sighed in relief. “I am glad she asked no more questions about that sketch. I would hate for her to guess I thought her fine noble husband made the perfect model for a coward. I played it off lightly, she will never think of it again.”

Piero was far too optimistic, as open-hearted men often are when they try to pull off a small lie. The image of that painted sketch burned brighter and brighter in Romola’s mind as she walked home. She could not untangle it: the hidden chain armour, the prisoner who had stumbled into Tito that very same morning, that look of unmistakeable terror on his face Piero had captured. It all lined up too neatly to be a random coincidence.
“It means nothing,” she told herself, forcing herself to steady her steps. “It was just a chance encounter. I should ask Tito about it.” Then her pride, and her stubborn devotion to the ideal of perfect trust, pushed back. No, she decided. I will never question him about anything he did not choose to tell me of his own free will. To do so would be an insult to the loyalty I owe him.
And then her heart whispered the quiet, unvarnished truth: she dared not ask. There was a terrible crack in the trust she had built her whole marriage around. She carried her belief in Tito like someone holding a precious, fragile glass vase they fear is already shattered, too afraid to jostle it even the smallest bit to find out for certain.

“The old oddity’s vanished, left for Arezzo the very next morning—couldn’t stomach the stench of the French, I reckon, after being their captive. I stopped by the hospital to ask after him, wanted to see if those broth-dispensing monks had figured out if he was mad or merely wandering. They said no signs of frenzy at all, only that he ignored every question, like his mind was miles off tending a vineyard twenty miles outside the city. A proper mysterious old tiger, that one. I’d give a pretty penny to know more of his story.”

It was Piero di Cosimo, the sharp, idiosyncratic painter, speaking in Nello’s barber shop on the 24th of November, exactly one week after King Charles VIII of France had marched his troops through Florence’s gates. Six or seven regulars lingered in the cluttered, soap-scented space at the unheard-of hour of three in the afternoon, for the whole city hummed with electric tension, all waiting word of a fateful final decision on the treaty with the French king. Every public lounging spot was packed, every shopkeeper without a wife or trusted deputy to mind his counter stood propped in his doorway, thumbs hooked in his belt. Artisans drifted through the streets like loose splinters bobbing on a current, pausing mid-step to peer towards the piazza, ready to surge forward in a rush the second any exciting news broke. Nello had been half-perched on a wooden sill against the shop window, strumming his lute and keeping a constant eye fixed on the square outside.

“Ah!” he exclaimed, setting the lute down with a sharp thud of emphasis. “I’d not trade a gold florin for having missed the sight of those French soldiers waddling in their absurd broad-soled boots after their runaway prisoner! That’s what I get for stepping away to shave the chins of grand men. It never fails: the second I leave this little navel of the world, something remarkable happens right outside in my piazza.”

“Aye, you should have been there,” Piero retorted in his usual biting dry tone, “just to watch your favourite Greek scholar look so terrified you’d think old Satan himself had a grip on his coat. I love seeing those smooth, ever-smiling gentlemen of yours caught off guard by a sudden gust of trouble, forced to show their true colours no matter how hard they try to hide them. What colour do you think a man’s liver turns, that goes as pale as a bleached deer the moment a stranger grabs him unawares?”

“Piero, save that vinegar of yours as sauce for your own dinner!” Nello laughed. “What fault is it of my *bel erudito*’s that he looked startled when a pair of rough claws clamped onto him and he saw an unchained madman at his elbow? Your scholar is nothing like those brutish Swiss and German mercenaries, whose heads are only good for ramming down walls, whose appetites are so huge they’d think nothing of swallowing a cannonball for breakfast. We Florentines value more in a man than that vulgar stuff they call bravery, the kind you can buy by the dozen for a few coins a head. I tell you: the second men realized they had more wits than oxen, they set the oxen to pull their carts. When we Florentines realized we had more wits than other men, we set other men to do our fighting for us.”

“Treason, Nello!” a voice called out from the back parlour. “That’s not the state’s doctrine! Florence is sharpening her weapons, and the Frate’s most recent verified vision showed Mars standing atop Palazzo Vecchio, arm slung over the shoulder of Saint John the Baptist, who was offering him a piece of honeycomb.”

“Fair enough, Francesco,” Nello waved a hand dismissively. “Florence has a few thick-skulled lads who’ll do just fine bombarding Pisa. The finer wits can stay home to do the thinking and the shaving. And if Piero here is so very set on valour, let him grab his biggest paintbrush for a weapon and his palette for a shield, and challenge the loudest-mouthed Swiss brute he can find in the Prato to single combat.”

“Pah, Nello,” Piero growled. “Your tongue runs nonstop, like a mill when the Arno’s in flood—grist or no grist, it keeps spinning.”

“Plenty of good grist, I promise you! It’s every bit as ridiculous to expect a grizzled old painter like you to run willingly at a javelin as it is to expect a man who’s honed his wits on the classics to enjoy having his handsome face clawed by a wild beast.”

“There you go again, pretending people will slip their legs into a sack just because you call it a fine pair of hose,” Piero shot back. “Who said anything about wild beasts, or unarmed men rushing headlong into battle? Fighting is a trade, and it’s not my trade. I’m no fool to go chasing danger, but I’d face it straight on if it came for me.”

“Then how is it you hide under the table with the rats the second thunder starts?” Nello teased, determined to catch the painter out. “You, of all people, ought to understand why a man might shake at something that seems trivial to others—you, who scurry for cover the moment a storm rumbles.”

“That’s a peculiar sensitivity to loud noise, nothing to do with my courage or my conscience,” Piero huffed, stuffing his ears with tow from his leather pouch in indignant contempt.

“Well then! Tito Melema may have a peculiar sensitivity to being grabbed unawares by prisoners fresh from running away from French soldiers,” Nello went on triumphantly, drawing a round of laughter from the others. It was clear Piero’s grudge against Tito was not shared by the group. “Men are born with all kinds of silly antipathies: I myself can’t stand the smell of mint. Tito was born with an antipathy to old stumbling prisoners who reach out and clutch people. There! Makes perfect sense.”

“By the by,” Francesco Cei spoke up, “did you hear Camilla Rucellai has outdone the Frate at his own prophecy game? She predicted two years ago that Pico della Mirandola would die in the time of lilies. He died in November, so the scoffers mocked her. ‘Nonsense,’ Camilla said, ‘the lilies I meant are the lilies of France, and they’re sitting right under your noses now.’ Euge, Camilla! If the Frate can show a single one of his visions came true that neatly, I’ll turn Piagnone tomorrow.”

“You’re too flippant about the Frate, Francesco,” the scholarly Pietro Cennini put in gently. “We all owe him a great debt these weeks, for preaching peace and calm, for telling the people to set aside their old party feuds. Only fools would be glad to see the people slip the leash of his guidance right now. And if the Most Christian King digs in his heels today and refuses to sign a treaty fair and honourable to Florence, Fra Girolamo is the only man we can trust to bring him round.”

“You speak true, Messer Pietro,” Nello nodded. “The Frate is one of the sturdiest nails Florence has to hang her hopes on—at least, that’s the opinion of every most respectable chin I’ve had the honour of shaving. But young Messer Niccolò was saying here the other morning, and I suspect Francesco means much the same: visions stretch as easily as Dido’s oxhide. A dream can mean whatever happens to come after it. As our Franco Sacchetti wrote: a woman dreams a serpent bit her overnight, breaks a drinking cup the next day, and cries ‘See! I knew something bad would happen—now it’s obvious the serpent meant the cup!’”

“The Frate’s visions are nothing of the sort,” Cronaca cut in. “He doesn’t just say vague things—he says the Church will be scourged, renewed, and all heathens converted, and that it will happen soon. He’s no slippery pretender hiding behind loopholes, he’s—”

“What’s that? What’s all that noise?” Nello yelped, leaping off the sill and sticking his head out the shop door. “People are flooding into the piazza, shouting. Something must have happened in Via Larga! Aha!” He burst out laughing, waving his cap above his head in delighted surprise.

The rest of the group scrambled to the door to see. News from Via Larga was exactly what they’d all been waiting for, but no one expected it to arrive the way it did. Hoisted high above the crowd, perched on a wrenched-up street bench, sat Tito Melema himself, grinning in amused embarrassment at being manhandled by the cheering people. His cap had slipped off his head, hanging loose by the soft silk becchetto wrapped around his neck, and when he spotted the group gathered at Nello’s door, he lifted his fingers in a playful beckoning wave. A moment later he had leaped from the bench onto a cart stacked high with wool bales parked in the wide space between the Baptistery and the Duomo steps, the crowd swarming around him as noisy and eager as chickens gathered for feed. The second he opened his mouth in his clear, rich, melodic voice, the noise died completely.

“Citizens of Florence! I have no official right to share this news, save for the fact that you all want to hear it. But it is good news, and no harm will come of speaking it aloud. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty honourable to Florence. And you owe that victory to one of your own citizens, a man who spoke a word worthy of the ancient Romans: you owe it to Piero Capponi!”

A roar of voices exploded at once: “Capponi! Capponi! What did our Piero say? We knew he wouldn’t stand to be passed back and forth like a letter between Herod and Pilate!”

When the shouts died down just enough, Tito spoke again: “The Most Christian King made demands far too unfair, dug in his heels, and finally declared ‘I will order my trumpets to sound for attack.’ Then, citizens of Florence, your Piero Capponi spoke with the full voice of a free city: ‘If you sound your trumpets, we will ring our bells!’ He snatched the copy of those dishonourable terms right out of the secretary’s hands, tore it to shreds, and turned to walk straight out of the royal presence.”

Loud shouts burst out again, the crowd clamouring for more details. “Then, Florentines, the great majesty of France felt, perhaps for the very first time, all the weight of the majesty of a free people. The Most Christian King himself rushed from his throne to call Piero Capponi back. The great spirit of your city did its work with a single brave word, no need for the armed force all of you had waiting behind your walls. The King has agreed to sign a treaty that defends Florence’s honour and her safety both. The banner of France will fly above every Florentine galley as a sign of friendship and shared privilege—but written in letters above that French banner will be one word: *Liberty*!”

As the shouts rose again, Tito glanced over the crowd with quiet, amused contempt. Every last man there was swelling with the conviction that Piero Capponi had spoken for him personally, that he was the very mind behind Capponi’s bold words. He delighted in the absurdity of the moment: he, a Greek foreigner, a former associate of the exiled Medici, had been transformed in the space of ten minutes into a popular orator feeding the crowd’s hunger for a hero of liberty. He was glad the crowd had grabbed him and swept him off the street as he left the palace in Via Larga with a message for the Signoria. It was all so easy, so pleasant, to speak and leave every party convinced you were on their side. A man who knew how to persuade never need fear trouble. The grinning, excited faces of the weavers, dyers, and wool-carders below him were wonderfully entertaining to watch from his perch atop the wool cart.

Tito was just settling comfortably into that easy confidence, feeling his old unflappable self, one hand resting on his recovered cap and the other hooked at his belt, his long dark eyes glinting with a complacent smile, ready to bow to the crowd before leaping down—when his gaze locked onto a face at the far edge of the throng that was nothing like the jubilant artisans surrounding it. The man was clean-shaven, his hair cropped short, wearing a plain, decent felt hat. Any other person would have barely recognised him as the escaped prisoner who had grabbed him on the palace steps, but Tito knew him at once. It was Baldassarre. The sight of that face, long thought lost to him, shot through him like a searing fiery arrow, every memory of his old betrayal flashing across his mind in a single heartbeat. He leaped down from the cart immediately, fast enough that no one noticed the flicker of terror cross his face. He told himself he had moved fast anyway, that he was in a hurry to deliver his message to Palazzo Vecchio, that he had given no sign of panic. He would be ready for this face now, he told himself, ready for it to pop up like a recurring blot in diseased vision, over and over. But seeing Baldassarre shaven, neatly dressed, looking sane and steady, stripped away all his excuses that the old man was mad. If Baldassarre was in full possession of all his wits, all his old knowledge, why was he lingering? It had to be to make his revenge perfect. Flight, Tito thought suddenly, flight is the only way out. He turned his back on the crowd, his smug new role as a popular hero completely forgotten, his whole mind caught up in the dark, narrow private terror of the man hunting him.

All the while, the crowd Tito had stoked so casually had no idea of the secret fear he carried away with him. The people were just turning to head toward Via Larga, when four mace-bearers stepped into the piazza from Via de’ Martelli, announcing the approach of the city’s highest dignitaries. They were the syndics, the commissioners who had negotiated the treaty. Piero Capponi himself would be with them. The crowd fell completely still, voices softening, until the silence was so total the crisp tread of their feet on the stone pavement and the rustle of their heavy black silk robes sounded like soft rain falling in the dark of night. The crowd did not wait for the learned lawyers Vespucci and Bonsi, nor for Francesco Valori, popular as he had become in recent weeks. This moment belonged to Piero Capponi, the stolid, resolute merchant-soldier who cared nothing for flattering the crowd, only for preserving order and Florentine freedom. Only when he stepped fully into the piazza did the silence break, and a single thunderous roar rolled over the square: “Capponi! Capponi! Well done, Capponi!”

The plain, unpretentious warrior looked around at the cheering faces, grave quiet joy softening his stern features. Two years later, he would die in battle, and the city would give him a grand state funeral, torches carried by every magistrate, rows of banners fluttering over his bier. No record says he felt any joy at the orations spoken in his honour that day. We may be glad he got to feel the warm, living praise of his fellow citizens while he still walked among them, in that bright moment of triumph in the Piazza del Duomo.

It was the first time Baldassarre had set foot in the Piazza del Duomo since the day he escaped captivity, and old, sharp shame coiled tight in his chest like a thorned vine. He had craved for months to hear the fiery, famous monk preach again, but he had flinched from returning to the spot where he had been dragged half-naked, his matted white hair hanging over his face, a coarse rope knotted around his throat, while strangers pointed and shouted that he was mad. The sting of that humiliation was still too raw, even after he had traded rags for a rough new tunic, cropped his unkempt beard short, and built back the sturdiness in his limbs. For when Tito’s cold, easy words “some madman, surely” had fallen on his ears, it was not only their cruelty that cut like a viper’s fang: it was Baldassarre’s instant, bitter, undeniable knowledge that he could not, in that moment, prove them false.

Alongside the roiling, all-consuming hunger for vengeance that possessed him, a sharp, gnawing doubt had taken root: he could not be sure he had the power to make that vengeance real. It felt as if some devil had whispered his most vulnerable secret straight into the traitor Tito’s ear. He was not mad—he carried the clear, piteous sanity of a man who knows exactly how much of his own mind has been shattered, who measures every gap in his memory and every fumble of his thoughts like a tally of wounds. That awareness spawned a wary, feral caution in him, the same wariness of a fierce but limping wild beast that pauses before advancing on a stronger prey, or a crawling insect that stops dead when the sliver of earth it stands on crumbles beneath it. That was why he had turned away Piero di Cosimo’s gentle friendly offers of help, why he had told the hospital friars no more than the bare, safe truth that French soldiers had captured him on his journey from Genoa.

His age, and the quiet refinement in his speech that marked him as better than the common beggars and wandering labourers the hospital usually sheltered, moved the monks to extra kindness: they gave him a thick coarse woollen tunic to fend off the November chill, a pair of scuffed but sturdy peasant shoes, and a handful of tiny copper danari, the smallest coin in Florence, to help him on his way. He set out for Arezzo at dawn the next day, but when he reached the first small hill town, he stopped, spent two of his tiny coins at the barber’s stall to have his beard shaved and his white hair cropped short, just as he had worn it for decades before his capture. The barber pulled out a polished hand mirror of bright steel: it had been years, more than he could clearly count, since Baldassarre had seen his own face. When his gaze fell on his reflection, a new thought struck him like a physical blow: could Tito truly not recognize him, after all this change? His hands shook like wind-tousled leaves as he pushed the barber’s arm away and demanded the mirror for himself, before the shaving began.

No. He was not changed so much that Tito could miss him. He recognized the familiar wrinkles, only cut deeper now; the same rough, thick skin of his brow, marked with little bumps that looked like half-erased cipher marks, only yellower now, tough and lifeless as dried fruit rind. That long shaggy white beard was no disguise for eyes that had studied his face every day for sixteen years, eyes that should have been searching for him as men search for their beloved among the bodies washed ashore by a storm. There was a new, wild edge to his glance, but that only made recognition more shocking—like a familiar beloved voice heard screaming in pain. That doubt was a foolish lie: he had felt Tito’s recognition in that piazza, sharp and terrified, the second their eyes met. He shoved the mirror away, the hot currents of hatred and vengeful purpose surging back to sweep away all soft hesitation.

He turned back toward Florence then, but he did not want to pass through the city gates before dusk, so he turned off the main road and sat down beside a small still pool, shaded on one side by alder bushes still dotted with fading yellow leaves. It was a calm, mild November day, and the still flat surface of the water caught his eye as a better, longer mirror. He wanted to study his face slowly, unobserved, as he had not dared to do at the barber’s stall. He leaned forward, staring intently at his own wavering reflection. Was there something vacant, imbecile, wandering in his gaze? Not now, not when he was fixed on this sharp, urgent question—his eyes burned with fierce, single-minded purpose. But in the long empty hours, when half-remembered whispers teased him from the fog of his lost memory, when half-seen faces faded away before he could reach out to grasp them? There must be a blank, bewildered confusion in his face then, he knew it, like a man struck suddenly blind.

How could he prove anything to anyone? How could he even begin to tell his story, without fear the threads of his thought would snap mid-sentence? Who would believe he had once been a scholar, his mind brimming with rare knowledge, fluent in half a dozen languages, sharp and quick in debate? All that carefully hoarded, life-long learning had slipped away from him, like water spilled from an urn lost to the wide ocean. Or was it still trapped inside him, locked behind some thin barrier that might one day shatter? He clung tight to that hope. Since the day he had first dragged himself weakly off his straw prison couch and stepped out into the sunlight to find half his mind gone dark, his strength had returned in fits and bursts. His body had healed, his will had grown firm again, he could recall every memory woven tight to his strongest emotions—his love for Tito, his rage at betrayal. Even better, twice before, when he had been seized by a surge of sharp excitement, he had felt his old self snap back into place for a breathless instant: he could see the lines of Greek script clear in his head, understand every word, feel his mind move light and un-numbed through all the ideas he had once known so well. The flash faded fast, leaving the dark heavier than before, but what if it stayed longer? Long enough for him to craft a revenge so slow, so exquisite, that a single strike of a fist could never equal it?

He stood up, folded his arms, and focused all his will on the plan he had to carry out. He could not beg. He could not let anyone mark him as a helpless, foolish old man. He remembered the name he had repeated over and over until it was stuck fast in his head: Bratti Ferravecchi. Back in Genoa, the stranger who had worn Tito’s carved onyx ring had told Baldassarre he had bought it in Florence, from a handsome young Greek, in the shop of a rag merchant named Bratti Ferravecchi, on the street that bore his very name. That single discovery had shattered every last shred of the faith Baldassarre had clung to for years: once, he had told himself Tito was dead, or captured too, that his letters had never reached the boy, that Tito was searching for him even now. He had persuaded his Corinthian captors to leave messages for any inquirer looking for him, certain Tito would find him there. Then the illness had come, and he had woken half-mad, useless to his owners, who had gladly passed him off to a compassionate Genoese merchant to get rid of him. For months he had sailed the Aegean, shore to shore, slowly regaining his bodily strength, until he landed in Genoa almost ready to give up on life entirely—until he saw that ring.

The moment he recognized the onyx, carved with the tiny image of Bacchus that he had given Tito as a boy, all that soft, sad longing turned to black venom. Tito was alive. Tito was rich, successful, living in Florence, selling the most precious keepsake Baldassarre had ever owned, while he rotted half-dead in chains. There was no more hope of finding his beloved son. There was only vengeance.

He did not even recognize the gaunt, haggard old man staring back at him in the pool water. That man was nothing but fuel for the fire of his hatred. He muttered to his reflection, recounting all the years he had poured into the boy he had raised: how he had loved him more than his own life, would have torn open his own chest to warm the child with his blood if Tito had but cared a little for his pain. He had spent his whole life chasing one drop of unselfish love, and got nothing for it. The world was a lie, full of nothing but betrayal. But there were deep, heady draughts to be drunk from the cup of hatred and revenge.

He would not waste his vengeance on a quick, easy knife stroke. He wanted Tito to suffer slow, every bit as much as he had suffered. But to do that, he had to survive. He had no skills left to offer, but his limbs had grown strong again: he could work as a porter, carry heavy loads through Florence’s narrow streets to earn his bread. Then his hand fell on the tiny blackened parchment bag strung around his bare neck, a charm his mother had hung around his neck when he was a small boy in Naples, on the sunlit balcony overlooking the bright blue bay. He had never dared open it, seeing it as a sacred relic of her love. But now, he tore at the parchment with desperate, nervous fingers, and pulled out what was inside: a tiny polished sapphire, as blue as that Neapolitan sea, worth several gold ducats.

He would sell it. He would buy himself a sharp dagger, something he could hold onto even when his memories slipped away, a loyal friend to feeble, righteous justice. He tucked the sapphire back into its wrappings, stood up as dusk painted the Florentine sky deep purple, and walked back toward the city gates.

He found a bare outhouse in the quiet, thinly populated Oltrarno district, with a heap of dry straw to sleep on, and sold the sapphire a few days later. He bought new warm clothes, and still had two gold florins left after he purchased the bright, sharp dagger he had set his heart on—at Bratti Ferravecchi’s own shop, no less. He bargained with the garrulous rag merchant, let slip that he was from Genoa, had been directed there by a man who had bought a precious ring from Bratti some months prior. Bratti preened, bragged about his famous connections, and spilled every scrap of gossip he knew about that handsome young Greek, Tito Melema: how he had found him sleeping under the Loggia de’ Cerchi, how Tito had sold all his precious jewels, settled fast in Florence, married a nobleman’s daughter, and risen to great fame and prosperity. Bratti never suspected the quiet, slightly deaf old man asking all the questions cared anything for the Greek youth—he thought the curiosity was all about Bratti’s own great cleverness as a merchant.

Baldassarre walked back to his straw heap with the dagger tucked at his hip, and all the scattered facts he could cling to about Tito’s new life. The memories were slippery, he knew; he had to repeat every name, every fact, ten times over to keep it from slipping away into the fog. As he passed the booksellers’ stalls on the Via del Garbo, he paused, staring at the open Greek volumes displayed there. If he stared long enough at the script, could he yank the old, lost knowledge back into his grasp? He bought the cheapest Greek book he could find, carried it home, and sat on his straw heap staring at the black marks on the white pages long after the sun went down, waiting for the light of old learning to dawn, waiting for the power to make his perfect, long-awaited vengeance real.

“My Romola,” Tito said on the second morning after he had stepped up to speak to the crowds gathered in the Piazza del Duomo, “I am expecting very grand visitors today. The Milanese Count will call again, alongside the Seneschal de Beaucaire, that great favourite of the French king Charles VIII, whom they name the Cristianissimo. I know you have no taste for going through all the smiling, bowing ceremonies with these rustling, pompous magnates who will likely never set foot in Florence again; and since they are sure to demand a tour of our collection of antiquities and the library, it would be far more pleasant for you to set aside your work for the day and pay a visit to your cousin Brigida.”

Romola caught the quiet, unspoken wish behind his suggestion at once, and agreed without hesitation. But a few minutes later, when she returned wrapped in her hooded mantle ready to go, she exclaimed, “Oh, what a long, relieved breath Florence will draw the moment the city gates are flung wide, and the very last French soldier marches out! Even you are growing tired of it all, for all your endless patience, my Tito—confess it. Ah, your head is burning hot.”

He was leaning over his desk, pen flying across a sheet of parchment, and she rested her hand lightly on his head, meaning to give him the soft parting caress she had offered him a hundred times before. In all their months of marriage, he had always responded to that small touch by lifting his head, leaning back into her space, and looking up to meet her eyes. But now he felt as incapable of raising his head as if her hand had been heavy as a leaden cowl pulled down over his face. He kept writing, speaking in a deliberately light, careless tone. “The French are as eager to leave Florence as wasps are to abandon a ripe pear the moment they have drained every drop of its juice.”

Romola, keenly attuned to every small shift in his behaviour, felt the absence of their familiar, easy ritual sharp as a pinprick. She pulled her hand away. “I am going, Tito.”
“Farewell, my sweet one. I must wait here to receive our guests. Take Maso as your escort, to keep you safe on the streets.”
Still he did not lift his eyes from his page. Romola walked out of the house without saying another word.

The smallest, unremarkable moments can mark quiet turning points in a marriage, and that morning, for the first time, Romola admitted fully to herself not just that Tito had changed, but that he had changed *towards her*. For a fleeting second she wondered if the fault lay in some failing of her own—if she had been too cold, too stubborn, too wrapped up in her father’s old books to notice what he needed. But then she thought of the half-hidden suit of armour she had stumbled on in their store rooms, the strange, veiled painting he refused to explain, and she knew there was some secret external event twisting him away from her, a riddle she had no way of solving.

The moment Tito was certain Romola was well out of the house, he dropped his pen at once and lifted his head, breathing out in relief that he no longer had to face anything but stacks of parchment and fragments of broken antique marble. He felt faintly angry with himself that he had not been able to school his features enough to meet her gaze and act as normal. He had wanted, if anything, to be warmer and more affectionate than usual—he just could not shake the involuntary, deep-rooted recoil from her that had seized him the second she laid her hand on his head. That shrinking was tied directly to the very part of him that most wanted to preserve her love: for he was about to take a step he knew would rouse her deepest, fiercest indignation, and he dreaded every unpleasant, angry moment he would have to weather before he could possibly win her forgiveness. Tito had never been the sort of man who revelled in defiance or impudence. Every part of his nature leaned away from conflict, away from harsh words, away from the sight of the tenderness he cherished in Romola curdling into anger. He had talked himself out of all the old sentimental scruples he had once held, reasoning that devotion to Bardo’s dying wish to keep the library intact was nothing but foolish, impractical sentiment that deprived both him and Romola of real, tangible comfort. But being free of moral scruples did not make him free of the pain of discomfort. Unscrupulousness can save a man from many burdens, but it cannot cure a toothache, or soothe a bruised vanity, or chase away the sharp fear of loneliness—none of which have any remedy but a sound body, and a just, loving soul. At that moment, Tito felt acutely that no amount of smooth words or charming persuasion could cushion him against the shock of the collision coming with Romola. He felt like a timid hare, forced to make a desperate leap over a chasm because the fangs and claws of a hunter were snapping at its heels behind it.

That quiet, long-held thought that clinging to Bardo’s library was foolish folly would never have pushed him to action if not for the events of the past week, which had brought both a sharp new urgency to make money, and a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to sell his collection to wealthy visiting French and Milanese nobles. It was only when he had spotted Baldassarre in the streets a few days prior, looking far more lucid and self-possessed than the raving beggar he had seemed before, that he had fully resolved to use his legal right as Bardo’s son-in-law to sell the entire library before this rare chance slipped through his fingers. He could not rule out that he might have to flee Florence at any moment, and he had no wish to run away a destitute wanderer. He had grown used to ease, to respect, to pleasant comforts, and he intended to carry all the resources he could to keep that ease wherever he went. More than anything, he wanted to take Romola with him, and if at all possible, to carry no stain of infamy that would ruin his social standing. He had no desire to sneak away under a cloud of dishonour, even with a bag of jewels, to live the rest of his life as a nameless, rootless adventurer. He could not bear to lose the goodwill of the Florentine citizens who now greeted him with respect, and far less could he bear to lose Romola. She had been the great love of his youth, he still loved her now, and she was part of the gentle, comfortable furniture of his life that he shrank from tearing himself away from. The quiet sense of power most husbands feel over their wives, the boldness that lets them risk betrayals no lover would ever dare attempt, was not enough to still his unease. That was the heavy leaden weight that had settled on him that morning, and made him incapable of lifting his head to meet her pure, clear gaze, a gaze that shone too bright a light on the painful struggle he knew was coming.

There was no way around it: if they had to flee Florence, they would need gold. Tito could not imagine arranging his life to suit himself without a large sum of money in hand, and that single, endless desire to build a perfectly comfortable, trouble-free life for himself was the root of every wrong he had done. He would have eagerly made any sacrifice that did not come with pain or discomfort.

The grand, rustling dignitaries came and went, the bargains were fully struck, and by the time Romola returned home all the deals were signed. No grave words were exchanged that night. Tito was unusually gay and chatty, pouring out a flood of stories and vivid descriptions of everything he had seen during the French court’s visit, as he had not done in months. Romola thought she could see the strain of effort underpinning that forced liveliness, and assumed it was his way of making amends for the coldness he had shown her that morning. She accepted it as a quiet act of penitence, her heart aching a little at that small new sign of the growing distance between them—that there was now a small offence hanging between them that neither of them dared name.

The next day, Tito stayed out of the house until long after dark. It was a day that stood out sharply in Romola’s memory: Piero di Cosimo, spurred to finish the work faster by the fear that he had caused her pain the week before, had sent home the finished portrait of her father Bardo. She had propped it up against the back of his old, worn favourite chair, and had been staring at it for a long time as the light faded, when the door opened behind her and Bernardo del Nero stepped inside.
“It is you, godfather! I only wish you had come a little sooner, it is growing quite dark in here,” Romola said, hurrying to meet him.
“I only popped in to bring you good news, for I knew Tito had not come home yet,” Bernardo said. “The French king marches out tomorrow, and not a moment too soon. There was another scuffle between our citizens and his soldiers this very morning. Now there is every chance the city will return to order, and trade will start moving again.”
“That is such happy news,” Romola said. “But it is very sudden, is it not? Tito told me only yesterday that there was little chance of the king leaving anytime soon.”
“He got a good loud barking-to, that is why,” Bernardo said, smiling. “His own generals shouted at him quite fiercely, and at last our Signoria sent the city’s fiercest mastiff: Fra Girolamo. The Cristianissimo got thoroughly frightened by that thunder of preaching, and gave the order to depart at once. I fear we will have plenty of infighting among ourselves once he is gone, but every party in the city is agreed on being glad to no longer have Florence stifled under a swarm of foreign soldiers. The Frate’s barking has done some good at last. Ah, what is this?” he added, as Romola slipped her hand through his arm and led him over to stand in front of the portrait. “Let me have a look.”
He began to unwind his long woollen scarf while Romola pulled a chair out for him.
“Do you not want your spectacles, godfather?” Romola asked, anxious that he should see every detail of the portrait exactly as she saw it.
“No, child, no,” Bernardo said, uncovering his grey head as he sat down, straight and firm in his seat. “For seeing at this distance, my old eyes are likely better than your young ones. Old men’s eyes are like old men’s memories: they are strongest for things that happened a very long time ago.”
“It is better than having no portrait of him at all,” Romola said apologetically, after Bernardo had sat silent for several minutes. “It does not look quite like him now as the image I carry in my head, but that memory might fade with the passing of years.” She rested her arm on the old man’s shoulder as she spoke, drawn close to him by their shared love for the dead man they both missed.
“I do not know,” Bernardo said. “I almost think I see young Bardo, all fiery and bright-eyed, in that face more than I see the old blind man we knew in his last years. Your father had a great deal of fire in his gaze when he was young. I never could understand it: a man with that much passionate, impatient spirit, far more fiery than my own, could spend his whole life hunched over old books, living in the company of shadows. Still, he put his whole heart into that library of his.” Bernardo gave a small, faint shrug as he said the last words, but Romola could hear the warm affection in his tone that matched her own.
“And he was disappointed right to the very end,” Romola said, before she could stop herself. Afraid her words might sound like an implied accusation against Tito, she hurried on. “If only we could see his longest, dearest wish fulfilled exactly as he wanted it!”
“Well, there is every chance we will,” Bernardo said kindly, rising to his feet and settling his cap back on his head. “The times are cloudy right now, but fish are always caught by waiting. Who knows? When the wheel of fortune has turned enough times, I may yet be elected Gonfaloniere, the highest magistrate of Florence, before I die, and no foreign buyer could ever lay a finger on those books. I have made sure of it.” He glanced around the walls of the library as he spoke, then turned back to her and patted her cheek gently. “And you need never fear me dying and leaving any trouble behind, my ghost will not come to claim anything. I have written all that out clearly in my will.”
Romola seized the hand resting against her cheek, and pressed it to her lips in silence.
“Have you been scolding your husband for being away from home so much lately? I see him everywhere I go but here,” Bernardo said, eager to change the subject to something lighter.
Romola felt a hot flush creep up her neck and across her face. “He is very much needed by the city, you know. He speaks so well in the councils, and I am glad everyone is finally recognising how valuable he is.”
“So you are content then, Madonna Orgogliosa—my proud girl?” Bernardo said, smiling as he stepped toward the door.
“Assuredly.”
Poor Romola. There was one thing that would have made the sharp pain of disappointment in her husband even harder to bear, and that was having anyone else guess that he had given her cause to be disappointed. It might seem like a foolish woman’s weakness, but it is a weakness that sits very close to a woman’s greatest nobleness. The wife who willingly flings wide the veil that shrouds her married life has profaned what should be a quiet sanctuary, and turned it into just another vulgar, public place for strangers to gawk at.

All Florence hummed with wild, bright excitement the next day: the French were leaving, at last. For Romola, wrapped in her heavy wool mantle on the high house loggia, there was a half-superstitious, dim hope tangled in her gladness—surely the strange new anxieties that had settled over her home with the foreign troops would vanish alongside them. The French had occupied the city barely eleven days, but in that short stretch Romola had known sharper unhappiness than in all her twenty-one years of quiet, book-filled life before. Tito had donned his hateful hidden armour the very day the invaders arrived, and though she could not articulate exactly why their departure should erase the fear that haunted him, the memory of the prisoner grasping his wrist, the image she had seen in Piero’s sketch, rose unbidden whenever she tried to puzzle it out. At the very least, when the French were gone, she would be rid of the heavy, oppressive foreign presence bound so tight to all her recent pain.

She lingered on the loggia long after the last plume of the French royal retinue had vanished over the bridges leading toward Porta San Piero, the gate that faced Siena and Rome. Even after the city gates slammed shut for the night, she stayed, letting the vibration of the great city-wide bell peal thrumb up through the stone paving under her feet, until dusk bled purple over the Arno. When at last she descended into her father’s library, she lit the tiny guttering oil lamp, resolved to shake off the day’s agitation and get back to work copying the catalogue of Bardo’s ancient manuscripts. Tito had left at dawn, and she had not expected him home for hours; she had planned to move to the little frescoed saloon, the walls painted with dancing nymphs and songbirds, as she did every evening since Tito had complained the library was too chill and gloomy.

To her utter surprise, she had barely set pen to paper when Tito stepped through the door. Her first lurch of thought was how forlorn he must feel in the vast shadowy room, the fire almost dead on the hearth, only the single small lamp burning far off at the end of the long oak table. She almost ran toward him.
“Tito, dearest, I did not know you would come so soon,” she said, voice soft and nervous, lifting her white arms to unwind the silk becchetto from his shoulders.
“Am I not welcome then?” he teased, with one of his old bright, easy smiles, pulling her close even as he playfully held his face back from hers.
“Tito!” she chided, in that soft, loving lilt she used for him, and he kissed her fondly, stroking her hair the way he had in the first months of their marriage. Romola quivered with delight. All the day’s tangled emotions had sharpened her sensitivity to that familiar, warm affection. It will come back, she told herself, the old happiness will return. He is like himself again.

Tito was working very hard to be like his old self; under his tunic, his heart was hammering with unspoken anxiety.
“If I had expected you so soon,” she said, as she helped him shed his outer wrappings, “I would have laid out a little feast to match the bells. I never meant to be here in the library when you came home.”
“Never mind, sweet,” he said carelessly. “Pay no mind to the fire. Come, sit here with me.”
The low carved stool tucked beside his chair was Romola’s usual seat for their evening talks, the same one she had sat on beside Bardo’s chair for years. She rested her arm on his knee, as she had once rested it on her father’s, and looked up at him, the unmentioned portrait of Bardo propped against the bookshelf at her back.
“I have never enjoyed the clang of the bells so much before, Tito,” she began. “I let them deafen me, shake me right down to my bones. I felt half like a Bacchante, possessed by some divine, wild joy. Are not the people so happy tonight?”
“Happy after that sour, pious Florentine fashion,” Tito said, shrugging. “But truth be told, no one left in Florence has much to rejoice over. There are three rival factions in the city all ready to tear each other’s throats out. If the Frate’s party grows strong enough to silence the other two, as looks likely, life here will be as cheerful as a funeral. They are already hatching plans for their Great Council, and soon the man who shouts sacred lauds the loudest will be the only one allowed to hold office. On top of that, the city is bled dry paying the ransom to the French king, and the war to take back Pisa will drain the last of our gold. Florence will be a paradise for fanatics who spend their evenings lashing themselves in crypts, but for anyone who loves ease and light? The exiles have it far better. For my own part, I have been thinking very seriously that we would be wise to quit Florence, my Romola.”

Romola started back, cold trembling seeping through her limbs. “Tito, how could we leave Florence? Surely you cannot think I could leave — not yet, not for a very long while.”
“That is just your secluded imagination weaving fantasies, my sweet,” he said, leaning to kiss her brow, his hand brushing her fair hair. “You lay false weight on a handful of tiny local ties. Before we married, I told you I always wished we were somewhere far from Florence. The people here are as sharp and biting as their spring winds. I like places where life moves softer, slower. It would do you good, too, to dip yourself in the soft waters of forgetfulness, and leave these narrow, petty duties behind.”
Romola felt his caress no more than if he had kissed a painted mask. She was too stunned by the gulf between their thoughts to even register his touch. “I do not cling to Florence because it is the pleasantest place in the world,” she said, quiet and tight. “I stay here because we must fulfill my father’s last wish. My godfather is seventy-one years old. We cannot dump that burden on him.”
“Those are the very superstitious mists clouding your mind, my Romola,” he said, his tone gentle but unyielding. “I have to look out for you, even against your own will. If those dear tender eyes of yours see falsely, I must see for them, and save you from wasting your life chasing impossible dreams.”

Romola sat silent, motionless. She could not pretend not to see where his words were leading: he meant to persuade her to let the library be locked away in some monastery, to cast aside the sacred promise made to her dead father. She was determined she would never submit her judgment of that duty to his. But the first shock of realizing that she and Tito were now openly divided, that the love she had trusted was built on nothing, crushed her into stillness. Tito mistook her silence for wavering, and went on in the same soft, remonstrating tone, arguing that preserving Bardo’s library as a single collection under his name was a pointless, petty goal, pointing to how the Medici’s great collection had been scattered, how it was better for all knowledge to spread across the whole civilized world than be hoarded for Florentine pride.

Romola’s silence broke, a cold, sharp contempt rising in her throat as she listened to the smooth, clever words that had no pulse for love, for faith, for the dead man whose whole life had been wrapped up in that library. “You talk of substantial good, Tito!” she said, her voice rising, a ring of scorn cutting through her words. “Are faithfulness, and love, and grateful memories no good? Is it no good that we should keep the silent promises people trust us to keep? Is it no good that a just life should be justly honoured? What good can clever talk do, if men harden their hearts against the people who loved them and depended on them? I would leave Florence, I would give up anything else, Tito — what else did I live for but my father and you? But I will not give up that duty. It was the yearning of his heart, and so it is mine.”

Tito’s face hardened. He could see he would never persuade her to agree. “I am sorry to hear you speak with such blind stubbornness,” he said, quiet. “It obliges me to give you pain. I foresaw your opposition, and since a quick decision had to be made, I avoided that obstacle, and acted without consulting you. A husband must sometimes act separately for his wife’s good, even when he has a wife as good as you.”
Romola looked at him, breathless, eyes wide with question.
“I mean,” he said, answering her unspoken look, “that I have arranged the transfer of all the books and all the antiquities where they will do the most good. The books have been bought by the Duke of Milan. The marbles, bronzes, and the rest are going to France. They will be protected by the stability of great powers, instead of rotting in a city that is always on the edge of ruin.”

Before he had finished speaking, Romola had sprung to her feet, standing over him, hands clenched tight, a flash of fierce, Bardo pride flaring in her eyes she had never felt before in her life. “You have sold them?” she whispered, as if she could not trust her own ears.
“I have,” Tito said, quailing a little under the scorching weight of her scorn.
“You are a treacherous man!” she said, her voice grating, sharp as shards of broken glass. She stood silent a minute, then turned away, agitated, saying, “It may still be stopped. I am going to my godfather.”

In an instant Tito leapt up, crossed the room, locked the library door, and slipped the key into his leather scarsella pouch at his belt. He was not angry — only very, very unwilling, to face this unpleasant scene, but he knew he had to subdue her before she could run off to Bernardo. He leaned against the marble pedestal under the bust of a grim old Roman statesman, feigning ease, but his olive cheeks had paled. No chain armour in the world could protect him from the moral dread thrumming in his chest: he had locked his wife’s rage and scorn in the room with him, and there was no escaping it.
“Try to calm yourself, Romola,” he said. “Messser Bernardo cannot reverse what I have done. There is no point in going to him. You would not want, if you were in your right mind, to air our private quarrels to a third party, to make your own husband look like a villain before an old man you care for.”

Romola did not sit down, her whole frame thrumming with impetuous, furious force. “Why can it not be reversed?” she demanded. “Nothing has even been moved yet.”
“The sale is signed and sealed with written agreement. The buyers, Count di San Severino and the Seneschal de Beaucaire, rode out with the King this morning on the road to Siena, and I hold the bonds for the purchase money.”
“If my father had suspected you would prove a faithless man,” Romola said, the bitter scorn spilling over before she could stop it, “he would have locked the library safely out of your reach. But death overtook him too soon, and you robbed him when you knew his ear was deaf, and his hands were stiff in the grave.” She paused, passion gathering in her throat, and burst out the question she had hidden away for weeks: “Have you robbed someone else, someone who is not dead? Is that the reason you wear that armour under your tunic?”

For a second Tito was cowed, terrified that the retribution he had feared was about to break. Then his resistance hardened. He told her she was mad with exaggerated grief, that any rational person would see he had done the wisest possible thing, that even Bernardo would agree if he thought about it clearly. When Romola protested that Bernardo would never agree, that they could chase the buyers down and offer to pay Tito back the full sum for the library, Tito coolly told her it was impossible: Bernardo would never sink three thousand florins more into the library, and he, Tito, would never allow the old man to do such a foolish thing anyway.
“Think carefully before you take any step to oppose me,” he warned her, voice calm and cold as a winter stream. “What will you gain by disgracing me before him? The library is sold, the event is irrevocable. And you are my wife.”

Romola stared at the ground for a long, quiet several minutes. When she spoke again, her voice was dead, cold, perfectly still. “I have one thing to ask.”
“Ask anything I can do without harming us both, Romola.”
“Give me the portion of the sale money that belongs to my godfather. Let me pay him what he is owed.”
“I must have your assurance first that you will not try to harm me, or speak of this to anyone.”
“Do you believe in assurances, Tito?” she said, a flicker of bitter smile crossing her face.
“From you, I do.”
“I will do you no harm. I will tell no one. I will say nothing to pain him or you. You are right. It is irrevocable.”
“Then I will do as you ask tomorrow.”
“Tonight, if possible,” she said. “So we never have to speak of this hateful thing again.”

He moved to the lamp, wrote out the draft for the sum, then came back and bent over her, pressing the paper into her hand. Now that he was sure she would not defy him, he leaned in to press a light kiss to her brow. She did not react at all, as if she had not felt his lips touch her skin. She listened as he turned the key in the lock, stepped out, and the heavy great door of the courtyard boomed shut behind him.
Then she stood up, as if a chain had snapped loose from around her chest. She stumbled over to Bardo’s propped portrait, fell on her knees on the cold stone floor before it, and burst into great, heaving sobs.

Wandering the crowded streets of Florence in search of the cheapest possible shelter — a spare outhouse where he could bed down out of the wind and rain — Baldassarre’s steps drew him to the only patch of ground inside the city walls that broke the flat sprawl of terracotta roofs. From that small rise, a passerby lifted high enough could see past the clustered houses to the soft line of the protecting Tuscan hills and the wide, sun-dappled valley beyond, views otherwise hidden save for the narrow opening cut by the winding course of the Arno. This stretch of slope was not the quarry side of Bogoli Hill he had caught glimpses of before, but the quieter incline rising behind Via de’ Bardi, known locally as the hill of San Giorgio. Bratti the peddler had told him Tito’s home stood on Via de’ Bardi, and after scoping the narrow, shadowed thoroughfare, Baldassarre turned to trudge up the slope he had spotted from the bridge. A shelter on this hill would suit him perfectly: years of wandering under open skies had left him claustrophobic in the cramped medieval streets, where only a thin strip of sky peeked overhead between the towering walls, and the twisting labyrinth of alleys frayed his already spotty, faltering memory further still.

The hill was sparsely populated, mostly dotted with vegetable plots and untended overgrown gardens, save for one jagged stretch of rough, stone-strewn ground that had never been cultivated since a 13th century landslide had swallowed an entire row of houses there. Perched at the edge of that broken patch stood a strange little square building, like a truncated tower capped with fluted terracotta tiles, and tucked against a crumbled section of old stone wall right beside it was a small, low outhouse. Under a gnarled half-dead mulberry tree, shedding its last rustling autumn leaves through the open doorways, a shrivelled, weathered old woman named Monna Lisa was untying a nanny goat and her two frolicking kids. Half the outhouse was clearly given over to livestock, the other half empty save for a stack of fresh straw and a handful of rusted farm tools. It was exactly the shelter he was looking for.

He had to lean in and shout right into her ear to make the hard-of-hearing old woman understand he was a poor stranger from across the sea, could not afford a hostel, and would happily pay a quattrino or two a week just to sleep on that straw. At first she only shook her head, muttering “No — no lodging” in a muffled, uncomprehending tone. But after persistent pleading, she gestured to a large half-buried log in the corner, left rotting there since her husband died decades prior. If he chopped the entire log into kindling, she told him, he could stay one single night. He agreed. Monna Lisa stood arms akimbo watching him swing the axe, a sharp little cunning smile on her face as she whispered to herself: “That log’s sat there useless for years. I couldn’t have paid a local boy half a quattrino to split it. This odd-acting foreigner chops very well. And if he only wants a bed of straw, I can set him running errands up and down the hill, no one need be the wiser. Hidden sin is half forgiven, after all. He’s a stranger, he won’t take any notice of the girl up in the cottage, and I’ll tell her to keep her mouth shut tight.”

That very girl, Tessa, was pressed to a four-inch round hole cut in the closed upper shutter at that exact moment, craning to see him. The shutter was not fastened to punish her: the window on the opposite wall was the only one in the whole tiny cottage fitted with expensive glass, and the weather was far too cool to leave that shutter propped open. The hole was cut just a little too high, forcing her to stand on a rickety low stool to peer through, but Tessa would have endured far more discomfort for a glimpse of any new, different thing to break up the quiet monotony of her hidden life. She had darted to the shutter the moment she heard Baldassarre’s loud unfamiliar voice speaking to Monna Lisa, and stayed there until dusk fell, watching him finish chopping the wood, trudge back into the outhouse, and sink heavily down onto the straw, his whole frame heavy with exhaustion and loneliness.

A wild, giddy little temptation seized Tessa. She would sneak down and bring the old man a portion of her supper, talk to him for a while. He was not deaf like Monna Lisa, after all — she could prattle to him about all the silly little thoughts and small joys she had no one else to share them with. She knew it was naughty: obedience was the core of what Tessa considered duty, and her husband Naldo had strictly forbidden her from making any acquaintances, warning her that if anyone outside their tiny hidden circle learned of her life here, all her new comforts and happiness would vanish as quickly as hidden treasure that turns to lead the moment it is pulled into daylight. She had been perfectly obedient up to that point, even pulling her hood low over her face in church and pursing her lips tight to keep from greeting any passing stranger, half terrified her cruel brute stepfather Nofri would track her all the way from her old farm and drag her back to backbreaking work. But this old stranger was no proper acquaintance, right? He was a homeless wanderer with no ties anywhere, he would never tell anyone about her. She could even save the small trespass to confess to the Padre the next Easter, when she went to confession. It was hardly a real sin at all.

Her face flushed and excited, she sat on her low stool for a minute balancing on the edge of the small transgression, before jumping up to smooth down her festa-day contadina gown: a crisp clean dark green serge, cinched with a bright red girdle, red glass prayer beads strung around her neck, her curly brown hair knotted tight and pinned with the silver pin Naldo had gifted her, and the new fine gold ring on her finger she was still absurdly proud of. She tripped downstairs, feigned innocent curiosity to ask Monna Lisa what all the shouting outside had been about, then put on a fierce little scowl like a tiny kitten trying to look formidable, and ordered the old woman to go sit by the fire upstairs — she wanted to eat her supper down by the door. Monna Lisa huffed and obeyed, and three minutes later Tessa was knocking soft on the outhouse door, one hand clutching a tin lantern, the other hefting a wooden bowl piled high with steaming pecorino-flavoured macaroni.

Baldassarre, who had been gnawing on a crumb of stale dry bread he’d fished from his tunic pocket, thought he was trapped in a strange sad dream when he opened the door and found this round-faced, blue-eyed little girl beaming at him, bright with uncomplicated delight, breaking into his dark loneliness. “I’ve brought you supper!” she shouted, leaning right towards his ear, having forgotten he was not deaf like Monna Lisa. “Sit down and eat it, I’ll stay with you a while.”

Surprise and wariness surged in Baldassarre first, but he had no urge at all to turn her away. He sank passively back onto his straw bed, Tessa set the warm bowl on his lap, placed the lantern on the dirt floor between them, and crossed her hands over her lap, nodding at the food with a reassuring little smile as if to say there were no tricks here. He was ravenous, and the warm macaroni tasted like nothing he had eaten in months. Tessa watched him take two or three bites before she realized he could hear perfectly well. “Oh! I forgot!” she gasped, clapping her hands over her mouth, “I’ve spent so long shouting at Monna Lisa I do it to everyone!”

She prattled on happily, chattering that she lived in the little cottage with the old deaf woman, that they had plenty of nice things, that she could eat nuts whenever she wanted and never had to do hard farm work anymore. She said she missed her old mule Giannetta from the farm sometimes, and used to talk for hours to the goat because there was no one else to speak to. Then she teased him that she had a secret: she had a tiny, perfect bambino, born on Christmas Day, that she had been married one Christmas long ago and no one in the whole world knew. She clapped a hand over her mouth like she had accidentally revealed a great forbidden truth, and Baldassarre stared at her dreamily, as if this garrulous soft young thing was a half-forgotten memory drifting up out of his own lost youth.

He told her he only knew one man in all the world. Tessa’s face fell, and she asked anxiously if his name was Nofri, her cruel old stepfather. Baldassarre shook his head, and Tessa laughed, delighted, saying that was not her husband’s name at all — her husband was the most beautiful, gentle, perfect man in the whole world, only he did not come to visit often, he only ever arrived after dark, sometimes staying away for weeks at a time. She said she would bring the baby down to show him tomorrow, then picked up her empty bowl, wished him good night, and slipped back into the cottage. She immediately told Monna Lisa what she had done, and insisted the stranger be allowed to stay as long as he wanted, to help carry water up the hill and run small errands. Monna Lisa pretended to grumble that Naldo would be angry, but she had already been planning to turn the free laborer to her own use, and readily agreed as long as Tessa swore never to tell a single soul of his presence. Tessa promised, saying she would only ever tell the baby.

The next morning, Tessa hurried through all her tiny chores to bring Baldassarre a bowl of steaming hot broth and soft crusty bread, then ran back to grab her swaddled baby, all big dark eyes and barely a hint of a nose, that she swore looked exactly like the infant Jesus in the parish church painting. She handed the small warm bundle to Baldassarre to hold, and his rough deep voice rumbled out “Poor thing, poor thing,” so low it felt almost threatening under the soft apparent pity — as if the sight of this guileless unprotected girl and her child had linked them in his mind to all the cruelty that had been done to him, two innocent creatures wronged by the world, who deserved to be avenged. Tessa laughed and shook her head, saying she was not poor at all, she had almost everything she ever wanted, except to see her husband more often. When she asked him what his name was, Baldassarre rubbed at his furrowed forehead, and his memory went completely, helplessly blank. Tessa saw his confused, lost look, and patted his arm gently. “Never mind,” she said softly, “you are a stranger, you don’t need a name for us. We will be good to you.” She ran off to eat her own breakfast, and Baldassarre stared after her, muttering “Poor thing,” once more under his breath, as the first pale streaks of sun flooded over the slope of San Giorgio hill.

Messer Naldo returned far sooner than anyone expected, turning up on the evening of the twenty-eighth of November a mere eleven days after his last visit. It was clear he had never traveled far past the Tuscan mountain passes, and the tense encounter we witnessed earlier that very night in the Via de’ Bardi perfectly explains the quiet, urgent impulse that turned his steps toward the slopes of San Giorgio hill.

When Tito had first arranged this hidden cottage home for Tessa more than eighteen months prior, fresh back from his journey to Rome, he had convinced himself he acted only out of soft, uncalculating kindness after the foolish mishap that led the simple young contadina to mistake their playful mock marriage for a real, sacred bond. It was true he was drawn to her pretty, trusting warmth, to the easy charm of her prattle, but all the grand, ideal passion he held in his heart belonged entirely to Romola: the first and only woman who had ever moved him to that depth, whose mere presence wrapped around him so thoroughly that the brush of her long hair across his cheek would hum through every hour that followed. He was no gross, reckless pleasure-seeker, his intellect too sharp, his sensibilities too fine, to sink into careless vice. But a web had wrapped itself around him and Tessa that he could not find the strength to break: what had begun as a small, calculated lie to preserve her illusion for his own possible convenience had grown thick and tangled all on its own, beyond any power he had to unpick it. It was easy for Tito, with his soft, self-deceiving nature, to frame his choice to keep her in ignorance and maintain the cottage as an act of pure kindness, and he clung to that self-justification fiercely in the early days of his devotion to Romola. He could not bear the quiet weight of a secret offence against the wife he adored, whose deep, noble trust in him was rooted in moral purpose, not simple, unthinking innocence.

He took every possible precaution to keep the secret hidden. Monna Lisa, his deaf, elderly landlady, never left the cottage except to visit two nearby shops and make her annual confession, and knew nothing of his real name or identity, only that he paid her generously enough to make her old age comfortable. Tito had strictly warned Tessa that if she ever breathed a word about the farcical "marriage" ceremony, she would never see him again, and Monna Lisa’s deafness meant that no careless, unplanned chatter could ever slip past to reveal the truth. His visits were rare at first, only long enough to check that Tessa and the baby were well, and he was determined to keep her exactly the simple, unworldly country girl he had first met: a wild little field flower, untransplanted into the stifling constraints of fine clothes and complicated social pretensions that would rob her of all her gentle charm.
