Even the little luxuries he provided were scaled to her simple tastes, capped at the endless supply of roasted nuts she loved to hoard and nibble, a coincidence that conveniently kept his hidden expenses low at a time when every coin he owned was spoken for by his public life in Florentine high society.

All this had held true until a few weeks prior, the exact day he had stumbled on Baldassarre on the Duomo steps and bought the chain mail he now wore under his tunic. That night, walking across the bridge back home, he had found it impossible to face Romola. She trusted him, yes, but he had known deep in his bones that behind her frank, bright eyes lay a judgment he could not outrun, a moral standard that would cut through every lie he wove the second she suspected the truth. He had fled up the hill instead, craving an hour of total unconstraint, no need to perform, no need to keep up appearances, with the sweet adoring girl who thought the very sun rose and set on him, who would believe any fable he told and never care a fig for public opinion.

It was no surprise, then, that he turned his feet up the same stony path eleven days later, fresh from Romola’s first sharp, burning outburst of scorn. He did not wish Tessa in Romola’s place: Romola was the partner for his grand ambitions, his place among Florence’s scholars and statesmen, the entire wider world he had built his life within. But he craved a refuge from that unyielding, unforgiving standard he could never dismiss as mere foolishness, and Tessa’s small, uncomplicated soul was exactly that safe haven.

It was barely past eight o’clock when he climbed the steps to the cottage door. Normally Tessa would hear his footfall and come running to meet him before he even turned the handle, but tonight she did not. He stepped inside, and the dim guttering lamp hung above the dying hearth showed him why: Tessa was kneeling beside the baby’s small bed, her head lolled sideways on the pillow, fast asleep over her brown rosary, which had slipped loose from her hand to rest beside the painted image of the Madonna and its gilded palm fronds. Tito crossed the room softly, sat down beside her, and before two seconds had passed she opened her eyes, no startled gasp, no surprise, just a quiet, dreamy certainty that he was there. “I dreamed it,” she murmured, “and then I told myself it was only a dream — and then I woke, and it was true.”

“Little sinner,” he teased, pinching her cheek gently, “you’ve said barely half your prayers. I’ll punish you by refusing to look at your baby. He’s quite ugly, you know.”

Tessa’s face crumpled into a soft pout, and she leaned anxiously over the sleeping infant. “Ah, that’s not true! He’s prettier than anything in the whole world. You can’t really think he’s ugly. Look at him — he’s far bigger than when you saw him last, his hair is growing so fast, isn’t that wonderful? Kiss him very gently, and he won’t wake. You want to kiss him, don’t you?”

He pressed a light, butterfly-soft kiss to the baby’s swaddled forehead, then turned her face back to his. “You like staring at that baby better than looking at your own husband, you false thing.”

She stayed kneeling, resting her hands on his knees, gazing up at him with round, pink cheeks exactly like one of Fra Lippo Lippi’s adoring, wide-eyed angels. “No,” she said, shaking her head earnestly, “I will always love you most. I just wanted you to love the bambino too, once I only cared about having you all to myself.”

He drew her onto his knee, eager to listen to her soft prattle to ease the raw, jarring agitation left by his fight with Romola. “Did you expect me to come back so soon?”

“Ah no,” she said, “I counted every single day on my thumbs, starting back at the first day after you got that beautiful chain coat — the one Messer San Michele gave you to keep you safe on all your journeys. You’re wearing it right now, aren’t you?” she added, peeping through the slit in his tunic. “It must be the magic in it that made you come home faster.”

“Perhaps it did, Tessa,” he said. “Never mind the coat now. Tell me everything that’s happened since I was last here. Did you see the tents out at Prato, the horsemen and soldiers marching over the bridges? Hear the drums and trumpets blaring?”

“I did, and I was so frightened I thought the soldiers would climb right up the hill. Monna Lisa was scared too, she said they would steal our goats off to make mischief. But the Holy Madonna kept us safe — we never saw a single one of them up here. But something did happen, something I hardly dare tell you. I’ve been saying extra Hail Marys to ask forgiveness for it.”

“What do you mean, little one?” Tito asked, a flicker of anxiety pricking through his calm. “Tell me quickly.”

“Only let me sit on your knee while I tell you, then I won’t be so scared.”

He wrapped his arm around her, but his face had gone grave. It seemed even this quiet refuge could not escape the unpleasant consequences of his choices.

“At first I didn’t mean to tell you,” she whispered, as if speaking softer would lessen her fault, “because we thought the old man would be gone long before you came back, and it would be like it never happened at all. But he’s still here, and you’re here, and I’ve never disobeyed you before. I thought if I told you, you might forgive me, since it’s such a long time till my next confession.”

“Go on,” Tito said, already hoping it was a trivial, harmless mistake.

“Oh, you’d feel sorry for him. I think he cries when he thinks no one is watching. I first talked to him because he was wandering all alone, nowhere to stay, and I wanted to show him the baby. I thought you wouldn’t mind if I spoke to a stranger. He wanted to sleep on the straw in the shed next to the goats, so I talked Monna Lisa into saying yes. He’s gone almost all day, but when he comes back I bring him little bits of food to eat.”

“Some beggar, I suppose,” Tito said, frowning. “That was very naughty of you, and I’m cross with Monna Lisa. I’ll have him sent away at once.”

“No, he’s not a beggar,” Tessa protested. “He tried to pay Monna Lisa, but she said he could work odd jobs for her instead. He gets himself shaved every few days, his clothes are neat. Monna Lisa says he’s a proper, decent man. Sometimes I think he’s not quite in his right mind, though. Lupo, the simple boy from Peretola, looked just like him sometimes, like he didn’t know where he was. He never can remember his own name — everyone has a name, don’t they? He carries a book with him now, stares at it for hours on end like a priest, but his lips never move, he’s not saying prayers.”

Tito’s heart began to hammer so hard he could hear it in his ears. Every detail lined up perfectly with Baldassarre, the man he had disowned on the Duomo steps. “Fetch your stool, little Tessa, sit down on it,” he said, his voice strangely steady. His eyes were fixed on her, but he no longer saw her at all, only the vivid image his words conjured up.

A wild, new possibility bloomed in his mind. He could walk out to that shed right now, no witnesses anywhere. He could beg his father for forgiveness, offer him all the money he had gotten from selling Bardo’s great library, take him south to Naples where the coming French rule would grant them protection, no one the wiser. Romola would never have to learn the full truth of what happened on the Duomo steps, and if Baldassarre accepted his kindness, he would bury the secret forever. All the fear, all the tension, all the looming threat hanging over his life could vanish in a single moment, no public shame, no messy exposure, just soft, easy reconciliation that would let his world wrap itself in comfort again. He longed for that relief more than ever, fresh from the sting of Romola’s scorn.

He stood up, stepping toward the door, and Tessa dropped her rosary with a small startled cry. He turned back, his face soft again. “My little Tessa, run get me a lantern. Don’t cry, little pigeon, I’m not angry at all.”

They went down the stairs together, Tessa about to shout her request for a lantern loud enough to reach Monna Lisa’s deaf ears, when Tito, who had already pulled the cottage front door open, stopped her. “No, Tessa, I don’t need a lantern. Go back upstairs, be quiet, don’t say a word to Monna Lisa about any of this.”

Thirty seconds later he was standing before the closed door of the outhouse, the moon shining bright white on its old, unpainted wooden planks. For a split second he hesitated, instinct flaring up to shrink back from the tiger-like glare he was about to face — but he was young, strong, and wearing armour under his tunic, the old man was weak and unarmed. He laid his hand on the door, pulled it open, its rough wood scraping over the dirt floor.

Baldassarre was not asleep. A square high window in the shed wall let a stream of pale moonlight fall across him as he sat on the straw, a small dagger glinting in his hand, running his thumb again and again along its sharp edge. He was at the lowest point of his helpless, raging despair: he had pored over his book till the light faded, trying to recall the names of the jewels Tito had stolen from him, the symbols carved into their surfaces, but all those memories had slipped away into total darkness. He was left with nothing but a searing, blank rage, fixed on the single, desperate thought of driving that blade through the base heart that had betrayed him.

The door scraped open, and he looked up. There in the moonlight, backlit by the silver glow, was the very man he had been dreaming of killing, close enough to touch. Before Tito could leap back, Baldassarre launched himself forward, the dagger flashing out. The blade snapped clean in two against Tito’s hidden chain mail. Baldassarre stumbled backward onto the straw, clutching the broken hilt in his fist, the sharp pointed half of the blade glinting on the dirt right at Tito’s feet.

Tito gasped in a rush of triumphant relief. He was unharmed, his armour had held, the vengeance he had feared for weeks was broken and helpless before him. That relief softened him, any urge to cruelty melting away. He spoke in the soft, familiar voice he had used as a boy on the Greek shores, the tone Baldassarre had not heard in years. “Padre mio,” he said. “I came to ask your forgiveness.”

He paused, letting the words sink in, then continued. “I was taken completely by surprise that morning on the Duomo steps. I want to be your son again. I want to make the rest of your life happy, so you can forget all the suffering you have endured.”

Baldassarre lay motionless for a long moment, seething with defeated rage. Then he threw the broken hilt aside, dragged himself shakily to his feet, and clutched Tito’s outstretched helping hand in a grip so tight his knuckles went white, their faces barely a foot apart. “I saved you,” he growled, his voice deep and trembling with raw fury. “I nurtured you. I loved you. You forsook me. You robbed me. You denied me. What can you possibly give me? You have made the whole world nothing but bitterness for me. But there is one last draught of sweetness left. That you shall know agony.”

He dropped Tito’s hand, stumbled back, and sank down onto the straw again, spent completely by the force of his desperate lunge. There was no soft reconciliation here, no easy, secret repentance that would let Tito wipe his slate clean with no cost, no public shame, no real atonement. There was no place for it at all.

For days after Tito’s quiet request to clear the library, he saw barely anything of Romola. The next morning, when he found her seated in her private room beside the little painted household tabernacle, he spoke to her soft and gentle, explaining that agents would soon arrive to pack up all the antiquities her father had spent his life collecting, and it would be far more peaceful for her to stay tucked away in this small, familiar chamber, far from the noise of strange footsteps tramping through the rest of the house. Romola said no word of protest, no sign of anger; the night before had been one long, unbroken stretch of wakefulness, and even with her robust, healthy frame, every sensation in her bones had curdled into a dull, constant ache, as if she had been stunned and bruised all over.

Tito guessed she was unwell, but dared not press her for more than that quiet assent. When he noticed her hand and brow were stone cold, he slipped away to fetch a thick furred mantle, and draped it lightly over her shoulders without another word. Every short interval he stole home between errands, he tried to win her back to him with small, unobtrusive acts of softness: a handful of the candied citron she had loved as a girl, a new small devotional print to prop beside the tabernacle, a quiet murmur that the packers were making good progress, that the noise would be over soon. But she seemed to have lost the ability to speak to him, or even to lift her eyes to his face. *Patience*, he told himself over and over. She would get over this hurt, forgive him at last. The bond between them was still the strongest thing in her life. When the person who strikes a blow suffers no bruise of their own, while the one struck lingers in their pain instead of acting as if nothing has happened, it is only too easy for the striker to slip into the role of the aggrieved party, smugly conscious of how amiable and gentle they have been ever since the harm was done. Tito was not naturally inclined to nurse that petty grievance, though: his whole instinct was pointed towards propitiation, and he would have endured a great deal just to feel Romola’s hand rest lightly on his head again, the way it had that morning he had first been too cowardly to meet her eyes.

It grew easier and easier for him to wait patiently for that return of domestic peace, because his life outside the house was growing more absorbing, more thrilling, by the hour. A course of action that seems the deliberate, premeditated work of a whole life’s character can almost always trace its apparent origin back to one single sharp, defining impression. Ever since that moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito had perched atop a stack of trade bales and tasted the sharp, bright pleasure of realizing he could wrap any phrase in a shape that would make any crowd cheer him, his imagination had been turning over the thought of political activity, exactly the kind of activity Florence’s current unrest was certain to create openings for. The dread of Baldassarre, woken that same exact moment, had lain like an immovable boulder across that path, pushing him to sell the library as a safety net for the day he might be forced to flee Florence, just when he was starting to feel the city hold a new, irresistible attraction. Now that dread was nearly gone. He would still keep his guard up, still keep his wits sharp for any demand for quick thinking or coolness, but he no longer felt forced to take the inconvenient step of running from Florence to hunt for new fortune. The old man had rejected the atonement Tito had offered, forced him into open defiance; a lost old foreigner in a strange city, his memory frayed to nothing, was weak enough to defy with no real risk.

Tito’s half-hidden unspoken desires were hardening now into clear, definite plans. As the hot flush of young romantic passion faded, life was coming more and more to feel like a game to him, a perfectly pleasant mix of practiced skill and lucky chance. And the game unfolding in Florence promised to be fast, fierce, electrifying: a game of revolution and factional clash, full of all those unspoken, unacknowledged maneuvers where a sharp, brilliant ingenuity that casts aside every inconvenient scruple save the obvious truth that “ginger is hot in the mouth” sees its own path as the height of superior wisdom.

No sooner had the last of the French guests marched out of the city gates than Florence buzzed with the restlessness of an anthill after a terrifying shadow has lifted, every scurrying worker rushing to repair the damage done while the dark hung over them. *How are we to raise the ransom money we owe the French king? How are we to crush those stubborn Pisan rebels who have broken away from us? Most of all, how do we rewrite our whole system of government, to make sure our magistrates are chosen fairly and our laws are passed without half the city rioting in protest?* Until those questions were answered, all trade risked grinding to a halt, and the huge mass of working men who were not even counted as citizens, who had no vote to distract them from their empty bellies, were certain to grow impatient. Something had to be done, and fast.

First, the great bell of the Palazzo della Signoria was rung, summoning every citizen to a public parliament in the Piazza de’ Signori. When the crowd was packed so tight no one could move, and armed men were posted at every exit to seal the square off, the Signoria — the ruling Gonfaloniere and his eight sitting Priors — stepped out onto the stone platform beside the carved marble lion in front of the Old Palace, and made their proposal. Twenty of the city’s most respected leading men would be granted full dictatorial authority, for a term of one full year, during which they would appoint every magistrate and restructure the entire framework of government to set things right. The crowd roared their assent, cheering loud enough to rattle the windows of the palace, and felt as if they themselves had freely elected that Council of Twenty. It was the same old Florentine trick, the ancient custom that made the narrow will of the small ruling few look like the free, chosen desire of the many.

The shouting in the square died away quickly, but the debate inside the palace stretched on for days, far into the small hours of every night. Should Florence adopt the Great Council model used by Venice, where all qualified citizens of a certain age could vote to elect all government officers and pass all laws, no matter their rank or party loyalty, no favor granted to old noble lineages? Or should the city be governed under a narrower, more exclusive system, where the hereditary influence of long established wealthy families would not be watered down by the votes of ordinary shopkeepers? Learned doctors of law argued back and forth endlessly: Messer Pagolantonio Soderini laid out a mountain of excellent, airtight reasons in favor of the popular Great Council, while Messer Guidantonio Vespucci laid out an equally unassailable stack of reasons in favor of the more aristocratic form of rule. It was the old, trivial argument over whether to boil meat or roast it, already decided long before the debate began by the personal appetites of the men arguing, and all that brilliant rhetoric would have dragged on indefinitely if there had not been a force outside the palace walls pushing the vague discontent of the majority of disenfranchised Florentines into a concrete, determined will.

That force was the preaching of Savonarola. Driven half by the absolute spiritual conviction laid upon him to guide his people to good, half prompted by public leaders who could not get any popular measure passed without his backing, his daily sermons had rapidly shifted from general exhortations for all citizens to set aside private passion and personal interest for the public good, to explicit, concrete demands: he did not just tell his flock to choose whatever was best for the whole community, he told them exactly what government they must have to achieve that good. *Choose the Great Council*, he thundered from the pulpit. The Great Council is the will of God. For Fra Girolamo, those two statements were one and the same. The Great Council was the only workable plan to give the public will a voice large enough to drown out the corrupting influence of petty factional interests, the only system that could make honest, impartial public action even possible. The purer Florence’s government became, the more secure from the designs of men who profited from the moral degradation of their neighbors, the closer the Florentine people would come to being a pure, holy community, worthy to lead the whole Christian world in the renovation of the Church and the renewal of all human society. Fra Girolamo’s mind never stopped short of that most sublime of ends: every goal he worked towards held that same immense moral magnificence. He nursed no private grudges, sought no petty personal gratification. Even in those final terrible days to come, when ignominy, torture, and the threat of more torture had laid bare every hidden weakness of his soul, he would say to the judges who hounded him for endless lists of names and confessions: “Do not marvel that I have told so few things. The purposes I held were few, and great.”

Nearly three full weeks slipped by before every scroll, manuscript, and carved folio of Bardo’s great library was packed tight in straw and loaded onto waiting carts. Instead of turning away and blocking out the noise of workmen’s boots and the crack of packing twine, Romola watched every step of the process. The hollow exhaustion that follows a shattering, violent grief breeds that dreamy, half-disbelieving fog where the worst of one’s pain feels almost unreal; and every evening, after the laborers trundled out the door, she would take her small hand-lamp and drift slow through the scattered straw and empty wooden crates, pausing at every bare pedestal, every well-loved marble bust laid prone on its side, driven by a bitter, hungry urge to prove to herself that there was good reason her love was dead, and the whole world had turned barren.
As the days wore on, she returned to that half-dismantled room evening after evening, no longer seeking proof of her loss, but because prodding that raw, throbbing grief tied to her father’s memory was the only living, feeling thing left in her empty affections. On the 23rd of December, she knew the very last crates would be carted away. She ran for the loggia at the roof of the house, determined not to miss that final sharp pang of watching the slow, heavy wheels of the last wagon creep across the bridge.

The day was overcast, drawing close to dusk. The Arno ran dark and shivering, its surface rippling grey and cold; the surrounding hills hung low and mournful, and Florence, girdled by its ancient stone towers, had the silent, tomb-like cast unbroken shadow casts over a city seen from on high. Santa Croce, where her father lay buried, was swallowed whole in that deep dusk, and slowly crawling across the bridge, then vanishing up the narrow winding street beyond, was the wagon’s pale white load, moving like a cruel, deliberate Fate carrying away her father’s lifelong, laborious hope to bury it in an unmarked, forgotten grave. Romola hardly felt like she was watching it herself: it felt as if her father, helpless under the cold imprisoning stone of his tomb, was the one conscious of the theft, and her hand could not reach through the earth to touch his and tell him he was not alone.

She stood long after the last wagon had vanished, heedless of the sharp winter cold, soothed by the gloom that wrapped around her like a mourning cloak, shutting out the jarring sound of other people’s joy. Then, all at once, the great bell in the Palazzo della Signoria’s tower boomed out a mighty peal: not the sharp, staccato clang of alarm, but an agitated, rolling peal of triumph. One after another, every bell in every tower across the city caught the vibration, and joined the roaring chorus. As the sound swelled higher and higher until the air itself felt thick with noise, tiny dancing flames, vibrating as if the sound itself had caught fire, burst out between the palace turrets and along the parapets of every girdling tower.

That sudden clang, that leaping firelight, struck Romola like a dozen sharp wounds. It felt to her like demons cheering the success of her husband’s treachery, and the total desolation of her life. Barely three weeks prior, she had drunk deep of the very sound of those same bells, giddy with Florence’s celebration, and heard in the city’s joy a prophecy of her own coming happiness. Now the crowd’s exultation felt nothing but cruel: she stood cut off from that common, loud-living Florence that fling its raucous joy out to stun the ears of anyone grieving or lonely. She would never clasp hands with gladness again, she thought, only with the people that hard, bright joy was always bound to forget. In her bitterness, all rejoicing felt like mockery: men shouted paeans with their souls heavy as lead, then glanced at their neighbors’ faces just to check if joy was even a real thing. Romola no longer believed in the happiness she had once thirsted for: it was a hateful, smiling, soft-handed thing with a narrow, selfish little heart.

She ran down from the loggia, hands pressed tight over her ears, and was hurrying across the antechamber when she started violently, coming face to face with her husband, who had been coming to find her. His step was light and springy, and there was a glow of uncommonly bright satisfaction about him.
“What! The noise was a little too much for you?” he said, noticing she had clamped her hands even tighter over her ears when she saw him. He took her gently by the wrist, drew her arm through his, and led her into the saloon ringed with frescoed dancing nymphs and fauns, before continuing. “Florence has gone quite mad with glee over its new Great Council, which is supposed to wipe every evil under the sun out of existence—even the very vice of merriment, if the friar has his way. No wonder you look stunned, my Romola, and you’re ice cold. You must never stay out on that windy loggia without wrapping up warm. I came to tell you I’ve been suddenly called to Rome on learned business for Bernardo Rucellai. I leave immediately, to meet my party at San Gaggio tonight so we can set out at dawn tomorrow. You need not trouble yourself at all for me, I had all my bags packed days ago. I shall be back before very long.”

He knew he could expect nothing from her now but quiet endurance of whatever he said or did. He did not even dare to lean in and kiss her forehead that evening, only pressed her hand briefly to his lips, and left. Tito told himself Romola was far more unforgiving than he had ever imagined: her love was not that soft, clinging instinct stronger than all judgment, which he was just now realizing was the true great charm of a wife. Still, this petrified coldness was far better than passionate, useless opposition. Her pride, and her knack for seeing exactly when resistance was pointless, had its conveniences for him.

The second the door clicked shut behind Tito, the frozen, immobile mask that settled over Romola’s face like frost whenever he was near shattered completely. Inwardly, she was far from quiet endurance: every day since the terrible fight that had split them irrevocably apart, she had been actively planning and preparing to carry out a purpose she had set for herself.

The first thing she did was call old Maso to her.
“Maso,” she said, her tone steady and unyielding, “we leave on our journey at dawn tomorrow. We can catch that first cloth caravan they say is lingering at San Piero before it departs north. Arrange for two mules tonight, be ready to set off with them at first light, and wait for me at Trespiano.”

She meant to take Maso with her as far as Bologna, then send him back with letters to her godfather Bernardo del Nero and Tito, telling them she was gone, and never intended to return. She had planned her departure to be perfectly secret, so that her broken love and her broken life would never be picked apart and gawked at by vulgar, curious eyes. Bernardo had been away at his villa, escaping political suspicion to tend to his beloved fields, and Romola had repaid the large debt Tito owed him without ever meeting him face to face. He did not even know the library had been sold, and would simply assume some sudden stroke of good fortune had let Tito raise the sum. Maso knew no more than that her intended journey was a secret, and to do exactly what she told him was the greatest pleasure left to him in his withered, wintry old age.

Romola had no intention of going to bed that night. When she had bolted her door, she carried her taper over to the carved, painted chest that held her wedding clothes. The white silk stitched with gold, the long flowing white veil, and the circlet strung with pearls lay there, and a great sob tore out of her as she stared at them: they looked exactly like a shroud for her dead happiness. Tucked in the tiny gold loop of the pearl circlet, a single pink sugarplum had lodged, one of the shower of sweet treats tossed at the crowd on their wedding day. Tito had spotted it first, and said it should stay there forever. In moments like this one, a sudden wave of memory swept her back to that time of perfect, unshaken trust, and she could feel the presence of the husband whose love had made the whole world as fresh and wonderful to her as it is to a small quiet child sitting in sun-dappled flowers: she could hear his soft gentle tones, see his kind eyes with no lie in them, and breathe again that vast freedom of the soul that comes from believing the person nearest you is better, greater than yourself. Those moments always brought hot, stinging tears: her loving woman’s heart ached with something akin to what a bereaved mother feels when tiny ghost fingers seem to lie warm on her breast, yet turn cold as marble when she presses her lips to the silent bed.

But there was something else tucked at the very bottom of the chest, dark and coarse, rolled up tight in a bundle. She tore her eyes away from the glimmering white and gold finery to the dark bundle, and the second her hands brushed the rough serge, her tears slowed and stopped. That coarse, unyielding texture pulled her fully back to the present, where all love and delight were long gone. She undid the thick white cord wrapped around it, and spread the bundle out on the table. It was the plain grey serge habit of a lay sister of the third order of Saint Francis, one who lived in the world but devoted herself entirely to pious deeds—a woman the Florentines called a Pinzochera. Romola meant to wear it as a disguise, and she determined to put it on at once, so that if she did need to rest a little before dawn, she would wake up already fully ready to leave.
She slipped off her soft black wool gown, and as she pushed her soft pale white arms into the harsh scratchy sleeves of the serge mantle, and the hard twisted rope girdle bit into her fingers when she tied it tight at her waist, she welcomed those rude, sharp sensations. They fit perfectly with her new scorn for that thing called pleasure that made men base—that clever trick of chasing selfish ease, of shrinking from hardship and strain while everyone else bore burdens too heavy to carry, which had become exactly the image of her husband in her mind. She gathered up her long loose hair, pulled it tight away from her face, twisted it into a hard heavy knot at the back of her head, and tied a square of black silk tight across her forehead and under her chin like a kerchief, then pulled the habit’s cowl up over her head.
She held the candle up to the mirror. Her disguise was perfect for anyone who had not lived very close to her. To her own eyes, she looked shockingly like her brother Dino: the full soft oval of her cheek only needed to waste away, her sad, heavy eyes only needed to sink a little further, and she would be indistinguishable from him. Was she growing more like him in any other way? Only in this: she understood now what had driven men like him to run away from all earthly joy forever, to dwell on images of sorrow rather than beauty and delight.

She did not linger at the mirror. She set about gathering every small relic of her father and mother that was too large to tuck into her small travelling wallet, to lay them all in the chest with her wedding clothes, and leave the whole locked chest in her godfather’s care once she was safely gone. First she laid in their painted portraits, then one by one every tiny trinket tangled with sacred memory went either into her wallet or the chest.

She paused. There was still one last thing she needed to strip away from herself, belonging to that old past she was turning her back on forever. She pressed her thumb and forefinger to her betrothal ring, but rested there, not pulling it off. Romola’s mind had been rushing full speed towards this act, the act of quitting the husband who had betrayed every last shred of her trust, of breaking an outward tie that no longer matched any inner bond of love left between them. But the strange power of outward symbols, which knit our whole lives together and give us an unshakable external identity no wavering feeling can shake, made that tiny small movement to take off her ring feel enormous. It gave her a vague, arresting sense that she was violently tearing her whole life clean in two: a presentiment that the sure, unhesitating impulse that had seemed to clear her path of all doubt might after all be a form of blindness, that human bonds carried something in them that could not be broken just because the illusions tied to them had shattered.

If the beloved Tito who had slipped that betrothal ring onto her finger that day was not in any real sense the same false Tito she now despised, why return the sign of their union to him? Why not keep it as a memento? That small act, which felt like tangible proof of both their identities, shook Romola in a way she could not explain. Half the truth we live amid only haunts us that way, making dull, wordless little thuds in our chest that never rise to become clear thought. But a passionate, loud voice speaking up inside her soon drowned out all those soft muffled murmurs.
“It cannot be! I will not be subject to him. He is false. I shrink from him. I despise him!”

She wrenched the ring off her finger, and dropped it on the table right next to the pen she meant to use to write her parting letters. Again she knew there could be no law for her but the law of her own affections. That tender, deep fellow-feeling for the people she loved, which had been the core of her whole religion up to this point, had made her patient despite her natural impetuousness, and would have made her heroic. But now all that strength was gone, or rather, it had turned into the strength of revulsion. She recoiled from Tito in exact measure to the fierceness of the young love and trust he had betrayed, of the lifelong devotion to her father he had trampled to commit that irredeemable crime. It felt as if every other motive had slipped away from her, leaving only the indignation and scorn that made her tear herself clean away from him.

She was not following any old precedent, or obeying any set of moral rules. The grand, severe stoical philosophy her father had made sure to teach her was familiar to her lips, and its lofty spirit had stirred echoes in her soul, but she had never used it, never needed it, as a rule to live by. She had endured and forborne because she loved: maxims that told her to feel less, not to cling too close lest the great onward march of the world jar her, had been as useless to her tender heart as they had been to her father’s lifelong yearning for just fame. She had never picked up theories to live by: she had simply felt strong in the strength of her love, and life without that energy was an entirely new, unwritten problem.

She planned to solve that problem in what seemed to her a very simple way. Her mind had never bowed to any sense of obligation apart from personal love and reverence; she had no keen sense of any other human ties, and the only thing she knew she must obey now was the instinct to sever herself completely from the man she no longer loved at all.

But her unshakable resolution came wrapped in phases of shifting, sharp anguish. Now that all the active work of preparing to leave was almost done, she lingered, putting off writing those irrevocable words that would cut her off from every small familiar thing in her world. The emotions of the past weeks rushed back over her all at once, taking possession even of her limbs. She meant to write, and her hand fell limp at her side. Bitter tears spilled now for the delusion that had blighted her young years, tears nothing like the soft sobs of remembered happiness she had shed looking at the pearl circlet and its trapped pink sugarplum. Now she felt a hot, stinging shame at the cruel words of ignominy she had spat at Tito in their fight: “Have you robbed some one else who is not dead?” To have been forced to say those words, to hurl them at her own husband, felt like a degradation of her whole life. Harsh, cruel speech between two people who once loved each other is hideous in memory, like the sight of something once great and beautiful sunk into rags and vice.

That heart-wrenching comparison between the bright past and the desolate present pressed on Romola until it turned into raw physical sensation: she felt numb to everything but the throbbing of her own heart, and craved some hard, sharp contact to ground her. She ran her hands tight along the rough knotted cord hanging from her waist. She jumped to her feet and seized the rough carved lid of the chest: was there nothing else left to tuck inside? No. She slammed the lid shut, pressed her palm down hard on the rough carvings, and locked it.

Then she remembered she had one last piece of equipment to complete her disguise as a Pinzochera. The large leather scarsella purse, stuffed with small coins, had to hang from her girdle on one side (her florins and small jewels, gifts from her godfather and cousin Brigida, were safely tucked hidden inside the lining of her serge mantle), and on the other side she hung her rosary.

As she tied that rosary to her waist, it never crossed her mind that more than just the clothes would be needed to pass as a devout lay sister: her whole proud, upright bearing and her sad, sharp gaze was nothing like the meek women who kept their eyes bowed and their lips moving in silent, constant prayer. Her inexperience kept her from picturing all the small distant details of the journey ahead, and her proud courage shut out all forebodings of danger or insult. She did not know any other Florentine woman had ever done exactly what she was setting out to do: unhappy wives often took refuge with friends, or fled to a convent, she knew, but both paths were impossible for her. She had carved a new life for herself: she would travel all the way to Venice, to the most learned woman in the world, Cassandra Fedele, and ask her how a woman of education could support herself in a lonely, honourable life there.

She was not daunted by the practical difficulties of the road, or the dark uncertainty waiting for her at the end. Her life could never be happy again, but it must not, could not, be ignoble. In a pathetic, tender mix of childish romance and hard womanly grief, the stoical philosophy that had nothing to do with this great decisive act had found a place in her imagination of the future: so far as she could picture her solitary, loveless

Romola was roused at dawn by a soft tap at her bedchamber door. Thin, frigid winter light seeped through the window slats, and there stood old Maso, come to collect the travelling wallet he had stowed for her the night before. The old servant started so hard when she pulled the door open he nearly dropped his staff: where he had always known the soft, graceful line of her figure framed by the bright spill of her golden hair, there was only the thick heavy folds of a rough grey pilgrim’s mantle, her pale face half-shrouded in the deep shadow of a dark cowl.
“All is well, Maso,” Romola said, forcing her voice to stay calm and steady, to ease the old man’s surprise. “Here is the wallet, all packed. Go on slowly, and I shall not be far behind you. Once you pass the city gates you may linger at a gentler pace; I will likely catch up to you long before you reach Trespiano.”
She shut the door after him, and her fingers closed around the small brass key she had slipped from her father’s old oak casket the very last thing before she slept. It was the original key to the little painted tabernacle Tito had forgotten to hurl into the Arno years before — it had lodged, as tiny, easily missed objects often do, in the stitched corner of the embroidered scarlet pouch he wore slung at his belt with his formal purple tunic. Romola had found it there months after their wedding, tucked it away without a word, never using it, but clinging to the quiet satisfaction that it was hers to call on whenever she needed it.
The small cabinet that held the tabernacle had been moved to the room’s edge, set close enough to the window that the pale dawn light spilled soft across its painted panels, clear enough for Romola, who knew every inch of the art by heart, to make out every detail: the triumphant Bacchus, heavy with grape clusters, his thyrsus wound thick with vines, clasping the crowned Ariadne to his chest; winged cupids showering rose petals, gilded wreathed ewers, dolphins with sly, knowing eyes leaping through ripples of painted blue sea, all framed in a border of wild summer blooms that looked like the walls of a small enclosed paradise bower.
On this cold, bleak morning, when she woke determined to walk away from every part of her old life, the once-beloved images felt far more cruel and hollow than they ever had. They were no gentle memento of lost joy, but a lying, glittering screen that had hidden years of betrayal from her. Foolish Ariadne, gazing up at her divine lover with all her faith, as if that bright face framed in hyacinth curls that twisted like vine tendrils held no secret that would leave her abandoned and alone on a rocky shore. “That girl would not recognise herself now,” Romola thought to herself, “wrapped in this coarse grey, tramping the cold roads in the dark of winter.”
She picked up her small hand mirror, glanced at her face for one last check, and flinched, setting it down fast. The sight of herself framed by the cowl, pale and sharp, sent a shiver through her. That old, once-disdained dread pricked at her skin: the fear that she might finally end up one of those raving fanatics, those weeping cloistered nuns she had scorned from girlhood, surrendering every part of her own sharp, curious mind to a blind, mindless superstition.
She jammed the key into the tabernacle’s lock, twisting it fast, yanking the tiny door open. She grabbed the carved crucifix inside without daring to look at it, fumbled a length of thin cord through the small iron ring at its top, hung it around her neck, and tucked it deep into the folds of her mantle, pressed to her breast. For Dino’s sake, she whispered to herself, honoring her brother’s final dying wish.
Only two brief letters remained to write, to send back with Maso when he turned for Bologna. The first, addressed to Tito, was spare, no softening words:
> “Tito, my love for you is dead, and so the part of me that belonged to you as your wife is dead too. Do not waste your effort invoking Florentine law to drag me back: it will bring you no joy, no comfort. The Romola you married can never return. I need explain nothing to you after the hard truths I spoke the last long night we argued. If you mistook those words for transient, angry spite, you will know now they were the sign of an irreversible end.
> “I ask that you send my bridal chest, gifted to me by my godfather, to him at his home. It holds my wedding clothes, my parents’ portraits, and every small memento I kept of them.”
She slipped her wedding ring inside the folded letter, sealed it, and scrawled Tito’s name across the front.
The second letter was for Bernardo del Nero, her godfather:
> “Dearest second father, — If I could have stayed and brought any comfort to your life, I would never have wandered so far from Florence. But I cannot stay here any longer. Do not ask anyone to hunt for me, and if you ever loved my father Baldassarre, do all you can to stop others from seeking me out. My life in Florence had grown unendurable, and I cannot bear to speak of the reasons to a single soul. Help me hide what has become of me in quiet, no prying eyes, no gossip. I have asked Tito to send my bridal chest to you: when you open it, all that I cannot say will be clear. Give every one of my mother’s old belongings to my cousin Brigida, and tell her I beg her forgiveness for not stopping to say my goodbyes.
> Farewell. The greatest gift I carry with me out of this city is the memory of your lifelong kindness, and my gratitude will never fade.
> Romola.”
She tucked both letters next to the crucifix, pressed to her heart, and realized every last loose end was tied. She was ready to leave.
No one else in the household had stirred yet. She drifted down the stairs as quiet as a grey phantom, stepping out onto the flagstone street that still lay empty of passersby. Her heart thudded so hard she could hear it in her ears, but the firm, steady weight of her boots on the broad paving stones brought her a sharp, bright sense of release: the chains she had carried for years, forged out of blind faith and misplaced loyalty, were unshackled at last. All her dread of being caught, of someone stopping her, pushed grief far from her mind, and when she crossed Ponte Rubaconte, she barely even registered the distant spires of Santa Croce rising ahead. The thin yellow streak of dawn splitting the grey sky was growing broader by the minute, and if she did not hurry, the city would wake, and curious eyes would start turning toward the tall cowled stranger hurrying alone down the road.
Her intended route led her straight along Borgo Pinti, then parallel to the outer city walls to the Porta San Gallo, the gate she needed to exit Florence. That path carried her right through the Piazza di Santa Croce, but she kept her gaze fixed to the paving at her feet, walking as fast and steady as she could, never daring to glance toward the great stone church. The thought of strangers recognizing her, staring, gossiping over her private sorrows, digging into the shame Tito had left her to bear, made her shrink as if from torture. No one peered out of their windows to notice her, though: tall, cowled, her head held with a quiet proud tilt, her grey mantle swishing around her ankles, she looked like any other devout pilgrim heading out to the hill shrines at dawn. The streets were still clear of early traffic, and when she reached the Porta San Gallo, the guards were so busy bickering with a crowd of peasant farmers over the toll for panniers of eggs and root vegetables that she slipped through the gate completely unchallenged.
She was out. Past the last huddled houses of the Borgo, the fringe of the city fell away, and the wide open sky stretched over her head. She had stepped into her new life: a life of loneliness, of hardship, of quiet endurance, but unshakably free. She would never again feel the pressure of Tito’s soft, hated lips against her cheek, never again breathe the stifling air of his lies, his cold calculated selfishness that had been slowly smothering her own spirit for years. The harsh chill of the wintry air, the bare leafless trees lining the road, the dark sombre hills rolling out before her, were welcome for their honesty, no trace left of the glittering false gods of joy and beauty she had worshipped for so long, and now renounced forever.
Then the sun burst over the eastern horizon all at once, flooding the road with golden light, throwing long sharp shadows across the path. There is no moment that makes the dawn light feel more like a tangible divine presence, stirring all the quiet, wordless feelings we carry deepest in our bones, than that exact second when it wakes every sleeping shadow. A soft, reverent awe settled over Romola as she stared at her own long, shrouded shadow stretching out ahead of her, a spectre she could never outrun. Until that point she had met no one on the road but the occasional peasant leading a mule, and the many gentle curves of the flat lane had hidden Maso from her sight. But when she had passed the small village of Pietra and set foot on rising ground, she lifted the brim of her cowl to peek ahead — and dropped it fast. Two black-robed monks were barely a few yards away, walking downhill straight toward her.
The edge of her cowl had formed a little awning over her brow that blocked everything above her line of sight, and for minutes she had been staring at nothing but the gilded path and her own long shadow, never seeing them coming. The false disguise she had hidden under now filled her with sharp unease: they might ask her for a pious password, a prayer or pilgrimage ritual she knew nothing of, and unmask her. She walked onward, her face arranged in the blank, faraway look of a pilgrim lost in devout thought, until the hems of their black robes passed her by, and they vanished down the hill behind her.
The encounter had left her heart thudding unpleasantly. The shame of her false piety, the discomfort of this carefully constructed lie, burned bright under her skin. But the monks were well on their way now, and she spotted a broad flat stone set beside a tall cypress tree growing out of a grassy bank, and sank down to rest, yielding to the quiet urge to pause for breath after the shock. She turned her back to Florence, refusing to glance toward the city until the monks were completely out of sight, lifted the brim of her cowl again, and spotted Maso and the mules bobbing slowly along far ahead, moving at a leisurely pace while the old man waited for her to catch up.
For the first time in years, she was free. She was alone. No obligations, no lies, no one to answer to. The open road stretched out before her, and she was entirely her own.

That sudden journey Tito Melema would soon take to Rome — the very trip that would remove so many barriers standing in the way of Romola’s later unplanned departure from his side — had been decided on entirely out of the blue, at a supper feast only the evening before.

Tito had set off toward that supper in the highest of spirits. The food was sure to be delicate, the wine of exceptional vintage, and the company the most distinguished Florence had to offer; for the venue was the Selva, or Orto de’ Rucellai, the celebrated Rucellai Gardens, and his host Bernardo Rucellai was the very model of a Florentine grandee. Even his family name carried a softly symbolic history: traced to its root, it called to mind the tiny lichen popularly known as *orcella* or *roccella*, which grew on the rocky isles of the Greek archipelago and the Canary Islands, drinking so much sunlight into its thin stems and knobby little heads that it could later release that stored light as a rich reddish-purple dye that delighted the eye of every man who saw it. Nearly a hundred years before Bernardo’s time, a merchant who brought the closely guarded secret of this dye, called *oricello*, home to Florence from the Levant had earned his family a vast fortune, and the pleasantly evocative surname Oricellari, or Roccellari, which softened over time on Tuscan tongues into Rucellai.

Bernardo Rucellai stood out as the brightest star on that rich purple family background, adding distinction after distinction to the name. He had married the sister of Lorenzo de’ Medici, and the wedding feast held for the pair was the most lavish display of Florentine finery a generation could remember. For these and other acts of state service, he had been sent as ambassador to France and Venice, and elected Gonfaloniere of Florence. He had not only built himself a grand new palace, but finished the striking black-and-white marble facade of the church of Santa Maria Novella. He had planted his garden full of rare, exotic trees, and consecrated the ground as a classic cultural landmark by hosting the regular meetings of the Platonic Academy, left adrift and orphaned by the death of Lorenzo de Medici. He had written a groundbreaking, erudite topographical volume on ancient Rome, collected a vast hoard of antiquities, and composed Latin prose of near-perfect purity. Even the simplest summary of his life reads like a glowing epitaph, at the end of which the Greek and Ausonian muses could be forgiven for tearing out their hair in disbelief that nature could ever combine so many virtues in a single set of human bones.

Tito’s invitation to the supper had come through Lorenzo Tornabuoni, with an undercurrent of gravity that would have told him the gathering was political even if Florence had not been in the grip of unprecedented public unrest. As it was, Tito felt certain that important party aims would be advanced over plates of delicate stewed fish and mellow old Greek wine; Bernardo Rucellai was not merely an influential noble, he was one of the secretive elect Twenty who had held the full reins of Florentine governance for the past three weeks. This certainty put Tito in even better spirits as he made his way down the Via della Scala to the famous garden grounds. Without that assurance, he might have found himself worrying the distinguished company would be as dull as it was elite; he had sat through more than a few tedious suppers in the Rucellai gardens before, especially the stuffy Platonic philosophy gatherings where he was expected not just to nod along to a fully mapped out theory of the universe, but to sit for hours listening to it be expounded from the very origin of all things to their final, perfect fulfillment in the speaker’s own treatise.

The night was dark, and passersby could only make out the outline of Tito’s tall, graceful form, wrapped tight in a warm mantle, when he crossed the occasional pool of light cast by a lamp strung before a street corner shrine to the Virgin Mary. Anyone lingering between those pools of light would have noticed that Tito was followed at a steady, unobtrusive distance by a very different figure: thickset, elderly, clad in a plain serge tunic and scuffed felt hat. Most onlookers would have dismissed the pairing as mere chance, for the streets were crowded with pedestrians that hour, but when Tito paused at the gate to the Rucellai gardens, the figure behind him stopped too.

The small side *sportello* door set in the main gate had been left ajar by the servant, who had been distracted answering a guest’s question and not yet latched it after the last arrival. Tito slipped through quickly, gave his name to the servant, and wandered on past the evergreen bushes that glinted like polished dark metal in the torchlight. His follower slipped in right behind him.

“Your name?” the servant asked sharply.

“Baldassarre Calvo,” came the immediate, unhesitating answer.

“You are no guest. All the guests have already entered.”

“I belong to Tito Melema, who just went past. I was told to wait for him inside the gardens.”

The servant hesitated. “I was ordered to admit no one but guests. Are you a servant of Messer Tito?”

“No, friend. I am no servant. I am a scholar.”

There are men who, faced with a quiet, unshakable tone of that sort, will let you pass even if you claim to be a wild buffalo, no further questions asked. The porter stepped aside at once, Baldassarre crossed the threshold, and heard the door clank shut and chain behind him before he vanished into the shadow of the glistening bushes.

That steady, unflappable composure marked a staggering shift in Baldassarre since his last face-to-face meeting with Tito, when his dagger had snapped clean in two mid-strike. The change had revealed itself in a startling, sudden wave. The moment Tito’s shadow had passed the opening of the hovel on the Bogoli hill as he left to head home, Baldassarre had been left sitting in the trembling aftershock familiar to anyone who has ever expended all their strength in a burst of rage: a state where physical helplessness often pairs with an almost supernatural clarity of thought, as if the spent passion has burned away all the fog in the mind to leave everything sharp and unobscured. He could not stand or walk away just then; his limbs felt numb, his hands shook, a cold chill ran through his bones. But in that bodily lassitude, he was surrounded not by the usual dim, fading half-memories, but by perfectly clear images of the past, unspooling in an unbroken line through all the long years of his life that had seemed a slow, cruel preparation for the bitterness of Tito’s betrayal.

For several minutes he was so absorbed in the flood of recollections he did not even register that his mind had healed, that the fog was gone. But when that wave of clear memory traveled all the way back to the scene just played out in the hovel, he realized exactly where he was, and remembered Monna Lisa and Tessa. That smiling stranger who had visited the hovel was Tessa’s secret husband — the same man who had a legal wife, Romola, in the house on the Via de’ Bardi. It was time to pick up the broken halves of his dagger and leave, leave no trace of the weakness he had just shown; to hide his feebleness was the only form of power he had left.

He leaned down to grab the broken dagger shards, and his gaze fell on the large manuscript volume open on the dirt floor beside him, a tattered odd volume of Pausanias. Moonlight spilled across its pages, and he could make out the large Greek lettering at the head of the chapter: *ΜΕΣΣΕΝΙΚΑ. ΚΒ′*. Only an hour or two earlier, he had stared hopelessly at that exact same page, and the characters had meant no more to him than random black weather stains on a stone wall. But in that moment, they were magic sigils that conjured up an entire world for him. That single moonbeam falling on the letters had called the whole of ancient Messenia to his mind, and its generations-long struggle to throw off Spartan oppression. He snatched up the book; the moonlight was too faint to read further by, but he did not need to. He knew that chapter by heart, and read it inwardly, word for word. He saw the entire people of Messenia stone the traitor Aristocrates to death, drag his body outside their borders to rot unburied, and erect a pillar carved with verses that bore witness to how time always brings justice home to the unjust. The lines echoed inside him, waking thousands of half-lost memories he had grieved as irretrievable. He forgot his old age, he nearly shouted out loud. The light had come back, that mother of all knowledge and joy! In that rush of exultation, his numb limbs flooded with strength. He jumped to his feet, broken dagger and book clutched tight, and stepped out under the broad, bright moonlight.

The air was sharp and frosty, but Baldassarre felt no chill. He only felt the glow of regained power thrumming under his skin. He wandered the open stretches of that high ground, pausing to look down on Florence sleeping dark under the watch of its guardian mountains, its domes and towers faint in the silver light, the Arno a pale gleam far below, the valley stretching out toward the distant snow-capped peaks, and for a moment he felt master of all of it. That rare, sharp sense of mental command that visits all men in moments of perfect lucidity was amplified tenfold for him after the long weeks and months where his memory had been nothing but a hollow, dim awareness that something vital was missing. That city, which had once felt like a confusing, endless labyrinth to his fogged mind, was now a set of facts he could arrange, bend fully to his purposes. He knew cities again, his old sharp worldly experience was all back, and he drank in the keen delight of holding every name, every secret, every old story in the full grasp of his working mind, like a man stepping over the threshold of a vast long-lost inheritance he never expected to claim.

But presiding over all that rushing, eager wealth of recovered memory was one single, dark unshakable End: a dark deity hidden in the innermost chamber of his soul, who had only been out of sight while the sacrifices for his altar were being prepared. When the first rush of triumph at regaining his full mental power faded, all his thoughts centered on Tito. That fair, slippery viper could not escape him now. Justice, after a long struggle, had given him the means to pierce that soft, selfish heart that had never once known a spark of genuine tenderness for another, through the sensitive, self-serving fibres that could be lanced through by the sharp point of anguish. The man who bowed to no law, no moral right, would bow to Pain, the great lord of all mortals. He could dig out every secret Tito had ever buried, and he already knew more than a few of them. The failed dagger strike, which had felt like total frustration at the time, had actually been the first step to victory. Twice before, partial flashes of his returning memory had come after sudden shocks: once when he had fought off a rabid dog that attacked him, once when he had nearly drowned in the Arno and scrambled up a jagged rock to save himself. But what if this new light died out too, and the dreary blank fog rolled back over his mind before he could act? The fear made him terrified that this long wakeful night on the hill would burn through his recovered strength too fast, so he hunted out a sheltered nook, curled up against a heap of warm decomposing straw from a nearby garden, and fell asleep.

When he opened his eyes again, it was full daylight. The first moments were thick with disorientation: he was a man with two identities. Which one had he woken up as? The dim, addled beggar whose memories were the sad, tattered heirloom of a fallen great man, or the sharp, whole scholar he had been the night before? It was the latter. Every event of the night came flooding back to him: the moment he recognized the Greek characters on the Pausanias page, the tide of names and facts surging back into his mind, that wide view of the city that had filled him with the wild, ecstatic wonder of a Maenad waking at sunrise on a mountain top.

He picked up the book again, read a few lines, and found he could recall the rest of the chapter without even looking. A single name called up a whole tapestry of associated deeds, a single reference to an event linked back perfectly to every person tangled up in it. He read stories of terrible, unforgivable crimes, and stories of guilty men who had seemed to triumph utterly, who had found sanctuary in hidden places, whose baseness wore armor that deflected every blow of justice. But none of that mattered. Even if baseness triumphed everywhere else, even if it hoarded all the goods of the world and held the keys to hell itself, it could never triumph over the unyielding, white-hot hatred it had itself awakened in its victim. No torture Tito could ever devise would be worse than the torture of submitting to his false, patronizing smile. Baldassarre felt the indestructible, independent force of that supreme emotion, which knows no fear, needs no other motive, and is itself an ever-burning fire that consumes all other desires. By that full morning light, when he confirmed his memory was still sharp, no fog creeping in to blur its edges, all his joy was nothing but the hungry hope of vengeance.

From that sunrise all the way to the evening he slipped through the Rucellai garden gate, Baldassarre had moved cautiously, quietly, inquiring after Tito’s position, his connections, his daily habits, trailing him almost every day. He did not want to arouse Tito’s alarm. He wanted to strike at the exact moment that smug, lucky favorite of blind fortune was at the peak of his confident ease, surrounded by the most powerful men in Florence, the very men whose good opinion he depended on to climb higher. He did not crave petty personal recognition or small retribution for his own sake; he wanted to find the sharpest possible edge of public disgrace and shame that could pierce a selfish smiler like Tito, to send a shock of sudden dread rippling through his very marrow. He lived rough, ate sparingly, and spent most of his last remaining coins buying a new sharp poniard. His hunger and thirst were not for fine things, but for a perfectly exact, exquisite vengeance. He avoided anyone he knew was close to Tito: he never stepped foot in Nello’s barber shop, even though he saw Tito visit there constantly, and he turned down side alleys to avoid crossing paths with Piero di Cosimo. He trembled at the thought of frustration, most of all at the thought his memory might slip away again before he got his chance. Twice, he had felt a terrible mental dizziness, where the distinct shapes of things in his mind seemed to blur and fade. Once he had tried to slip into the Palazzo Vecchio and make his way to the council chamber where Tito was attending a meeting, and had failed, his head spinning too hard to move forward. But now, on this evening, standing in the torchlit bushes of the Rucellai gardens, he knew his occasion had finally come.

The moment Tito stepped across the pavilion threshold, his sharp eyes scanned the assembled guests and confirmed his quiet guess: this was no casual, leisurely feast, but a political gathering, engineered to bind party loyalties over good wine and richer food. The Florentines of 1492 held a firm, unshakable belief that a well-filled stomach softened reserve and sharpened conviction far better than any stuffy council speech, that the warm ease of after-supper banter would clarify a man’s own political loyalties with a clarity no hungry, uninvited person could ever achieve. Though Florence was by and large a sober, frugal republic, the Rucellai household was one of the rare few Florentine families that kept a lavish, open table, welcoming guests to a spread fine enough to rival the banquets of Roman nobility. No heavy philosophical theses were going to be forced on anyone that night, of that Tito was certain. The venerable marble bust of Plato over the mantel could look down unmolested, as could the modest frescoed figures of the cardinal virtues lining the walls.

That same bust of Plato had presided over far more rarefied, transcendental gatherings for decades, carried over from Lorenzo de’ Medici’s villa after his death, when the meetings of the famous Platonic Academy moved to these very gardens. Every thirteenth of November, the reputed anniversary of Plato’s death, it had gazed down from under a woven crown of laurel at a hand-picked circle of humanists and scholars: Pico della Mirandola, once a wild, curly-haired young prodigy who shocked all Rome with his unorthodox religious theses, later humbled by the realization that the vast universe was far more astonishing than his own cleverness; gentle, tireless Marsilio Ficino, raised from childhood to be a custodian of Platonism, fed on the master’s works so exclusively his mind grew soft and saturated as overripe fruit; Angelo Poliziano, the age’s greatest living literary genius, a poet whose lines still thrum with warm, living blood centuries later; even old Leon Battista Alberti, that universal man of robust, unparalleled intellect, equal parts architect, scientist, inventor, and poet, decades older than the other three. And beneath the bust’s gaze sat dozens more unnamed, uncelebrated diligent workers whose quiet labours form an unrecognized part of our modern inheritance, no less valuable than the ploughing and sowing of generations of anonymous farmers whose fields we walk today.

Bernardo Rucellai, their host, was a man who would have held a distinguished place in that old Academy even before he became its patron. He was barely forty-four, still in the prime of life, with a haughty, carefully dignified bearing, famed for his immaculate, perfect Latin that no visiting German scholar could ever coax out of him in casual speech. He greeted Tito with more open favour than usual, and seated him between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, two polished, accomplished young loyalists of the old Medicean party.

The opening chatter was light as the rose-scented water in the brass hand-washing bowls passed around the table, rings glinting on pale white fingers under the glow of the wax tapers, the fresh, soft scent of newly imported French white damask hanging in the air. The joke of the hour was a familiar Florentine one: someone asked what Dante’s archetypal old 13th century Florentine, leather belt and bone clasps and all, would make of waking to see silver forks laid out on the table before him. Every guest agreed with laughter that their medieval ancestors would be endlessly bewildered by the small luxuries their descendants took for granted.

As the appetizers arrived—delicate slivers of liver cooked to such perfection they dissolved the moment they touched the tongue, whetting appetites for the heavier main courses—guests paused to admire the enameled silver inlays set at the centre of each brass serving dish, chatting about the famous confetti platter, a masterpiece by Antonio Pollajuolo that a wealthy Pope had lured away from his native Florence to work on grander commissions in Rome.

“Ah, I remember Pollajuolo flying into a rage once over all this precious metalwork,” said Niccolò Ridolfi, a middle-aged man with the lazy, unassuming ease that comes of a lifetime of unassailable noble rank. “He threw down his chisel and swore to stick to painting, saying, ‘The artist who casts his work in gold and silver is melting his own brains down into the pot with the rest.’”

“He may well have spoken a true prophecy,” said Giannozzo Pucci, swirling his wine gently. “If this war with Pisa drags on, and the revolt spreads to our other subject towns, we won’t just be losing our silver dishes. I half expect Pollajuolo’s silver saints around the altar of San Giovanni to be melted down into coin one day, to feed the war chest.”

“The Frate is already prepping the people for that,” put in Tornabuoni. “He’s telling them God cares far more about full bellies than silver crucifixes, that the church’s true adornment is holiness and brotherly love, not precious gems and metal.”

“A very convenient doctrine for war finance, as every mercenary captain worth his salt has known for a hundred years,” Rucellai said dryly. “But politics come after the sweetmeats, Lorenzo, when we’ve drunk enough wine to wash that heavy stuff down. It’s far too solid to digest alongside roast and boiled meat.”

Ridolfi agreed, launching into a story about the late, irreverent poet Luigi Pulci, who’d once joked that all opinion corrupted the saliva, hence why people started using pepper, and only perfect scepticism left the palate untainted. He recalled Lorenzo de’ Medici teasing Pulci that their friend Matteo Franco, the self-proclaimed sceptic, used more hot sauce than anyone at the table, and Pulci zinged right back that it was because Franco’s only real unshakable belief was in the immortality of his own verses, a man stubborn enough to debate a preaching friar for hours about the exact layout of the paving stones in the bottomless pit. They all agreed that Pulci, for all his sharp, cutting wit that sliced through every pretension, was as gentle as a butterfly, and as temperate as a child no matter how rhapsodic he grew about fine food and drink.

The supper dragged on slow and sumptuous: roasted and boiled meats gave way to tender capon, game birds, and the centerpiece dish of the night, a peacock prepared according to the ancient Roman gourmet Apicius’s recipe, its full iridescent feathers left intact so it looked as if it was peacefully roosting alive on the platter. The official carver, a trusted senior servant, carefully turned the great bird on its back to slice the tender breast for each guest, no small feat of skill. Hardly any diner dared turn down the expensive novelty. Tito quoted a line from Horace as he scattered his tiny slice across his plate, Rucellai delivered a learned disquisition on the exorbitant price of peacock eggs in ancient Rome without taking a single bite, and Ridolfi held a mouthful on his fork to tell a joke about a thrifty Sienese host who dressed up a wild goose with clipped beak and webbed feet, boiled in its own feathers, to pass it off as a peahen for a grand party. Barely any of the peacock was eaten, but every man there felt the quiet, proud satisfaction of knowing he was part of that tiny, wealthy elite that could afford such absurd, unnecessary luxuries—all the more meaningful at a time when Fra Girolamo was railing from the pulpit against rich men hoarding finery while the poor starved.

Outside the warm, glowing pavilion, in the cold dark of the gardens, Baldassarre wandered in ever-tightening circuits among the cypress trees. He paused beneath a window, pressing his face close to the glass, to stare in at the scene of light and laughter. He could see Tito, gesturing easily, laughing without a care, his voice rising and falling through the merry confusion of overlapping speeches. Baldassarre’s mind was strung tight as a bowstring, steeling himself for the moment he could burst in and confront his betrayer, a savage, cruel satisfaction curling in his chest at the sight of Tito’s unknowing, happy ease. But none of the men inside, clustered under the branching wax tapers, their goblets glinting, had any idea the pale, feral face watched them from the dark beyond the light. The glow of candle and hearth makes as impenetrable a curtain as any pitch black midnight.

Back inside, the chatter grew less trivial, sharper, more political. Every last man at the table had the firebrand Friar Girolamo’s words and schemes at the front of his mind, and before the stewed fish course was cleared and the favourite sweets were brought out, his name slipped to the surface of the conversation, and politics took over the table, Rucellai’s earlier gentle ban forgotten. As long as the servants lingered, it was harmless gossip: how the first votes for the new Great Council had gone in the Palazzo della Signoria, how overbearing Francesco Valori was, acting as if his austere piety gave him the right to dictate every decision, and how obvious it was that Soderini’s pro-Council speeches were exactly aligned with the sermons the Frate was preaching in the Duomo.

“I say the Frate is the one running the show, not Soderini,” said Ridolfi, leaning back in his chair. “Soderini is nothing but his mouthpiece.”

Rucellai shook his head. “No, Niccolò. The Frate is sharp, sure, and knows how to use events to his own ends, but Soderini has decades of political experience, has studied the Venetian Council for years. He did not need a monk to tell him how to structure the Great Council. Soderini loads the cannon, I will grant you that, but Fra Girolamo brings the powder and lights the fuse. The people are his, and the people are starting to run all of us.”

Once all the servants had cleared out, leaving only bottles of wine passing between the guests, Tornabuoni leaned forward, and revealed he had met with the Frate privately, in his own cell—a favour that even Francesco Valori rarely got. He told the table he had gone in as a sceptic, and left convinced that the only safe path for the old Medicean faction was to throw their weight behind the popular party and the Frate. If the Frate’s government fell, the vicious Arrabbiati faction, the bitter enemies of both Mediceans and the Frate’s Piagnoni followers, would seize power, and they would tear every last Medici supporter apart, half the city exiled in a week.

All the men around the table murmured their full agreement. Tornabuoni laid out their quiet, long-game plan: pretend to be loyal followers of the new popular government, bide their time, until the natural restlessness of Florentine politics swung power back to a single family, the Medici, not the fantasy of a perfectly equal, selfless republic the Frate was preaching. Ridolfi agreed, blunt and plainspoken, saying he was far too old to pretend he had ever changed his loyalties, they could all just stay quiet, lie low like sleeping old hounds, no need to bark when it would only get them in trouble. The new Great Council would be formally voted into being tomorrow, he said, but it would go the way every other government ever had, either people snarl at each other, or they grovel before whoever is strongest. Bernardo Rucellai, ever the dignified, intellectual sceptic, agreed that allying with the popular party was the most prudent course, even if he did not fully endorse any of the current factions, all of which were full of crude, untested ideas.

As the wine flowed freer, the great table broke off into small clusters of twos and threes, chatting privately. Tornabuoni and Pucci pulled their chairs close to Tito, their backs to the rest of the room, and laid out their secret offer. They told Tito he was the perfect man for their covert missions, far better than any of their other known loyalists, who were too obvious, their prejudices too easy to spot. They wanted him to travel to Rome as their secret agent, to negotiate with Piero de’ Medici and the French nobles, using his reputation as a wandering scholar as perfect, unassailable cover. With his charm and his wit, they told him, he could rise farther than he ever dreamed, even make his way to a cardinal’s hat one day if he chose to take holy orders.

Tito laughed, flattered even if he saw straight through their exaggerated flattery. He said he would be happy to settle for the generous income of a couple of abbeys without needing to shave his head, for the moment. They went on to tell him he should win the trust of the followers of San Marco, the Frate’s circle, once he returned from Rome, to gather intelligence on their plans. Then they added that they needed a second, completely unknown agent to infiltrate the Arrabbiati, the violent secretive rival faction, to spy on their plots, since they were far more dangerous than the Frate’s open, idealistic followers.

Tito did not hesitate for a second. He said no other agent was needed. He could easily win over Dolfo Spini, the hot-headed leader of the Arrabbiati, become his closest confidant, and know all their plans before even Spini himself knew what he intended to do. A thrill of exaltation shot through him, sudden and bright. The game before him was far bigger than he had ever dreamed. He was not just going to be a favoured hanger-on any longer—he could play all three factions against each other, hold quiet, unacknowledged power in his own hands. His status as a foreigner, his complete indifference to all their ideological loyalties and old family feuds, that had once seemed like a weakness, was now his greatest strength. All the small lies and compromises he had made since he first arrived in Florence had slowly stripped away every last moral scruple that might have held him back, and now the triple deceit he was about to embark on looked like nothing more than a brilliant, easy game, with every prize Florence had to offer already within his grasp.

When Romola sank onto the lichen-crusted stone beneath the gnarled old cypress on the slopes of Bogoli, every breath of the morning wove around her a new feeling of unaccustomed freedom. She had slipped out of Florence before the dawn, wrapped in the rough wool of a pilgrim’s disguise, putting the labyrinthine streets, the weight of her dead father’s empty library, and the treachery of her husband behind her.
