By this hour, Tito was already miles along the road to Siena, every hoofbeat of his horse widening the gulf between them, while every step she took turned her further away from the life that had curdled into bitterness. Below the roadside, the land fell away into a green hollow, and the sombre bulk of the distant mountains hung softened in the haze, no human face, no familiar wall, no old obligation pressing in on her. For the first time in her life, she felt herself alone under the wide sky, answering to no one but her own will.

A voice spoke so close to her ear she could feel the faint stir of breath against her hair.
“You are Romola de’ Bardi, wife of Tito Melema.”

She knew that voice. It had boomed from the pulpit of the Duomo, it had vibrated through the crowd at the street sermons, it had pricked at her conscience more than once when she had thought to ignore it. She did not turn her head, did not lift her gaze from the wild thyme at her feet. Dread coiled cold in her chest even as she raged at the intrusion: some black-robed friar, daring to track her down, daring to break in on the solitude she had fought so hard to claim. She shook as if the very Destiny men painted as a crowned, distant goddess had stepped down from the clouds to grasp her with flesh and blood fingers.

“You flee Florence in disguise,” the voice went on, unyielding. “I hold a command from God to stop you. You are not permitted to run.”

Anger flared hot in her throat. Still she would not turn, would not let him see the resentment burning in her face at the way his gaze picked her apart. “What right have you to speak to me, or to bar my way?”

“The right of a messenger. You wear this pilgrim’s garb with no pilgrim’s purpose, only to hide your true name, to slip free of the lot God has laid at your feet, so you may carve out a new life ruled by no will but your own. I was shown you before you passed by this spot. My daughter, you must go back.”

Every word stoked her rebellion higher, and the very fact that she felt shaken, that some part of her already wavered, made her cling harder to her resolve. “I will not return. I acknowledge no right of priests or monks to meddle in my choices. You have no power over me.”

“I know you were raised to scorn obedience,” the voice said, soft but unshakable. “It is not this poor monk that commands you. It is the truth. You cannot outrun it. Obey, and it will lead you. Resist, and it will hang around your neck like an iron chain to drag you for all your days. You will obey. Your old servant, who was to travel with you, will return to you with the mules; my companion has gone to fetch him. You will go back to Florence.”

Romola sprang to her feet, anger blazing in her eyes, and spun to face him. She had known full well who it was all along: Fra Girolamo Savonarola. She stood nearly as tall as he, their faces almost level, the defiant words on her lips dying unspoken the moment their gazes met. There was no celestial beauty in his face, no otherworldly glow. His features were harsh, strong, refined only by years of fast and prayer and rigid discipline. But his gaze did not carry the prurient curiosity of a stranger, nor the greedy admiration Tito had once turned on her. It held a simple, fierce care for her, for her soul, that she had never encountered in any living human being. She could not find it in herself to argue he had no right to speak. She stood silent, looking at him.

“You are proud of your freedom, my daughter,” he said. “But who is the base debtor that ever thinks himself free?”

The words cut like a sharp sting, and Romola’s face blanched as if a pale flash of lightning had passed over it.
“You flee your debts. The debt of a Florentine citizen. The debt of a wife. You turn your back on the life appointed you, to choose a new one, as if any man or woman gets to pick their own duties any more than they get to pick their birthplace, or their father, or their mother. You are running from the presence of God into the wilderness.”

Her anger had melted away, leaving behind a strange, unnameable presentiment that there might be strength in submission, if this man, whom she had always half-disdained as a firebrand preacher, held some law worth following. She could not simply refuse any longer.
“My father,” she said, the title slipping unbidden from her lips, a title she had never once used for him before, “you cannot know the sorrow that drives me. No one knows but me. No one can judge for me. I have borne so much pain I cannot stay.”

“I know enough. My mind has been illuminated on your behalf. I do not seek the secrets of your marriage, that belong under the seal of confession. But I have a divine warrant to stop you that needs no such knowledge. You were warned, in my presence, before you ever wed Tito, that you might still choose to remain free. You chose the bond. To break it now in secret, in disguise, is to commit one of the greatest wrongs a woman and a citizen can do: to abandon a pledge you swore before God and all your Florentine neighbours. You would complain of the wrongs done to you, while you yourself break the most basic thread that ties man to man—faith to the word you have given? Is that the wisdom your old humanist tutors taught you, that you cannot see the bare duty of integrity where the Church would have taught you to see not just integrity, but holy purpose?”

Blood rushed to Romola’s face, and she shrank back as if she had been struck. She had never thought of her flight as the same kind of cowardly evasion Tito had always practiced, but the words left her too shaken to speak.

“You run from that pledge to leave Florence,” he went on. “Florence, the only home you have, the only people on this earth to whom you owe the debt of shared citizenship.”

“I would never have left Florence,” she said, her voice trembling, “as long as there was any hope I could serve my father there.”

“Do you own no tie but the love of a daughter for her fleshly father?” he said. “You have lived your life blind, surrounded by men who sit on their hill of old manuscripts and look down on the lives of their fellow citizens, chattering about the wisdom of dead pagans while they scorn the work God is doing in the present. They told you stories of pagan women who lived for their republic, but never taught you that you, a Florentine woman, owe your life to Florence itself. Your people groan under a yoke of tyranny, of hunger, of corruption, and you would slip away from under it instead of standing with them to lighten the load? The streets are full of starving children, of widows crying out for bread, and you say you will go away to ease your own private sorrow? The servants of God are fighting to build a city of justice and peace, and you would spread your wings like a bird and fly away, thinking none of it is any of your concern? You scorn the Church’s teachings, but you would be far better than you are now if you knelt beside the humblest illiterate weaver’s wife who prays for Florence, who feels sisterhood with every neighbour, who waits and endures because she knows the work God has planned for the city is too great for her small strength to abandon.”

“I am not running to ease and luxury!” Romola lifted her head, stung to defend herself. “I go to hardship. I expect no joy. It left my life long ago.”

“You still seek your own will, over the law you are bound to obey,” he said. “Good is not a thing you can pick and choose at will. It is a river that flows from the foot of the Invisible Throne, and it runs only along the path of obedience. Run from your duties, and you will find nothing but sorrow without purpose, bitter herbs with no bread to sustain you.”

“If you only knew what I have had to bear,” Romola whispered, clasping her hands tight until her knuckles went white, pleading up at him. “How impossible it felt to stay.”

“Draw forth the thing you carry tucked in your mantle, at the cord around your neck,” he said, pointing to her chest. “Look at it.”

Romola started, but her will was no longer her own to resist. She pulled out the small crucifix he had given her months before, the one she had carried through every dark day since.

“There, my daughter, is the image of the Supreme Offering, made out of perfect love, for the great need of suffering humanity.” He paused, and she held the crucifix trembling, thinking of all the miles she had walked since she first took it from his hands, all the grief she had endured. “Conform your life to that image. Make your own sorrow an offering. When the fire of divine charity burns in you, you will look back and see you were foolish to think your private pain mattered half so much as the pain of a hundred thousand souls around you. You held yourself proud, apart from the common folk of Florence, but you were not yet born to the true life of a child of God. If the love you bore your father is no longer enough to keep you here, you say, then you have no law, no faith? That is no life at all. That is the life of a beast robbed of its young, that wanders aimless in the dark. If you shared the faith of the believers, you would see Florence is the home of your soul as much as your birthplace, that there is work there for you that no one else can do. Who will fill your empty spot if you run away? The world is full of iniquity, full of groans, light fighting against darkness, and you say you will burst your bonds and go where no one will claim anything of you? Every bond in your life is a debt. The only right path is paying it. You may wander the whole earth, and you will never outrun that truth.”

“I thought my brother Dino was right!” Romola burst out, fighting the last of her resistance. “He broke all his family ties, he forsook his place, to follow God. Why can I not do the same?”

“That was a special, singular vocation. He could not attain the higher life if he had stayed, for it would have been stifled in him. You are no Dino. You are a wife. You would break your ties out of anger and self-will, not because God calls you to renounce them. The higher life begins the moment you set your own will aside to bow to divine law. It is the portal to wisdom, to true freedom, to blessedness. Your old pagan wisdom, the wisdom of dead men long turned to dust, has left you empty. It gave you no care for your neighbours, no stake in the work of regenerating Florence, no share in that divine love that burns away the petty pain of the self. Now your own sword of sorrow has pierced your soul, and you would run, leaving all the suffering of your city behind you? If the streets are full of wickedness, your steps should shine with purity. If there are cries of anguish, you should be there to still them. Sorrow has come to teach you a new worship. The sign of it is in your hand.”

Romola still wavered, seeing the path he laid out before her, and knowing it would be hard, hot with the pain of returning to a husband she no longer loved, to a life that had brought her nothing but grief. But his words had reached a place in her she had never known existed, a place that had already begun to wake when she fled Florence, sick at heart of selfish ease. She turned her eyes away from his face, stood silent for minutes, her hands hanging clasped before her.
“My husband,” she whispered, at last, the words dragged out of her, “my love for him is long dead.”

“Marriage is no mere fleshly bond built on fleeting passion,” Savonarola said, his voice softening. “It is a sacramental vow that only God can break. Your husband is no malefactor, is he?”

Romola started. “No. Heaven forbid. I accuse him of no crime.”

“If he were a malefactor,” he said, “your place would be in the prison cell beside him. If the cross of a difficult marriage is laid on your shoulders, you must carry it. You may say you will forsake him, but you can never cease to be a wife.”

“Make that sorrow an offering too,” he went on, his voice growing more intense, his face lit up with the inner fire that had moved all of Florence. “Lay it on the altar of the great work God is doing here, where sin and sorrow will be made to cease. The work is already beginning. The eyes of faith see it. It may be our blessedness to die for it, to die daily by crucifying our own selfish will, to lay our bodies on the altar at the last. You are a child of Florence. Fulfil the great inheritance that is yours. Live for your people. Bear the anguish. The iron is sharp, I know. It rends tender flesh. The draught is bitter on the lips. But there is rapture in that cup, a vision that will make all petty, personal sorrow seem as nothing.”

Romola felt all her doubts melt away in the glow of his fierce, unshakable faith. She felt a new strength rising in her, a purpose she had never known before.
“Father,” she said, in a low, praying cry, “I will be guided. Teach me. I will go back.”

Almost unconsciously, she sank to her knees in the grass. Savonarola stretched his hands out over her, and no words were needed. The morning stillness wrapped around them, and the road to Siena, the road to the empty, wandering freedom she had once craved, vanished from her mind.

The crisp Tuscan wind bit at the hill of Bogoli, the same stretch of high ground we have stood on before, and far below, the faint, hazy spires of Florence peeked over the wintry treeline. At last Fra Girolamo Savonarola spoke, his voice low and steady as he bade her rise. “Rise, my daughter. Your servant waits not far off with the mules. It is time we turn our steps back toward Florence.”

Romola pushed herself up from her knees. That long, silent stretch of kneeling had been a quiet, unspoken sacrament for her, sealing the new, tender state of surrendered yearning she had only just stumbled into. The single act of renouncing her desperate plan to flee her husband had bruised her will so thoroughly that she ached for guidance even over the smallest, trivial choices. She lifted the edge of her cowl and spotted Maso and the second Dominican monk, their backs turned a dozen paces off along the hill’s edge to give her privacy. But she said no word to call them, only looked back at Savonarola with a flicker of appeal, as if the order for Maso to turn home must come from him, not from her.

“I will go and call them,” he said, answering her unspoken plea. “And I will commend you to the Brother who travels with me. You seek to place yourself under guidance now, to learn that wisdom which until now has seemed no better than foolishness to you. One of its greatest gates is the sacrament of confession. You need a confessor, my daughter, and I wish to place you in the care of Fra Salvestro, one of the San Marco brethren, a man I trust deeply.”

“I would take no guidance but yours, father,” Romola said, her tone tight with quiet anxiety.

“My daughter, I do not act as a confessor. The vocation laid on me draws me away from offices that would force me into constant close contact with the laity, and distract me from the special duties I owe to all of Florence.”

“Then I shall not be able to speak to you in private? If I waver, if—” Romola cut herself off, her breath catching on a surge of sudden agitation. A sharp alarm pricked at her: what little new strength she had found in her choice to renounce flight would melt away if she lost the immediate, steadying pull of Savonarola’s personal influence.

“If your soul has need of a private word from my lips, you will send notice to me through Fra Salvestro, and I will meet you in the San Marco sacristy or the choir. I will never cease to watch over you. I will instruct my brother well in your case, that he may lead you into the path of labour for the suffering and the hungry, the work you are called to as a daughter of Florence in these hard, needy days. I long to see you stand among the feebler, more ignorant sisters of the city as the apple tree stands among the gnarled wild trees of the forest: your fairness, all your fine natural gifts, will be no more than a lamp through which Divine light may shine all the more purely. I will go now and call your servant.”

When Maso had been sent a little way ahead on the path, Fra Salvestro stepped forward, and Savonarola led Romola to meet him. She had already felt a quiet inward flinch at the thought of submitting to a total stranger as her spiritual guide, but to resist Savonarola’s advice would have been to cling to the very independence she had just sworn to renounce. Every fibre of her mind now leaned toward choosing the hard thing over the easy one. She bowed reverently before Fra Salvestro before she lifted her eyes to look at him full, and when she did, her reluctance curdled into a fluttering, unsteady doubt.

There are men whose very presence wraps you in unforced trust and reverence. There are others to whom you must bring your trust and reverence ready-made, with no help from them. Romola felt that difference flash through her the second Savonarola stepped aside and left her facing Fra Salvestro Maruffi. There was nothing openly repulsive about him: no hint of hypocrisy, no streak of coarse ill manners. His face was handsomer than Savonarola’s, his stance a little taller. He had long served as the trusted confessor for many of Florence’s most elite households, and carried years of experience as a spiritual director. But there was a perpetual, wavering vagueness to his expression, the look of a mind that could never focus fully on one single great emotion or unshakable belief—a flaw that was fatal to any chance of holding influence over a passionate, ardent nature like Romola’s. That vacuous look was not the stamp of insincerity, merely the mark of a shallow soul: the kind of soul that can genuinely strive to fulfill a high, sacred calling, genuinely arrange its features to utter the most sublime of prayers, yet find its muscles twitch and slack against its will, as plain prose forces its way onto the page instead of poetry for a man who has never known the divine frenzy of true conviction.

Fra Salvestro was prone to sudden, vivid visions, apparently sparked by a constitution given to somnambulism. Savonarola believed those visions were supernatural, while Fra Salvestro himself had once resisted that very interpretation, and even rebuked Savonarola for his public prophetic preaching. It was one more proof, if proof was needed, that the greatness of a man can never be measured by his willingness to reject the superstitions of his age. No one could have mistaken which of these two was the great man, and which was the small.

Romola’s shifting feeling toward Fra Salvestro measured that gap perfectly. The hot, angry rebellion she had once felt toward Savonarola was long gone, and she had all but forgotten her old, lingering dread that some narrow religious influence would drag her into the bitter, sour circle of fanatical monkish piety. Now that dread stole over her again, cold as a winter draft. It could not break the strong forward momentum her mind had built with her choice, but it was like grey cloud sliding over the face of sunrise, turning the whole road back toward Florence monotonous and dim. There is no sombre path more testing to the fire of a hard-won renunciation than the path we walk back, after having once resolved to never tread it again.

When they passed through Florence’s city gates, soft light snowflakes drifted down all around them. When they parted at the doors of San Marco, Romola walked hastily away across the familiar stone bridge, and turned in through the great carved doorway of her home on the Via de’ Bardi. Her dark footprints marked clear patterns in the thin new carpet of snow on the threshold, and her damp cowl hung heavy and cold around her face. She climbed the stairs to her chamber, stripped off the coarse grey serge of the pilgrim she had almost become, burned all the farewell letters she had written, put every one of her small precious trifles back in its usual place, unbound her hair, and slipped back into the plain black dress she always wore. Instead of being hours deep into a thrilling, life-altering voyage far away, she was settling back into the same old familiar chair by the window. Snow tapped against the panes, and she was entirely alone.

The dreariness settled thick around her, but her courage burned bright, as bright as a prospector’s who has just spotted the first glint of gold in the dirt. She was going to thread the rest of her life by a new, untested clue. She had poured every last ounce of her will into this act of renunciation. The empty little tabernacle that had once held her father’s lost manuscripts stayed locked, and she set Dino’s worn wooden crucifix on the shelf just outside it. No sound broke the outer monotony of her silent, empty home, until night pressed pale as a white ghost against the frosted windowpanes. It was Christmas Eve, 1494, and it would be the most memorable night of Romola’s whole life.

It was the thirtieth of October, 1496. The Florentine sky dawned clear, gilded by crisp autumnal sun, and a soft land breeze drifted down the Arno valley, carrying the sharp scent of crushed vine leaves and woodsmoke from the hills. But none of the city’s inhabitants spared it more than a passing glance. Their minds were fixed on the gales howling out at sea, gales that had conspired with every other ill turn of fortune to prove Fra Girolamo Savonarola’s bold claim of Heaven’s special protection for Florence a cruel mockery.

Those tempestuous off-shore winds had driven a fleet of Marseilles supply ships off course, ships loaded down with soldiers and, most desperately needed of all, grain. Florence was reeling: famine walked her cobblestone streets, and every one of her territory’s borders bristled with hostile forces. The French king Charles VIII, that would-be new Charlemagne who had swept through Italy unopposed to seize Naples fifteen months prior, had slipped away north soon after, and word had spread that the grief of losing his newborn son had all but erased his half-hearted intention to return and set the Italian states to rights. A new Holy League had been forged against him, led by Pope Alexander Borgia, framed as a patriotic pact to “drive out the barbarian” French garrisons still holding Naples’ fortress. The fine rhetoric hid a far uglier truth: it was no more than a pack of wolves agreeing to chase off all competing packs, then tear the carcass of Italy apart between them. And Florence, stubbornly clinging to her old French alliance and refusing to join the League, was marked out not as a fellow wolf, but as the juiciest, most desirable carcass of all.

Pisa, still fighting tooth and nail for its independence from Florentine rule, was now backed by thousands of Venetian and Milanese troops, and the German Emperor Maximilian, invited by the League, had arrived to join the Pisan forces in a full siege of the port of Leghorn, while Venetian and Genoese galleys blockaded the entire coast. If Leghorn fell, Florence was doomed: that single port was her only outlet to the sea, her only lifeline for imported grain, and every land route surrounding her was already closed off by the Pope’s bitter enmity and the greedy jealousy of smaller neighbouring states.

The Signoria, dominated by Savonarola’s Piagnone followers, had met the crisis with remarkable courage: they raised new funds, levied fresh militia, dispatched conciliatory embassies to neighbouring courts, and made the radical, unheard-of choice to not bar their gates against the starving rural peasants and homeless beggars being turned out of every other city in Tuscany. But every act of mercy only stoked the anger of their political rivals, who grumbled loudly in every piazza and tavern that the government was dooming the city: Florence should join the League at once, they insisted, instead of courting ruin for a useless foreign ally, and they should feed their own citizens first before letting hordes of starving strangers drag famine and pestilence into the streets.

Day by day the hunger grew sharper, the murmurs louder. To make matters far worse, Fra Girolamo had been silenced by a papal mandate a month earlier, forbidden from preaching. But when word arrived that the Marseilles grain ships had been lost at sea, the city’s desperate hunger for the friar’s unshakable, fire-infused faith became too strong to ignore. Defying the Pope’s order, the Signoria summoned Savonarola back to the Duomo’s pulpit, and two days prior he had stood before the packed crowds and told them only to remain steadfast in piety and good citizenship, and divine deliverance would surely come. He had even dared to vow publicly that he would let his friar’s frock be stripped from his back in the streets if God did not come to Florence’s aid.

But no sign of that deliverance had appeared on this crisp October morning. So the city had turned to its oldest, most beloved intercessor: the miraculous hidden image of the Madonna dell’ Impruneta, whose sacred tabernacle had been brought into the outskirts of Florence the previous evening, escorted by a procession of priests and noble patrons and left in the church of San Gaggio just outside the San Piero gate. For a hundred and fifty years, Florentines had fetched her image into the city in times of drought, flood, plague, and war, and every time they had been granted relief. The saying went that the Madonna dell’ Impruneta loved Florence so well she would grant whatever the city asked of her. Surely she would not turn away from them now, when hunger bit so deep it was turning to plague, when hospital wards overflowed and even private house courtyards had been converted to makeshift infirmaries, when the burial brotherhoods were out before dawn every day gathering the corpses of those who had dropped dead of hunger in the gutters overnight.

Moving among those streets that morning was Romola de’ Bardi. Clad in the plainest black serge gown, a simple length of unadorned black drapery drawn over her head to hide almost all her hair, save for the faint ripples of gold that peeked out across her brow, she was walking west from the Ponte Vecchio along Por’ Santa Maria when her path was blocked by a halted bier carried by members of the San Jacopo del Popolo burial brotherhood. The brethren at the front of the bier were stooped over a huddled figure they had found collapsed by the roadside, surrounded by a crowd of gaunt, sharp-featured workmen, all chattering at once in the raw dialect of the city’s poorer quarters.

“He’s dead, I tell you! The Lord loved him so much he took him straight home.”
“Ah, wouldn’t it be a blessing for all of us to stretch out our legs and follow him? Standing upright on nothing but empty air in your stomach is no way to live.”
“He’s an old fellow anyway. Death got a poor bargain of him. Life already took all that was worth having.”
“Ten to one he’s no Florentine! A beggar thrown out of Siena. San Giovanni defend us—they don’t even need to send soldiers to fight us. They send an army of starving men to eat us out of house and home.”
“No, no, he’s one of the prisoners they kicked out of the Stinche gaol. I can see the grey patch where his prison badge was sewn on.”

Romola stepped forward, pulling the small wine flask slung from her belt free. “Let me try giving him some wine,” she said. She pried the old man’s jaws gently open with a small ivory aid she carried for this exact purpose, trickling a few drops of spiced vernaccia past his lips. He swallowed, his head lolled a little towards her, and his dark wild eyes fluttered open.

In that instant, Romola’s blood ran cold. She knew that face: the deep-lined sallow cheeks, the long white beard, those fierce unmistakeable eyes. This was the escaped prisoner she had seen in the Duomo months before, the stranger at whose very grasp Tito had blanched so pale she had thought he would faint, the figure whose likeness Piero di Cosimo had once sketched in his studio, caught mid-leap to lunge for her husband. For months she had sensed a terrible, unspoken secret coiled at the heart of all Tito’s betrayals, and now here he was, half-dead at her feet. She felt a wild impulse to turn and run, as if from a waking nightmare—then a fiercer, more unshakable resolve to stay, for some deep, unnameable instinct told her this old man had been hurt, gravely, irreparably hurt, by Tito’s treachery.

She did not run. She lifted his head gently with one arm, still dribbling wine between his lips, her hands trembling but guided by the long habit of care she had built for herself in her months of charity work. Baldassarre, for his part, looked up at her in dazed recognition. The descriptions he had heard of the golden-haired wife who lived in the de’ Bardi palace had slipped from his mind like water, and for the first time, he was looking straight at her.

“It would be best to carry him just a little further, to the steps of San Stefano,” Romola suggested. “We will not block the street there, and your brethren can go on with your work.”

They moved the thirty yards to the church steps, and by then Baldassarre had strength enough to prop himself up against the stone doorframe. The burial brethren went on their way, but the crowd of onlookers swelled, their murmurs growing bolder now that it was clear the old man was not a corpse after all. A rough man in a tattered red night-cap elbowed his way to the front of the circle that had narrowed tight around Romola, who had just pulled a hunk of soft bread from her basket, soaked a chunk of it in wine, and held it out to Baldassarre.

“Madonna, why waste that good bread on a thief who was half dead five minutes ago?” the man jeered. “We’re all starving here. Give it to us, we could do a far better job eating it than he could.”

Romola stood, her pale face drawn thin by weeks of shared fasts, her hazel eyes steady and unflinching as she held her basket of bread out to the crowd. “I know exactly how cruel hunger is,” she said, no trace of anger in her voice. “You are all strong men. You could take this bread from me right now, if you chose. But these loaves were saved for sick women, for hungry children who cannot fight for themselves. You can take all of it, if you will—but I will not let you take the piece I have set aside for this helpless old man. I will stand between you and him, for as long as I can.”

The crowd fell dead silent. The men nearest her stepped back, suddenly ashamed, each of them trying to look as if someone else had pushed them forward. At that moment, the armed patrols of the Signoria, clearing the streets ahead of the Madonna dell’ Impruneta procession, rounded the corner, waving their maces to disperse the loiterers.

A ragged, broad-shouldered man with a bristly black beard and a red rag tied around his head stepped up to Romola, bowing awkwardly. “Don’t you worry, Madonna. If you’ve got errands to run, I’ll watch the old fellow for you. I’ve got a hungry old mother waiting for me at home, I know what it is to look out for people who can’t look out for themselves. He’ll be right here when you get back.”

Romola smiled, penitent for the flicker of doubt she had felt at his rough appearance. She turned back to Baldassarre, who was already nibbling at the soaked bread, and touched his arm gently. “Wait for me here,” she said. “I will come back, and take you to a warm place to rest.”

He nodded, faint and slow, and Romola turned to make her way towards the Hospital of San Matteo, in the Piazza di San Marco, her mind already racing with the knowledge that the truth she had spent so long running from was waiting for her, there on the stone steps of San Stefano.

More than an hour after leaving the city hospital, Romola took a winding detour away from her usual path, looping farther west toward the Arno until she reached the riverbank well outside the Ponte Vecchio. She turned straight for the famous old bridge, intent on hurrying to San Stefano to search for Baldassarre. Dread coiled tight in her chest at the thought of what she might learn of him, yet she knew to abandon him would be to betray a sharp, unignorable claim he held on her conscience.

But when she neared the crossroads where the Por’ Santa Maria branched right and the road to Ponte Vecchio ran left, the crowd around her dropped to their knees all at once, and she followed suit without a second’s hesitation. The Great Cross of the Duomo was at the head of a procession moving toward them, and she would not be able to reach San Stefano until the full march had passed. When the Cross had faded from view and she stood back upright, the idle waiting hit her with a surge of bone-deep fatigue she had not noticed while walking with purpose.
A local hose vendor at her elbow greeted her warmly: “Madonna Romola, you must be worn through standing. My door is right here—Gian Fantoni will be proud to give you a seat. We all love God and the Frate as you do, this house is yours for the taking.”

Romola had grown used to this fraternal welcome from ordinary Florentine citizens over the past two years, faces she recognized from seeing them week after week at the Duomo. The quiet loneliness of her empty Via de’ Bardi home no longer felt like her true anchor; she felt far more at home inside the towered circuit of the city, where almost every street corner brought her a look of friendliness or quiet appeal. She stepped through the open door with gratitude, and the vendor led her up to an upper window where his stout Piagnoni-clad wife and three children made space for her behind bright, hanging draperies. From her vantage point, she had a clear view of the procession moving at a solemn crawl across the full length of the Ponte Vecchio, and out across the Arno Lung’ Arno all the way to the distant Santa Trinita bridge.

The procession moved in almost total stillness, no wailing hymn breaking its quiet plea for mercy—only the steady tramp of hundreds of feet, and the faint soft drag of wool robes against stone. The first ranks that passed under Romola’s window were the city’s youth: a long line of beardless Florentine boys bearing high above their midst a white statue of the Child Jesus, its golden halo glowing above its head, standing beside a tall cross laid out with thorns and iron nails.

After them came the mysterious Companies of Discipline, their ranks bound by secret oaths to self-chastisement and devout works, every inch of their bodies covered by shrouds save for their eyes. Every onlooker knew the forms walking past were ordinary Florentines of all ranks—shopkeepers, merchants, statesmen—who went about their public lives as usual by day, but no one could pick out a son, a husband, a father under their hoods. They had shed all individual identity to walk as living symbols of a shared vow, each company marked only by its coloured shroud and badge, their faces erased of all expression save quiet fellowship.

They were followed by wave after wave of monastic orders: first a white stream of reformed Benedictines, then the far longer grey line of the Franciscans, their waists bound with knotted cord, many shod in rough wooden zoccoli over bare feet, the largest and most zealous order in Florence at the time, many of them devoted to the poor and openly critical of the Dominicans. Next came the black-robed Augustinians of San Spirito, their faces sharper and more learned, the heirs to Boccaccio’s legendary library and long Florence’s most respected community of scholars, followed by the Carmelites in white over dark, then the all-black Servites, the famous local order founded seven hundred years prior by seven wealthy merchants who had abandoned all their profits to worship the Divine Mother.

At the sight of black mantles worn over white scapularies, the whole crowd’s pulse quickened, split between bitter hatred and fierce devotion: the Dominicans, the followers of Savonarola, were approaching. The order’s Fiesole branch marched first, their mantles coarse, most threadbare if not outright ragged, stripped to absolute poverty by the strict discipline their Prior of San Marco had enforced. At last, one figure in a mantle no more worn than the rest moved to the front of the line, a tonsured head above it that would have seemed unremarkable to a stranger who had never seen it stamped on bronze medals emblazoned with the Sword of God, or flanked by armed guards on the steps of the Duomo, or lit from within by the fire of preaching as it turned to face a rapt, screaming crowd of thousands.

No one dared break the stillness enough to cheer or jeer openly as Savonarola drew near, but every ear caught a mingled murmur of hissing that strained to be curses, and soft whispers that strained to be blessings. The hisses seemed loudest to the small group of his disciples at the crossroads, who dropped to their knees as if divine presence was moving past them, and the wave of silent homage rippled down the length of the street like a subtle shock, moving many to bend the knee even as others stood unmoved, their faces twisted with hatred. As Savonarola passed down the Por’ Santa Maria, Romola spotted a man at an upper window spit down directly at him.

More orders followed: the Frati Umiliati, the Humbled Brethren of Ognissanti, famous as the first workers to build Florence’s wool trade centuries before, then ranks of Vallombrosan Benedictines, their small variations of hood cut and dye a quiet nod to the generations of reformers who had marked shifts in piety with new robes. At last the line of shaven crowns ended, and the untonsured secular priests of the city marched past, followed by the full assembled ranks of Florence’s twenty-one incorporated Arts, their banners flying high to mark every distinct station of the city’s work, from the bakers who supplied its bread all the way up to its judges and notaries. Next came all the junior state officers, rising in rank until the secular line was broken by the Duomo’s canons, carrying a silver casket that held the relic of the head of San Zenobio, Florence’s immortal patron saint whose virtues were said to have saved the city from ruin a thousand years prior.

This was the heart of the procession. Behind the relic walked the archbishop under a rich embroidered canopy, and after him came the long-awaited hidden tabernacle: draped first in heavy brocade curtains around an outer painted shrine, and within that, the far older inner shrine no living person, nor any of their fathers, had ever opened. Inside rested the Pitying Mother, the Unseen Madonna, dug up out of the soil of L’Impruneta ages before, who had cried out the moment the spade struck her wooden form. For centuries the Madonna had never been carried to the Duomo without a stream of rich offerings borne before her, but today there were no gifts for the shrine, no torches lit, no precious veils laid out. By Fra Girolamo’s direct order, every donation that day went straight to the hungry poor, not to the altars. He had always preached that help for suffering living people mattered far more than offerings to distant divine powers, and temporary altars for alms had been set up outside every major church along the route. Florence was at her final point of extremity, and even the most pious knew the hidden Madonna cared more for the sound of a hungry child’s cry than gilded brocade. Many clung harder to the Frate’s public promise that deliverance would come than they did to the ancient miracle of the unseen statue, while his enemies waited in secret glee for the day his word would be proven false.

Slowly the tabernacle moved on, knees bending before it as it passed, the whole street wrapped in profound stillness. The procession was nearly at its end, the Priors and the Gonfaloniere waiting to bring up the rear, and Romola, half heavy with foreboding and half lifted by the warm, familiar fellowship she had learned to cherish in Florence over the last two years, sighed deep and sank back to her knees in utter languor.

Then a flash of bright colour burst between the houses, out over the distant Santa Trinita bridge.

Romola shot to her feet, leaning far out the window, her black veil slipping off her head so her golden hair caught the sunlight, the flush high on her cheeks glowing as if lit by a sudden spark. A roar burst from the crowd at the exact same instant: the last ranks of the procession froze, every face turning toward the distant bridge. A rider on a lathered bay horse galloped full tilt along the Arno embankment, foam streaked white along his horse’s sides from the hard ride, his cap flying loose by its red trailing becchetto, waving a green olive branch high over his head. A messenger. A messenger of good tidings. The crowd surged forward to meet him, seizing his horse’s reins, pushing and trampling in their eagerness to hear his news, and Romola recognized him in an instant. It was Tito, her husband, who had ridden out on a private embassy to Pisa only a few days prior.

No spark of joy lit her face. The first rush of expectation faded fast, and her only care was what relief he had brought for the starving city. The crowd’s teasing cries of “Give us hose-worthy news!” circled around him as he laughed off their questions, pushing his horse steadily forward until he reached the Gonfaloniere and Priors waiting at the crossroads. He bowed low, and announced clearly for all to hear: “Magnificent Signori! I bring joyful tidings: the French galleys, laden with corn and armed men, have docked safe in the port of Leghorn. A strong west wind held the enemy blockading fleet far out at sea.”

The words vibrated through the streets, a great shout ringing out all the way along the Arno, then another and another, the cheers spreading back up the length of the procession toward the Duomo, distant answering cries rippling outward like waves across a wide lake. For minutes no one could speak: even the Signoria lifted their caps, standing bareheaded before the rescue that had arrived from beyond any human power, that grace all generations call divine.

At last, as the order was given for the procession to move forward again, Tito smiled and added gracefully: “I must note that the reward of hose the Signoria may bestow for these tidings is no due to me. Another man rode far harder than I to bring the news—Meo di Sasso, whose horse broke down only a mile outside Signa. He would have stood here in my place if he could, and he will arrive in another hour to claim the honour he has earned, after bearing all the labour and missing the greatest joy.”
The crowd cheered at his modesty, the Signoria’s spokesman spoke a quick word of praise, and the end of the procession moved on. The great bell of the Palazzo Vecchio began to swing, ringing out to amplify the people’s joy, as Tito turned his horse to follow behind the march. Romola watched him adjust his cap, playing the part of the weary exhausted rider, and she felt almost certain he had looked up and seen her leaning out that window. Tito had always had a gift for seeing everything, without ever letting anyone else notice he had looked. She drew her black veil back over her head, and sat quiet, hidden in the shadow of the window nook, as the roar of Florence’s celebration swelled around her.

The last echoes of the procession’s shuffling footsteps faded down the cobblestones, the dust of the throng settling soft over sun-warmed stone. As soon as the last straggler had rounded the corner towards the Piazza, Romola stepped down from her hiding place and hurried to the steps of San Stefano. The old church attendant Cecco had been drawn along with the rest of the crowd to gawk at the ceremony, so the only figure propped against the weathered oak church door was Baldassarre, his dented horn cup loose in his gnarled, calloused hand, waiting for her.
There was a stark, shocking change in him. The blank, foggy, half-waking glance of a man only just clawing his way back from the darkness of amnesia had vanished. What blazed in its place was raw, caged ferocity, the glare of a starved beast behind iron bars that spots its prey pass safely beyond the reach of the bars. When Romola stepped close and spoke to him, that fury snapped straight onto her, and she flinched. Her first thought was that he had seen Tito. And as that grating, unmistakeable hatred raked over her, a fragile, foolish spark of hope stirred in her chest: what if this ragged, raging old man was the one who had committed the wrong? What if her husband was innocent of the shadowy cruelty she had long suspected of him? If she could bring them face to face now, she could finally lay every unnameable fear to rest.
“If you come with me,” she said gently, “I can give you shelter and food until you are fully rested and strong again. Will you come?”
“Yes,” Baldassarre said, but the words were a mutter, spoken more to himself than to her. “I shall be glad to get my strength. I want to get my strength.”
“Come,” she invited, gesturing for him to walk at her side, and took the quieter, less crowded path along the banks of the Arno heading for Ponte Rubaconte. “I think you are no Florentine,” she ventured, once they had stepped onto the bridge.
He glanced around at her without speaking. The fog of his scattered, forgotten past had thickened again under the weight of his bodily exhaustion, and his suspicious caution had sharpened to a near-paranoid edge. But her eyes were soft, open, holding no fear of his rags or his fury, and at last the quiet in her gaze compelled him to answer, sharp and wary. “No. I am no Florentine. I am a lonely man.”
She saw he had no wish to speak more of himself, and dared not press him with further questions, lest he turn and abandon her. As she glanced at him from time to time, that fragile spark of hope that her husband was innocent guttered and died. If this old man had been the one in the wrong, why all that dread, all that secrecy?
They walked in silence until they reached the entrance to Via de’ Bardi, and Romola noticed Baldassarre jolt as if he had been scalded, turning to stare at her like he had just solved a riddle he had turned over in his head for months. A moment later she paused at the half-open courtyard door and turned to him.
“Ah!” he said, before she could speak. “You are his wife.”
“Whose wife?” Romola asked, her blood turning cold.
Baldassarre could not have dredged the name up at that moment if his life had depended on it. The sheer force of Tito’s smiling, treacherous image pressing on his mind had pushed every verbal marker of the man out of his reach. He made no answer, only stared back at her with strange, unblinking fixedness.
Romola swung the door wide to show him the courtyard, its stone floors covered in thick straw where four or five fevered sick people lay propped on pallets, while tiny pale children crawled and sat on the loose straw, gnawing at dry stalks and gurgling soft nonsense to themselves.
“If you will come in,” she said, her voice trembling, “I will find you a comfortable place to rest, and bring you more food.”
“No, I will not come in,” Baldassarre said. But he did not move away, arrested by the jumble of half-memories and rage churning in his overtaxed mind, too confused to choose a path forward.
“Is there nothing I can do for you?” Romola said, almost pleading. “Let me give you some money to buy food. It will be plentiful soon.” She slipped her hand into her leather scarsella as she spoke, and held out her palm cupped with several silver grossi. She deliberately gave him far more than she would have handed to any other beggar in the same circumstances.
He stared at the coins for a long moment, then said, “Yes. I will take them.”
She poured the money into his palm, and he curled his fingers tight around the cool silver. “Tell me,” Romola began, almost beseeching, “what shall you—”
But Baldassarre had already turned away from her, striding back towards the bridge. He crossed it without pausing, and walked straight up the Via del Fosso until he came upon Niccolò Caparra’s armourer’s shop, turning towards it without a second’s pause, as if it had been the very object he had been searching for all along.
Niccolò the armourer was out in the guild procession with the rest of Florence’s armourers, and only one young apprentice was left minding the store. The walls hung thick with all kinds of blades and weapons, and Baldassarre’s eyes locked on the one he hungered for far more than bread. Niccolò would almost certainly have refused to sell a sharp blade to this ragged man who bore the faint, unmistakable marks of prison on his wrists, but the younger, less observant apprentice did not hesitate at all: he took three grossi for a sharp, compact hunting knife, thin and light, perfect for slipping inside the breast of his tunic. Baldassarre walked on, feeling stronger, sturdier than he had in months. That sharp edge could give the thrust of an aged arm deadly force. It was a companion now, a power that was finally on his side, even if it failed. It would shatter against plate armour, but why would Tito think to wear armour every hour of the day, after all these months he had spent safe, comfortable, forgetting the man he had left to rot in a dungeon? The knife had been bought with the traitor’s own wife’s money. That was justice.
Before he had even taken the coins, he had known exactly what he would do with them: buy a weapon. And then, if he could, buy food: food to nourish the arm that would grip that blade, food to nourish the body that was the temple of his vengeance. With the thought of hiding fresh in his mind, lest Tito’s men drag him back to prison before he could strike, he turned down the narrowest, shadowiest side streets, bought himself a hunk of meat and a crust of bread, and sat down under the first derelict loggia he found to eat. The city’s bells pealed louder and louder in celebration, their roar throbbing through the air, through his bones, and to him they sounded like nothing more than the roar of a great, cruel world that was all aligned against him.
Back in the courtyard, Romola watched Baldassarre until he vanished around the corner into Piazza de’ Mozzi, half feeling his departure was a quiet relief, half scolding herself for not being bolder, for not pushing harder to drag the full truth out of him, for not making sure there was no innocent suffering there she could ease. Yet what could she have done, if the truth had turned out to be some dark, ugly secret about her husband, on top of all the anxieties that already weighed heavy on her shoulders? Surely a wife was permitted to wish to remain ignorant of her husband’s wrongdoings, when she of all people could never protest against him, never warn the rest of Florence of his treachery? That thought tangled up so many of her conflicting feelings that she could not bear to unpack it in her exhaustion. This was supposed to be a time of joy, for the relief had arrived for starving Florence. She stepped back into the courtyard, shut the door behind her so the bells would not drown out her voice, and pushed her heavy black veil back off her face so the sick women could see her clearly, then stood in their midst and told them the good news: the grain ships were coming, the bells were ringing for gladness at the news that the famine was over.
They all sat up to listen, while the little children trotted or crawled towards her, tugging at her long black skirts, impatient to be close enough to see her face. She yielded to them, tired as she was, and sank down on the straw, while the tiny pale creatures peered into her wicker basket and pulled loose strands of her hair, and the feeble voices all around her cried out “Praised be the Holy Virgin!” “It was the procession that brought us the news!” “The Mother of God has taken pity on us!”
At last Romola got to her feet from the straw, too weary to force a smile any longer, saying as she turned up the stone steps, “I will come back soon, to bring you all your dinner.”
“Bless you, madonna! Bless you!” the faint chorus called back, in almost the exact same tone of reverence they had used only minutes earlier to praise the unseen, painted Madonna they prayed to.
That quiet, sweet reverence mattered more to Romola than she could say. She had never had any innate taste for tending the sick, for patching rags and spooning broth to the dying, like other women who found such small labours their natural calling. Her early noble, scholarly upbringing at her father’s side had kept her far from these kinds of womanly works, and if she had not brought to them all the burning, unspent passion of her deepest feelings, they would have felt irksome to her. But they had become the one unshaken resting place of her mind, the one narrow pathway lit by clear, uncomplicated light. When the gulf between herself and Tito seemed to widen further every time she tried to bridge it with gentle submission, when she left her confessor Fra Salvestro or the other followers of Savonarola she worshipped among, reeling with the small, narrow superstitions they parroted, falling back to her old sharp intellectual contempt for their pious platitudes, she would return to these works of mercy, and find her footing again. Whatever else filled her with doubt, the help she gave to her fellow citizens assured her that Fra Girolamo had been right to call her back. Her place in Florence had never been empty: it had been filled with her love and her labour. Florence had needed her, and the heavier her own private sorrow pressed on her, the more gladness she felt in two full years of memories, of small hours where she had lightened the burden of life for someone else. All that ardour of her nature that could no longer spend itself in the tender affection of a daughter for her father or a wife for her husband had transformed itself into a burning enthusiasm of sympathy for the whole common life of the city. She had stopped believing her own lot could ever be happy, had stopped even thinking of personal happiness at all: the only end of her life that seemed worth chasing was the diminishing of sorrow for everyone around her.
Her enthusiasm was stirred to fresh, constant strength by Savonarola’s influence. Even when she recoiled in disgust from the wearisome, stale visions and allegories that his lesser followers droned on about, her deep affinity for his passionate sympathy and the grandeur of his aims had lost none of its power. His burning indignation against the abuses and oppression that formed the daily story of the Church and the city’s ruling elites had kindled that same ready fire in her. His special care for Florentine liberty, for purity of government, and his constant tying of that immediate local goal to the wider end of a universal regeneration of humanity, had woken in her a new consciousness of the great, sweeping drama of human existence of which her own small life was a part. Through her daily, helpful contact with the poorest and most suffering of her neighbours, that vague sentiment hardened into a definite, living motive for self-denying practice. She thought little about obscure theological dogmas, and shrank from reflecting too closely on Fra Girolamo’s prophecies of immediate scourge and the soon-to-come regeneration of the world. She had submitted her mind to his guidance and entered fully into communion with the Church, because it was there that she found an immediate satisfaction for deep, hungry moral needs that all the classical culture and early experience of her life had left completely unmet. Fra Girolamo’s voice had woken in her a reason to live that stood entirely apart from personal enjoyment and personal affection. But it was a reason that seemed to need feeding with greater forces than she possessed within herself, and her submissive observance of all the offices of the Church was simply a kind of watching and waiting, hoping that fresh strength might come to her somehow. The pressing problem for Romola at that moment was not to settle questions of religious controversy, but to keep that flame of unselfish emotion alive, by which a life of deep private sadness could still be a life of active, generous love.
Her trust that Savonarola’s nature was far greater than her own made up a huge part of the strength she had found. And that trust was not to be lightly shaken. It is not force of intellect that makes people recoil ready at the oddities and aberrations of great men, any more than it is force of vision that makes the eye stare obsessively at the warts on a face bright with human warmth: it is simply the absence of high, generous sensibilities. Romola was so deeply moved by the grand, world-shaking energies of Savonarola’s nature that she listened patiently to every dogma and prophecy that came wrapped in the vehicle of his own ardent, deeply felt faith. No soul is ever fully desolate as long as there is one human being for whom it can feel trust and reverence. Romola’s trust in Savonarola was like a strong rope strung securely along the edge of the steep cliff path she walked, making her step light and elastic as long as she grasped it. If it were suddenly wrenched away, no matter how firm the ground she trod beneath her feet, she would stagger, and perhaps even fall.
[1] He himself had noted many times the power of that living, ardent voice. “If,” he writes in the *Compendium Revelationum*, “you speak of those who have not heard these things from my own lips, I admit that the disbelievers are far more numerous than the believers, because it is one thing to hear a man who feels these things deep in his bones, and another to hear a man who has no feeling of them at all. And therefore it was well said by Saint Jerome: ‘The act of a living voice carries with it a certain hidden energy that cannot be named, and the strong sound transfused from the teacher’s own mouth rings loud in the ears of the disciple.’”

The happy stroke of fortune that had cast Tito as the unexpected olive-branch messenger, bearing word of the grain convoy that had averted Florence’s starvation, had earned him loud, impromptu cheers in the street—but he still held several far more deliberate, secret commissions to fulfill before he might think of rest. He lingered in the cool stone corridors of the Palazzo Vecchio, waiting for the return of the Ten, the governing council that oversaw Florence’s foreign policy and war affairs, to deliver a full report of his private scouting mission to Pisa. The trip had been arranged as a precursor to an official, public embassy led by Bernardo Rucellai, tasked with brokering a fragile peace with Emperor Maximilian and the hostile anti-Florentine League.

Tito’s knack for diplomatic detail was well proven, and as he laid out every interview, every scrap of intelligence he had gathered, with perfect clarity and precision, Bernardo del Nero—who sat on the Ten that term—could not suppress his quiet admiration, though he tried. His old dislike of the Greek had curdled into something far sharper ever since Tito had sold Bardo’s priceless library. Romola had never breathed a single word of the hurt the sale had caused her to her godfather, and Bernardo respected that silence as a clear ban on ever bringing the subject up, but his sharp old eyes had picked up every small sign that his goddaughter’s marriage was no happy one. *Ah*, he thought to himself, no one else would have spotted the faint tightness around her smile, the way she slipped off to San Marco for Savonarola’s sermons more and more often. “These women, if they are unblessed with children and unhappy in their homes, turn either to folly or some overstrained piety that makes them think they carry all of heaven’s work on their shoulders. Poor Romola—all that Latin and Greek learning never changed the fact she was every bit a soft, loving woman, no different than if she’d spent all her days pricking her fingers with a needle. And this husband of hers, who gets handed every good post because he’s a tool with a smooth polished handle? I wish the rest of the council won’t find their fingers sliced to the bone one day. Well, well—crooked furrow, full sack. Many a man who hires only perfectly honest helpers ends up with nothing to show for his trouble.”

Bernardo’s long-held conviction that moral perfection was a useless standard for political agents kept him from ever acting against Tito. Beyond the private hurt he could not name for Romola’s sake, he had no concrete grievance against the useful young Greek save for the fact that he was Greek, and Bernardo simply did not like him. The trick of feigning wholehearted loyalty to Florence’s popular republican government while being a secret Medicean sympathizer was shared by half the old Medici party, anyway—Tito was only better at hiding it than the rest. So Bernardo treated him with cold, distant courtesy, which Tito returned with scrupulous, formal respect, and most of Florence still assumed the old bond between Bernardo and Bardo made any favor shown to Romola’s husband a welcome tribute to her godfather.

When Tito finished giving his report at the Palazzo, the official business of the day was done. He was dusty, his riding clothes splashed with mud, his skin sunburnt from hours in the saddle—but he did not turn for home. There were things tucked in his leather scarsella money pouch, and weighing on his mind, that he needed to be rid of as soon as possible, and he meant to find opportunities to offload them so naturally no one would ever suspect he had sought them out on purpose. He sauntered slowly toward the Piazza del Duomo. The public victory procession had long since ended, but the bells still rang out overhead, and the crowds milled restlessly through the streets, their joy looking for a sharper outlet. The reformers under Savonarola had declared Christ the official King of Florence, and ordered all public pleasures to be strictly Christian—but many of the young men who had been shouting “Viva Gesù!” all morning were still secretly craving a good, vigorous stone-throwing brawl to give their thanks some teeth.

Tito could not walk the whole length of the square without being recognized as the olive-branch messenger. He weaseled out of the crowd of pressing, question-hungry admirers adroitly, telling them the real official messenger from Leghorn, Meo di Sasso, would be coming in through Porta San Frediano at any minute, and they ought to go meet him there. Off the mob rushed, and Tito was free to scan the piazza casually, his long eyes drifting here and there, while he pretended to be entirely aimless. The procession had ended at the Duomo, so he knew the area around Nello’s famous barber shop would be packed with loitering gossips, exactly the sort of crowd he wanted.

He spotted them right away, leaning against the Baptistery’s iron rails by the north gates: the tall, broad-shouldered figure of Dolfo Spini, leader of the Compagnacci— the so-called Evil Companions, a band of dissolute young aristocrats who hated the Mediceans, hated the republican government, and hated Savonarola more than anything else. Spini wore a richly embroidered tunic cut in the flashy new French style, his belt hung with a fine sword and dagger, his hat topped with a bright red feather that stood as a deliberate insult to the somber, plain dress the Piagnoni followers of Savonarola had made fashionable. His broad, coarse face, his thick waist, and the rude, unblinking stare he turned on every passerby were instantly recognizable. He and his friends, all richly dressed, armed young men, were laughing ostentatiously, as if trying to prove they were not at all bitter that the grain convoy had just validated the friar’s prophecy they had mocked all week.

Leaning against the barber shop doorpost at the center of the group was sharp-eyed, clean-shaven Niccolò Machiavelli, still young but already shrewd enough to see every petty secret of self-interest that lurked under Florentine public life. “Messer Dolfo’s skull is even bigger and emptier than a pumpkin, I see,” he was saying as Tito drew near. “I measure a man’s stupidity by the tricks he uses to fool other people. The dumbest creature alive is the one who grins and says he doesn’t mind right after someone has just kicked his shins. If I were just a little dumber,” he added, smiling as the group made space to let Tito join, “I’d pretend to be very fond of this Melema, who’s got a secretary post that would suit me perfectly—as if a poorly paid Latin scribe could ever love a better paid Latin secretary! You, Melema, are pestiferously clever, always getting in my way, and I was very annoyed to hear you stumbled on another piece of good luck this morning.”

“Luck that’s questionable at best, Niccolò,” Tito said, clapping him on the shoulder in easy friendliness. “I haven’t gained anything from it yet but having a crowd of wool-beaters grab hold of me and breathe all over me, when I’m as dirty and battered from riding as a letter carrier all the way from Bologna.”

“Ah! You need my art, Master Orator,” Nello the barber called, hurrying forward at the sound of Tito’s voice. “Your chin, I see, still has yesterday’s stubble all over it. Come, submit yourself to the priest of all the Muses. Sandro, quick with the lather!”

“Truly Nello, that’s exactly what I was craving the moment I left the Palazzo,” Tito said, dropping gratefully into the barber’s chair. “That’s why I turned my steps straight for your shop, instead of tramping home dusty.”

“Of course, no one would dare present themselves to the Madonna Romola with a rusty chin and a tangled head of hair,” Nello chattered as he worked. “Nothing unkempt ought to come near our Florentine lily—though I see her walking about our ragged city corners like a sunbeam every day, or these days more like a moonbeam. I met her yesterday, and she looked as pale and worn as that fainting Madonna painting by Fra Giovanni you see hanging in the San Marco cloisters. You ought to do something about it, my learned one: she fasts and keeps vigils far too often when you’re away.”

Tito let out a melancholy little shrug. “It’s only too true. She’s been depriving herself of half her meals every single day through this famine. But what can a husband do? Her whole mind is caught fire by the friar. No influence of mine can stand against Fra Girolamo.”

“Nor can the Pope’s influence, for that matter, I’d wager,” said Domenico Cennini, who had drifted in the door with Tito. “I don’t suppose you heard any whisper of how the wind is blowing in Rome on your trip to Pisa, Melema?”

“Secrets of the council chamber, Messer Domenico!” Tito said, smiling, holding up his palms in a mock-apologetic gesture. “An envoy has to be as tight-lipped as a father confessor.”

“Of course, of course,” Cennini said. “I don’t ask you to break your oath. But my belief is, if Pope Alexander pushes the friar too far, Fra Girolamo will move heaven and earth to call a General Council of the Church to reform it—and he’ll succeed, too. I’m no Piagnone myself, but I wouldn’t mind seeing that.”

“With all respect to your greater experience, Messer Domenico,” Machiavelli cut in, “I have to disagree. Not about wishing for a General Council that might clean up Rome’s vices, but about your belief the friar will outmaneuver the Pope. Fra Girolamo’s game is an impossible one. If he’d only stuck to preaching against Rome’s sins, and making vague prophecies that Italy would be scourged somehow, the Pope would have let him shout until his throat was hoarse, no questions asked. Spiritual blasts that don’t knock down any actual walls? No one minds those. But the friar won’t be just a spiritual trumpet. He wants to be a lever, and more than that—he *is* a lever. He wants to spread the teachings of Christ by propping up Florence’s popular government, and the Pope, from what I’ve heard on the best authority, has very different plans for this city.”

“Then Florence will stand by the friar!” Cennini broke in, heated. “I wish he’d leave his prophecies alone myself, but if anyone tries to attack our right to choose our own government? I’m an obedient son of the Church, but I’d vote to resist Pope Alexander the Sixth just as our forefathers resisted Pope Gregory the Eleventh.”

“Pardon me one moment, Messer Domenico,” Machiavelli said, tucking his thumbs into his belt, smiling with the quiet delight of a man laying out a perfectly logical argument. “Have you really understood the friar’s position? How did he become such a powerful lever that the wily Pope feels the need to attack him? Because he has the ear of the whole populace. He gives them threats and promises that they believe come straight from God—not just about hell, purgatory, and heaven, but about Pisa, and our Great Council, and the grain convoys. But if events turn against him, if the people’s faith in those promises cracks? The very source of his power will be the source of his fall. He has piled three separate kinds of hatred on his head: the hatred of every ordinary person who resents being forced to wear the strict yoke of his new moral laws; the hatred of all the powerful lords of Italy who want to carve Florence up for their own profit; and the hatred of the common people themselves, whom he dared to promise good things *in this life*, instead of only in the next. If a prophet wants to keep his power, he has to be a prophet like Mahomet, with an army at his back, to frighten the people’s wavering faith back into life when it fades.”

“You can roll all three of those hatreds into one,” the poet Francesco Cei burst out impetuously. “He has earned the hatred of every honest, sensible man by spinning nothing but hypocritical lies. He belongs in the Inferno with the false prophets, walking around with their heads twisted backward on their shoulders.”

“You’re too hot-tempered, my Francesco,” Machiavelli said, laughing. “Poets always slash through the clouds in their rage. I’m no devotee of the friar’s, and I wouldn’t lift my little finger to defend his truthfulness. But perfect truthfulness is a plant that only grows in Paradise. Its seeds have never thrived outside those walls. You, my poet friend, tell poetical lies all the time—half because you’re caught up in the heat of composition, half to please your audience—but you refuse to accept lies told in prose. That’s all the difference between you and Fra Girolamo: he draws the line for what counts as poetry a little further out than you do. When he steps into the Duomo pulpit, the fire is in his bones, and the crowd is there, waiting for him to please them. That is all.”

“You’re far too lax with your morals, Niccolò,” Cennini said gravely. “I for one believe the friar is a man of integrity, even if I don’t believe all his prophecies. As long as no one proves him a fraud, the popular party is strong enough to protect him and beat back all foreign interference.”

“A party that *looks* strong,” Machiavelli said, shrugging, casting an almost imperceptible glance over at Tito, who was leaning back in the barber’s chair, clearly enjoying the way Nello combed and scented his hair. “But how many secret Mediceans are hidden among you? How many people will flip sides the second a private grudge or a private bribe comes along?”

“Hardly any true Medicean loyalists left,” Cennini said. “No one would risk anything for Piero de’ Medici these days. Only a handful of old, staunch friends like Bernardo del Nero. Even many people who are related to the Medici family by blood are loud and proud supporters of the popular government, and would fight for the friar. I talked to Giannozzo Pucci not long ago, and I’m convinced there is nothing he would oppose more fiercely than any attempt to undo the new order of things.”

“You are quite right there, Messer Domenico,” Tito said, rising from the shaving chair, a knowing little laugh glinting in his eyes. “And I fancy a romantic bit of jealousy helped push him away from Piero de’ Medici, not just politics. A man as gentle and amiable as Giannozzo would never speak so bitterly of Piero otherwise. He was with you in the procession this morning, I assume?”

“No,” Cennini answered. “He left for his country villa three days ago. He’s not back yet.”

Tito adjusted his cap, glancing down at his mud-splashed hose like the answer hardly mattered to him. In truth, he had just got the piece of information he’d been craving all day. Tucked in his scarsella was a crumpled gold ring, a secret sign he had promised to deliver to Giannozzo Pucci, given to him by Piero de’ Medici’s envoy, who Tito had ridden miles out of his way to meet at Certaldo, on the Siena road, the week before. Since Pucci was out of town, he could easily send the ring by way of Fra Michele, a Carthusian lay brother who worked secretly for the remaining Medicean loyalists. The moment Pucci received the sign, he would hurry back to Florence to hear the verbal part of Tito’s mission from Piero.

“Behold the finished result!” Nello cried, brandishing his comb and pointing it at Tito. “The handsomest scholar in all the world, or all the wild Maremma marshes, now that he has passed through my hands! Though your face is a little thinner than when you first came to Florence in the bloom of your youth, eh? And I swear, there are faint little lines just starting to hint themselves around your mouth, Master Orator! Ah, the life of the mind is the enemy of beauty! I myself was once thought very handsome by all the ladies, back when I was still in swaddling bands. But now—oh woe is me! I carry all my unwritten poems written in cipher across my own face!”

Tito laughed along with the rest of the group as Nello made a tragically comical face at his own reflection in the hand mirror, then waved a general farewell to the shop full of men and stepped out onto the street.

“I stand by old Piero di Cosimo’s opinion,” Francesco Cei said the second the door shut behind him. “I never half liked that Melema. That constant, never-fading smile of his gets more unnatural every day—no wonder he’s got lines around his mouth.”

“He’s far too successful for his own good,” Machiavelli joked. “I’m sure there’s something off about him, else he never would have landed that secretary post everyone craves.”

“He’s an able man,” Cennini said, in a tone of fair, judicial judgment. “My brother and I have always found him very helpful with our Greek manuscripts, and he does excellent work for the Ten. I like to see a young man work his way up through merit. And Secretary Scala, who took him under his wing when he first arrived, still thinks the world of him, I know.”

“Of course he does,” a notary in the back of the shop put in. “Tito writes all his official letters for him, or corrects them, and gets paid very well for it.”

“I wish Messer Bartolommeo would pay me to doctor his gouty, clumsy Latin,” Machiavelli said, shrugging. “Did he tell you about the pay, Ser Ceccone, or was it Melema himself who bragged?”

“Melema? No, never!” the notary replied. “He’s as tight-lipped as a closed nut. He never brags at all. That’s why everyone employs him. They say he’s getting very rich doing all sorts of quiet, under-the-table jobs for all sorts of people.”

“It’s a bit unfair, that,” Machiavelli said. “There are so many perfectly able notaries out there with no work at all!”

“Well, I still have to say that old story that came out a year or two back, about the madman who accused him of stealing jewels, left a very bad taste in my mouth,” Cei said. “It got hushed up very quickly somehow, but I

The evening had drawn dark across Florence, heavy with the threat of rain, when Romola made her way home from the hospital of San Matteo. She had stopped there after vespers, and walked beside the old servant Maso, whose small, shuffling steps slowed their progress, his guttering lantern casting wavering gold over the glistening cobblestones. Just as they turned onto the Via de’ Bardi, she came face to face with her husband, Tito, who was just stepping out of the cool, shaded archway of the San Marco monastery. He had returned home briefly earlier that day, then slipped out again, and had barely crossed paths with her at all in the hours since. He dismissed Maso at once, complaining that the old man’s lagging pace would have them caught in the coming storm, and offered to escort her the rest of the way home.

Such small, careful acts of attention were rare from Tito these days, only ever offered when he sensed they were expected of him. The two of them never argued, never raised their voices, never remonstrated with each other. The chasm between their inner lives had grown so wide that they had long abandoned the effort of fighting towards any kind of shared understanding. They spoke at length of all public and private matters, adhering rigidly to a carefully constructed, polite routine: if Tito asked for a supper to be laid out in the old library, now converted to a bright, pleasant banqueting room, Romola would assent at once and arrange every detail. In turn, Tito never interfered with her daily habits, accepted her help transcribing texts and compiling digests for his work, and quietly supplied the funds she needed for her charities. All the while, he avoided meeting her eyes, pretended he thought she was out of the house to avoid knocking at her rooms, and laughed off her absences as a preference for solitude over his company.

When she had first returned to Florence, abandoning her plan to flee the city for good, Romola had made shy, tentative efforts to rebuild the frank, easy intimacy they had once shared. But that intimacy could only ever be restored by open conversation about the rifts between them, by trying to reach a shared moral understanding. Tito, meanwhile, could only ever have felt close to her again if she had returned to the soft, effusive fondness of their early marriage, a state that would have required her to forget every betrayal she had witnessed. He wanted no explanations, knew full well a full accounting between them was impossible, and never guessed how utterly she could not feign the tenderness he craved. She could be gentle, submissive, and repress every flicker of repulsion, but she could not summon love that did not exist. The gap between them felt uncrossable, a fact Tito used to justify keeping her far removed from the political secrets he guarded so closely.

He tried to convince her that he believed the Medici cause was hopeless, and that he served the popular Florentine government, which she now supported wholeheartedly, both out of practicality and sincere conviction. But faint, unplaceable impressions, subtle as the scent of incense clinging to a monk’s robes, left her deeply uneasy about his ties to the circle at San Marco. She was torn constantly: dreading that she might find concrete proof of his treachery, but driven by a desperate urge to watch for harm before it could unfold.

As they walked that evening, Tito said, “My work for the day is not yet done. I will see you safely to our door, my Romola, and then I have one more commission to fulfil that may take an hour, before I can rest as I well deserve to.” He went on to chat lightly, amusing her with stories of what he had seen on his recent trip to Pisa, until they drew near a small open loggia where a guttering oil lamp hung before a painted image of the Virgin. The street was quiet, and they had passed barely a handful of people all evening — but now they heard the heavy sound of many approaching footsteps, and the hum of confused, loud voices.

“We will get soaked through if we do not take shelter under this loggia,” Tito said hastily, hurrying her up its steps, his movement sharp and startled.

“Surely this fine drizzle is nothing to wait out,” Romola said, surprised by his urgency.
