“No — I can feel it growing heavier. Let us pause here a little.” His mind, perpetually tensed for the faintest sign of danger, had caught a glint in the lamplight: the leader of the oncoming group wore a bright red feather in his cap, and a glittering, polished sword hilt. It was Dolfo Spini, the last person on earth he would have chosen to meet with Romola at his side at this hour. He had already held one critical, secret meeting with Spini that day, and the “errand” he had mentioned to Romola was a second, follow-up meeting, arranged just after his visit to San Marco.

Tito had carried carefully forged letters to Savonarola that very afternoon. One of them bore the exact signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples, the man who had fought harder than any other member of the Sacred College in Rome to advocate for the Frate. The letters claimed the Cardinal was travelling north from Pisa, and for a number of strong, private reasons could not enter Florence itself. He wanted to meet Savonarola at this fraught political juncture, the letters said, and had arranged to stop at San Casciano, ten miles outside the city. At sunrise the next morning, he would ride out disguised as an ordinary priest, and meet Savonarola as if by chance, at a lonely spot five miles outside Florence on the Pisa road.

The full plot, for which these forged letters were the first step, was this: Dolfo Spini and his band of Compagnacci street fighters would lie in ambush on that road at dawn, seize Savonarola and the single Dominican brother he would bring with him for company, and hand them over to a small detachment of Milanese cavalry waiting nearby. They would carry the Frate straight across the border into Papal territory, far out of Florentine reach. There was a strong chance that the sharp, wary Savonarola would suspect a trap, and refuse to leave the city walls — for even when he preached publicly, his friends insisted he be surrounded by an armed guard. But Tito had left him barely any time to consider the request, and gambled that Savonarola’s burning eagerness to rally Church support for a general council, and to win the Cardinal’s help in his ongoing fight against the Pope, would override his usual caution. He had refused to hand the letters to any messenger, insisting he must deliver them to the Frate personally, and had even pretended to worry aloud that the meeting was risky, urging Savonarola to request an armed guard from the Signoria, offering to carry that request for him in total secrecy. Savonarola had only replied that an armed guard would destroy the required privacy, his eyes flashing with resolve, and Tito had left the interview fully convinced he meant to go through with the meeting.

Tito cared very little how the plot turned out. He had arranged his affairs so cleverly that every possible outcome worked to his advantage. If Savonarola fell into the trap, he would be richly rewarded with papal gold and favour. If he did not, his careful, apparently loyal behaviour would strengthen his standing with Savonarola’s followers, his position with Spini and his faction, and keep his secret Medici patrons happy, who valued him all the more for the confidence both sides placed in him.

Spini, though, was a dangerous, clumsy accomplice. He loved the thrill of plots, but had none of the self-restraint or intelligence to keep a secret. He drank often, joining the bands of tavern topers that even staid Florence counted among its citizens, and despite their agreement to pretend they were barely acquaintances in public, there was every chance he would get drunk and loud, and blurt out their whole plot at any moment. Tito had hoped Spini had not spotted them in the dark — but Spini, standing in shadow, had seen Tito lit up full in the glow of the Virgin’s lamp for a split second, and recognised him at once. Romola, wrapped in her dark cloak, had gone unnoticed, and she now stood behind Tito, tucked into the far corner of the loggia.

“Ha! My little carrier pigeon!” Spini’s harsh, grating voice hissed, loud enough to carry, as he clamped a hand down hard on Tito’s shoulder. “What were you hiding for? You did not know it was comrades coming. It is lucky I spotted you — saves us so much time. What of the chase tomorrow morning? Will that bald old game come out of his den? Do we need to get the falcons ready?”

If Tito had been capable of unthinking, unplanned rage, he would have felt it then, towards this bull-headed fool unfit to be either a leader or a tool. His lips went white, his mind racing for a way out. If he tried to shush Spini, Romola’s suspicions would only deepen — he knew well enough that once Romola’s alarm was roused, she could not be silenced or tricked. If he pushed Spini away angrily, the wine-flushed Compagnaccio would turn violent, quick to take offence and slow to see any subtlety of motive. He chose a third path, one that revealed how much power Romola still held over him: the power to make him fear the consequences of his own actions. He pressed her hand lightly, as if to signal her to be quiet, and spoke in a warm, easy tone of equal camaraderie.

“Yes, my Dolfo, you can prepare fully. But do not bring trumpets with you.”

“Never fear,” Spini said, a little stung. “You do not need to act all high and mighty with me. I know exactly where the devil hides his tail, same as you. Wait — the old fool swallowed the bait whole? His famous prophetic nose never scented the hook?”

“Swallowed it as easily as you swallow a cup of Trebbiano wine,” Tito said aloud, while he thought to himself, The brute will not rest until he has ruined every last careful plan. “Look, there are torches coming. I hear there has been a fresh outbreak of the pestilence — that must be a bier coming back from the hospital.”

“Santiddio! I cannot stand the sight of those plague biers. Good night!” Spini said, stumbling away fast, his band of men trailing after him.

The torches were real enough, but they preceded a church vicar returning home, not a dead body. Tito had invented the bier story to get rid of Spini without ordering him to leave. The moment Spini was out of earshot, he turned to Romola, and said calmly, “Do not be alarmed by anything that brute said, my Romola. The rain has not gotten heavier — we can go home now.”

Romola was shaking, every inch of her thrumming with indignant, determined resolve. Tito’s casual, unconcerned tone made her trust him even less. “I will not take another step,” she said. “I will not move nearer home until I have your guarantee that this treachery against the Frate will never happen.”

“At least wait until these torches pass,” Tito said, perfectly self-possessed, but a cold new dislike rising in him for a wife who now, he saw clearly, had the power to thwart him, no matter how he claimed authority over their household.

The procession passed, Tito tipping his hat in respect to the cleric, but Romola saw nothing of it. If it had been necessary to hurl herself and her husband over a precipice right then to stop the planned betrayal, she felt she would have done it. All the two years of careful, self-imposed discipline she had practised since her return felt to her like it had vanished in an instant. The gulf between her and Tito felt wider than ever, unbridgeable. They stood almost entirely in shadow, able only to see the dim outline of each other’s faces.

“Tell me the truth, Tito,” Romola said, her voice low and trembling. “For once, tell me the truth. It will be far safer for you.”

“Why would I tell you anything else, my angry little saint?” Tito said, a faint flicker of contempt slipping into his voice as an outlet for his annoyance. “The truth is exactly what you will be most glad to hear: I know all about Spini’s plot, and that knowledge means I can stop the Frate from falling victim to it.”

“What is the plot?” Romola demanded.

“I will not tell you that,” Tito replied. “It is enough that the Frate will be kept safe.”

“It is a trap to lure him outside the gates so Spini can murder him!”

“There was never any plan to murder him. It is simply a scheme to force him to obey the Pope’s summons to Rome. But I serve the popular government, and I know the Frate’s presence in Florence is needed to keep it stable right now — so I choose to stop him from being taken away. You can sleep entirely easy tonight.”

Romola was silent for a long moment. Then she spoke, her voice thick with anguish. “Tito, it is no use. I do not believe a single word you say.”

She could just make out him shrug his shoulders, and spread his palms in a dismissive, silent gesture. The cold, unpassionate anger of a man who feels no real rage, only annoyed dislike, was hardening in his chest. “If the Frate leaves the city — if any harm comes to him,” Romola said, after a pause, her tone sharp with unshakable resolve, “I will go to the Signoria and declare everything I have heard. You will be disgraced. I am your wife, but that will not stop me. I will be disgraced right alongside you. If we are bound together, then I am the part of you that will force you to stop this crime. No one else will be betrayed because of your secrets.”

“I am perfectly aware of what you would do, anima mia,” Tito said, his voice smooth and unhurried. “So use a little reason now, if you can: if you cannot believe anything else I say, believe this — I will take every possible step to protect myself, and I will never give you the chance to ruin me.”

“Then you promise me the Frate has been warned? He will not go outside the city gates tomorrow?”

“He shall not go outside the gates.”

Romola did not relax. “I will go back to San Marco right now to confirm it,” she said, stepping forward.

“You will not,” Tito hissed in a bitter whisper, seizing her wrists hard with all his masculine strength. “I am your husband. I am master here. You will not defy me.” He had heard the approaching footsteps of passersby, which was why he had kept his voice so low. Romola did not struggle. Her sense of dignity, sharp as always, made her yield at once as Tito turned to lead her out of the loggia, not wanting to draw a crowd and humiliate them both in the street.

They walked on in silence through the fine, misty drizzle. Romola’s first rush of hot, unthinking indignation faded, making space for more complicated feelings that made speech and action feel almost impossible. For a bright, heated moment, defying her husband openly had felt like the easiest path — but now, the habits of years of self-restraint, the memories of all the impulses she had tamed after her failed flight, returned to her. Tito’s harsh grip on her wrists, rather than stoking her anger, filled her with a shuddering horror of this open, physical conflict between the two of them, the first time they had ever been at declared war since she had come back to Florence. She began to reason that Tito would never risk her exposing him. No matter what his original plan had been, the fact that she knew all his secrets now would force him to act to keep her from revealing them. She did not need to do anything drastic, she told herself, as long as she made it perfectly clear she would follow through on her threat if he betrayed her. It was still possible, she clung to the hope, that he had never meant to help betray the Frate at all.

Tito, meanwhile, was turning the situation over in his mind, calculating every possible outcome, and did not speak again until they were almost at their door. “Well, Romola,” he said then, “have you had time to calm down now? You do not need to trust my words — you can use simple logic. You can see, surely, that if I had ever intended to help Spini carry out his plot, I would never have let my wife, a known Piagnone loyal to Savonarola, learn its secret. That would be the height of foolishness.”

Romola spoke in a low, tired voice. “Yes, Tito. You are right. You know I will defend the Republic from treachery. You are correct to fear that — if the Frate is harmed, I will denounce you. But perhaps I spoke too hastily. Perhaps you never meant to go through with it. I only wish you did not need to be seen as that man’s friend.”

“Practical men must make these kinds of connections, my Romola,” Tito said, pleased to see her struggling with her own doubt. “You fair, noble creatures live far above the messy realities of the world. Go inside and rest easy,” he added, holding the door open for her.

Romola stepped across the threshold, the weight of unspoken, unresolvable distrust hanging thick in the air between them.

Tito’s carefully laid, clever arrangements had been unpleasantly derailed by trivial mishaps no sharp, calculating man could ever factor into his plans. It was rare that he walked with Romola after dark, yet by some cruel quirk of fortune, that exact evening—when her presence was the most inconvenient possible—was the one he had chosen to stroll at her side. Life was such a tangled, unpredictable game that the sharpest skill of a wily man could be defeated at any turn by wind-tossed chances, as weightless and incalculable as thistledown drifting down from the sky.

He did not grieve over Spini’s failed ambush itself. What gnawed at him was the tight, awkward corner he was backed into: he had no way to warn Savonarola on one hand and Spini on the other without rousing dangerous suspicion. Suspicion from Florence’s populist party could shatter his carefully curated reputation and public standing for good. Suspicion from Dolfo Spini, meanwhile, would be as unpleasant and lethal as the hatred of a feral hound no chain could restrain. If he hurried straight to San Marco that very night, before the friars retired to their cells, his warning would land so soon after he had handed over the forged luring letters that no one could miss the obvious, damning connection. He could not warn Spini immediately, either, without admitting the full truth that Savonarola had foiled the plot, and he knew Spini would leap straight to the conclusion that Tito had double-crossed him on purpose. On the other hand, if he delayed his warning to Savonarola until sunrise, he would almost certainly lose the chance to warn Spini that the Frate had changed his plans, leaving the gang of Compagnacci thugs to return seething with unspent rage. This last was the risk he chose, telling himself he could charm Spini into accepting the story that the fiasco owed nothing to Tito, and everything to the Frate’s overblown caution.

Tito was furious. Even summoning his usual easy, winning smile would have felt like an unnatural, heavy effort. He decided he would not return home to face Romola that night at all.

Romola never undressed a single stitch all through that long dark stretch, sitting awake by the window while the rain beat harder and harder against the panes. The sound calmed her, in a grim way: the storm-wracked sky felt like a safeguard against men’s schemes, forcing their hidden plans to a standstill. Her mind was torn raw, not just by the final, sinking doubt of her husband’s character, but by torturing doubt of her own conduct. What other lies had he told her? What other secret harm did he still plan, hidden from her sight? Everyone who trusted Tito walked in mortal danger—she could no longer lie to herself to pretend otherwise. Was she being selfish, guided by nothing but her own pride, when she shrank from warning all the people of Florence against the man who lived under her own roof? “If her husband was a malefactor, her place was in the prison by his side”—that much she was willing to do. But was that the full sum of her wifely duty? Could she stand idle and let him inflict the injuries that would make him a criminal, when a word from her might stop him? She could not bring herself to pray. Her thoughts raced too fast, too sharply, to sink into the quiet, waiting passivity that lay at the heart of all her old devotions.

As the night wore on, her restlessness only mounted. Her overwrought imagination spun a dozen scenarios where Tito had tricked her after all, evaded her threat and lured Savonarola out to his doom. Towards dawn, the rain softened, then stopped entirely. A fresh breeze rose to tear the clouds to shreds, and clear bright sunlight spilled over every terracotta rooftop in the city. That sharp, unobscured morning light made her unease almost unbearable. She threw her wool mantle around her shoulders and ran up to the loggia, staring out over the rolling Tuscan landscape as if somewhere out there the land might give her a sign of what to do—when all she could see was a maze of rooftops hiding the very streets where Savonarola might be walking straight into treachery.

She thought of going to her old godfather, of persuading him without naming Tito or revealing too much to post extra guards at the gates and stop Fra Girolamo from leaving. But by then, the plan felt far too late. It was long past seven o’clock, and she had gleaned no specific guiding details of the plot from Tito the night before. There was only one thing left to do: go to San Marco herself.

She flew down the stairs, barely pausing to greet the sick neighbours lingering at her door, and walked at a near-run down the Via de’ Bardi towards the Ponte Vecchio. Taking the straight route through the heart of the city was the fastest way, and more than that: in the main public piazza, there was a faint, desperate chance she might run into Tito, and he would assure her the Frate was safe, so she would have no need to make the journey to San Marco.

When she reached the Palazzo Vecchio, she craned her neck to scan every shadow of its pillared courtyard, then swept her eyes across the whole crowded Piazza della Signoria. But the figure she knew so well—once painted in her heart by the soft glow of young love, now seared there like a brand by bitter, eating pain—was nowhere to be seen. She hurried on straight to the Piazza del Duomo, which was already thrumming with early morning life: worshippers mounting and descending the cathedral’s white marble steps, neighbours pausing to chat in doorways, market porters hefting their heavy bundles through the crowd. Between all the moving bodies, she caught a glimpse of her husband. He had come from San Marco, turned in at Nello the bookseller’s shop, and was leaning lazily against the doorpost. As she drew closer, she saw he was chatting with perfect unruffled ease, cap in one hand, shaking back his freshly combed dark hair. The casual unburdened sight of him, when she had spent a whole night wrung dry by fear for the lives he endangered, sent a convulsion of raw indignation through her. His utter lack of guilt, the hard proof he felt no scrap of remorse, made her dread spike higher. She spotted Cronaca and two other regular followers of Savonarola standing close beside him. A sharp thought flashed through her mind: I will force him to speak the truth before these men.

Her light, fast step carried her right up to him before he could move away, as Cronaca said aloud, “Why, here comes Madonna Romola.” Tito felt a faint jolt run through his whole frame as he turned and saw his wife face to face. Her face was haggard from a full night without sleep, but there was a sharp, burning light in her eyes far sharper than mere anxiety, as she said, “Is the Frate gone out beyond the city gates?”

“No,” Tito said. He was completely helpless, caught entirely off guard, and had to summon every scrap of his famous self-command to keep his face from showing anything more than mild unconcerned surprise.

“And you are certain he will not go?” she pressed.

“I am certain he will not go.”

“That is enough,” Romola said, and turned to climb the cathedral steps, slipping into the Duomo to find a quiet corner where she could recover from the storm of her agitation.

Tito watched her retreat up the marble steps, and he felt a surge of feeling closer to pure hatred than he had ever experienced in his life. Not far off, there stood not just loyal followers of Savonarola, but Ser Ceccone the notary, a man who at that very moment, like Tito himself, was a secret paid agent of the exiled Medici party. Ser Francesco di Ser Barone, better known to infamy under his nickname Ser Ceccone, was no scholar, no beauty, no success in life, and the furthest thing from a generous spirit. He was a traitor without a single charming or redeeming quality. Which meant, of course, that he had never liked Tito Melema one little bit.

Late golden afternoon light slanted thin through the leaded panes of Romola’s narrow workroom, gilding the edges of the half-transcribed scholarly texts stacked on the oak cabinet before her. She was just lifting her ink-stained fingers to set her pen aside, the dimness making the script blur, when the door swung open and Tito stepped across the threshold.

He had come straight to seek her, a clear, unshakable purpose hardening every line of his face—something new and unrecognizable to Romola, who had spent the whole day reassured of Fra Girolamo’s safety, softening into penitence for the public, sharp confrontation she had forced on her husband that morning. She had chided herself for the impulsive indignation that had led her to air a grievance he so fiercely wished kept private, telling herself that the unavoidable scheming of Florentine party politics, the very sort her godfather had once alluded to, could easily explain his ties to the unsavory Dolfo Spini without any deeper treachery on Tito’s part. For hours she had turned over the quiet hope that confessing her hastiness might finally break the two years of cold, unspoken silence between them. That silence had hung so thick neither had ever dared name the rift: Romola did not even know Tito was unaware she had fled him in the aftermath of their great falling out, then chosen to return, the two of them tiptoeing around the wreckage of their young love like guests avoiding the site of a death at a banquet.

She lifted her face to him now, soft with self-reproof, but the stark, new set of his features froze the apology on her tongue. He had not even doffed his velvet cap or damp travel-stained mantle, leaning one elbow on the carved cabinet directly in front of her, his dark eyes holding hers without a flicker of his usual easy charm. A crisis had arrived for their marriage: the quiet drive to mastery he had always hidden under his soft, winning demeanor had risen permanently to the surface, altering his face the way a man’s features twist tight when he is secretly, deliberately throttling something small and weak that still threatens to destroy him.

“Romola,” he began, in that cool, smooth, unhurried tone that sent a shiver down her spine, “it is time we understood each other.”
“That is all I desire, Tito,” she said faintly. Her pale, sweet face, stripped of all the morning’s anger, held only the timidity of self-doubt, making his dark, unyielding strength feel overwhelming in the small room.
“The step you took this morning,” he went on, “you must see for yourself now was useless. It drew eyes to you, and threatens to land me in very serious practical danger.”
“I know I was too hasty. I am sorry for any injustice I may have done you,” Romola said, her tone fuller, firmer, hoping her regret would soften that hard look, open the door for all the honest words she had been turning over in her head.
“I want you to understand once and for all,” he said, his tone unchanging, “that these sorts of public collisions are incompatible with our life as husband and wife. I want you to reflect on exactly how you came to take that foolish step, so it never happens again.”
“That depends far more on you than on me, Tito,” Romola flared, unexpectedly. It was nothing like the gentle overture she had planned, but words between two people who have hidden from each other for years never go the way you anticipate.
“You would say, I suppose,” Tito answered, “that nothing must ever happen again that sparks your unreasonable suspicions. You were frank enough last night to tell me you no longer trust me. I do not blame you for drawing wild, exaggerated conclusions from the smallest scraps of evidence, but I must show you what will happen if you act on those conclusions, meddling in affairs you know nothing of. Are you listening closely?”
“Yes,” Romola said, flushing hot with irrepressible resentment at his cold, patronizing air of superiority.
“Very well. It will not be long before some chance word or trivial incident sets your imagination running wild, inventing crimes to pin on me, and you will go rushing off to the Palazzo Vecchio to alarm the Signoria, set the whole city in an uproar. Do you know what the result would be? It would not just be your husband’s disgrace, that heroic sacrifice you think you are so brave to face. It would be the arrest, the total ruin, of half the most prominent, respected men in Florence – including Messer Bernardo del Nero, your own godfather’s oldest friend.”

He had made his decisive, carefully planned move. The color drained from Romola’s face, leaving even her lips pale, a thing that almost never happened to her, so little used to personal fear. She could see the terrible, unavoidable truth of it all, rising sharp and clear before her. Tito saw he had succeeded.
“You might flatter yourself you were doing some great heroic act of deliverance,” he went on, “but you might as well try to turn iron locks with pretty poetry as apply your simple, unworldly moral rules to Florentine politics. The question now is not whether you choose to trust me. It is whether, now that I have warned you, you will dare to walk like a blind man holding a lit torch through a labyrinth of tangled affairs you cannot even begin to map.”
“I was too rash,” Romola said, quiet. “I will try not to be rash again.”
“Remember,” Tito pressed, unsparing, “that your act of distrust this morning could, for all you knew, have brought far more fatal harm than any brave sacrifice of your husband you think you are ready to make.”
“Tito, that is not true!” Romola burst out, tears stinging her eyes, rising to step closer to him, desperate now to speak every hidden word. “It is a lie that I would willingly sacrifice you. The hardest thing I ever did was cling to you, even when I was furious. I ran away two years ago, that day I left the house, but I came back. Because you were more to me than anything else on this earth. But it is useless. You shut me out of every corner of your mind. You pretend I am too unreasonable to be allowed to know anything of your affairs, and you will not be honest with me about anything.”

She looked like a good angel pleading with him, her eyes wide and bright as she leaned toward him, laying a light hand on his arm. But that touch, that gaze, stirred no flicker of tenderness left in Tito. The easy, good-natured man who had never before felt anything but gentle indifference to most people, found himself turning inexplicably, unyieldingly hard to the wife who had once been the whole center of his world. For all his soft, easy disposition, he had a sharp, driving edge to his intellect and purpose, and Romola’s own stubborn, principled energy had begun to thwart him at every turn. No man who is not weak will long endure being thwarted by his own wife. Marriage, for Tito, could only ever be a relationship of easy sympathy – or total conquest.

No emotion flickered across his face at her confession that she had run away two years prior. His lips only hardened a fraction, and he smiled a thin, cold smile.
“My Romola, when certain facts are settled, we have no choice but to adapt to them. Wishes will not fill the Arno, as the Florentines say, no more than they will turn a plum into an orange. Even prayers, I have noticed, have little power that way. You are made with a set of strong, fixed impressions that no reason can shift. I cannot share those impressions, and so you have withdrawn all your trust from me. You changed first toward me, and so I changed toward you. There is no use digging up the past. We only have to make the best of the conditions we have now.”
“Tito, speaking openly would not be useless!” Romola said, stung to exasperation, the way you are when you push with all your strength against a heavy, immovable stone wall. “The deception I sensed in you is what changed me, what has kept us apart. And you changed first. You changed to me the very first night you wore that chain armor, hiding a secret from me about that old man – the one I saw yesterday in the streets. Tito,” she begged, voice breaking, “if you would only tell me everything, no matter how painful, there would be no more walls between us. Could we not start a new life, together?”

For a split second, a flash of raw emotion crossed Tito’s face, and he went perfectly still, the color draining from his cheeks. He ignored her plea entirely, pausing a long beat before speaking in a light, cutting tone.
“Your impetuous obsession with trivial nonsense, Romola, has a coldness that could freeze even the baths of Nero.”
Romola flinched as if struck, drawing back to stand tall, wrapped again in her usual quiet self-poise.
“If by ‘that old man’ you mean that half-mad Jacopo di Nola who once tried to kill me, making wild, absurd accusations I never told you of for fear of scaring you for no reason – that poor wretch died in prison, not long ago. I saw his name on the list of dead inmates.”
“I do not know anything about his accusations,” Romola said. “But I know he is the man I saw in the Duomo, with a rope around his neck. The man Piero di Cosimo painted, his hand clamped tight around your arm, the exact way he grabbed you the day the French entered Florence, the very day you first put on that armor.”
“And where did you find him, pray?” Tito said, still pale, but holding tight control over his voice.
“He was lying in the street, half dead of starvation,” Romola said. “I revived him with bread and wine, brought him back to our door, but he refused to cross the threshold. I gave him money, and he left without telling me a single thing, though he learned I was your wife. Who is he, Tito?”
“Half a witless fool, half a madman. He was my father’s servant in Greece, once, and he has hated me ever since I had him dismissed for stealing. There is your whole mystery. Now you also know I face constant risk of assassination, thanks to him. That armor you obsessed over? You must have guessed I was in danger from this man – was that why you chose to tend to him, to invite the enemy of your own husband close to our doors?”

Romola had no words left. To speak would be nothing but throwing herself bare-breasted against a polished, unbreakable shield. Tito pushed off from the cabinet, slowly taking off his cap and mantle, running a hand back through his dark hair, gathering his thoughts for his final, decisive words. Romola stood straight before him, looking at him the way you would look at an oncoming, unstoppable force of destruction, ready to meet nothing but silence and endurance.
“We need never speak of these things again, Romola,” he said, his tone as level and unyielding as it had been at the start. “That is all. Only remember: the next time your generous, ardent heart leads you to meddle in political affairs, you will not save anyone from danger. You will only light scaffolds, and burn innocent houses down. You are not yet so devout a Piagnone that you believe Messer Bernardo del Nero is the devil incarnate and Messer Francesco Valori is the archangel Michael. I do not think I need to ask you for a promise.”
“I have understood you perfectly, Tito.”
“It is enough,” he said, and left the room.

Romola turned, despair written deep on her face, and sank back into her chair. The last of the daylight had faded completely, leaving the room shadowed. *Oh God, I have tried. I cannot reach him. We will always be divided*, the words ran silent through her mind. Then she spoke out loud, as if a sudden unbidden vision had wrenched the words from her: “Unless shared misery should come, strong enough to join us.”

Tito, too, carried a new, sharp thought with him as he pulled the door shut behind him. He had long planned to leave Florence, once his position there was high enough to lift him to far greater fortune at Rome, or Milan – and for the first time, he knew he wanted to leave Romola behind. She was no longer a pleasant, desirable piece of the comfortable life he had built for himself. There was no way to have an easy, peaceful marriage with her unless he was fully honest, and full honesty would mean admitting every secret of his past, destroying all the careful plans he had laid. He was no more inclined to do that than a full-grown leopard is to lap milk from a child’s bowl. He shrank from every difficulty, every discomfort – and Romola had made his life harder in far more ways than just their private strife. He had already had a vicious, unpleasant meeting with Dolfo Spini that day, who had returned furious after spending hours soaked through in the rain, waiting in vain for an ambush that never came. Word of Romola’s public confrontation with Tito outside Nello’s shop had already reached Spini’s ears, and that small incident could easily spiral into a danger he could not control. He thought, for the moment, that he had mastered her: he had stirred her affection and her conscience with the shadow of innocent lives ruined, he had dazzled her intellect with the idea that Florentine politics were too tangled, too complex for any simple moral judgment to untangle.

But Tito was not entirely at ease. The world around him was not yet cushioned soft with velvet. Before he stepped out into the dark, lamp-lit streets of Florence that evening, he reached into his chest, and pulled on his fine hidden coat of chain armor, the very armor Romola had first seen him wear the day the French marched through the city gates.

The wintry weeks slipped past Romola the way white-sailed gliders drift by a lone figure standing on a shore—silent, nearly indistinguishable from one another, yet each carrying a hidden cargo of unspooling change. Tito’s offhand warning had tangled so much quiet dread into her curiosity over Florence’s shifting public fate that she had begun to avoid news rather than seek it. The menacing advance of the German Emperor had fizzled out, and other pressures weighing on the city had eased, but widespread hardship lingered, keeping Romola’s days full of charitable work that offered her the only refuge she knew from her tangled, unnameable doubts.

She dared not rejoice that the desperate last-minute relief that had seemingly vindicated Savonarola’s Piagnoni party had swept them to such sweeping power: hot-tempered Francesco Valori, the fiery chieftain of the Piagnoni, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year, and raced to push through his liberal reforms in the narrow two months of his term. For the moment it looked like a victory for the faction that defended republican freedom, a new bulwark to shield Savonarola from his enemies—but Romola was attuned to every faint undercurrent that fed her foreboding, that the present was nothing but a quiet brood of mixed seeds of change that could burst into tragedy at any moment. By Carnival, just after mid-February, her premonition proved right: the Medici sympathizers had stopped lying low, and were openly campaigning to secure Bernardo del Nero as the next Gonfaloniere.

On the final day of Carnival, between ten and eleven in the morning, Romola stepped out as promised, heading for the Corso degli Albizzi to collect her cousin Brigida. The pair planned to return to the Via de’ Bardi by early afternoon to claim the spot Tito had reserved for them at a window overlooking the Piazza della Signoria, where a spectacle so unprecedented was set to unfold that every last Florentine was desperate to see it. The Piagnoni had seized full control of Carnival celebrations, and Dolfo Spini and his rowdy crew had fought in vain to resurrect their beloved old lewd masques and crude, indecent pranks—there was no place for such things in a city that had formally declared Christ its king.

Romola’s mood was listless, the dull lassitude of someone dragging themselves through a full day of sightseeing only to please a dear friend. This reimagined Carnival was a historic turning point for the city, but it stirred no fire of enthusiasm in her. She had no idea that it would mark a turning point in her own life, tying a separate, hidden fate irrevocably to her own where no one could fail to see it. She chose to cut straight through the main Piazza first, to sneak a first look at the legendary spectacle while she was still alone. Entering from the south, she spotted it: a monstrous, riotously colored pyramid, more like a giant sixty-foot fir tree lined with tiered shelves that flared wider and wider down to its base, where its full circumference stretched eighty yards.

The Piazza thronged with life. Slim young boys in white linen, olive wreaths circling their heads, darted back and forth at the pyramid’s base carrying baskets of bright trinkets, while older men—some in monk’s frocks, some in the loose tunics and deep red caps of Florentine artists—helped arrange the displays, stepping back at intervals to admire the full wondrous structure. A small, tight group of onlookers huddled on the marble steps of Orcagna’s Loggia, keeping their distance in clear discontent and scorn, and Romola recognized the painter Piero di Cosimo among them.

Stepping closer, she paused to scan the objects stacked tier by tier from the base to the peak: indecent tapestries and brocades, paintings and sculptures deemed too likely to stir vice, game boards, playing cards and their printing blocks, dice and every gambling apparatus imaginable, secular songbooks, lutes, drums, cymbals and trumpets of every make, old Carnival masks and masquerade costumes, elegantly bound copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarch, Pulci and all other books labeled vain or impure, and all the tools of feminine vanity: rouge pots, false hair, mirrors, perfumes, face powders, sheer veils stitched to draw greedy, wandering gazes. At the very top, perched above it all, was the unflattering effigy of a mythical Venetian merchant rumored to have offered a vast sum to buy the whole collection of these “abominations,” and above him, even uglier, the grotesque symbolic figure of the old debauched Carnival itself.

This was the setup for the new Bonfire of the Vanities: dry tinder and gunpowder were hidden inside the pyramid’s frame, and that evening, as trumpets blared, the whole pile would be set alight, the old Carnival effigy tumbling into the flames amid hymns of reformist triumph. This strange new tradition could only exist thanks to the youth organization Savonarola had founded two years prior. No longer were Florence’s boys and young men left to their own devices, running wild in the streets with foul tongues and crude amusements. Trained by Fra Domenico, Savonarola’s trusted lieutenant, these lads—called the future hope of Florence—were taught to speak no impure word, to burn with a zeal for the unseen good that shamed their lukewarm elders, to take no joy but the angelic kind: singing sacred hymns, processing in white robes. Rows of raised seats had been built against the Duomo’s walls for them, and Savonarola constantly preached to them that they were the chosen future glory of a city anointed to do God’s work.

These bright-cheeked troops were the backbone of the new pious Carnival, a sacred parody of the old unruly one. The old Carnival had bonfires? This new one had a bonfire that burned all impurity off the face of the earth. Old Carnival had symbolic processions? This new one had processions of white robes, red crosses, olive wreaths, emblems of peace and innocent joy, with banners and icons held aloft to celebrate the triumph of goodness. Old Carnival had ring dancing in the open Piazza air to loose, bawdy songs? This new one had ring dancing too, monks and lay folk dancing side by side in fraternal divine joy, their voices lifted in hymns. The old Carnival collected alms from passersby for gluttonous excess and feasts? This new one collected far more, all for the hungry and destitute, alongside collecting the *Anathema*—the Vanities destined for the great pyramid bonfire.

Squads of these young self-appointed inquisitors went door to door, demanding households surrender their vanities. After the obvious public vanities were handed over, what if the matriarch of the house still had little red pellets from the Levant, meant to paint a sallow cheek with a false, deceptively innocent bloom? She was to bring them down and cast them into the basket of doom. What if she had coils of “dead hair” to hide the gray streaks of age under a ghastly mockery of lost youth? She was to bring those curls to her front door in her hands, not on her head, and publicly renounce the Anathema. As a reward, the fresh young voices of the boys would call down a blessing on her and her whole house.

These beardless little regiments threw themselves eagerly into the work. Coercing people, through shame and gentle spiritual harassment, to hand over trinkets they would grieve to lose, is a brand of piety that comes very naturally to boyhood. And if some stubborn, wicked man flew into a rage and threatened them with a whip or cudgel? That only made the work more exciting. Savonarola himself struggled with the flaws of this youth training, as all men who chase noble great ends do, when they lack a clear path and grasp at supernatural zeal as the only sure route. Florentine youth had once had terrible habits and foul tongues: at first, it seemed a pure unalloyed good to get them shouting *“Viva Gesù!”* But Savonarola was eventually forced to correct them from the pulpit: “You shout ‘Viva Gesù!’ far too often. This constant repetition of sacred words will make them trivial, make them an object of contempt. I will hear no more of this shouting until the next high feast day.”

Even so, that morning, when the long procession of white-robed youth, bearing their small red crosses and olive wreaths, filed into the Duomo at dawn to take communion from Savonarola’s own hands, it was a sight of unearthly beauty. Many of those young souls would carry memories of that awe and hope for the rest of their lives, saving them from ever settling for a trivial, vulgar view of their duty as men and citizens. Any deliberate, disciplined obedience is an improvement over unbridled lawlessness, and these very boys would grow to be the generation of men who fought and endured heroically through the Republic’s final, desperate struggle. Right now, between that early communion and their midday meal, they were making their last rounds to collect alms and remaining vanities—exactly why Romola saw the slim white figures flitting around the base of the great pyramid.

“What do you make of this folly, Madonna Romola?” a brusque voice cut in right at her ear. “Your Piagnoni will turn hell itself into a desirable prospect for us, if they keep running things this way on earth. It’s enough to make a man grab a cudgel and storm over the mountains, to see painters like Lorenzo di Credi and that young Baccio over there helping burn all the color out of life with this nonsense.”

“My good Piero,” Romola said, looking up to smile at the gruff old painter, “even you must be glad to see some of these things burned. Look at all those gewgaws, wigs, and rouge-pots: I’ve heard you rail against those things as indignantly as Fra Girolamo himself.”

“So what?” Piero snapped, spinning sharply on her. “I never said a woman should scrub herself plain to blend into the drab background. Pah! Madonna Antigone, it’s a crime for a woman with hair and shoulders like yours to buy into this nonsense—leave it to women who aren’t worth painting. The most Holy Virgin herself has always been properly dressed, that’s Church doctrine! And don’t talk to me about heresy! I’d like to know what your father, the excellent Messer Bardo, would have said to these friars burning the divine poets, men no more fit to imitate real humanity than an onion stuck root-down on its top. Look at that Petrarch perched right next to a rouge-pot: do these idiots seriously claim that the heavenly Laura was some painted harridan? And Boccaccio now—you, Madonna Romola, you who are fit to be the model for a wise Saint Catherine of Egypt—are you going to stand there and tell me you’ve never read the stories of the immortal Messer Giovanni?”

“It’s true I have read them, Piero,” Romola said softly. “I read many of them over and over when I was a little girl. I used to sneak the book down from the shelf when my father was napping, so I could read to myself.”

“Ebbene?” Piero’s tone was fiercely challenging.

“There are parts of them I never want to forget,” Romola admitted. “But you must confess, Piero, that so many of those stories center on cheap deceit for the cheapest, lowest ends. Men don’t need books that teach them to treat vice as a trivial joke, as if all life is nothing but a silly farce. I can’t blame Fra Girolamo for teaching us our time is meant for far better things.”

“Yes, of course it’s easy to say that now that you’ve gotten all the pleasure of reading them,” Piero said bitterly, turning on his heel to storm away.

Romola walked on, smiling at the painter’s petty anger, with soft fondness for his outrage—she knew her father would have felt much the same. For her own part, she felt no inner friction with the strict, sombre view of pleasure that sought to stamp out vice even if it must smother poetry in the process. Sorrow and joy each have their own narrow blind spots. A religious enthusiasm like Savonarola’s, one that would eventually bless all of humankind by driving the soul to feel deep sympathy for suffering, boil over with indignation at injustice, and cast off the chains of sensual greed, will always be accused of denying life’s small joys. Romola’s own life had taught her to lean into sadness, a bias that made her unconsciously dismiss lighthearted merriment. That subtle refinement of the mind we call Taste had been completely overruled by her hunger for deeper, meaningful purpose, the same way a finicky, delicate palate vanishes completely when a man is starving. She moved constantly through scenes of widespread suffering, and carried the heaviest disappointment a woman could bear in her heart—so this brand of severity, stitched to selfless, generous strength, felt no discord to her at all.

Down the sun-dappled February lanes leading toward Florence’s Piazza della Signoria that Carnival morning stepped a figure all but glowing in her faded red, green, and white peasant dress: Tessa. She no longer called the hill of San Giorgio home, not after the frightening run-in with the raving stranger Baldassarre there. Tito had arranged their quiet new house, tucked against wide, sunlit garden grounds just north of the Porta Santa Croce, for her and the children, far enough from the bustle that she could raise little Lillo and tottering Ninna undisturbed.

She had not ventured out to see the Carnival without careful planning. The night before, she had held her plea tight in her throat, waiting for the moment Tito’s work-weary scowl softened, the moment he sat in the carved oak armchair he had brought specially for his visits, one arm slung around sturdy curly-haired Lillo, the other resting light on her shoulder as she helped wobbly Ninna find her feet. That was the Naldo she loved, the soft, unguarded version of the charming young man who had stumbled lost under the Loggia de’ Cerchi five years prior, before the weight of lies and debts had hardened the edges of him. Tito himself did not quite understand the relief that washed over him in those small hours with Tessa and the children: no guile was needed there, no pretenses, no hidden agendas. The high, sweet voices calling him *Babbo* were a refuge he had never dared name to himself. Poor Romola, for all her noble self-sacrifice, had only driven him further into layers of deceit with her quiet, cold disappointment, but Tessa’s uncomplicated affection kept the last soft springs of kindness alive in his chest.

“Ninna’s quite content to stay with Monna Lisa for hours now,” Tessa ventured at last, letting the toddler plop down to pat the straw mat on the floor. “If she cries in her cradle, Lillo is so clever he goes right over to thump Monna Lisa’s arm till she wakes, no matter how deep in her spinning she is.”

Lillo, whose dark eyes stood all the brighter against his mother’s same warm light brown curls, promptly hopped off Tito’s knee to prove his point, thumping the old servant’s shoulder so hard she chuckled mid-spin. Tito laughed, and Tessa leaned in, eager. “So I might go see the Carnival tomorrow, just for an hour or two, mightn’t I? I won’t stay late, I promise.”

“Oh, you wicked little pigeon,” Tito teased, pinching her pink cheek. “What use have you for Carnivals now you’re a proper old married woman with two children?”

“Old women like to look at pretty things too,” Tessa pouted, her lower lip drooping. “Monna Lisa says she’d love to go, only she’s so deaf she can’t hear anyone coming up behind her, and she says she’d never be able to keep an eye on both babies in a crowd.”

Tito’s face turned serious. “You must not dream of bringing Lillo or Ninna into those packed streets, Tessa, or I will be very angry.”

“I haven’t stepped foot in the Piazza without leave since that terrible Holy Saturday, and I think old Nofri must be dead now, his poor wife gone too,” Tessa pleaded, her voice soft and scared. “I still remember that one Carnival I saw once, all strewn with roses, a king and queen parading under garlands, people singing. I liked it better than the feast of San Giovanni.”

“There’s nothing like that now, my Tessa. They’re only building a huge bonfire in the Piazza this year for Fra Girolamo’s Bonfire of Vanities. But I won’t let you wander the streets alone after dark, that’s out of the question.”

“I don’t want to go at all in the evening!” she assured him, breathless. “I only want to see the daytime procession. There *will* be a procession, won’t there?”

“Oh, in a fashion,” Tito said, smiling. “Lively as a flock of slow-flying cranes, no doubt. No glittering kings or rose petals, I’m afraid. But there’s the great pile they’ve stacked for the bonfire in the Piazza della Signoria, you might find that interesting. Come home early, be the grave little matron you ought to be, and keep well clear of any men running about with feathers in their hats and swords at their hips: they’re a fierce lot, and like to lop the heads off silly old women who get in their way.”

“Santa Madonna!” Tessa gasped, then saw the crinkle at the corner of his eye. “You’re teasing me. But I’ll stay far away, I promise. Only — what if I take Lillo with me? He’s so sensible now.”

“Who will thump Monna Lisa awake then if she nods off?” Tito countered, pretending to be solemn. “No, Tessa, you’d never keep hold of him in a crowd, and he’s far too heavy for you to carry for long.”

“That’s true,” Tessa sighed, a little sad. “He’s always trying to run off. I’ll go alone then. But look at Ninna, you haven’t even stopped to marvel at her new teeth.”

Tito knelt to coo over the tot, and Tessa leaned in to whisper, “May I buy confetti for the babies? The coloured sweet kind?” He poured a handful of small coins into her palm, and Tessa’s face lit up. “That’s more than enough! I won’t mind leaving Lillo at home half so much if I bring him a whole pocketful of treats.”

So she set out early the next morning, the February breeze soft on her cheeks, no heavy mantle weighing her down. She had no thought of showing off her figure — no one had ever told her she was pretty — but she had no wish to hide the new silver necklace and matching clasp Tito had given her, the only fine ornaments she had ever owned. She arranged her white linen hood just so, to show the silverwork glinting at her throat, certain no contadina in Florence had anything half so pretty, sure it would make passersby respect her as the wife of a man who could afford such gifts.

She tripped along cheerily, planning what sweets to tuck into her small wicker basket, never noticing the ragged man carrying a heavy bundle of yarn who had been watching her door since dawn. Baldassarre had dogged Tito’s steps to this very house dozens of times before, waiting for proof that the young Greek kept a secret wife there, and that morning he had his confirmation. Years in the dungeons had sharpened his vengeful paranoia to a knife’s edge: he dared not confront Tito openly, terrified the younger man would have him dragged back to a dark cell, his knife taken away, his vengeance snuffed out before he could strike. He had lingered in the Duomo a hundred times, waiting for that roaring call for justice from Savonarola’s pulpit that had once roused the crowd, but the voice that might have helped him seemed to have forgotten his wrongs. Now he had settled on a new plan: he would find Tito’s other wife, the noble one who lived in the old house on Via de’ Bardi, who knew of scholarship and treachery, who was already wronged by Tito’s secrets. If he could win her to his side, her anger at his betrayal would be the strength he needed to finally strike.

Tessa wandered the streets for an hour before she spotted any sign of the coming Carnival, only groups of country people in their holiday best, loitering and chatting, passing jugs of wine back and forth. Then she saw a pedlar with a basket slung over his arm, handing out little red paper crosses to every passenger. A red cross would look so nice hung over her bed, she thought, keep Ninna safe from fevers. She crossed the street to ask the price, and recognized the pedlar instantly: Bratti Ferravecchi, the old trader she had known back in the Mercato Vecchio. She tried to slip past unseen, but Bratti’s sharp eyes caught her, and he tapped her on the shoulder with one of the crosses.

“Little Tessa, is it you? As plump and pretty as a ripe peach!” he cackled. “You did well to run off from old Nofri, you know. He’s on crutches now, and a crabbed man on crutches can beat his wife from across the whole room without even standing up. Four quattrini for this blessed cross, the Padre himself sprinkled holy water on the whole batch.”

Tessa fidgeted, worried she was spending too much of her confetti money. Bratti haggled her down to two, then tricked her into revealing exactly where she lived, under the pretense of calling by later to collect the two quattrini and see if she had any old rags to trade. Flustered, Tessa hurried to the confectioner’s shop, her basket soon brimming with coloured sugar almonds, honey cakes, and twisted sweet pastries for the children, forgetting all about Baldassarre trailing her a dozen paces back.

Just as she turned down the narrow Via de’ Libraj, she saw the procession coming: a group of boys all clad in spotless white, green wreaths woven through their hair, a banner of the Madonna and Child held high at their head. Her heart fluttered, half in awe, half delight. But the boys turned straight toward her, their faces serious, and before she could slip away, the tallest of them leaned in.

“Sister, you carry vanity that is anathema to the Lord,” he said. “Yield up that silver necklace and clasp on your belt, that they may burn in the holy Bonfire of Vanities, and save you from the fires of hell.”

Tessa froze, tears rushing to her eyes. She could not bear to give up the gifts Naldo had bought her, not for anything. Her lips trembled, great fat tears rolling down her cheeks, when a soft, warm hand settled on her arm.

“Do not be afraid,” said a gentle, musical voice. Tessa looked up to see a lady in plain black wool, her face soft and kind, hazel eyes brimming with pity. It was Romola. “You must not take these things from her against her will,” she said to the white-robed boys, her tone firm but kind. “Fra Girolamo does not ask for offerings that are not given freely. To force her to give these trinkets up would do no good for her soul.”

The boys, unwilling to argue with the woman everyone in Florence knew as the godfather’s noble, pious daughter, hurried off to join the rest of their procession. Romola led Tessa under a sheltered archway, and helped her tuck the silver necklace and clasp carefully under the sweet parcels in her wicker basket, safe from any other would-be vanities collectors. “You said you were heading toward Porta Santa Croce, right?” she said, taking Tessa’s hand to lead her to the end of the alley. “Go straight down that lane, and you will be on the quiet road straight home. No one will bother you again. *Addio*, and greet your little ones for me.”

Tessa could only whisper a shaky thank you, staring after Romola’s tall, gliding figure as she vanished around a stone corner, certain the lady was an angel sent from the Madonna herself. She hurried home as fast as her feet could carry her, never wanting to go near a Carnival spectacle again.

Baldassarre had seen the whole exchange, lingering half a block back. The two wives of Tito, side by side, stood clear in his mind now. The noble one, Romola, had all the cause in the world to hate the man who had deceived her, stolen her father’s books, betrayed every trust she had ever placed in him. That would be his ally, he thought, shifting his heavy yarn bundle on his back, a faint, terrible smile tugging at his ragged lips. He would bide his time, watch the house on Via de’ Bardi, wait for the perfect moment to tell her the truth, to turn her righteous anger to his long-simmering vengeance. At last, his path was clear.

When Romola had told the distressed young contadina Tessa that a companion waited for her nearby, that companion was none other than her cousin Monna Brigida — and the elder woman had not the faintest idea how desperately she would soon need Romola there to rescue her. The pair had been walking back toward the main Piazza together when they spotted the roving band of pious Piagnoni youths come to a halt around the frightened Tessa, and when Romola stepped forward to soothe the girl, she called over her shoulder, “Wait for me a moment, cousin.”

But Monna Brigida had no intention of lingering. She had turned pale, and blurted hastily, “I will not stay out here! Go to Boni the druggist’s shop to fetch me — I will retreat there at once.”

The truth was, she had been tangled for weeks in a miserable internal struggle. On one hand, she carried all her usual small vanities on her person that day: rouge, a set of glossy false black braids, and a berretta stitched all over with fine seed pearls. On the other, the fevered fervor of Fra Girolamo’s followers hung over Florence like thick, scented smoke, and every old pleasure of hers tasted dull and guilt-edged now. The Piagnoni insisted rouge, false hair, and ornamental finery were not just silly, but soul-damning — and Romola herself, young, beautiful, and utterly unadorned, led a life that was a quiet, unignorable rebuke to every hour Brigida spent fussing at her dressing table.

At fifty-five, a widow whose whole sense of joy has long rested on how she perceives her own looks, and how she believes others see her, needs a great store of small illusions to keep her spirits high. Every morning now she hesitated over her toilet: if her soul would prosper better without the paint and braids, was it really worth the effort to put them on? But the moment she set down her hand mirror and stared at her bare, sallow face, sagging cheeks, and crow’s feet no smile could erase, her courage failed. If she wore her plain grey hair loose in the new Piagnone fashion, Monna Berta — two years her junior, and long her petty rival — would burst out laughing and call her a dried-up old hag. Every woman who had not converted to the new strict piety would shrug and whisper behind her back, and every man on the street would address her as if she was his grandmother. At fifty-five, she was not so very old, after all — a little touching up was only sensible. So the rouge went on, the braids were pinned in place, the pearl berretta settled on her head, and she draped her finest necklaces over her throat, certain they made it look far smoother and more handsome than Monna Berta’s lined, unadorned neck. That very day, on the eve of the reformed Piagnone Carnival, she had fought that exact battle all over again — and her conflicting fears and longings had made her desperate to run from the youth collectors of the “Anathema” rather than face them alone.

She was not fast enough. Even before she could turn away, the white-robed boys at the back of the cluster that had swarmed Tessa had spotted her, and the ease with which they abandoned Tessa to turn after Brigida was partly because they had caught sight of a far more promising target laden with forbidden finery. One of the smallest, most earnest of the group — a cherubic ten-year-old with olive wreath perched on his curly brown hair, his whole imagination fired with the belief that every small choice for or against vanity carried eternal weight — was the first to dart forward and bar her path.

A fifteen-year-old youth holding one handle of a wide wicker basket spoke first. “Venerable mother! The blessed Lord commands you to surrender the Anathema you carry on your person. That pearl-embroidered cap, the jewels that pin your false hair in place — give them over to be sold for the poor, and cast the false hair away, for it is a lie fit only for burning. No doubt you have other jewels hidden beneath your silk mantle, too.”

“Truly, they are too heavy for you,” the youth on the other handle chimed in, who had memorized every line of Savonarola’s most fiery sermons. “They weigh more than a millstone, dragging your soul down to perdition. Would you deck yourself out in the bread stolen from starving children, and wear God’s own curse upon your head?”

“Buona madre,” the little cherub piped up in a clear, sweet soprano, “you look very ugly with that red on your cheeks and that shiny black false hair and those fine things. Only Satan likes to see you that way. Your guardian angel is sad. He wants you to rub the red off.” He held out a soft silk scarf pulled from the basket.

Monna Brigida’s hot anger curdled at once into unshakable spiritual alarm. Monna Berta and her gossiping circle were nowhere in sight, and the ring of white-robed boys, with their red crosses and unblinking, unflinching candour, felt like a crowd of holy judges. Her usual confessor, the Franciscan Fra Cristoforo of Santa Croce, who always laughed off Savonarola’s rules as silly Dominican excess, was nowhere nearby. Shaking, helpless, and certain this was a divine warning she could not ignore, she took the scarf and scrubbed the rouge clean from her cheeks.

“That is a holy beginning, madonna,” the taller youth said, and reached out to pluck one of the jewelled pins fastening her false braids to her berretta. The heavy black plait slid down across Monna Brigida’s face, breaking the last of her remaining resistance. She fumbled wildly at the rest of the fastenings, tore off her beloved crimson velvet berretta with all its rare pearl embroidery, and dumped the whole lot into the basket. There she stood, unrouged, with her grey hair pushed back from her face, all the deep lines of age that fat and paint had hidden for years now plain to see.

But the boys, their mischief heightened by the thrill of the chase, lifted the berretta up high and held it aloft for every passing citizen to gawk at, the false hair dangling from its edges. “See, venerable mother! What ugly lies you have freed yourself from! Now you look like Saint Anna herself, the mother of the Holy Virgin!”

Monna Brigida’s eyes stung with hot, hard tears that only elderly eyes shed, damp and sharp. She half made up her mind to run straight to a convent and never show her face in the world again. She was certain every onlooker was laughing at her — but perhaps, she thought dimly, it was for the best, that she would be forced to think only of Paradise from now on. A rough, guttural voice boomed over the boys’ shoulders, and a large calloused hand holding a cheap painted red cross was thrust over their heads right in front of her.

“Only four quattrini, madonna! Blessing and all included! Buy it, you’ll find it a great comfort now your wig’s gone! Deh, what do us sinners do all our lives? We make soup in a basket, end up with nothing but scum for our supper. The profit is less than the smell of a single danaro, all the money goes straight to the poor.”

Monna Brigida was fumbling for her coin purse hidden under her mantle, ready to hand over whatever he asked, when the crowd of boys parted to make way for a figure more welcome than an angel sent to loose prison bolts. “Romola! Look at me!” she wailed, holding out both her trembling hands. The white-robed troop was already drifting away, their zeal sated after their uncommonly successful haul of vanities, and they felt no need to press Brigida for more offerings.

“Dear cousin, do not distress yourself,” Romola said, her heart softening with pity even as she fought a smile at the strange, unmasked sight of her kinswoman, stripped of all the adornment she had worn for decades. She pulled the black linen drapery from her own head and wrapped it around Monna Brigida’s shoulders. “There. No one will recognise you now. We will take the narrow Via del Palagio and walk straight home.”

They hurried away, Brigida clinging tight to Romola’s hand as if it was a lifeline to steady her wobbly steps. “Oh my Romola, my dear child! What a frumpy old scarecrow I am! But I will be good, I swear it. I mean to be good, just like you!”

“Buy that cross, madonna!” the guttural voice piped up again. Bratti the peddler had trailed after them, unashamed even in front of the noble, respected Romola, determined not to lose a sure sale. “Only four quattrini, blessing and all — every penny goes to the poor!”

Monna Brigida held up one hand to stop Romola from remonstrating with the peddler, and fished out a large silver grosso worth many times the four quattrini he asked for, pressing it into his palm. “Take it, good man, and begone.”

“You are in the right of it, madonna,” Bratti said, snatching the coin quickly and shoving the cheap cross into her free hand. “I won’t bother giving you change — that would be the same as robbing you of a mass. What! We all get a little singed in this life, but you’ll come off easy: better to fall from a window than a whole roof. A good Easter to you, and a good year!”

Left alone once more, Monna Brigida huffed, but her eyes were bright with earnest resolve. “Well, Romola, if I am to be a proper Piagnone, it does not matter a bit how I look!”

“Dear cousin,” Romola said, smiling warmly at her, “you have no idea how much lovelier you look now than you ever did before. I can see your kind, good nature shining through, where all that rouge and finery used to hide your expression. Ask our Piero, any painter in the city, and he will tell you he would far rather paint your portrait now than the old painted mask. Every line on a human face is either touching or noble, unless it springs from low, petty passions. Old men like my godfather look so grand and dignified — why should old women not look the same?”

“Well, that is all very well when one gets to be sixty, my Romola,” Brigida said, a flicker of her old familiar vanity peeking through. “But I am only fifty-five, and Monna Berta, and all the rest — but no matter. I will be good, like you. Your mother was my own cousin, you know. If she had lived, she would be my exact age now. One has to either die or grow old, after all. But it does not matter a bit that I am old, if I am living right as a Piagnone.”

For Romola, the final echoes of that Carnival day had at first seemed to leave no heavier personal burden than the new, gentle task of tending to her ageing kinswoman Brigida, who swung by turns between complaining bitterly of her grey hairs and making wobbly, half-hearted peace with the slow creep of old age. But those feasting days had ushered in a Lenten season that swept her up in a near-constant whirlwind of sharp mental tension and urgent, ceaseless work.

Bernardo del Nero had just been elected Gonfaloniere. After months of furious campaigning, the Medicean faction had claimed that narrow triumph, and the victory stoked Romola’s unshakable, gnawing premonition: some long-hidden, carefully laid scheme was going to ripen into either glorious success or total ruin during the two months her godfather held supreme authority in Florence. Every dim, pale dawn seeping through her bedroom shutters felt like that old fear stepping in to keep her company. It walked with her through the cobblestone streets at sunrise, as she joined the crowds heading to the Duomo for the early Lenten sermon — but once inside the vast, thrumming cathedral, the cold weight of it faded, the way a soldier’s dread of death vanishes the second he steps onto the battlefield.

In the Duomo, she felt part of a conflict far larger than the walls of Florence could contain. Girolamo Savonarola was preaching the final full cycle of Lenten sermons he would ever be permitted to finish inside the cathedral’s walls. He knew excommunication was only days away, and he had reached the point of defying it openly. He held a merciless, unflinching mirror up to the corruption rotting through the Church, calling every vice and falsehood by its true name, no soft polite periphrases to soften the blow; and with mounting, burning confidence, he prophesied a coming renovation, a mass revolt against institutional greed and moral decay. His own fate straddled two competing visions in his mind, shifting by the minute: sometimes he saw himself taking a glorious lead in that uprising, his voice ringing across every corner of Christendom, rousing the Church’s dead, stagnant body back to life the way Christ’s call had dragged Lazarus out of his sealed tomb. Other times he saw nothing ahead but persecution and martyrdom: this mortal life was nothing more than a long vigil, and the true dawn would only break after he was dead.

The grandeur of that position would have impressed any mind not dulled completely by cynicism — even men like Machiavelli, who were inclined to write the friar off as a calculating opportunist first, could not deny the raw, unignorable power of his speeches. For Romola, who shared his fire and believed unshakably in the purity of his core purpose, the crisis felt as personal as any struggle in her own family. It wove itself into every small, daily labour she performed, labours that demanded not just dogged perseverance, but new reserves of courage. The famine that had gripped Florence for months had never lifted, and the long stretch of unrelenting hardship had made every form of suffering sharper to bear. Disease spread through the overcrowded narrow streets, and word was going round that the Plague would soon take hold. Trudging wearily from hovel to hovel among the sick, the starving, the angry murmuring crowds, Romola drew strength from more than just her own soft pity: she drew it from the belief that a great hero was fighting for a sublime, world-altering cause, toward which her small acts of mercy could only creep slowly, the way morning dew freshening weed-choked soil tends a harvest no one alive now will ever live to see.

But the grand, rousing music of his sermons held jarring, dissonant notes. Those early, glowing days of hope, when the friar had preached peace, charity, and full amnesty for old political grudges, when he believed the French king’s arrival would purify the Republic, were long past. Political intrigue had proved too stubborn to let that amnesty take root. The myth of the French “deliverer”, who had abandoned his grand mission and turned his back on Florence, had done far more harm than good. Hostility against the Prophet, both petty and grand-scale, was mounting, and his enemies wielded new weapons with unrelenting determination.

Bit by bit, the tone of contention and self-defence crept into his sermons. He began to yield to popular demand, speaking in far greater detail of his private visions and revelations, adopting a sharper, more defiant tone against every detractor. Once, he had denounced obsession with miracles, saying true faith had no need for them — now he openly declared that at the exact right moment, divine power would send a public miracle to prove his prophecies true. And every time the promised triumph of good receded further behind the unignorable reality of prevailing evil, his threats of coming vengeance against corrupt priests and vicious tyrants grew sharper, stoked not just by righteous indignation, but by his own personal exasperation. For a great public orator who gives himself entirely to the fire of the moment, that quiet struggle between selfish and unselfish motive that most men hide deep in the private chambers of their souls is written out in letters of fire for the whole world to see.

But if those notes of angry exasperation jarred harshly on Romola’s ears, there was another listener in the crowd for whom those sharp tones were the only ones that stirred his blood, the deep thrumming bass that cuts through all other sound for a man nearly deaf. Baldassarre had learned the great friar was preaching again, and he came to every sermon he possibly could, drinking in that voice full of threats, as if it were a power risen up on the side of long-delayed justice. He came all the more often because he had spotted Romola in the crowds too, waiting and watching for the exact moment — both the right set of outer circumstances, and the right shift in his own wavering, fraying mind — to speak to her face. Twice Romola had caught sight of his dark, fixed stare across the throng in the Duomo. She did not want to see his face ever again, but she found herself searching for it regardless, the way men cannot look away from a terrible portent hanging over their lives. But whatever secret the old man was still holding back felt like a trivial fear next to her present anxieties: it belonged, she thought, only to the past, and her every worry was absorbed by the looming present.

The turbulent Lenten season slipped by, anyway. April, the second and final month of her godfather’s rule, was drawing to a close, and nothing had happened to fulfill her premonition. The wider public had shared her fears, too, with rumours spilling out of Rome that Piero de’ Medici was plotting a violent takeover — but in just a few days, the suspected Bernardo would step down from power, and the danger would pass.

Romola was trying to draw a little courage from the fact that all her worst fears had come to nothing, when on the twenty-seventh of April, as she walked her usual afternoon rounds of mercy visits to the poor and sick, a messenger found her, summoning her at once to the home of Camilla Rucellai, the most famous of Florence’s self-anointed prophetesses, to discuss a matter of the utmost public importance.

Romola felt a deep, unshakable repulsion for the shrill, over-excited volatility of these “illuminated” women, and at that moment she had a particular loathing for Camilla: word had spread that the woman was spouting “revelations” that directly accused Bernardo del Nero of treachery. At first she was fully inclined to send the messenger back with a flat refusal. Camilla’s summons almost certainly related to public affairs, and Romola’s first instinct was to shut out any new information that would only add to the weight she was already carrying. But it had long since become her unshakable habit to set aside her own impulses and yield passively to every outward claim on her sense of duty. She chided herself for letting her vague fears make her cowardly and selfish, and finally complied, turning her steps straight for Camilla’s house.

She found the nervous, grey-haired woman in a chamber arranged to look as bare and ascetic as a convent cell. Camilla’s thin, wiry fingers clamped tight around Romola’s wrist as soon as she sat down, and her eager voice addressed her first in a loud, urgent whisper, the contact sending a physical jolt of revulsion through Romola that made it almost impossible to stay seated. Camilla had a vision to share: her guardian angel, she claimed, had appeared to her, and revealed that Romola held secret knowledge about her godfather Bernardo del Nero that, if disclosed, could save the entire Republic from ruin. Camilla’s voice rose higher and shriller as she told the tale, ending in a fevered exhortation that Romola must obey her angel’s command, and separate herself from this known enemy of God.

Romola’s temper was the slow-burning, massive kind, and save for moments of deep agitation, she was always calm and self-possessed. She had a natural, instinctive disgust for the shallow excitability of women like Camilla, whose entire faculties seemed to have dissolved into wild fantasy, leaving no room for sober thought or genuine feeling. Before the prophetess had finished speaking, Romola jumped to her feet, trying to wrench her arm free of Camilla’s tightening grip. It was useless. Camilla held on like a crab, and Romola’s resistance only spurred her on to wilder, more desperate exhortation, spilling over her own intended limits to scream a string of other corroborating visions. Christ himself, she shrieked, had appeared to her, and ordered her to command the city’s ruling magistrates to throw Bernardo del Nero out of the high window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Fra Girolamo himself knew all about it, she cawed, and this time he did not dare declare the vision unholy.

“And since then!” Camilla cried, straining upward, wild eyes fixed on Romola’s face, “the Blessed Infant came to me, and laid a sweet, sweet wafer on my tongue, to reward me for doing His will!”

“Let me go!” Romola said, her voice deep and shaking with anger. “God grant you are mad — for if you are not, you are abominably wicked!”

This time her furious effort to free herself was too strong for Camilla’s grip. She tore her arm away and rushed out of the house, not pausing till she had hurried far down the street, and found herself standing before the church of the Badia. She slipped behind the heavy curtain under the old weathered stone arch to step into the quiet sanctuary, cut off entirely from the noise and rush of the street, where every object and every custom spoke of eternal peace that persisted even at the heart of turmoil.

She sank down on the step of the altar, in front of Filippino Lippi’s serene painting of the Virgin appearing to Saint Bernard, and waited, hoping the roiling tumult in her chest would soon ebb. The thought that stung her most sharply was the claim that Savonarola sanctioned this murderous, foolish nonsense. Not for a second did she believe he had approved throwing Bernardo out a window as a divine command: his entire political teachings were rooted in absolute respect for law and order, and she knew he could never endorse such a thing. But the painful, unavoidable question rose in her mind: if he knew how deadly and dangerous these false visions of women like Camilla were, if he so distrusted these supposed spirit-seers that he kept his distance from them as much as possible, why did he not denounce them openly from the pulpit? Why did he not shout down these pretended revelations that draped the very idea of a just God in new darkness?

The answer came to her with brutal, cutting clarity: he was trapped inwardly, because he could not separate the basis of Camilla’s visions from his own.
