He was trapped outwardly too, knowing that if he denounced these revelations, his own followers would turn on him, screaming that he was trying to smother all divine inspiration that did not come straight from his own mouth — or the mouth of his trusted confidant and fellow visionary, Fra Salvestro.

Romola knelt there, her face buried against the cold stone of the altar step, enduring one of those sickening, disillusioning moments when the great enthusiasm that had been the only force strong enough to make life feel worth living suddenly seemed inextricably tangled up in empty fantasy and willful blindness. Her mind turned with new, sharp longing toward the steady, untheoretical prudence, the quiet dignity of her godfather, who was being marked for death as if he were the evil King Agag, simply because he believed a more limited form of government would serve Florence better than the unruly Great Council, and because he would not pretend to forget his old loyalties to the exiled Medici family.

But that thought was no sooner out than the old premonition of a secret Medici plot rose up again, and she acknowledged that the popular party’s fierce suspicion was half-justified. Keeping Florence’s government pure, keeping it free of vicious tyrants, was a sacred cause — the friar had been right about that, and Romola’s reason had always agreed with him. But right then, her reason agreed only reluctantly. Her heart recoiled from that “right” tangled up in so much narrow, rigid judgment, a right that demanded men be judged entirely by shallow markers of assent or denial to political creeds, ignoring the full, complicated humanity inside them. Her affection and respect clung tighter than ever to her godfather, and through him, to the memories of her father, who had spent his whole life rejecting the lazy, cruel habit of sorting all men into sheep and goats based on nothing more than a religious or political label.

Calm would not come, not even kneeling on that altar step, not even looking at that serene painting where Saint Bernard, writing in his quiet rocky solitude, was visited by faces bathed in celestial peace. Romola was trapped in the thick, urgent press of human troubles, and that quiet rocky solitude felt a million miles away. She rose to her feet, determined to go back to the sick people in the courtyard of the poor quarter, to lose herself in small, immediate acts of kindness, and revive that sense that her life held value, now that that larger, grander faith had failed her.

But when she turned around, she was face to face with Baldassarre. He was standing only two yards away from her.

The low, honeyed glow of the late Tuscan afternoon sun gilded Florence’s terracotta rooftops far below as Romola paused on the quiet side street, the ragged, heavy-shouldered old man she had half-dreaded and half-expected to confront her stepping out from a doorway shadow.
“I would speak with you,” Baldassarre said, his voice rough and hoarse. It was plain he had followed her, waited for her there. The secret that had lurked behind Tito’s polished, smiling lies for months was finally unspooling, and Romola met his gaze with the same quiet submission she might have brought to a required penance.
“Yes,” she said. “You wish to go somewhere no one can overhear us?”
“Somewhere *he* cannot stumble on us unawares,” Baldassarre muttered, glancing over his shoulder with a skittish, hunted wariness. “Out in the open air, far from the crowded streets.”
“I often walk up to San Miniato at this hour,” Romola offered. “If you would like, I will set out ahead, and you may follow. It is a steep climb, but we shall find total solitude there.”
He nodded assent, and Romola turned to start the long upward path. For many women of Florence, venturing to a remote, half-wild hilltop with a ragged stranger Tito had publicly labeled a madman would have seemed unthinkably reckless. But Romola was no stranger to hardship, and she welcomed the long stretch of climbing as a reprieve: a small delay before the next inevitable blow of truth landed on her.
By the time they neared the line of gnarled old cypresses cresting the hill, the sun hung low enough to cast long, deep shadows across the stony ground. Romola paused and turned, waiting for Baldassarre to catch up, and watched him sink heavily onto a weathered stone ledge the moment he reached her. The thick, sturdy frame that had shouldered its way through the Duomo crowds months earlier, rope slung over his shoulder, was gaunt now, sapped by years of slavery, prison, and gnawing hunger. The exertion of the climb left him trembling, his eyes distant and vague, as if his thoughts kept slipping their moorings.
“The hill is unforgiving,” Romola said gently, sitting beside him. “I fear long hardship has robbed you of your strength?”
He turned his head and fixed his eyes on her, silent for long moments, the words he had rehearsed so many times slipping just out of his reach now that he stood face to face with the man’s wife who had every reason to hate Tito. She stayed perfectly still, not wishing to seem impatient, watching the distant, unkempt old man who looked like nothing more than a common street beggar, even as the faint, familiar pang of pity she had learned to carry for Florence’s forgotten poor welled in her chest.
Slowly, his gaze sharpened, and he spoke with abrupt, burning emphasis:
“Ah! You would have been my daughter.”
A hot flush surged up Romola’s cheeks, then drained away as swiftly as it had come, leaving her lips pale and parted, like a marble statue frozen in horror. In that single instant, every scattered, inconsistent detail of Tito’s origin story slotted into place, and she understood the full truth without needing to hear another word.
Baldassarre felt the shock of her recognition, and his voice gathered new, eager urgency, his rough, calloused hand closing around her arm.
“You are a woman of proud, noble blood, are you not? You go to hear Fra Girolamo preach, you hate all falseness that smiles and pretends to goodness. You hate your husband, do you not?”
“O God—were you truly his father?” Romola breathed, too caught up in the rush of old memories to register his question. “Or was it as Tito told us? Did you take him in when he was small, alone and unclaimed?”
“Ah! You believe me—you know what he truly is!” Baldassarre exulted, his grip on her arm tightening as if the contact with her gave him new strength. “You will help me, will you not?”
“Yes,” Romola said, not understanding the vengeful weight he put to the question. She laid her soft palm over his rough hand, tears stinging her eyes. “It is so piteous. Tell me—you were a great scholar, once, you taught him all you knew. How did it come to this?”
Her words trailed off, a flicker of Tito’s story of this man’s madness darting across her mind as she scanned his ragged appearance for any trace of the refinement of a learned man. But she did not pull her hand away, holding perfectly still, listening with new, quiet caution.
“It is all gone,” Baldassarre said, his voice cracking. “All of it. They would not listen to me, because he lied and called me mad, and they dragged me off to a dark prison cell. I am old now, and my wits will never return. The whole world has turned against me.” He paused, his eyes dropping as a wave of hopeless despondency washed over him, before he looked back up at her, fierce and urgent again. “But *you* are not against me. He tricked you into loving him, and he has betrayed you. You hate him, do you not? He tricked me too. I was a lonely old man, and he was beautiful and soft-spoken, a little boy being beaten in the streets, and I took him in. He slept curled against my chest when he was small, I taught him every line of the classics I knew, every secret of old languages, I meant for all my books, my gold, my rare gems, to be his one day. He stole my gems and sold them, and left me rotting in slavery in a Turkish camp. He never came looking for me. When I stumbled back to Florence, ragged and destitute, he denied he knew me. He said I was a raving madman.”
“Tito told us his father was dead—drowned at sea,” Romola whispered, faint with shock. “Surely… surely he must have believed that once, in those early days, when we first knew each other. He could not have been so base *then*.” A vivid memory rose up of the soft, laughing young man who had sat beside her in her father’s library, turning the pages of ancient manuscripts with her, and the tears burst forth in a great, shaking sob.
“Tears are no use for us,” Baldassarre said, with a flicker of impatience. “They only douse the fire that burns in my chest, the fire that does the work. They will only slow us down. Listen to me.”
Romola started a little, a flash of fear darting through her that this might be a madman’s raving after all, but her deep, gut belief in the truth of his story far outweighed that fear, and she leaned in, gentle so as not to wound him with any sign of doubt.
“Tell me,” she said softly, “how did you lose all your memories, all your scholarship?”
“I was very ill, for so long I cannot count the days. It was all a blank. I remember nothing of that time, until I woke sitting in the sun on a pile of stones, and everything else was dark around me. Slowly, little by little, I began to feel a hunger for something I could not name, a hunger that would not leave me. When I was on the ship sailing back to Italy, I finally knew what I was longing for: for the Boy to come back, for all my old thoughts to come home to me, for I was locked outside my own mind, as if behind a wall of stone. I am still outside that wall. All I can feel is dark.”
Baldassarre’s voice went soft and dreamy, and he sank his head into his hands, falling quiet. All of Romola’s caution melted away, replaced by a sharp, familiar pity: had she not watched her own father grieve for years over the lost son, Dino, who had thrown away his birthright?
“Once, it all came back to me,” Baldassarre said after a long pause. “I had all my wits back, I could see all my old books, my gems, I knew I held him in my power. I went to denounce him where the lanterns burned bright and the trees grew in the square, but he lied again, called me mad, and they dragged me back to prison. Wickedness wears armor, it is strong, no one will see past his fine smile.”
Fierceness flamed back in his eyes, and he grasped her arm again.
“But *you* will help me. He has betrayed you too. He has another wife, and small children with her. He lets her think he is lawfully married to her, and she is a foolish, helpless little thing, no wits to see through his lies. I will show you where she lives.”
A hot, sharp flush of outraged indignity burned through Romola at those words, the woman’s natural rage at this double betrayal of the vows he had sworn to her surging to the surface. Baldassarre felt her sympathy, and pressed on, eager.
“You hate him, do you not? There is no love left between you. I know women can hate, when they have proud blood. You hate falseness more than anything, and you crave revenge.”
Romola sat frozen, overwhelmed by the clashing storm of feelings warring in her chest, not even noticing that his rough grip was bruising the soft skin of her arm.
“You will plan it for us,” Baldassarre whispered, leaning in, his voice tight with eagerness. “I have learned it by heart, you are his rightful, noble wife. You go to hear the preacher who speaks of justice and vengeance, you will help us make things right. You can think clearly when my wits slip away, when everything but the fire vanishes. That fire is justice, it is the hand of God, it will never die. You believe that, do you not? If no one will hang him for robbing me, for leaving me to rot in chains, you will strip off that armor of his, that mask of fine lies, so he is left exposed. Then I will stab him. I have a knife, and my arm is still strong enough to drive it home.”
He slid his hand under his tunic, pulling out a sharp, worn blade, running his thumb along the edge absently, as if the cold metal was the only thing keeping his ragged train of thought from slipping away.
Romola felt her breath catch, every new truth he spoke making the path ahead look steeper and more impossible. She knew better than to spout useless pleas for mercy to a man in this fevered state, so she chose to say nothing of his plan for vengeance, buying time for his excitement to fade by asking a question of her own, her voice trembling a little.
“You say this other wife is foolish and helpless, and truly believes he is her lawful husband. Perhaps… perhaps he married her first, before he married me.”
“I cannot tell,” Baldassarre said, pausing mid-stroke along the knife’s edge, his face going blank and bewildered. “I cannot remember that part. I only know where she lives. I will take you to see her, but not now,” he added quickly, glancing down at the darkening valleys. “He might be there. Night is falling fast.”
“It is true,” Romola said, starting to her feet, suddenly aware the sun had vanished entirely below the horizon, the distant hills turning deep purple in the dusk. “But you will come to take me? When?”
“At midday tomorrow,” Baldassarre said, already dreaming that she burned with the same hunger for vengeance as he did. “Meet me where you first saw me, in the Duomo. I will be there at ten, if you do not come I will wait until noon. Can you remember that?”
“Midday,” he repeated, gripping her arm again as he staggered to his feet, the knife still held in his other hand. “Same spot, midday. And after that, we will have our revenge. He shall feel the sharp edge of justice. The whole world is against me, but you will help me.”
“I would help you in other ways,” Romola said, making a soft, careful first attempt to break his illusion. “I fear you go hungry, you work hard for no pay. I would bring you food, warm clothes, a place to rest, so you know there is someone who cares for you.”
“Speak no more of that,” Baldassarre snarled, fierce and angry. “I will take nothing less. Help me wring one drop of vengeance out of this life before I die. All I have left is my knife. It is sharp, and when the blade sinks in, a man sees the face of the one who kills him before he dies. His last sight will be my face.”
He loosed his hold on her arm, sinking back down onto the stone ledge, exhaustion washing over him. Romola knew she could not reason with him tonight, not when his mind was still burning with that hot, unquenchable fire.
“Midday, then,” she said, clear and steady.
“Go,” he murmured, the energy draining out of him entirely. “I will rest here a while.”
She hurried down the hill, her heart heavy. When she reached the last bend in the path where she could still see the stone ledge, she turned back, and saw he was still seated there, alone in the gathering dark, long after the last of the sunset light had faded.

Romola hurried through the dusk with a single unshakable purpose burning bright in her chest, a resolve that had swelled through every hour of the afternoon like a small stream rising to merge with the full, rushing current of her heart’s desperation. This was no casual choice: it was a necessity her soul could not outrun. She paid no mind to the darkening, uneven cobblestones of the streets, and did not bother to rouse slow old Maso to escort her, her boots tapping fast across the Arno bridge where the river had already turned inky black beneath the last fading streaks of crimson sunset on the horizon. She took the shortest possible route straight to the Palazzo Vecchio, the Old Palace. She might encounter her husband Tito there, for all she knew. It did not matter. She could not stop to weigh odds or second-guess herself: she had to unburden the terrible weight clamped around her heart. She barely registered the carved pillars of the courtyard as she passed through, barely felt the wide stone stairs under her feet, only stopping long enough to give her name to an usher and beg to be shown to a private room to speak with the Gonfaloniere.

She was not left alone for long with the faded frescoed figures on the walls and the newly lit guttering tallow candles. Soon the door swung open, and Bernardo del Nero stepped in, his white head held as proudly erect as ever above the heavy folds of his silk lucco robe. “Romola, my child, what is this that brings you here?” he asked, his voice thick with anxious surprise as he shut the door firmly behind him. She had already pushed her hood back, and she walked toward him without a word. He rested a firm, gentle hand on her shoulder, holding her a little distance away to get a clear look at her face. It was haggard from hours of fatigue and unceasing agitation, her hair spilling loose in tangled disorder, but there was a bright, burning fire in her eyes that seemed to triumph over every trace of bodily weariness. “What has he done to you?” Bernardo said bluntly. “Tell me everything, child. Throw aside your pride. I am your father in all but blood.”

“It is not about myself—nothing at all to do with my own grievances,” Romola said quickly, her words tumbling over each other in her haste. “Dearest godfather, it is you who is in danger. I have heard things—some of which I cannot bring myself to speak aloud. But you are not safe here in the palace, you are not safe anywhere in Florence. There are fanatical men who wish you harm, and worse—there are traitors moving among your supposed allies. Trust no one. If you place your confidence in the wrong person, you will be betrayed.”

Bernardo smiled a soft, pitying smile, raising his hand to pat her tangled hair gently. “Have you worked yourself up into this terrible agitation, my poor child,” he said, “just to tell an old man like me a truth I have known for forty years?”

“Oh no, no! These are not old truths,” Romola cried, clasping her hands so tight her knuckles blanched, as if the sharp pain would help her hold back the forbidden secret she dared not name. “They are fresh, terrible new things I cannot tell. Dearest godfather, you know I am no foolish, flighty girl. I would never come rushing to you like this without grave reason. Is it too late to warn you against every single person who claims to be fighting on your side? Is it too late to beg you to go to your country villa and stay far away once these last three days of your term in office are over? Oh God, perhaps it is already too late! If any harm comes to you, it will feel as if I brought it on you myself!” The last words burst out involuntarily, a long-stifled sob tearing through her. She flinched, correcting herself hesitantly, “I— I have no solid, concrete proof. I only know what fills me with these terrible, unshakable fears.”

“Poor child,” Bernardo said, looking at her with quiet, sharp penetration for a long moment. Then he spoke again, slow and steady. “Go home, Romola, rest. These fears of yours are likely nothing but big, ugly shadows cast by something tiny and harmless. Even traitors only betray for profit—rats run only where they can smell cheese, and no one yet knows which way that scent will blow.” He paused, his gaze drifting away from her, lost in thought, before he gave a slow, heavy shrug. “And warnings are of no use to me, child. I enter into no plots, but I will never abandon the colours I have sworn to stand for. If I march shoulder to shoulder with stubborn men who will run headfirst into pikes and gunfire, I must share whatever consequences come of it. We will say no more of this. I have so few years left at the bottom of the sack of my life, there is little left for them to steal. Go, child; go home and rest.”

He rested his hand on her head again, tender and familiar, and she could not help clinging to his arm, pressing her forehead to his shoulder. Her godfather’s caress felt like the last remaining piece of her old, sheltered girlhood, a time that now glowed golden in her memory even through its small troubles, for none of those old sorrows were tainted by the ugly treachery that had poisoned her world. “Is silence best, my Romola?” the old man asked. “Yes, for now,” she answered, lifting her head to meet his gaze with unspoken pleading. “But I cannot tell if it will always be.” “You will always have a father’s ear while I draw breath,” he said, pulling the heavy black drapery of her hood back over her head, tucking it tight against the night chill. “And a father’s home, if you ever need it. Remember that.” Then he opened the door for her, nudging her gently toward the landing. “There, hasten away. You look like a wandering ghost, no one will stop you, you will be safe enough.”

When Romola fell asleep that night, she sank into a deep, dreamless rest. Every scrap of her agitation had reached its limit; she needed to gather every ounce of strength before she could bear whatever came next, and against all her usual rigid habits, she slept long past sunrise. She woke to the roar of cannon fire. Piero de’ Medici, with thirteen hundred armed men at his back, was camped outside the city gate that faced Rome. That was the first news Maso brought her, with a dozen dubious, exaggerated details tacked on. A peasant from the countryside had run all the way to the Palazzo before dawn to warn the Signoria, the old man said, else the city would have been taken completely by surprise. Bernardo del Nero had been summoned to the palace hours earlier, he added, and was not at home. She sent Maso out again to gather more news, while she climbed to the loggia every few minutes, craning over the rooftops to spot any sign that Piero’s forces had breached the gates, or that they had been effectively repelled.

Maso returned breathless with word that the great Piazza della Signoria was packed full of armed citizens, and that every leading Florentine suspected of being a Medici sympathiser had been summoned to the palace and detained. Some people on the streets did not seem to care at all whether Piero got in, others whispered that the Signoria themselves had invited him secretly—but whatever the truth, they were giving him a fierce welcome, and soldiers summoned from Pisa were marching fast to meet his forces.

Looking back on those morning hours later, Romola could barely distinguish many real external moments from the whirlwind of old memories and terrifying imaginings that swirled through her head. She knew she had walked to the Badia at the appointed hour, in spite of all the street alarms, and waited there for hours in vain. The scene that greeted her when she stepped out of the church, standing on the stone steps as the doors were barred behind her for the midday lull, remained etched in her memory as clearly as any waking moment. There was a sharp, bitter new energy in the faces and voices of every person hurrying past, armed or unarmed. The guns were firing again, but no one flinched—people were laughing, harsh, relieved laughs. She soon learned why: Piero de’ Medici and all his horsemen had turned tail, and were galloping as fast as they could down the road toward Siena. A stout, shopkeeping Piagnone man, still clutching his pike, told her the news, a sharp edge of bitterness in his tone. “It is true,” he said, nodding grimly. “Piero is gone, but the men who plotted to let him in are still right here, hiding among us. We all know who most of them are. If the new Signoria does its duty, we will have all their names dragged into the light before long.”

The words stabbed through Romola like a sharp spasm of pain, but the evil they foreshadowed was not yet right on her doorstep. As she stepped back over the threshold of her home, her most urgent, pressing anxiety was the terrible fear that she had lost all trace of Baldassarre, and might never find him again in the chaos of the city.

The sun-drenched summer days stretched long and slow, bringing neither the truth Romola ached to uncover, nor the unthinkable disaster she dreaded. No word came from Baldassarre, and for all the Florentine government’s posted watchmen and sharpened investigations, no trace of the suspected anti-Savonarola conspiracy ever surfaced. But the empty, anxious stretch of days was not left to be haunted only by her private fears: two events landed hard on the city, wrapping her vague unease in urgent, immediate work that bound her even closer to the man who had first awoken her to a life of purpose: the creeping, spreading plague, and the formal excommunication of Fra Girolamo Savonarola.

She could not walk day after day into the pestilence-choked hovel, brimming with the sharp, bright joy of knowing she existed to soften some of that unendurable suffering, without recognizing that that transcendent urge to serve had been planted in her entirely by the Frate. Nor could she stand by and watch a man who had devoted every breath to making a real, living Christian virtue out of empty ritual be silenced and cast out, simply because he dared push the corrupt clergy to live by the values they preached, without feeling her old attachment to him reforge itself, stronger than before.

Late in the swelter of June, the excommunication edict that had arrived from Rome weeks prior was formally, solemnly read aloud in the Duomo. Romola chose to attend the ceremony, hoping the outrage it stoked would feed the strength she drew from standing on Savonarola’s side. The dim evening scene could not have been more starkly different from the bright morning gatherings she had known there: no packed crowd of Florentine faces stacked amphitheatre-style, young people ringing the old like a garland of hope, no thundering voice rousing every heart to fight for great causes both earthly and unseen. Instead the bare stone walls were darkened further by the guttering glimmer of tapers, a huddled flock of black-robed monks and dour parish priests stood hunched and listless, a droning, flat voice read out the sentence that had already been nailed to every church door in the city for weeks, broken only by the occasional faint tinkle of small altar bells. At last every taper was snuffed out, and the soft, shuffling tread of monastic slippers faded away into thick, heavy silence.

Romola’s resolve to stand with the Frate was doubled by what she saw around her: coarse, hard-faced men grinning in open triumph at his downfall, while even his most loyal followers stood shaking, confused and terror-struck. For most pious Florentines, the question of where a Christian’s duty to obey the Church ended and their duty to resist injustice began was nearly unanswerable. Their belief that the Church was a living, divine power with the right to bless and curse was no abstract opinion—it was an unshakable impression as obvious and unarguable as the curve of the blue sky overhead. Savonarola’s published writings arguing that an unjust excommunication carried no weight only made those loyal followers tremble harder, as if he was defying some invisible, all-powerful specter no sword could cut, no wall could hide from.

But Romola had not been raised steeped in those inherited traditions, not with her father who had lived his whole life apart from Florence’s devout community. For her, her connection to the Church ran entirely through Savonarola: his moral fire was the only authority she had ever bowed to. All she saw in the excommunication was the petty malice of corrupt, threatened men: on one side a man who had given everything to the cause of public virtue and spiritual purity, on the other an attack led by Rodrigo Borgia, that lecherous, greedy, lying, murderous old man who now sat on the papal throne as Pope Alexander VI. Detached neutrals might have spotted small follies in the martyr, even faint shreds of political sense in his persecutors, but Romola had no use for neutrality. The edict pulled Savonarola out of the petty, local squabbles he had often fallen into from the pulpit, and turned him into a standard-bearer leaping straight into the breach, appealing to the whole Christian world against a gross abuse of ecclesiastical power. Life never feels clearer than when you watch a brave man risk everything for an unassailable good, and that new rush of enthusiasm carried her through every hard, sweltering day.

She never breathed a word to Tito about her encounter with Baldassarre, or the terrible secrets he had laid at her feet. What good would it do? Tito would never admit the truth, he would only sneer and deliver some cold sarcasm about her sympathizing with the man who had tried to murder him. Baldassarre was clearly helpless now, anyway. Her fear was not that he would harm Tito, but that Tito would track him down, and concoct some new cruel scheme to rid himself of the destitute man who haunted every corner of his conscience. She knew she could do nothing decisive until she found Baldassarre again, and learned the full truth about that “other wife” he had referenced—learn if Tito had been bound to another woman long before he ever married her.

That possibility gnawed at her, eating away every last faint lingering trace of tenderness for the early, illusory love she had once held for her husband, wounding the ancient family pride she had inherited from her father. The horror of all Baldassarre had revealed made her shrink from Tito with a visceral repulsion that would have pushed her to some explosive, unplanned outburst if he had not been nearly absent from home that entire stretch. Like most of Florence’s wealthy citizens fleeing the plague, Tito spent almost all his free time out at country villas outside the city limits: the charming, quick-witted Melema was a welcome guest at every estate, and since Romola had refused point-blank to leave the city and abandon the plague sufferers who relied on her, he had no reason to maintain a private country home for them both.

By the last weeks of July, the worst of the public crises that had absorbed all her energy began to ease. The plague had almost entirely disappeared, and the new, sympathetic governing magistracy had begun drafting urgent letters to Rome, pleading for the excommunication to be withdrawn. Romola’s strong, healthy frame began to sink under the inevitable languor that follows weeks of nonstop overexertion, but her restless mind would not let her stay cooped up at home for long outside the hottest midday hours. Every cool morning and evening, she wandered the streets, taking new routes every time, clinging to the faint, vague hope that if Baldassarre was still alive, she might chance upon him. Sometimes she let herself dare hope the plague fever had stolen his memory again, that he had forgotten where she lived, even forgotten she existed. The far likelier, grimmer explanation she tried not to name to herself was that he had fallen ill of the plague, and died alone, unmarked, before he could ever find her again.

The summer morning’s warmth had already grown thick and oppressive on Romola’s skin by the time she turned off the shaded path along Florence’s walls, fresh from visiting San Marco, and made her way back into the tangled web of streets through the gate of Santa Croce.

Borgo La Croce was so still that the tap of her leather sandals on sunbaked paving stones echoed loud and clear in the hush, until she rounded a gentle curve and spotted a tiny figure not three paces ahead: a boy no older than three, clad in nothing but a thin white linen shift, who paused mid-waddle and blinked around the empty street, lost. At first she only saw his back—sturdy, square-shouldered, crowned with a cloud of coppery auburn curls—but the next instant he twisted toward her, his dark eyes swimming with unshed tears, his lower lip trembling and pushed out in a wail. A long shadow falling over him as Romola drew closer pushed his bewildered fear over the edge, and he burst into ragged, sobbing cries, big tears rolling down his plump brown cheeks.

Romola’s long-buried maternal instinct, the soft, untested core of all her deep, tender affection, rose unbidden. She pulled her veil back from her face, knelt on the warm stone, and wrapped her arms around him, pressing her cheek to his damp one while she murmured soft, lulling endearments in the Florentine lilt he knew. His sobs rose louder at first, but he made no move to squirm away. Then, as abruptly as children’s griefs often vanish, the wailing cut off entirely. His face smoothed out of its crumpled distress, and he stared up at her, mouth hanging open in awe.

“Poor little one, you’ve gone and lost yourself,” she said, kissing his curls. “Never mind. We’ll find your home again, I promise. I expect your mama is out looking for you right now.”

She guessed he had wriggled free the moment his mother’s gaze had strayed, and that his family would be close on his heels. “Oh, but you are a heavy, heavy lad,” she laughed, trying to heft him to her feet and finding him far too solid to carry. “Come then, you shall toddle along right beside me.”

His lips stayed parted in that still, shocked silence, one fist still clutching the hem of his shift as tight as ever, but the other slipped willingly into Romola’s pale, strong, soft hand.
“You do have a mama, don’t you?” Romola asked, as they set off down the lane, looking down at him with a sharp, unnameable yearning. He said nothing. A little girl might have chattered through a dozen stories by now, but this square, stubborn little man with the cloud of curls held his tongue.

But he knew his own neighborhood the moment they neared it. At the corner before the Church of San Ambrogio, he tugged hard on her hand, craning his neck to point down the side street.
“Ah, that’s the way home, is it?” Romola smiled. He only leaned forward and pulled harder, wordlessly urging her to walk faster. He made two more decisive turns of his own before they came to a short lane opening onto sunlit garden ground, and the little fellow dragged her toward the stone steps leading up to a small house at the lane’s end. He showed no sign of wanting to let go of her hand, and Romola had no wish to leave him until she was certain she had delivered him safe to his family.

They climbed the steps, blinking in the dimness of the shadowed landing after the bright street, until a flood of warm light poured out of an open doorway. They passed through a tiny lobby, and Romola paused at the second open door. No one inside had heard their approach.
On a low chair at the far end of the room, back half turned to the light, sat Tessa, one hand resting on the edge of a wicker cradle, her head lolled gently to one side, fast asleep by the swaying cradle’s side. Near the window, her back to the door, sat Monna Lisa, deaf as a post, chopping salad with slow, unhurried motions. Romola only had a heartbeat to take in the quiet, still scene before Lillo wrenched his hand free and scrambled across the floor to press his head against his mother’s arm, not waking her outright, but staring back at Romola from that safe vantage point, his face perfectly serious.

Tessa jolted awake as Lillo pushed against her, and looked up in dazed confusion. The second her eyes landed on Romola in the doorway, she shot to her feet, blushed bright crimson, and began to tremble, too startled to speak or step forward.
“Ah, we have met before, you and I,” Romola said, stepping forward with a soft smile. “I am so glad this is your little boy. He was crying all alone in the street, you see—he must have slipped away when you weren’t looking. We walked together for a little way, and then he knew the road home and brought me right to your door. You didn’t even miss him? That is a mercy, else you would have been half out of your wits with fear.”

The shock that Lillo had wandered so far wiped every other feeling from Tessa’s mind for a moment. The color drained from her face, she snatched Lillo by the arm and dragged him over to Monna Lisa, calling loudly, half-sobbing, right in the old deaf woman’s ear, “Oh Lisa, you are wicked! Why do you stand there with your back turned to the door? Lillo ran all the way out into the street!”
“Holy Mother of God!” Monna Lisa yelped, letting her salad spoon clatter onto the board. “Where were you, then? I thought you were right there, keeping an eye on him!”
“But you know I always nod off when I’m rocking the cradle,” Tessa protested, pouting.
“Well, well, we shall have to bar the outer door, or tie the little rascal to the table leg soon enough,” Monna Lisa huffed. “He’ll be as tricky as Satan himself before long, that’s the honest truth. But how did he find his way back?”

The question pulled Tessa back to the full consciousness of Romola standing in her parlor. She turned back to Romola, blushing and shy again, and Monna Lisa’s gaze followed hers. The old woman bowed low, then shuffled a step closer.
“Doubtless this most noble lady is the one who brought him safe home,” she said. “It shames me that he was found running the streets with nothing on but his shift—he kicked and screamed the whole morning when we tried to dress him, and his poor soft mother will never suffer a hand to be laid on him. What’s a half-deaf old woman to do, with no stick to threaten him, when his little legs get so strong? Look at those plump little legs, your nobleness.”

Lillo, hearing his legs discussed, hitched his shift up higher and stared down at his own round olive calves with calm, curious detachment. Romola laughed, stooped to give him a playful little shake and a kiss on the ankle, and the small, easy gesture chased the last of Tessa’s fear away. Back during that wild Carnival years ago, when Romola had stepped out of the crowd to save her, Tessa had asked Naldo what kind of woman could be so kind and vanish so fast, if she was the Holy Madonna herself. He had laughed and said no, not exactly a Madonna, just one of the good saints, and said no more. So Tessa had carried that half-childish, half-reverent image of Romola in her head ever since, and seeing her laugh and fuss over Lillo felt as warm and ordinary as a blessing from a friendly saint who would never smite anyone the way Nofri and the devil did.
“Ninna is asleep in the cradle,” Tessa said, pointing. “She’s pretty too.”

Romola went over to lean over the wicker cradle, and saw the baby stir, then blink open her bright blue eyes. “Ah, she’s waking up now,” Romola said. “You pick her up, and I’ll sit in this chair—may I?—and hold Lillo. Come here, little one.”
She sank into Tito’s favourite cushioned chair, and held out her arms. Lillo hesitated, pointed a tiny finger at her, half confused, half cross, and said, “That’s Babbo’s chair.” He couldn’t imagine what would happen if Babbo came home and found a stranger sitting in his spot.
“But Babbo isn’t here right now, and I’ll be gone soon,” Romola said, laughing, wondering for the first time what sort of man this Naldo was, who had a wife dressed like a contadina but every bit of her small space so neat and soft, no sign of hard work or want. Lillo climbed up into her lap, found it far softer than any he’d known, and immediately began prodding at the embroidery on her dress, her fingers, hunting for any trinkets besides the rosary hanging at her belt.

Tessa had been occupied soothing the fussy newly-woken Ninna, but now she sat down in her low chair near Romola’s knee, preening the baby’s little dress, a little jealous that the strange lovely lady seemed to notice the boy first, the same way Naldo always did.
“Lillo was going to be cross with me for sitting in Babbo’s chair,” Romola said, leaning down to kiss the baby’s tiny bare foot. “Will Babbo be home soon to want his seat back?”
“Oh no!” Tessa said. “You can sit there as long as you like. I shall be so sad when you go away. The first time you came to take care of me at Carnival, I thought it was magic—you came and vanished so fast. Naldo said maybe you were a saint, and that made me tremble a little, but I know saints are very good, and you have always been so kind. Now you’ve saved Lillo too. Perhaps you will always come and take care of me. That’s how Naldo was, once, long ago: he came and took care of me when I was all alone and scared at the San Giovanni feast, and I never knew where he came from—he was so beautiful and good. And you are, too,” she finished, looking up at Romola with soft, devout admiration.

“Naldo is your husband,” Romola said, watching the little boy’s dark, sharply defined eyebrows, far too distinct for a toddler. She did not phrase it as a question, and Tessa’s eyes went wide with wonder.
“Ah! You know him!” she said. “Perhaps you know Nofri, and Peretola, and our little house on the hill, and everything? Yes, his eyes are just like Lillo’s, but his hair is long and dark, not the curls. Oh—look!”
She reached up to the thin red silk cord slung around her neck, pulled out a tiny tattered parchment breve, a little piece of red coral charm, and a long dark curl of hair, carefully tied at the end, hanging alongside the sacred trinkets. She held it out to Romola, keeping it well out of Ninna’s grabbing little hands.
“I cut that just last week,” she said, laying the soft dark lock across Romola’s pale fingers. “See how bright it still is! The old ones get dull after a while, so when his hair grows long again, he lets me cut a new one, and I keep it with the breve. Sometimes he goes away for such a long, long time, and I think it helps keep me safe while he’s gone.”

A faint, cold shiver ran through Romola the moment the curl touched her skin. The second Tessa had spoken of her husband appearing mysteriously out of nowhere years before, a wild, leaping hope had risen in Romola’s chest, beating fast: that this woman was Tito’s true wife, that her own forced, miserable marriage was null and void before God and man. The curl in her fingers was exactly the same as the one she herself had cut from Tito’s head, five years before, and twisted around one of her own locks for safekeeping. She kept her face calm, gentle, determined not to startle this soft, ignorant, trusting thing before her.
“It is a very beautiful curl,” she said, resisting the urge to pull her hand away. “Lillo’s curls will darken to match it, I think. But when he leaves you to go away, you never know where he goes?”
“No,” Tessa said, tucking her charms back into the neck of her dress, out of the children’s reach. “But I know Messer San Michele watches over him, because he gave him a beautiful coat made all of little linked chains, and if he puts that on, no one can hurt him. And if you really are a saint,” she added, hesitating, that old dreamy awe of Romola rising again, “you would take care of him too, wouldn’t you, because you already take care of me and Lillo?”

A hot, agitated flush rose over Romola’s face, the moment the last faint doubt vanished: this was Tito’s secret household, hidden from her, hidden from all of Florence. For a wild, dizzy instant she felt almost exultant—she might not have to carry the weight of this false, suffocating marriage any longer. But the hope died fast, as she leaned her cheek against Lillo’s soft curls. “I will always take care of you if I ever see you in need,” she said, clear and steady. “But that chain coat—he did not have that when you two were first married, did he? Those days he would be gone far longer, right?”
“Oh yes!” Tessa nodded. “Much, much longer. I thought he would never come back. I used to cry so hard. They beat me back then, long ago, at Peretola where we kept the goats and mules.”
“How long were you married before he got that chain coat?” Romola asked, her heart thudding so hard she could hear it in her ears.
Tessa frowned, counting slowly on her fingers, and Romola watched the little moving fingers as if they would unlock the whole secret of her fate.
“The chestnuts were ripe when we married,” Tessa said, “and then they ripened again at Peretola before he came back, and then they ripened a third time on the hill. And then the soldiers came, and we heard the trumpets, and that was when Naldo got the coat.”
“So you had been married for more than two years before that,” Romola said. “And in which church were you two wed?”
Tessa flinched at her sharp, direct tone, and looked up at her, hesitating. She had babbled every little trivial memory without thinking until then, but that part Naldo had strictly forbidden her to tell anyone. “Naldo said I must never speak of that,” she said doubtfully. “Do you think he would not be angry if I told you?”
“It is right that you should tell me,” Romola said, her voice soft but carrying a quiet, unshakable authority no one had ever taught Tessa to disobey.
Her voice dropping to a whisper, Tessa spilled it all: there was no church, no proper parish priest, no banns. They had been married at the fair for the Feast of the Nativity, out in the piazza, by a wandering friar who had set up a makeshift altar for the crowds, then Tito had led her back into the church and vanished immediately. She had gone home, her mother had died, Nofri had beaten her worse than ever, and she had not seen Tito for months until Carnival, when he had found her following him through the crowds, and promised he would come back for her soon, and eventually he had taken her away to the little house on the hill of Bogoli.

Romola sat silent, her gaze far away. There was no proof, no legal marriage here. Her own bond to Tito was not void. All that hope had been for nothing. The cold weight of disappointment settled over her shoulders, and she loosened her hold around Lillo, leaning her cheek on her hand, not seeing the room around her. The little boy immediately squirmed, reached up both his brown arms to tug at her face, cross that her attention had drifted away. “Play with me again!” he demanded.

Romola roused, pulled him close again, and looked from his small earnest face to Tessa, who was staring up at her with that soft, uncomplicated reverence. A gentle, new resolve settled over her, like warm sunlight breaking through storm clouds. She pulled a small pair of scissors from the leather pouch at her belt, and cut off one long, rippling lock of her pale golden hair, while all three pairs of eyes—Tessa’s, Lillo’s, Ninna’s wide blue one—watched her move like curious little kittens.
“I have to leave you now,” she said, pressing the lock of hair into Tessa’s palm. “Leave this with you, to remember me. If ever you are in trouble, if ever you need help, you can think of me, and I promise I will come. I cannot tell you where I live, but I will find you if you ever need me. Addio.”
She set Lillo down gently, held out her hand to Tessa, who kissed it with a mix of awe and quiet sadness at her leaving. Romola’s mind was full of heavy, whirling thoughts, and she longed to be alone at once, but before she left, she leaned down to lay a gentle hand on Monna Lisa’s shoulder, bidding her farewell. Before the old woman could finish her low, deep bow, Romola was gone.

Tessa and Monna Lisa turned to each other at the same time, the two children clinging to Tessa’s skirts as if they too could feel the soft, strange, sacred hush in the room.
“Do you truly think she was a saint?” Tessa whispered right into Monna Lisa’s ear, holding up the golden lock.
Monna Lisa shook her head sharply, stroked the silky waves of the hair with a gnarled finger. “She is a great noble lady,” she said. “I saw women just like her, when I was young.”

Romola walked home through the sweltering afternoon, and sat alone in her rooms, turning the heavy truth over and over: her lot was unchanged. She was caught between two truths, warring in her chest. Savonarola had taught her that the sacredness of human bonds—marriage, kinship, loyalty—was the very foundation of all virtue, that to abandon those ties the moment they stopped being pleasant was exactly the sin that had turned Tito Melema from a

Tito’s claim that he stood in no danger from the unspooling Medicean conspiracy was no idle boast. For three full months, ever since he first foresaw the plot’s exposure as a near-certainty, he had laid every possible contingency to secure his own advantage. He had strengthened his ties to powerful contacts in Rome and Milan, feeding them a steady stream of secret intelligence and subtle, unmarked schemes to undermine Savonarola and the popular republican party, while simultaneously courting the Florentine Piagnoni faction far more assiduously than ever, dropping careful, unforced hints that his political loyalties lay entirely on their side. All the while he never withdrew his service from the Medicean conspirators, instead pushing to take on more critical tasks and earn their complete, exclusive trust.

This triple game came easily to him. The Mediceans operated on the unspoken rule that deceiving one’s enemies was fair stratagem, and their own shared blood ties, inherited loyalties, and intergenerational grudges made them incapable of trusting any outsider as thoroughly as they trusted their own. It never occurred to them that the lack of traditional Medicean family stakes that made Tito such a convenient, unconnected agent was also the absence of the only guarantee of mutual honor they recognized among themselves. The hardline Arrabbiati anti-Savonarola factions in Rome and Milan ran spy networks where the most convincing hypocrisy counted as the highest form of service, so no one there would ever dream of suspecting a deep-cover agent simply because he played his part too well. And the Piagnoni, earnest and uncomplicated in their devout political convictions, were all the more ready to credit Tito’s loyalty to them precisely because he never affected any of the friar’s religious fervor, making his political alignment feel unfeigned.

The three months had been a stretch of unbroken, flattering success for Tito, and the goal he most coveted was a permanent, high-ranking post in one of the great northern Italian capitals, where life was softer, and the rewards for talent and learning far more glittering than anything Florence could offer. At present, Milan pulled ahead of Rome in his calculations: if he could deliver a few critical favors to Duke Ludovico Sforza before the year ended, he was assured of a place at the Milanese court that far outstripped any possible advantages a position in Rome could grant.

He had long foreseen the Medicean plot would eventually be uncovered, but he had never guessed how. The arrest of Lamberto dell’ Antella, caught red-handed with a damning letter and burning with personal rancor against the Medici, was a total, unforeseen wild card. Tito had never expected to escape implication if the full conspiracy came to light, no matter how carefully he had hidden his own agency behind layers of pretext. But his sharp, quick mind had mapped out the perfect path to safety almost instantly, one that would cost him as little unpleasantness as possible. Any man would prefer to keep his whole skin, after all — and Tito’s skin was peculiarly sensitive to the chill of public disapproval.

His reckoning was not wrong. By the time he turned for home that night, he had secured the three guarantees he cared for most: all charges of complicity in the Medicean plot against him would be dropped, he would retain his secretary post for a full year before he chose to resign it, and the price he had paid for those concessions would be sealed forever as a strict state secret. Most men would have called that price ruinously heavy, and Tito would far rather not have paid it at all.

He had gone first to win over Francesco Valori, not only his direct superior as one of the Ten of War, but the head of the Piagnoni and a core member of the special council appointed to investigate the conspiracy’s evidence. Valori was a man of fierce, unyielding virtue warped by violent partisanship, an arrogant temper that alienated even his allies, and a list of bitter personal feuds at the very top of which sat his lifelong hatred for Bernardo del Nero. Tito secured a private audience, extracted a pledge of secrecy, and openly confessed his past work for the Mediceans — framing it as a pragmatic move he had always known was futile, and swearing his true loyalties lay fully with the popular republican government. He even confided a faked personal dislike for del Nero to Valori, before dropping the critical revelation that a second, unexposed Medicean plot was underway: Piero de’ Medici was gathering forces in Romagna, and planned to march on Florence to seize power before the end of August. If the council granted him the immunity and guarantees he asked for, he claimed, he could travel to Siena and Romagna to retrieve the hard documentary evidence that would cement the conspirators’ guilt — but it was essential he kept his cover as a loyal Medicean agent fully intact, and his role as an informant remain entirely secret.

Valori never paused to weigh the moral character of Tito’s proposal. All his focus was fixed on securing an unarguably harsh sentence for the five arrested conspirators, for every prominent family in Florence would soon lobby fiercely for their clemency. Legal procedure in 1490s Florence ran on the unspoken understanding that inconvenient evidence could only be extracted through torture; Lamberto dell’ Antella had already been tortured to make him spill every name he could possibly recall, but the council still lacked ironclad proof against Bernardo del Nero, who had never actively plotted to overthrow the republic, merely failed to report the conspiracy after he had tried and failed to talk the others out of it. Valori was absolutely convinced the safety of Florence required del Nero’s guilt to be spelled out in unignorable, unassailable terms — a conviction stoked far hotter by his personal hatred, even if he never admitted that truth to himself.

He accepted Tito’s offer, laid the proposal before his fellow council members without ever naming his source, and won their unanimous approval. Late that afternoon, Tito was brought before the twenty elders of the special council, standing graceful and unflappable as he laid out the details of the hidden second plot, speaking of treason and conspiracy with easy, unaffected phrasing, and framing his choices as the honest product of practical self-interest rather than lofty, unrealistic patriotism — an affectation so polished none of the men spotted it for the lie it was. They gave him official orders to leave for Siena at dawn, and tucked inside his tunic he carried a signed, written guarantee of his full immunity and his right to keep his post for a full year.

A handful of those councilmen, sharp sober minds not fully preoccupied with the urgency of the investigation, passed a quiet new judgment on Tito that day: this clever, useful Greek would not be a man to invite to family dinners, but he was perfect for the messy, underhanded work of state no man of good public reputation could sully his hands with. Their great poet Pulci had summed up that universal Florentine attitude for generations: *“Il tradimento a molti piace assai, / Ma il traditore a gnun non piacque mal.”* Many love the fruits of treachery well, but none the traitor for his part can tell. Their world was a messy, half-forged state of struggling order, where a man could be hanged for murder and hailed a martyr for the exact same act, hissed for a cowardly betrayal and hissed just as loud for speaking unvarnished, generous truth.

Tito was fully aware he had sunk a little in their estimation. He had always prided himself on being clever enough to act as he pleased while never giving anyone a clear reason to disapprove of him — the only person who had ever seen through him completely was Romola, which was why the rift between them had been uncrossable. The faint cold whiff of quiet disdain he could sense in the council chamber stung like unwelcome weather. But he reasoned to himself that no clear-headed, intelligent man would ruin a perfectly built, promising future simply because the Medicean faction lost. He bore Bernardo del Nero no real ill will, he had even genuinely liked Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, and served them faithfully when their side had seemed poised to win. He told himself no sensible man let his life be ruled by childish loyalty, family pride, or the delusional religious fervor of fanatics who believed in a coming heavenly renovation of Florence. Look at Pontanus, the great Latin poet and philosopher, who had given the grandest oration of his life welcoming the French king who came to dethrone his own royal patron, and still held his head high and prospered. Men never truly cared about moral scruples, he reasoned, they only cared if you held power. Weakness was the only thing the world ever truly scorned.

This act of deliberate betrayal, Tito knew, was a new threshold he had never crossed before. His earlier betrayal of Baldassarre had been hidden behind layer after layer of successful lies, but this new role as informant could never be fully concealed — he would carry the constant risk of public disgrace with him for the rest of his days. But it was temporary. In six months, a year at most, he would turn his back on Florence forever, leave behind all their petty, bitter factional fights, their fading fortunes. He was twenty-eight, at the peak of his wit and charm, he had already stashed a large sum of his fortune safely outside the city walls. He could shed every mistake of his past — his messy unhappy marriage, the ghost of Baldassarre, all his old tangled loyalties — like an old rehearsal costume, throw away the ragged old bundle, and robe himself for the grand, glittering real life that waited for him in a northern royal court.

He never even noticed, as he brushed past a man in the dim lantern glow on his way down the palace stairs, that the man who had stepped aside to watch him go was Ser Ceccone — another wily informant, perfectly happy to sell out his former employers to the state for his own profit, his sharp eyes fixed on Tito’s retreating back.

Tito had scarcely returned to Florence from Siena before he set out on a second urgent journey, and did not come back until the seventeenth of August. Nearly a fortnight had dragged past since the arrest of the five prominent citizens accused of plotting to restore the Medici, and still they languished in the city dungeons, their fates hanging entirely unwritten, no verdict handed down to end the limbo that stretched thin the nerves of every partisan in Florence.

For Romola, those two weeks had blotted out every other care, her whole life narrowing down to watching the ebb and flow of odds that might save her godfather, Bernardo del Nero, and the other imprisoned men. At times, hope seemed almost tangible: the long delay worked in their favour, letting influential allies quietly marshal support for leniency, as every official tasked with judging the case shrank from the odium of making a final call. One faction roared that the Republic would be fatally endangered by any mercy to the plotters, that to spare them was to invite every exiled Medicean ally to march on the city gates. The other side knew full well that to sentence five men of such ancient, distinguished Florentine lineages to death and confiscation would breed generations of bitter, vengeful relatives who would never rest until every judge who signed the warrant had paid for it.

The official responsibility for criminal judgements rested with the Eight, the magistracy that oversaw Florence’s justice system, and a sentence of death required a majority of six votes – the famous sei fave, or “six beans”, named for the black and white dried beans citizens used to cast their secret votes, a small, absurd marker of the republic’s parsimonious approach to political representation. But the Eight dared not bear the weight alone, and begged the Signoria – the ruling Gonfaloniere and his eight Priors – to share the burden. The Signoria in turn shrugged off the onus, proposing instead that the accused be allowed to appeal to the Great Council, per the law Savonarola had pushed through to wide acclaim nearly three years prior.

The friends of the prisoners resisted that appeal, though, determined to drag out the delay as long as possible – and strict legal precedent did say an appeal could only be filed after a formal sentence had already been passed. Their resistance won out, and a compromise was struck: the verdict would be decided by a special, broad assembly convened that very seventeenth of August, made up of all the highest magistrates, the eighty-member Senate, and a select body of leading citizens.

That same day, Romola, her anxiety spiking at the thought her godfather’s fate might be decided before sundown, had secured permission for a second, closely supervised visit to his cell, with two guards posted within earshot the whole time. She had walked back to the Via de’ Bardi house with her cousin Monna Brigida, still entirely in the dark about what the special council had voted to do. Monna Brigida had immediately hurried off again to wait for news at the home of a relative who sat on one of the magistracies, determined to bring back the first authentic, unrumoured account of the outcome.

Romola dropped onto the first bench she spotted in the bright, frescoed saloon, too shaken, too sick at heart to care about decorum or notice the fine statues and tapestries around her. She sat with her back to the door, her head resting in her hands, waiting. It felt like an hour had passed since Brigida left, so when the door creaked open, she did not even lift her head – until the step on the stone floor told her at once this was not her cousin.

Since that terrible night she had turned Tito out of her rooms, she had held no concrete, provable evidence that he had bought his own safety by betraying his old father Baldassarre, or the secrets of the conspiracy, to the Signoria. To the contrary, all she could see was evidence that the exiled Mediceans still trusted him, believed he was running their secret errands across Romagna while posing as an agent of the Florentine republic. No official had ever named him as a conspirator, the evidence against the plotters was murky enough to leave his name entirely clear. But Romola’s suspicion would not budge: the horror of what she had seen him do to the old man blotted out every other fact about him, as if she had watched him commit a murder and could never shake the sick, certain impression his hands were still sticky with fresh blood.

A cold shudder ran down her spine as his step crossed the threshold. She could not turn, could not stand to greet him. He said nothing at first, pausing only a moment before taking a seat across the table, directly facing her. Then she lifted her eyes to meet his, still completely silent. He showed no irritation, his voice even and calm.
“This meeting mirrors our parting, Romola. But I know full well you are living through a moment of terrible, gnawing suspense. I have come, if you will listen, to bring you the relief of real hope.”

She started, shifting her weight, staring at him with open, distrustful doubt.
“The council has voted to prorogue, to delay any final verdict, until the twenty-first. The Eight were finally bullied into issuing a formal sentence of condemnation, but a formal demand has now been filed on behalf of the five prisoners to allow them to appeal to the Great Council.”

Romola’s face softened, the doubt melting away. She leaned forward, eager. “When will that appeal be heard?”
“It has not been granted yet. But it could be. The special council will meet again on the twenty-first to vote on whether to allow the appeal at all. That gives three full days, a window where favour can be called in, influence can be brought to bear, and odds can shift sharply in the prisoners’ favour.”

Romola sprang to her feet, colour flooding her cheeks, her hands trembling. In that rush of hope, all her loathing for Tito vanished for a heartbeat.
“No doubt you have already thought of what I was about to suggest,” Tito said, standing as well. “You are thinking of the Frate.”
“I am,” Romola said, surprised. “Has he acted already? Is there news of him?”
“Only this: it was Messer Francesco Valori’s rabid, violent anger that pushed most of the council to vote for condemnation today. Half the men who sided with the death sentence did it out of fear, not conviction. There are dozens of Fra Girolamo’s followers in that special council and out of it who are dead set against putting five prominent citizens to death – Piero Guicciardini, for example, who was one of the Signoria’s most vocal resisters of the sentence, and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a devout Piagnone who will not easily forgive the death of his own brother Niccolò.”
“But how can they possibly deny the appeal?” Romola demanded, indignant. “It is the law! One of the proudest achievements of this very popular government, won by the Frate’s own preaching.”
“They claim this is an exceptional case, full of loud bluster about the Republic being on the brink of destruction. No amount of argument could stop them voting to adjourn, though. A single, powerful, well-placed influence used over these next three days could stiffen the spines of all the waverers who want to grant the appeal, and rein in Valori’s headlong hatred. I heard myself that the Frate has already sent a message to Valori to plead for mercy for Lorenzo Tornabuoni. You are the only lay person with regular, open access to the Frate. It would be more than worth your while to go to him now.”
“You are right,” Romola said, her eyes distant, lost in thought. “I cannot believe the Frate would ever stand by and let that law he fought so hard for be overturned.”
“I heard half the crowd in the Palazzo courtyard saying before I left that it would bring eternal shame on Fra Girolamo if he let a government made up almost entirely of his own followers deny that appeal, without lifting a single protest – after spending years boasting in his sermons and his books that he was the one who won that law for Florence.” Tito paused, his tone silkily smooth. “Though between us, for all your Frate’s gifts, he has fallen into the habit of preaching that violent sort of ‘human sacrifice’ that calls for killing tyrants and dissidents, and some of his followers are now wondering how to square that with showing mercy to these five men today.”

Romola winced, her face tight with pain. “I know. I know. He is driven to those excesses of speech, it was not always so. I will ask for an audience at once. I cannot rest until I speak to him. I trust the greatness of his heart.” She was not looking at Tito now, her gaze fixed on the floor, barely even registering that he was the one speaking to her.
“Then do not waste a moment,” Tito said, picking up his cap and twisting it in his hands, making as if to leave. “And now, Romola, I hope that in spite of all your prejudice against me, you can see that my own wishes are aligned with yours here. You will not hold the mere fact that I escaped implication in the conspiracy, that I kept my life, against me as an unforgivable crime.”

A jolt ran through Romola, sharp as an electric shock, dragging her back to full, sharp awareness of the man standing before her. She stared at him, silent.
“At the very least,” he added, his tone hardening a little, “you could treat our intercourse as something more than a test where if an evil deed is possible for a man, he must have done it. Am I the only person in the whole city you cannot extend your famous charity to?”

All the pain and resentment she had held back for a fortnight surged up in her, rising like a high tide that would not be dammed. She spoke with unflinching decision, unconcerned for any consequences.
“It is too late, Tito. A lie once told, a suspicion once planted, can never be killed. And I know everything now. I know who that old man was. He was your father. The man who raised you, who gave you everything, who risked his life for you more than once. Next to the horror of what you did to him, the fact that you broke my trust, and betrayed my father’s memory, is a small thing. As long as you lie about him, deny the truth of what you did, there is a chasm of loathing between us that can never be crossed. We are both human beings. I have a soul of my own, and it recoils from every action you take. Our marriage is nothing but a pretence. A perpetual lie can never be a sacred union.”

Tito did not reply at once, calculating, already spiked with alarm. When he spoke, his tone was carefully measured, cold and deliberate.
“And you intend to act on that independence by leaving me, I presume?”
“I do,” Romola said, sharp and impetuous.
“And what if I refuse to let you go?” Tito said, his tone sharpening with quiet menace. “The law gives me every right to keep my wife at my side. Will you run through every street in Florence screaming your story? Drag that mad, would-be assassin old man out of the gutters to prove your point, who everyone knows will do anything you tell him to get his revenge on me? Will you run first to the Signoria and tell them I am a secret Medicean conspirator, then run to the exiled Mediceans and tell them I betrayed their secrets? What proof do you have? Only your own word, that you are so certain I am capable of every evil imaginable. What a position that would be for a respected noble Florentine wife! If you did somehow manage to drag my name through the mud, you would be the most famous wronged heroine the Greek stage ever saw.” He paused, watching her stand mute, sensing he had the upper hand. “I do not believe you have any other grievance against me. Only that I failed to live up to some lofty, vague ideal of a husband you set for me, so you withdrew your affection, and left me to carefully tend to your public reputation as a pious, charitable leading Piagnone lady. You will find you cannot make the city believe your story. But no doubt you would start by running to your beloved godfather Bernardo del Nero with your wild tales, would you not?”

“Why am I even wasting breath talking about myself?” Romola cried out in anguish, sinking back onto her chair. It felt loathsome, selfish, to waste a single second fighting over their broken marriage when five men’s lives hung in the balance.

She did not notice when Tito slipped silently out of the room. She had no idea how long she sat there, lost in her own thoughts, until the door creaked open and Monna Brigida stepped over the threshold. Romola sprang to her feet at once.
“Cousin. We must go to San Marco this very instant. I have to see my confessor, Fra Salvestro – and the Frate.”

The first gilded glow of Tuscan dawn had barely touched the terracotta rooftops of Florence when Romola set out for San Marco. The evening before, she had persuaded Fra Salvestro to secure her an audience with Fra Girolamo Savonarola in the convent’s chapter house. Savonarola was famously rigorous about guarding his reputation from the faintest whisper of scandal, so such private interviews were extraordinarily rare, and never held behind closed, unobserved doors: the hour chosen was one when the outer cloisters would be thronged with other lay visitors, no room left for gossip of secret conspiracy.

She chose to wind through the busiest heart of the city, eager to read the pulse of public feeling. Every sun-dappled loggia, every sheltered piazza corner, every shop that doubled as a regular meeting spot for gossips hummed with heated, free-flowing debate. Trade was languishing so badly that every idle artisan, merchant, and clerk had hours to spend arguing politics. The factions for and against executing the recently uncovered Medicean conspirators were working furiously over the three-day grace period to sway the crowd, and handbills were already circulating everywhere: one set blared in huge, unmissable capital letters that to spare the conspirators meant total ruin for the Republic, the other screamed just as loudly that the law must be upheld and every condemned man granted his legal right to appeal to the Great Council. Around these bold, black headline phrases swarmed dense paragraphs of smaller, less eye-catching text, full of fine-grained arguments no one much bothered to read—for in that first giddy flush of the printing press’s novelty, Florence had decided no argument was more convincing than a bold, question-begging slogan in all caps.

But Romola was desperate to read those smaller, detailed arguments, and hurried along, craning her neck at every step to spot a spare copy. For blocks she only saw handbills clutched in the hands of avid readers, or pasted to walls where city sbirri were already tearing them down in ragged strips. It was only when she picked up her pace behind the Baptistery of San Giovanni, deliberately avoiding the hordes of acquaintances who loitered on the main piazza, that she spotted the familiar peddler Bratti, hawking stacks of the handbills to passersby for small coins. She had long known the humble street vendors of Florence like old friends, and walked straight over to him.
“Bratti, do you have both sorts of handbill? Hand me one of each, quickly.”
“Two sorts, Madonna,” Bratti said, peeling apart the damp printed sheets at a maddeningly slow pace that tested Romola’s frayed patience. “There’s ‘Law’ and there’s ‘Justice.’”
“Which sells faster?”
“‘Justice’ goes quickest by far, so I raised the price to two danari. But then I thought, ‘Law’ is good ware too, deserves to cost just as much as ‘Justice’—no one values cheap things, eh? If I sold ‘Law’ for one danaro I’d be doing it wrong. I’m a fair trader. Both came free, both sell for a fair profit. D’you want more than one of each?”
“No, no—here’s a white quattrino for the pair,” Romola said, tucking the papers into her sleeve and hurrying onward.

She was soon stepping into San Marco’s outer cloisters, where Fra Salvestro waited beneath the arches, chattering away to a group of lay visitors as he so loved to do. In the wake of Savonarola’s excommunication, crowds had once feared to be seen at the convent, but under the current government friendly to the Frate, that timidity had all but vanished. Romola recognized one of the visitors at once: Andrea Cambini, a staunch ally of Francesco Valori, who was gesturing wildly as he recounted some story, while Fra Salvestro listened with that familiar, trivial, gossip-hungry curiosity that always struck Romola as unfit for a confessor. She caught disjointed phrases about the conspirators’ fate drifting over to her, and her irritation spiked. Rather than approach the group, she caught Fra Salvestro’s eye, nodded toward the chapter house door, and he signed his understanding before vanishing into the inner cloister. A lay brother was waiting to open the chapter house door for her, and closed it softly behind her as she stepped inside.

The faded, mournful frescoed saints on the walls looked down on her just as they had the day she had come to the convent after her brother Dino’s death. The old memory tugged at her, soft and indistinct, blending into her sharp, present terror for her godfather’s life, but she gave it no room to linger. She pulled the handbills from her sleeve to read while she waited for Savonarola, but she had barely finished the one arguing for the right of appeal when the door creaked open. She folded the papers tight in her hand and stood, expectant.

She knelt to accept his blessing, as all visitors did for private audiences, and stood back a few paces once he had spoken his benediction. It had been months since she had seen him, during his long seclusion after the excommunication, and the marks of endless overwork, fasting, and sleepless nights had carved themselves deeper into his face. But what struck her most was a harder, less definable shift: the soft, serene elevation and warm, human fellowship that had once drawn her so deeply to him was no longer there, not as she remembered it. Half of it was her own alienation, born of the quiet grief that he had not stood by her godfather already. Half of it was the new hardness in his gaze, the preoccupation of a man who had already spent days arguing this matter with himself, and did not want to reopen the debate.
“It is a weighty matter that has brought you here, my daughter,” he said, his tone gentle from deliberate self-control, not spontaneous warmth. “I know you do not come to me over small trifles.”
“Father, you know what I need before I even say it,” Romola burst out, all pretense falling away as her plea poured out. “I am here for the life of the old man I have loved second only to my own father for every day I can remember. I beg you not to stay silent: use your power over the people of Florence to stop them from denying their own fellow citizens a right that you yourself fought so hard to win for them.”
“I do not meddle in the functions of the State, my daughter,” Savonarola said, clearly reluctant to rehash the internal debate he thought he had already settled. “I have preached and labored for Florence to have a just government, for a just government is needed to nurture the Christian life—but I do not interfere in specific disputes that are the province of experienced, sworn citizens.”
“Surely, father—” Romola began, then broke off, checking her own impetuosity. She was suddenly acutely aware that she was remonstrating with the man who had once been her whole source of guidance and strength, and the act of pushing back bruised her own long-held reverence.
Savonarola was sharp enough to read her quiet conflict, and too noble to retreat into the slippery, self-justifying evasions he often fell back on while preaching before great crowds. “Speak what is in your heart, my daughter,” he said, standing with his arms folded, watching her calmly.
“I was going to say that this matter is more important than so many others you have preached on with fiery fervor,” Romola said, her passion rising again. “If you counted it a victory to win the Florentines the right of appeal to the Great Council, how can you stand by and let that very right be snatched away from the first men who ever need it? That touches the Christian life far closer than debating whether the Dauphin would die, or whether Pisa would be conquered.”
A faint, flicker of pain twitched at Savonarola’s strong lips before he spoke. “My daughter, I only speak as I am given to speak: I do not command visions of the future outside the common wisdom of men. As for the Appeal law, I fought for it so that no Florentine could lose his life or property to the private hatred of a small handful of men in power. But these five men have plotted to overthrow our free government and bring back a corrupt tyrant, and they were condemned with the assent of a huge gathering of their fellow citizens. They refused to bring their case before the Great Council at the start, when they were first arrested. They have forfeited their right to appeal.”
“How can they have forfeited it?” Romola cried. “The right to appeal exists for people after condemnation, and they were not condemned until this week. Forgive me, father, but it is private hatred that denies them this right: it is the violence of a small few that frightens the rest into agreeing. You know the assembly was split again the moment after it looked to be of one mind. You yourself once preached, more earnestly than any other lesson, that there must be no room for party strife to spill over into bloodshed, no chance for private ill will to play out in public punishments. You know private grudges are at work here. Will you not interpose on the side of mercy, when so many hold mercy is also the side of law and justice?”
“My daughter,” Savonarola said, with more visible emotion than he had yet shown, “there is a mercy that is weakness, that is treason against the common good.
