Florence’s safety, which means more than just the welfare of its own people, demands severity now, just as it once demanded mercy. These men are not condemned for a plot that is already past: they are condemned for a plot that was still to be carried out. The tyrant is still gathering his troops in Romagna, and Florence’s enemies across all of Italy wait for any chance to crush us.”
“What plot?” Romola said, flushing, her hands trembling with shock.
“You hold papers in your hand, I see,” Savonarola said, nodding at the crumpled handbills in her fist. “The other one will tell you the government has uncovered new, positive evidence.”
Romola unfurled the unread handbill and saw the news of the second, planned August uprising at once. A cold, sick lurch went through her: she knew at once her husband Tito had turned that evidence over to the authorities to secure his own safety, buying his immunity with the lives of others. She crushed the paper tight in her fist.
“Father,” she said, new fire in her voice, “what safety is there for Florence if the worst, most treacherous men always escape scot-free? You yourself interceded with Francesco Valori to spare Lorenzo Tornabuoni, that two-faced man who flattered you and San Marco endlessly to curry favor—but my godfather Bernardo del Nero has always been honest, he never pretended to be your follower. Ask all of Florence which of the five condemned men has the truest heart, and no one will name anyone but Bernardo. You have already interfered to save one prisoner. You are not neutral. And your word could spare him.”
“I do not desire Bernardo’s death,” Savonarola said, his face flushing darkly. “It would be enough if he were exiled from the city.”
“Then why will you not speak up?” Romola burst out, all her long-suppressed indignation boiling over. “Why not save a seventy-five-year-old man from dying an ignominious death, grant him at least the fair chance the law promises? It is not that you are bound to neutrality! You spoke for Tornabuoni because he is friendly to San Marco. Bernardo does not feign friendship. He is not to die as a Medicean traitor—he is to die because you do not love him!”
Silence fell heavy in the frescoed room. Romola stood breathless, cheeks burning, lips quivering, and heard her own words echo in her head, jarring so painfully against all the old, reverent memories she held of Savonarola that she felt as if she had committed sacrilege. Her compunction swelled fast, and she stepped toward him impulsively.
“Forgive me, father. It pains me that I spoke those words—but I cannot help speaking. I am small and feeble next to you; you once brought me light and strength, and I submitted to you because I could see that light. But I cannot see it now. You yourself have preached that there comes a time when the soul must follow no guide but the voice within, to judge whether a consecrated thing holds true sacred virtue. So I had to speak.”
Savonarola’s face tightened. He was a man of great power, long accustomed to deference, and quick to resent opposition that challenged the grand, holy ends he believed he served. But for a moment, that irritation faded, swallowed up in the hard, constant struggle that defined half his life: the fight between his deep hunger for simplicity and purity, and the tangled, messy web of political compromises, egoistic demands, and impossible conditions he had wrapped himself in. He looked at Romola for a long, quiet moment.
“You are fully forgiven for your frankness, my daughter. You speak out of the fullness of your family’s love. But that personal affection must give way to the needs of the Republic. If the men who know all the State’s secrets truly believe that public safety demands the ultimate penalty for these five conspirators, I cannot overrule their judgment, when I have deliberately stepped aside from the administration of these affairs.”
“So you do not protest their death? You let them deny the appeal?” Romola said, repelled by what felt like a cowardly subterfuge.
“I have said I do not desire their death.”
“Then you are willing to stand by indifferent while Florentines inflict a death you do not want, when you could protest and stop it, when you could defend a law you fought so hard to pass?” Romola said, her indignation spiking again. “You did not used to stand aloof! You did not use to shrink from protest. Do not say you cannot interfere when men’s lives are at stake—say you want them dead. Say you think more blood and more hatred is good for Florence. Will killing five Medicean conspirators end factional strife? Will executing a noble, honest old man like Bernardo del Nero save a city that shelters men like Dolfo Spini?”
“My daughter, enough. The cause of freedom, the cause of God’s kingdom on earth, is often most endangered by enemies who hold certain genuine human virtues. The worst, most wicked man is not always the hardest obstacle to the triumph of good.”
“So you would rather Bernardo die, because his goodness makes him a more dangerous threat?” Romola said, half angry, half desperate. “I cannot untangle your thoughts, father. I cannot hear the true voice of your conscience.”
Savonarola paused, his own emotion sharper than it had ever been before in his talks with her. “Be thankful, my daughter, that your soul has been spared this kind of perplexity. Do not judge those who have been given a far harder lot to carry. You see only one path of action here. I see a dozen. I must choose the one that furthers the work God has entrusted to me. Minor respects must be sacrificed for the great end I serve. The deaths of five men—even if they were less guilty than these—are a light thing weighed against the fight to overthrow the vicious tyrannies that stifle the life of Italy, that rot the Church from within. A light thing weighed against building God’s kingdom on earth, the end I live for, and am ready to die for.”
But Romola could not hear the grandeur in his words. All she felt was total, uncrossable antagonism. What he called perplexity looked to her like sophistry, like double-dealing. His words only stoked the fire of her indignation, lighting up the memory of every small mistake he had ever made, turning all her old trust in him into what felt like a stupid, blind delusion. She spoke with bitter clarity.
“Do you know so perfectly what furthers God’s kingdom, father, that you dare to cast aside mercy, justice, the very teachings you once preached? The French king’s invasion did not bring renovation to Italy. Take care, father, that your enemies are not right when they say all your visions of furthering God’s kingdom only serve to strengthen your own party.”
“They are not wrong!” Savonarola cried, his eyes flashing. For a split second, Romola’s voice sounded to him exactly like the voice of his loudest enemies. “The cause of my party is the cause of God’s kingdom.”
“I do not believe it!” Romola said, her whole frame shaking with passionate repugnance. “God’s kingdom is far wider than your small faction. If that is the case, I will stand outside of it, with the people I love.”
They stood there, face to face, lit by two opposing certainties, no more words possible between them. Romola drew her hood over her head, and turned and walked out of the chapter house without another sound.

The moon, just peeking over the crenellated roofline of Florence’s Old Palace barely an hour past midnight, could not paint its usual broad silver swathes across the piazza pavement that night. Not a hand’s breadth of stone was visible anywhere beneath a heaving, surging sea of bobbing heads: nearly every soul who counted themselves part of Florence’s public life was awake, packed tight within the piazza’s borders or fighting to force their way past the armed guards at the entrances. The soft, familiar hush of Florence’s midnight hours—where the patter of late footsteps, the distant twang of lutes, or the scamper of stray cats stood out sharp and clear—was gone, swallowed whole by a roar of mingled shouts, curses, stampeding feet, and the accidental clink of jostled weapons. Only a sudden, darting shriek or the deep, heavy toll of a distant bell cut through the din.

Inside the palace council chamber, all the chief magistrates, the eighty Senate members, and the city’s most prominent select citizens had raged at one another for hours, from bright daylight deep into guttering torchlight, torn over two choices: grant the condemned Medicean conspirators the right of appeal, or execute them immediately before a rescue attempt could unravel all their work. The debate had curdled into open quarrel so loud its echoes filtered out to the crowd outside. It was only in the last hour that the deadlock broke: four of the Signoria had refused to budge on the appeal even when rioters threatened to sack their homes, until Francesco Valori, the man the city called its Florentine Cato, spat out a brief, furious speech. He declared that if the Signoria refused to defend Florence’s liberty by putting the five perfidious traitors to death, other citizens would take the matter into their own hands, no matter the risk to anyone who stood in their way. Valori triumphed. When the votes were counted again, all nine bean tokens were the fatal black, condemning the five prisoners to instant death. The same vote sealed Francesco Valori’s own fate too, quietly and a great deal further down the line.

While the council’s judicial Eight rode for the Bargello to make arrangements for the execution, the five condemned men were led barefoot, their ankles heavy with irons, right through the midst of the still assembled council. Their friends had arranged this deliberate display of public shame, hoping Florentine hearts would soften at the sight: two venerable, respected old men like Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi, whose loyalty to the Medici had long predated Savonarola’s new order, paired with two brilliant, beloved young nobles like Tornabuoni and Pucci, whose absence would leave a hollow at every major gathering of Florence’s elite. It was useless. The pity that stirred now was no soft feeling that would move anyone to rescue, only a cold, hopeless kind that would fester into slow, deliberate revenge later.

Down in the palace courtyard, Romola leaned her weight against one of the massive weathered stone pillars, waiting for the moment her godfather Bernardo would be led out on his way to execution. She had pulled every favor at her disposal to win permission to visit him that evening, to stay at his side until the council’s verdict was announced. Now she waited beside his confessor, ready to follow the guard detail all the way to the Bargello, determined to cling to the childless old man to his very last breath, just as her own father would have wished. She had brushed off every remonstrance that she leave. At her side stood Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a devout follower of Savonarola, who would soon have to watch his own elder brother Niccolò die, and had sworn to keep her safe through all of it.

Tito was also somewhere inside the palace, but Romola had not seen him. They had avoided each other entirely since the night of the seventeenth, and Tito only knew from reports that Savonarola had refused to intervene on the prisoners’ behalf, that her pleas had failed. He mingled easily with the crowd of waiting Florentine officials and foreign envoys, putting on a quiet, somber air, as if the events of the night had torn him apart between public duty and private grief. When someone mentioned Romola in connection to the condemned men, he hinted that the shock of her godfather’s sentence had unbalanced her, that she had turned violently, irrationally hostile to him simply for continuing to serve the state that had condemned Bernardo. For her own sake, he claimed, he thought it wisest to keep his distance.

“Ah, that old hot-tempered Bardi blood,” Cennini shrugged. “I would not be surprised if this whole business yanks her free of the Frate’s following, same as a dozen others I could name.”
“Any woman who is as beautiful as Messer Tito’s wife, of course, may be forgiven for thinking her personal affections should overrule the good of the state,” the young French envoy smiled, bowing politely to Tito. “To her, no one ought to be beheaded who is related to someone she loves. But that is a view we cannot afford to indulge in men. Your Florentine politics are far too weakened by that softness.”
“True enough,” Niccolò Machiavelli nodded, his sharp mouth quirking. “But when personal loyalties run deep, the hostilities they spawn must be accounted for. Half these half-hearted severities are just blundering hot-headedness. The only safe blows to strike at your enemies are blows so heavy no one can rise up to avenge them.”
“Niccolò,” Cennini huffed, “there is a clever, wicked edge to your talk sometimes that makes me stare at your pleasant young face like it is Satan’s own mask.”
“Nonsense, old friend,” Machiavelli laughed, clapping a hand on the older man’s shoulder. “Satan was a blunderer, forever chasing new novelties, and he failed spectacularly. If he had won, we would all be worshipping him, and no one would paint his portrait as anything but noble.”
“Fine, fine,” Cennini grumbled. “I never said your doctrine was too stupid for Satan, only that it is wicked enough for him.”
“My doctrine is no more than what every man follows who sets his sights further than the end of his own nose,” Machiavelli replied. “Ask our Frate, our prophet, how he will bring about his universal renovation of the city. He will tell you first we must build a free and pure government. And since we cannot make all Florentines love one another, we must simply lop off every head that is stupidly obstinate enough to get in the way. The only mistake a man can make is to mete out a severity not thorough enough to be final, that leaves survivors alive to hate him. I suspect the Frate made exactly that mistake tonight. He could have won great glory by throwing his weight behind the appeal, instead he has lost all lustre and gained no new followers.”

Before anyone could reply, the long-awaited announcement came: the prisoners were leaving the council chamber. Most of the assembled crowd hurried for the doors, eager to claim a spot trailing the guard all the way to the Bargello, for the execution drew every kind of onlooker, from those burning with deepest personal grief to those driven only by cold, idle curiosity. Tito was one of the few who stayed behind. He had always had a natural revulsion for the sight of pain and death, and five days earlier, when he had only considered the execution a distant possibility, he had hoped the sentence would be commuted to exile: his own safety demanded no more. But now, the knowledge that Bernardo del Nero’s head would soon be severed from his shoulders felt like a welcome guarantee of security. Ever since Romola had returned and revealed she knew the truth of his past, he had lived in terror of her power to ruin his reputation. If she won Bernardo over to her side, no one in Florence would doubt her word about Baldassarre. But with Bernardo dead, Romola’s proud, shrinking nature would never let her drag herself into public scandal to oppose her husband. Tito felt a quiet weight lift from his chest when he learned the judicial Eight had already ordered the scaffold erected immediately. Four of his own close associates were to die alongside Bernardo, but he told himself a man’s own safety is a god that sometimes makes very grim demands. As he heard the shuffle of feet and clink of irons echo down the stairs, he answered the young French envoy’s casual questions without showing anything but a sad, resigned acceptance of harsh state necessities.

For Romola, every sound struck her with a sharp, almost supernatural clarity. She did not need anyone to hold her up, she shed no tears. She had reached that peak of feeling that transcends both grief and joy, where the mind feels kin to the old, primal forces that shaped the world before pleasure or pain were ever born. Since the verdict had been handed down, all her earlier struggle had faded: she walked in spirit beside Bernardo, asserting for him that whatever the state called treason, he was no traitor, only a man torn apart between two different kinds of faithfulness. He had not died for the noblest of causes, but he died because he was too noble to compromise his loyalties.

The procession to the Bargello was hemmed in on all sides by three hundred armed men posted by Francesco Valori, a strange, contradictory precaution that the authorities claimed was both to hold back the mob baying for the prisoners’ blood and to crush any last-minute attempt to rescue them. When they reached the Bargello courtyard, Romola was allowed a single moment of farewell with Bernardo and his confessor. Every eye turned to the pair, even in the heaving throng: the old man, forgetting his bound wrists, lifted his hands to touch her golden bowed head, then checked himself to lean forward and kiss her forehead. Romola seized his fettered, hanging hands and kissed them like they were sacred relics.

“My poor Romola,” Bernardo whispered, low enough only for her to hear, “I only have to die. You have to live. And I will not be there to help you.”
“Yes,” Romola said hurriedly, “you will help me, always. I will never forget you.”

She was led away then, up the steps to the loggia ringing the Bargello’s grand old courtyard, and took her place there, determined to watch until the moment her godfather laid his head on the block. As the prisoners were given a few last minutes alone with their confessors, spectators packed tight into the courtyard, pressing all around the black draped scaffold. Torches wedged in iron rings on the pillars flickered, casting strobing light first on cold, passionless stone carvings, then on a pale face twisted with suppressed rage or silent grief—the face of one of the dozens of close relatives of the condemned, waiting to carry their dead home. Romola’s face looked like carved marble against the dark archway as she stood, staring for the first glimpse of Bernardo at the foot of the scaffold. He was to be executed first. Giovan Battista Ridolfi, standing at her side, had promised to lead her out through a hidden rear door the second she had seen the last of the man who had been the only other person in the world to share her gentle love for her dead father. Even now, a faint, stubborn hope flickered far in the back of her mind that some last-minute reprieve would still come, that the scaffold would never be stained with blood.

For a long while, the courtyard seethed with movement: lights flickered, heads swayed, confused voices rose and fell, waves of distant roar crashing in from the streets outside. Romola felt as if she was bobbing in the middle of a storm-tossed sea, caring nothing for the storm, only determined to hold her signal high until the man she loved could see it for the last time.

Suddenly, silence fell. Even the flickering tapers seemed to tremble into stillness. The executioner stepped onto the scaffold, and Bernardo del Nero was seen climbing the steps, slow and unwavering, his white head held high. Romola did not move, did not make a sound. She stood firmer, holding fast to his courage as if it was her own. She saw him pause, glance slowly out over the crowd, and speak in a clear, ringing voice that carried to every corner of the court: “It is but a short space of life that my fellow-citizens have taken from me.” She felt his eyes land on her, saw herself stretching her arms out towards him, and then everything went dark.

When she came to, a long while later, she found Bernardo’s confessor standing beside her in a quiet room, where other grave, robed men talked in low, subdued tones. “My daughter,” he said gently, “all is peace now. I can conduct you home.”

Romola started to her feet at once. “I am ready,” she said. “Let us lose no time.” Every last old tie that had bound her to the life she had lived, to the husband she now loathed, was snapped. All her strength now bent to escaping the grasp that made her shudder.

Eight days removed from that unforgettable stormy night in Florence, Romola stood at the edge of the Mediterranean, her grey penitent’s robes billowing soft around her ankles as she watched the slow, warm summer swell lap at the shore just above the tiny fishing village of Viareggio.

She had fled Florence for the second time, and not a single arresting voice had called her back to turn. This grey religious disguise she had worn before, and now, in the raw, hollow sickness of her heart, she did not even care that it was a disguise. A sharp new rebellion had risen in her breast, paired with a despair so heavy it seemed to press the very breath from her. What did it matter if she wore one badge or another, if anyone knew her true name? She had long since lost hope of finding any consistent, worthy duty that belonged to the name Romola de’ Bardi. Where was the force to carve out that sacred, all-consuming thing men called duty? It could never take root, she thought, without the anchor of some kind of believing love, and every bond of that love had been snapped clean away from her.

In her marriage, the highest, most sacred bond of all, she could no longer see the mystic union that was supposed to guarantee its indissolubility—could no longer even respect the weight of the voluntary pledge she had made. Had not every single thing she had bound herself to work for proven impossible? The urge to cast herself loose had risen again, overwhelming, even as she knew the freedom she sought would be no more than an exchange of one private calamity for another. There is no recompense for a woman who discovers the central, defining relationship of her life was nothing but a mistake. She has lost the crown of her own life, that secret core of human blessedness that had half-whispered itself to her, only to slip away forever.

Even the last great support that had held her upright under that unendurable sorrow had crumbled now. The vision of some grand, worthy purpose, some end to existence that could make endurance noble, that could gild the small, dusty, common deeds of daily life with a divine fire—all of it had been snuffed out. All she could see now was a tangle of human confusion, where every effort was no more than tugging at knotted threads that would never unravel, every alliance for good was lopsided, unfair, exclusive.

What of the man who had once embodied her highest idea of heroism? Not the cold, stoic heroism of grim self-denial, but the soft, burning heroism of a man who gave himself freely for others: Savonarola. Romola’s trust in him was gone. That burning admiration that had let her overlook his missteps, that let her only see the grand sweeping arc of his life’s work, had burnt out to ash. Now, after her fierce loyalty to her dying godfather had pitted her against the Frate, she could see every repulsive, contradictory detail of his teachings with a cruel, sharp clarity that blew every small flaw out of all proportion. In her bitter disappointment, she told herself his grand project to renovate the Church and the world was nothing but a hollow name, no more meaningful than the title on the cover of a book. In practice, all it amounted to was measures that strengthened his own hold on power in Florence, even questionable lies and compromises, all to keep his influence from crumbling beneath the weight of his own errors. That political reform that had once lit up new purpose in her days now seemed nothing but small, narrow schemes to keep Florence safe, shamefully at odds with his public professions of absolute blind trust in Divine providence.

It was no surprise that she judged the Frate unfairly in that hour of personal grief: she looked at his choices through the lens of tender, private love for her godfather, while he looked at them through the unyielding lens of his rigid theoretic conviction. When he declared that the cause of his party was the very cause of God’s kingdom, she heard nothing but the cold ring of egoism. Such words, true enough, almost never ring free of that meaner self-regard, even as they are the unspoken core of all passionate, energetic belief. And that kind of fierce belief, chasing a grand, distant end, can easily slip into a kind of demon worship, where the votary will send his own son and daughter through the fire with a readiness that barely counts as sacrifice. But tender, personal fellow-feeling for the people close to you has its own dangers too: it grows timid, cynical, dismissive of the large, far-off aims that lift human life beyond mere smallness. That was the trap Romola had fallen into now, blinded by her own tears.

No one who has ever known what it is to lose faith in a man they loved and revered with their whole heart can claim the shock leaves your faith in the Unseen Goodness untouched. When your high trust in the goodness of other people sinks, the dignity of your own life sinks right along with it. You stop believing in your own better self, because that better self is part of the same fallen, disappointing human nature you have come to despise. All the fine, generous impulses of the soul grow dull. Romola could feel the springs of her once abundant pity drying up inside her, leaving her with nothing but the barren, self-absorbed weight of her own complaints. Had she not borne more than her share of sorrow? So few had cared for her, while she had spent her whole life caring for others. She had done her part, striven after the impossible, and she was sick to death of this crowded, stifling life. She ached for the total, unthinking rest of mere sensation, the kind she had daydreamed of as a girl, in sultry Florentine afternoons, when she had imagined herself floating like a naiad in cool, weightless water.

The clear, glassy waves seemed to call to her. She wished she could lie down and sleep on their surface, glide straight from that sleep into death. But Romola could not willfully kill herself; the full, warm force of young life in her body would not let her. She could only wish that death would come and meet her halfway.

The cove where she stood curved deep into the shore, and a small, weathered sailboat was moored there, bobbing soft on the swell. As she ached to glide out over the waters now gilded by the low, slanting sun, a memory rose unbidden from her girlhood: of the old Boccaccio stories she had loved to sneak and read when her father nodded off over his manuscripts, slipping off her stool to sit cross-legged on the floor with the *Decamerone* in her lap. There was the story of fair Gostanza, heartbroken, who no longer wished to live but could not bring herself to harm her own young body, so she climbed into a small boat and pushed off to sea, wrapping her mantle around her head to wait for the wreck that would carry her to death without her needing to lift a hand.

The memory had never been more than a passing fancy before, but now, as she paced the sand, she saw a second dark boat glide across the gold-red surface of the water, carrying a lone fisherman heading straight for her cove. When he landed, he hauled his boat up onto the shore and began unloading his catch. He was almost certainly the owner of the small moored boat too. He would pack up and leave soon, and her chance would vanish with him: the chance to buy that little craft. She had not yet admitted to herself what she planned to do with it, but she felt a sharp, urgent eagerness to secure that possibility, proof that a half-formed thought had already grown deep into a quiet desire.

“Is that little boat moored yours as well?” she called to him. The fisherman looked up, startled by the tall, grey-robed figure wandering alone on the shore at dusk, and bowed respectfully to what he took for a holy sister, wandering in silent devotion.

It was his, he told her: an old craft, hardly seaworthy anymore, but worth patching up for any man who needed it. Thanks to the blessing of San Antonio, whose chapel stood at the edge of the village, his fishing had been good lately, and he had bought himself a newer, better boat—one that had belonged to old Gianni, before Gianni died. He had not gotten around to selling the old one yet.

Romola asked him what it was worth, and while he was turned away unloading his baskets of fish, she slipped the full sum of the price into the small satchel on the sand beside him, where he had stowed the remains of his midday meal. After that, she watched him furl his sail, asked him how he would tie the sail if he wanted to head out to sea, and then paced the sand while she waited for him to leave.

The fantasy of gliding away in that little boat on the darkening water grew into a sharp, burning longing, like the thirst for a cool stream on a sweltering midday. To be freed from the burden of making choices, when every motive that had ever guided her was bruised and broken. To commit herself, half-asleep, to the blind hand of fate: fate that might bring her death, or else new, unlooked-for necessities that might wake some new kind of life in her. The soft evening air made that thought all the more tempting, far better than tramping back to the hot, noisy village inn.

At last the slow, methodical fisherman gathered up all his belongings and tramped away toward the village. The golden glow of the sun shrank and dimmed across sea and sky, and there was not a living soul in sight. No sound broke the quiet but the soft, lulling lap of the waves against the shore. There was no strong tide here to carry her out to sea, but Romola could feel a soft offshore breeze picking up, blowing steadily out across the water. She climbed into the small boat, unfurled its tattered sail, and tied it exactly as the fisherman had shown her. She felt the breeze catch the light canvas, and that was all she needed. She untied the mooring rope, pushed off with an oar until she was far out from the shore, until even the western sky had gone dark, and the stars pricked through the dusk like tiny beating pulses of light across the wide empty sky.

At last she stopped rowing, leaned back, and pushed her cowl off her head. She untied the linen kerchief that pinned her hair back, and folded both the cowl and the kerchief to make a soft pillow on the hard wooden rib of the boat. Her fair, young head could bear the rough rest. So she lay, the soft night air brushing her face, as the little boat drifted slowly out across the water, watching the sky deepen into still, vast quiet.

She was alone now. She had freed herself from every claim, every obligation that had weighed on her. She had freed herself even from that heavy, grinding burden of making choices, that presses harder and harder when all the old guiding duties have slipped out of your reach.

Had she found the weightless rest she had daydreamed of as a girl? No. Memories hung to her like the weight of broken wings that could never fly again: memories of all the human love and sympathy she had known, even in its pain, that left a hunger no cold sea, no vast empty sky could ever feed. Romola felt orphaned, adrift between the wide stretches of sea and sky. She could read no message of love for her in the far-off symbolic writing of the stars, and a great, racking sob tore out of her throat, as she wished more than anything to glide straight on into death.

She pulled her cowl back over her head, covering her face, choosing total darkness over the light of the stars that felt like the cold, unseeing gaze of thousands of eyes staring right through her. Soon, it felt to her as if she lay in an unquiet grave, but not resting there: she could feel the hands of all the beloved dead lying beside her, and she was reaching out to touch them, trying to wake them.

The crisp, leftover tang of Carnival confetti and spiced fried pastries still clung to Florence’s cobblestones on the morning of February 27th, when the hands of the communal clocks neared ten. Ordinarily at that hour, the city’s foot traffic would split itself between the clatter of the Mercato Vecchio, the lines outside the Duomo, and the stalls lining the Arno embankment. That day, every current of passengers swept decidedly toward San Marco. All Florence knew a second grand Bonfire of the Vanities was being stacked in the piazza before the Palazzo Vecchio that very morning—pagan love poems, pearl-edged veils, chess sets, perfumes, all the fripperies Fra Girolamo had denounced as snares of the devil—but no one was hurrying there first. The entire pulse of the populace drew toward San Marco.
The Piazza di San Marco was packed shoulder to shoulder, the only movement rippling outward from new arrivals shoving their way through every narrow alley mouth feeding into the square. The front ranks stood pressed tight against a rough semicircle of wooden barriers strung up before the church doors, their weight steady enough to resist the crush of the crowd surging from behind. Inside those barriers, the black-robed Dominican brethren of San Marco filed into place, arranging their psalters on the stands set aside for the choral office. But the temporary wooden pulpit jutted out over the church’s main entrance sat empty.
It would be occupied at any moment by the man the Pope had formally banished from every Florentine pulpit, the figure all other local clergy were forbidden to speak to, the preacher the city’s citizens were threatened with excommunication for even hearing. This man had openly declared that the wicked, simoniac Pope who had bought the chair of Saint Peter with gold was no true Vicar of Christ: that his curses were broken swords, hollows hilts with no blade fixed to them, that his commands that ran counter to godly living were not just permissible to disobey, but *forbidden* to obey. And still the crowds flocked to hear him in his own church, still lined up to take Communion from his excommunicated hands, even as Rome threatened to lay all Florence under interdict, strangle its wool trade, and turn every hostile Italian state against the republic if it did not hand the schismatic over to be “corrected” in Rome.
For the mass of Florentines, the central question hung heavy in the February air: what if this ragged friar held far more sway over heaven’s lightnings than the silk-robed pontiff in Vatican palaces? They had no use for the Frate’s own rigorous test of spiritual truth, no appetite for parsing whether his words aligned with the highest divine law. They wanted a sign. As long as belief in Savonarola had brought only hope of safety and reform, no miracle was needed: his preaching was a rousing march rhythm the people had been happy to step to. But now that clinging to his cause meant immediate ruin for their commerce, the shattering of their republic’s alliances, a city-wide interdict that would doom every soul who dwelt within its walls to the risk of damnation, the fatal, unspoken question swelled louder every day: “What miracle can you show us?”
That demand had hummed in Savonarola’s ears for months, growing faster and faster, drawing first his public promise that a clear sign would come at the appointed hour, then his own private, wavering faith that the great, world-altering work he had begun was far too precious to the Divine will for God to let it falter. His faith shifted, as all mortal faith does, but he did not waver in his speech: it is the fate of every man who speaks to satisfy a crowd to draw courage from yesterday’s conviction, hoping it will return to him on the morrow.
The makeshift pulpit over the church door had been erected expressly to answer the populace’s growing impatience for supernatural proof of the Frate’s mission. While the ordinary black-mantled friars settled into their places, the crowd’s gazes were not yet fixed on that empty platform. Most were scanning the ranks of monks, picking out familiar faces: the sons of old patrician Florentine houses, the young men whose wool-carder fathers, baker brothers, and artisan cousins stood right there in the crowd around them. It was only when the last brother had taken his place, when they had rustled the pages of their psalters and begun the low, droning chant, that the crowd whispered to one another: “Fra Girolamo is coming now.”
That expectation, more than the hypnotic pull of the psalmody, spread a quiet, pale solemn light over the upturned sea of faces, all at last turned toward the empty pulpit. The next instant, it was no longer empty. A figure swathed from crown to toe in black cowl and mantle had stepped over its threshold, kneeling with his head bowed, his face turned away from the crowd. The minutes dragged for the throng of eager onlookers as the black figure knelt and the chant rolled on, but no one dared break the stillness: the Frate’s private audiences with Heaven still crackled with enough electric awe that even the men in the crowd who had come ready to stone him felt their arms go heavy, their anger drained out of them.
Then a ripple ran through the crowd, every man brushing his neighbor like a quivering aspen leaf, the way crowds move when they have been staring at the sky waiting for some promised portent and finally see it unfurl. The Frate had risen, turned to face the people, and pushed his cowl half back. The monotonous hum of psalmody cut off entirely, and for everyone within reach of the pulpit, the lingering sound of chant melted entirely into the sharp, burning weight of Savonarola’s gaze as he swept it over the silent piazza.
Then he stretched out his hands: exquisitely fine, delicate hands, that looked less like ordinary flesh made for grasping, and more like vessels of feeling so acute they had no use for gross, physical touch. They rose like a gentle, pleading speech from the softest part of his soul, hidden behind the harsh, passionate face carved far deeper with lines around the brow and mouth than forty-four years of ordinary life could ever leave.
At the first stretch of those hands, the people in the front ranks dropped to their knees, and here and there a devout follower knelt further back in the throng. The majority stood firm, though: some fighting the impulse to bow before an excommunicated man, half-convinced God might strike him dead mid-blessing, others burning with scorn for what they saw as a cheap, staged performance by an ambitious deceiver—yet all of them feeling powerless to resist the spell that hung over the crowd, as they would be powerless to push back against the tide of a city-wide craze.
Then his voice came: clear, low at first, uttering the opening words of absolution: *“Misereatur vestri.”* More knees hit the cobblestones. It rose higher, clearer, until at the final blessing, *“Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus,”* it burst forth in a ringing, masculine cry, as if fighting its way out from under the grip of a demon that sought to stifle it. It boomed like a trumpet call all the way to the far edges of the piazza, and under that sound, every head bowed.
When the blessing was done, Savonarola himself sank to his knees, hiding his face in his mantle, drained to the bone. These great surges of raw emotion were the very air he breathed: he had told the people long ago that he could not live without preaching. But that life was eating him apart, shattering his strength one great effort at a time.
A few minutes later, a handful of people had gotten back to their feet, but far more remained kneeling, every face fixed intently on him as he lifted a clear crystal vessel holding the consecrated Host. He spoke, his voice cutting through the stillness to remind them of the vow he had made three days prior: that when he held the Sacrament aloft before all of them, he had bid them pray that if his work was not truly from God, the Lord would send fire to consume him on the spot, to vanish into eternal darkness rather than lead his people astray with falsehood. “I beseech you to pray that prayer again now,” he said, “pray it this very instant.”
No one dared not to. The whole piazza held its breath as they heard Savonarola’s quiet, distinct voice rise again, cutting through the wide stillness: “Lord, if I have not wrought in utter sincerity, if my word does not come from Thee, strike me down this instant with Thy thunder, let the fires of Thy wrath wrap around me.”
He went silent, standing perfectly motionless, the consecrated Mystery held high in his hand, his eyes lifted to the pale grey February sky, a faint, quivering excitement thrumming through every line of his frame. The crowd was equally still, equally silent, as thin streaks of sunlight began to poke through the overcast, gilding patches of the convent wall and making more than one awe-struck spectator flinch, half-convinced they would see lightning crack down that very second.
Then the clouds pulled apart wider, soft and sudden as a smile, and a flood of warm, golden sunlight poured down. It hit the crystal vessel first, turning it to a sphere of glowing light, before spilling over Savonarola’s face, wrapping it in a mild, unearthly glow.
An instantaneous shout burst from a thousand throats, echoing off the church walls: “Behold the answer!”
The warm radiance seeped through Savonarola’s bones, and so did that shout. It was his last moment of unclouded triumph. In that rush of rapturous confidence, he saw a vision of a grander stage still to come, a crowd drawn from every corner of Christendom gathered before him, where he would be openly anointed as the messenger of supreme righteousness, armed full with divine strength. The vision lasted no more than a heartbeat. While the shout was still ringing in his ears, he turned and stepped back into the dimness of the church, the strain too great for his fraying strength to bear a second longer.
The moment he vanished, the sunlight lost its unearthly glow, spreading evenly over cobblestones, beggars’ rags, and the polished mantles of patricians alike. The crowd began to mill, shifting restlessly, and a confusion of voices rose up: sharp arguments, snickers, cynical laughter, all making plain that the hostility and scorn that had been cowed into silence by the momentary spell were bubbling back to the surface.
“Seems the cheers are already giving way to bickering,” Tito Melema remarked, leaning against the stone balustrade of an upper loggia in the house directly opposite the church, where he had watched the whole scene unfold with sharp, attentive eyes. “Still, it was a striking spectacle, eh, Messer Pietro? Fra Girolamo is exactly the sort of man to make you understand why once upon a time, a monk’s frock was a symbol of power over men’s minds, not a fool’s excuse to rummage through the keys of a rich woman’s wardrobe.”
“Assuredly,” Pietro Cennini replied, his face lined with quiet thought. “Until I see proof that Fra Girolamo has far less fear of God’s judgments than the common run of men, rather than far more, I will never believe he would dare to brave Heaven in this way if his soul was laden with a deliberate, conscious lie.”

A month after that wild, candlelit Carnival, on a crisp late March morning, Tito Melema descended the weathered marble steps of Florence’s Old Palace, bound for San Marco on an errand loaded with unspoken consequence. He spurned the direct, gently winding lane that would have gotten him to the convent in minutes, choosing instead to circle around the Piazza di Santa Croce, where crowds would soon spill out of the great stone church after the first Lenten sermon of the day.

Santa Croce had become the city’s most packed Lenten gathering spot of late. Girolamo Savonarola’s voice had been silenced even in his own home church of San Marco: a hostile Signoria had enforced a papal letter that threatened to lay the entire city under interdict if the friar, denounced in the pontiff’s note as a “wretched worm” and “monstrous idol,” did not stop preaching and travel to Rome to beg pardon for his errors. Next to hearing Fra Girolamo himself speak, no Lenten pastime thrilled Florentines more than gathering to hear him denounced and argued down from the pulpit — and that exact thrill was on offer at Santa Croce, where the Franciscan friar leading the Lenten Quaresimal sermons had publicly offered to walk through open fire side-by-side with Savonarola to settle their dispute once and for all.
Hadn’t that schismatic Dominican claimed his prophetic teachings would be vindicated by a miracle at the appointed hour? This, the Franciscan declared, was that hour. If Savonarola walked through the flames unhurt, his doctrine was proven divine. If he burned to ash, his lies would be laid bare for all to see. To leave him no room to evade the test, the Franciscan swore he would willingly burn alongside him, a sacrifice for the truth that no opponent could dismiss as a rigged game.

Savonarola, as was his habit, paid no public mind to the pulpit taunts. But the zealous Franciscan who had issued the challenge was no stranger: Fra Francesco di Puglia had tried to stage an identical duel of fire with Savonarola’s most loyal follower, Fra Domenico, in Prato the year prior, only to be recalled by his superiors before the argument ever moved beyond shouted rhetoric. Honest, fiery Fra Domenico, who was leading separate Lenten sermons for women in the Via del Cocomero, no sooner heard of this new challenge than he grabbed the gauntlet up on his master’s behalf, declaring he would meet Fra Francesco in the flames any day. The public had already grown giddy over what struck most as a delightfully simple, foolproof way to settle a religious argument — until Savonarola, acutely aware of all the hidden dangers lurking even in the discussion of such an unprecedented trial, ordered Fra Domenico to withdraw his acceptance and wash his hands of the affair. The Franciscan professed to be perfectly content: he had never aimed his challenge at a subordinate, he said, but at Fra Girolamo himself.

Popular interest in the Lenten sermons had flagged a little in the lull that followed. But that morning, when Tito stepped into the Piazza di Santa Croce, he found exactly what he expected: great swarms of people pouring out of the church doors, many of them lingering instead of heading home, clustering tight around a spot just outside the entrance to the Franciscan monastery. Tito drifted that same way, moving through the crowd with a lazy, unhurried air, his sharp eyes fixed steadily on the monastery doors as if he waited for someone of interest to step out.

The crowd’s fascination, though, was not a person, but a large official placard nailed to the wall by order of the Signoria, its letters bold and inked clear — but almost entirely written in Latin. Nearly every person gathered had a rough idea of what it said, but all ached for exact details, and every man present took petty, petty pleasure in deciding his neighbor was too ignorant to parse the learned words. The scraps of Latin phrases lay listeners picked up from constantly interpreted pulpit quotations were no help at all with written text; even the vernacular was still unstandardized and messy for most Florentines who could read at all, and the vast majority of the huddle pressing closest to the placard could not so much as sound out a simple Italian sentence, let alone a foreign tongue.

“It’s the Frate’s doctrines he has to prove by getting burned,” blustered Goro, the city’s ubiquitous loudmouth, who happened to be pressed to the very front of the gazers. “The Signoria’s taken the whole affair in hand, and this writing’s to let us know it’s settled. That’s exactly what the Padre was saying in his sermon just now.”

“Nay, Goro,” a sleek wool shopkeeper said pityingly, “you’ve twisted your hose so far your legs are tied in knots. The Frate has to prove his doctrines by *not* burning. He walks through the fire, comes out the other side whole and sound.”

“Aye, aye,” put in a young sculptor, his cap and tunic dusted with fine white marble flecks, “but Fra Girolamo’s refusing to walk through at all. He’s already whole and sound, why would he risk the fire to end up exactly the same? He’s left all that leftover odd work to Fra Domenico.”

“Then he’s a coward, flinching out,” Goro squawked in his wheezy high voice. “Suffocation! He did us the same trick at Carnival, herding us all into the Piazza waiting for lightning to strike him, and nothing happened.”

“Shut that bleating,” rumbled a tall shoemaker who had ducked into the sermon halfway through, bunches of half-finished slippers slung over his shoulders. “You’re as daft as a calf with water on the brain, friend. The Frate won’t flinch from nothing. He might say nothing beforehand, but when the time comes he’ll stride right through the fire without asking any grey-frock to keep him company. I’d give a whole shoestring to know what all that Latin actually says, though.”

“There’s so much of it,” the shopkeeper fretted, “else I’d make a fair guess. Isn’t there any scholar around to read it for us?”

Heads turned, and the group spotted Tito approaching from behind them.
“Here’s one,” the young sculptor said, smiling and tipping his cap.
“That’s the secretary of the Ten,” the shopkeeper added, doffing his own hat too — a rare show of deference from independent Florentines, reserved usually only for the highest of officials. The reverence was earned less by Tito’s office than by the striking grace and splendor of his bearing: his black mantle fastened with a gold fibula looked like a royal robe, his ordinary black velvet cap a unique, fine headpiece. The faint hardening of his jaw and mouth, the only notable change to his face since he had first arrived in Florence, only made his famous good looks read as more masculine and commanding to casual onlookers.
He tipped his cap back immediately, smiling. “Friends, I am as curious as you are to see what the placard says. Ah — it is exactly what I was told. The Signoria, in its characteristic liberality, permits any citizen who wishes to sign their name as a volunteer to enter the fire, of course reserving the right to select candidates. No doubt many of the Frate’s believers will leap to put their names forward. What is fire to a man whose faith is unshakable? We fear the flame only because we are sure it will burn us. But if a man truly believes it will not harm him?” He lifted his shoulders, pausing for dramatic effect. “That is why I have never once doubted the Frate when he says he would walk through the fire to prove his teachings. In his place, if you knew the flames would not touch you, which of you would not step into them as easily as you would stroll along the dry bed of the Mugnone river?”

As Tito looked around the crowd, half the faces before him shifted like eager dogs that have just been offered something sharp and acrid to sniff. Now that the trial by fire was no longer just a story, very few were foolish enough to commit publicly to the idea that faith could make you immune to flame. The scene would have made anyone less calm than Tito laugh out loud.
“Then Messer Segretario,” the young sculptor said, “it seems to me Fra Francesco is the greater hero. He offers to walk through the fire for the truth, when he is fully convinced it will burn him to ash.”
“I do not deny that,” Tito said smoothly. “But if it turns out Fra Francesco is wrong, he will have died for a false cause, and the Church never counts such victims as martyrs. We must all hold our judgment until the trial actually happens.”
“That is true, Messer Segretario,” the shopkeeper said, his patience wearing thin. “Will you do us the favor of translating the Latin for us?”
“Gladly,” Tito said. “It lists the core teachings of the Frate that the trial by fire will prove true or false — all doctrines you have heard a hundred times. First, *Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione*: the Church of God must be purified, renewed.”
“It is true,” several voices chorused at once.
“That just means the priests ought to stop living greedy, sinful lives,” the shoemaker put in. “No miracle needed to prove that, that’s what the Frate has been saying for years.”
“*Flagellabitur*,” Tito continued. “That means it will be scourged, punished. *Renovabitur*: it will be made new. *Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur*: Florence too, after its time of suffering, will be purified and prosper.”
“That means we’ll take Pisa back,” the shopkeeper nodded.
“And get our wool trade flowing from England again like the old days, I hope,” an elderly man in a vintage berretta spoke up, “We’ve had more than enough scourging with all our trade sinking to nothing.”

At that exact moment, a tall figure wearing a feathered red cap stepped out of the Franciscan monastery doors, exchanging a lazy, knowing glance with Tito. Tito flipped his becchetto negligently over his left shoulder and went back to reading the placard, while the crowd shrank back nervously to make way for Dolfo Spini, leader of the violent Compagnacci street gang.
“*Infideles convertentur ad Christum*,” Tito went on. “That means the Turks and Moors will be converted to the faith.”
“Got no quarrel with that,” the shopkeeper said dispassionately.
“*Haec autem omnia erunt temporibus nostris*: all these great things will happen in our own lifetimes.”
“What good would they be otherwise?” Goro huffed.
“*Excommunicato nuper lata contra Reverendum Patrem nostrum Fratrem Hieronymum nulla est*: the recent excommunication ordered against our reverend father Fra Girolamo is completely null. *Non observantes eam non peccant*: those of us who ignore it are committing no sin at all.”
“I’ll only know what I think of that once the trial by fire has settled everything,” the shopkeeper said.
“Which no doubt will clear all doubts up,” Tito agreed. “That is all the Latin. The rest is plain Tuscan, a proclamation from the Signoria calling all who are eager to take part in the fire trial to come to the Old Palace and sign their names. Is there any more I can do for you, friends?” He tipped his cap again and bowed slightly as he turned away, the gesture so easy and natural it felt like genuine respect, not performative politeness.

He quickened his pace the moment he left the piazza, turning down three quiet empty lanes before pausing at a plain, unmarked door. He gave a soft, distinct rap, and a young servant girl opened it. Tito chucked her playfully under the chin, asked if her master was home, then brushed past her without ceremony through a half-open door to his right, stepping into a grand but messy parlor. Dolfo Spini sat there on a bench, roughhousing with a fine staghound that alternated between sniffing at a basket of newborn puppies and licking his hands, the kind of loyal, unjudging affection many men preferred to that of other people. Spini only glanced up when Tito entered, then went right back to playing with the dog, the kind of stubborn irrelevant action slow-witted, sensual men always cling to even when they have urgent business to attend to. Tito waited patiently.
“That’s a fine hound you’ve got there,” he said casually, his thumbs hooked in his belt. A moment later he spoke in his cool, smooth, unignorable tone. “When you’re done with those caresses that can’t possibly wait, my Dolfo, we can talk business. I wish I could offer you all eternity of my time, but I am due back at San Marco shortly this morning.”
“Down, Mischief! Down!” Spini barked at the dog, shoving her roughly away. He stood up, moved to stand right next to Tito, and clapped a heavy hand on his shoulder. “I hope your sharp wits have mapped every twist of this business, my clever little necromancer, because it’s as clear to me as the dark inside a sack.”
“What part is tripping you up, my friend?”
“Those cursed Franciscans at Santa Croce, they’re backing out. Fra Francesco himself is scared now to stick to his own challenge. He’s blabbering that the Prophet will use black magic to fake a miracle, that he might get dragged into the fire and burn alive while Savonarola steps out unharmed, and the Church gets nothing out of it. Now there’s not a single lay brother among them fool enough to pair up with that pious sheep Fra Domenico and step into the flames.”
“It is the special stupidity of tonsured brains that they cannot see it does not matter at all if any of them actually go near the fire,” Tito said. “Have you sworn to them that they will face no risk at all of being singed?”
“No,” Spini said, frowning. “Because someone has to go in with Fra Domenico — he’s champing at the bit, ready for the fagots to be lit today.”
“Not at all,” Tito said. “Fra Domenico will never set foot in the flames. I told you before, my Dolfo, though your powerful mind always needs things repeated twice over to sink in: now that you have gotten the Signoria to take up this affair and stop Savonarola from sweeping it under the rug, you don’t need anything else but to pile the fuel in the main Piazza on the appointed day, and get all the city gathered there, expecting a miracle. If, after all that build-up, the Prophet steps out of the Piazza without producing a single wonder on his behalf, the people will turn on him. They will be ready to pelt him out of the city with stones, the Signoria will banish him from our lands without any pushback, and the Pope can do whatever he wishes with him. So swear to the Franciscans that their grey habits will never come within even a sniff of the fire.”

Spini rubbed the back of his head, tapped his sword against his calf to rouse his wits enough to follow Tito’s invisible machinations. “But if we don’t rush him in the Piazza when the crowd is raging, and kill him and all his lies right then, Valori and the Salviati and the Albizzi will grab their arms and raise a rebellion to save him. That’s what they were talking about the last time there was a big riot on Ascension Sunday. And the people might turn back around again — they’ll start spreading rumors the French king is coming back, or some other lucky break for that hypocrite. The city will never be safe until he’s dead and gone.”
“He will be gone before long, without you needing to swing a single sword,” Tito said. “Wine and sun make vinegar all on their own, no one needs to shout at it to help the process along, as your Florentine proverbs say. You will get all the credit for ridding the city of this incubus with a clever stratagem, no risk of blundering into a civil war you can’t control.”
“But what if he *does* use magic and the devil to help him, and walks right through the fire anyway?” Spini made a face, trying to hide that he was half-serious about the question. “How do you know there’s nothing to all those stories? Plenty of great scholars believe in magic, and that Frate is bad enough to make a deal with anything.”
“Oh, of course such things exist,” Tito shrugged. “But I have very particular reasons to know the Frate is not on close enough terms with the devil to pull off a miracle like that. The only magic he relies on is his own cleverness.”
“Cleverness?” Spini snorted. “You call it clever to set Florence at war with the Pope and every other power in Italy, all just to wave the French king over here who never even shows up? You can call him clever, I call him a hypocrite who wants to rule everyone and make himself Pope one day.”
“You judge with your usual sharp insight, my captain, we are in full agreement,” Tito said smoothly. “The Frate, who wants power, and to carry out his plans against the Pope, needs a foreign power as his lever, and Florence as his fulcrum. I used to think he was just a narrow-minded bigot, but now I see he is an ambitious, shrewd man who knows exactly what he is aiming for, and aims as true as you do when you play the game of maglio.”
“Aye, no one can aim a ball better than me,” Spini said, pleased.
“Exactly,” Tito said, keeping his face perfectly grave. “I would not waste your time commenting on his skill, except so you understand how high your reputation for this victory over him will soar in Rome and Milan, which will serve you very well when the city finally shifts its policy away from the Frate’s faction.”
“Well, you are a useful little demon, you will get your good reward,” Spini said, patting Tito’s shoulder patronizingly. “We will have supper at my palace tonight, and I will lay the whole new scheme out to all the men.”
“Pardon, my magnificent patron,” Tito said, smiling, “the scheme has never changed from the very start — it is only your memory that varies. Are you sure you have every part of it fixed in your head now?”

Tito was hurrying to leave when Spini called him back. “One more thing. That sharp-nosed notary Ser Ceccone, he has

Tito Melema’s audience at San Marco had been arranged in advance, and Fra Niccolò, Savonarola’s quiet secretary, led him straight up the worn spiral stone stairs into long corridors lined with narrow monk’s cells. Half-hidden on the plaster walls, Fra Angelico’s frescoes glowed soft and fragile as rainbow light on melting cloud: sudden, ethereal flashes of a world beyond the monastery, where the Madonna sat crowned in radiant light, and the Divine Infant gazed out with the quiet promise of eternity.

It was the community’s hour of rest, and most cells stood empty. Thin gold light filtering through the slitted windows fell on nothing but bare whitewashed walls, hard straw pallets, and a small wooden crucifix nailed above each bed. At the far end of the longest corridor, behind the door of the inner private cell that opened off the antechamber where the Prior usually worked at his oak desk, that same slant of light caught only one other familiar monastic sight: the back of a figure draped in the crisp white Dominican tunic and scapulary, kneeling with bowed head before a crucifix propped on a stool. For a passing glance, he might have been any ordinary brother, fretting over trivial sins: laughing too loud in the choir, feeling petty satisfaction when a fellow monk spilled ink all over his hand-illuminated breviary. His only aim would seem to be climbing slowly up the narrow ladder of prayer, fasting, and obedience to earn his place in Paradise.

But under that plain white wool tunic, Girolamo Savonarola’s heart beat with a conflict no average monk could ever fathom. Old, irreversible mistakes, small lapses from the truth he had justified for the sake of his holy work, were tangled so tight with his noblest purposes and sincere, unshakable beliefs, that he could no longer untangle the two strands. Self-serving expediency was stitched deep into the fabric of the great Church reform he had spent his whole life building, a work he could no more abandon than his body could stop trembling before the things he hoped for, or feared. No matter what path he chose, his conscience would never know perfect rest.

He was muttering Latin prayers out loud, the words *Cor mundum crea in me* — create in me a clean heart — falling softly from his lips, but he was not truly praying. He had fallen to his knees to beg God for a rush of calm, some sign that the crowding, conflicting thoughts in his head were not drawing him away from divine support. But the premonitions and urges churning inside him were too strong. Even as he pressed his palms to his face, his mind was full of vivid images of the trap his enemies had laid for him, full of arguments he could use to defend himself against their taunts and accusations.

He knew his own followers were pushing for the Trial by Fire just as hard as his Franciscan opponents. They wanted him to accept the challenge, to perform the long-awaited miracle that would wipe away every last trace of doubt and crush his enemies for good. Had he not publicly declared that God would make his will plainly known at the fitting time? To hot-tempered, party-loyal Florentines sick of endless political squabbling, there was no time more fitting than now. Everyone agreed that if the earnest, devout Fra Domenico walked through flames unharmed, that would be a miracle plain enough for every man, woman, and child in the city to see. But Savonarola was acutely, painfully aware that none of the justifications he had given for refusing the challenge had quieted his followers’ secret, urgent longing to see him step into the fire.

Satisfying them was impossible. With bitter, heavy distress, he was forced to admit he could no longer resist the trial going forward under Fra Domenico’s name. He had never lied when he declared his belief that God would one day send a supernatural sign to validate his work. He could easily wrap his faith around a distant, undefined miracle, screened from plain view behind all the strong reasons he knew it must happen, and he had never doubted the inner miracles of his own prophetic inspiration and divinely guided intuitions. But a concrete, immediate miracle like walking unharmed through fire, every brutal detail of which pressed sharp on his imagination, demanding not just abstract belief but action? His mind recoiled from it completely.

Savonarola’s nature was built of two near-equal, opposing strands: passionate sensibility that flooded every idea with emotion and pulled him toward contemplative ecstasy, and a sharp, unforgiving eye for outward facts, a vigorous practical judgment of men and events. The second trait was amplified by an acutely sensitive physical constitution, that made the idea of searing pain and burning destruction feel vivid, unavoidable, rooted in every memory of past hurt. A man might tell himself theoretically that a red hot iron will not burn him, but no amount of that abstract belief will make him press his bare hand to it. For Savonarola, when the Trial by Fire was presented as an immediate, unavoidable demand, it was impossible for him to believe that any human being could walk through flames unhurt — impossible to believe that even if he forced himself to volunteer, he would not flinch and run back at the final step.

The Florentine public would never grasp those fine nuances. Ordinary people have always taken it as a sign of strong sense to reduce moral questions to simple, black-and-white choices, and judge a man’s conduct by the most concise alternative available. It would seem obvious to every one of them that a man who once swore God would give him a miracle to prove his work was true, and then drew back when the chance to test that promise came, had never meant what he said. Scores of his Piagnoni followers were ready to walk into the fire, they would say — why would they be braver than their leader, unless their faith was stronger? Savonarola could not have explained his conflict to his followers, not even if he had been able to untangle and make sense of it fully for himself. Raw, unnameable feeling quickly wraps itself in the handy, familiar phrases we keep stored in our minds, and true self-awareness takes far more than mere unfeigned sincerity to achieve.

Kneeling there, listening no longer to the prayers he spoke aloud, he shaped his justifications over and over, refining them for an audience. *To rashly accept the challenge and call on Heaven for a miracle set by my petty, ignoble foes would be to tempt God, and He would not answer. If the Pope’s legate comes, if ambassadors from every great European power swear that a successful miracle will force the calling of a General Council and the full reform of this corrupt Church, then I will step into the flames, trusting that God will not refuse to stamp His seal on that great work. Until then I must reserve myself for the higher duties laid on me. I cannot leap off the chariot of this sacred work just to wrestle with every loud-mouthed vaunter that crosses my path.*

But the second his imagination drew close to the blazing pyre they would build in the Piazza, the image of a human body stepping into the flames, his faith recoiled again. He felt the burn shiver all the way down to the tips of his sensitive fingers. The miracle could not happen. He told himself he could still find a way to stop the trial. The fuel would be stacked, the crowds would gather, all the formalities might go forward, but the Franciscan challenger would hang back at the last minute, and they could find a way to prevent Fra Domenico from entering the flames. If worst came to worst, he decided, Fra Domenico could carry the consecrated Host into the fire with him, and the sacred Mystery held in his hand would stay the ordinary destructive power of flame. Or the Franciscans would refuse to let that happen, and that would be final cause to call the whole trial off.

But none of those quiet intentions could be spoken aloud. He would have to appear to await the trial openly, to trust fully in its miraculous outcome. That gap between his private doubt and his public mask was a betrayal of that simple, unfeigned Christian innocence he had striven for all his life, and preached as the highest fruit of a life rooted in the divine. In the heat of the crowd, with shouts ringing in your ears and cheeks burning hot, who is so blessed that they remember the quiet, pure yearnings they held in the cool of the dawn, and know they have not betrayed them? *O God, it is for the sake of the people — they are blind, their whole faith depends on me. If I put on sackcloth and throw myself in the ashes in front of them, who will pick up the standard, who will lead the battle? Have I not been led down a path I never expected, to this great work no one else can fulfil if I abandon it?*

The conflict had no end. In his effort to pray, his restive, aching mind soothed its smart by clinging to the grandeur of the work no other man in all of Europe was fit to carry out. The words of prayer died away on his lips. He stayed kneeling, his mind full of visions of the changes his reform would spread across the whole continent, the small immediate difficulties fading into the warm glow of that grand future — until a soft, deliberate knock rapped on his cell door.

Savonarola pulled on his heavy black mantle over his tunic before stepping out, his habit when receiving visitors, and the moment he stepped into the antechamber his whole demeanor shifted to the calm, powerful, self-possessed energy he was known for, as if he had risen from quiet resolution instead of hours of tearing internal conflict. Tito did not kneel, only offered a bow of deep, deliberate deference, which Savonarola acknowledged quietly without formal sacerdotal pleasantries before gesturing to a chair.

“Your business is weighty, my son, that you could not send word through another?” he asked directly.

“Assuredly, Father,” Tito said, no unnecessary preamble. “I heard a remark from Messer Domenico Mazzinghi that you would be glad to make use of the Ten’s special courier leaving for France with official despatches. Messer Domenico is away at his villa today, so I came to warn you the courier departs for Lyons at dawn tomorrow. I hope I have not been over-officious in coming to tell you myself.”

Savonarola’s facial muscles were firmly under control, as they must be for any man whose power rests on the force of his personality, and he was habitually cautious about sharing his intentions with anyone unnecessarily. But under the spark of strong excitement, his eyes dilated and flared bright with a light no force of will could hide. He stared steadily at Tito for a long beat, considering if the news met a purpose he held. Tito, who never seemed to stare too intently but rarely missed the smallest detail, had seen that exact flash many times before. He noticed it now, and busied himself pretending to adjust the gold fibula pin on his tunic, looking the picture of patient waiting.

Savonarola had indeed been expecting that very message from Mazzinghi, one of his most loyal followers on the Ten, who had already been asked to pave the way for a private letter from Savonarola to the King of France, tucked in the desk beside him. That letter called on the king to help summon a General Council to reform the abuses of the Church, starting by deposing Pope Alexander — a corrupt, vicious man who had bought his way into the papacy through simony, and had no right to his throne. Tito did not know the exact contents of the letter, but his sharp, intuitive wits had guided him to guess its purpose, and he had come to make certain he was the one who carried it.

“It is true, my son,” Savonarola said evenly. “I do have letters I wish to send safely under cover to our Florentine ambassador. The San Marco community, as you know, holds the Medici library we purchased some years back, and we owe a debt to that wise and experienced Frenchman, Messer Philippe de Comines, related to that transaction, so there is legitimate reason for correspondence. But Messer Domenico will most likely return to the city before evening, and I would have more time to prepare the letters fully if I wait to give them to him myself.”

“Of course, Reverend Father, that would be ideal in every respect save one,” Tito replied smoothly. “If any accident delays Messer Domenico’s return, the letters would either force me to come back to San Marco at a very late hour, or force you to send them to the Palazzo via your secretary. I know you are very careful to avoid any false political gossip that might arise from too frequent communication between yourself and any official of the government.”

Savonarola fell silent, his jaw set firm, a faint flush rising to his cheeks as suppressed excitement built in his chest. This was a critical moment, the second he passed the letter out of his own hands.

“I am certain Messer Domenico will return in plenty of time,” Tito said, acting as if the Prior’s decision was settled, and rising from his chair as if to leave. “I will not trespass further on your time now. But as I may not get the chance for another private audience, I venture to share news not yet known to anyone save the magnificent Ten: I intend to resign my secretaryship, and leave Florence very soon. Am I presuming too much on your goodwill to share a matter that concerns mostly myself?”

“Speak on, my son,” Savonarola said. “I would know your prospects.”

“I realize I misjudged my true calling when I left the life of pure scholarly letters I was raised for. Florence’s politics are worthy of the greatest minds — minds like yours — when a man is free to carry out the ideas he believes in. But for a man like me, who can only ever be a tool for shifting, changing party schemes, you need the deep, rooted loyalty of a man born Florentine to stay here. My wife Romola’s profound unhappiness with life in Florence after the terrible unrest of last August has also influenced my choice. I intend to join her.”

Savonarola nodded approvingly.

“I will travel to the chief courts of Europe, widen my acquaintance with learned men across all the great universities, first stopping at the court of Hungary, where scholars are always warmly welcomed. I do not hide from you, Father, that I am no religious enthusiast, I do not share my wife’s ardor. But religious enthusiasm is not required, I think, to see the grandeur and justice of your vision for reforming nations and the Church. If you ever choose to entrust me with any commission that furthers the connections you wish to build abroad, I will feel deeply honored. May I take my leave now?”

“Stay, my son. When you leave Florence I will send a letter to your wife, to reassure her of my care for her spiritual welfare — she left my side in anger, and I would see that mended. As for the letters to France that I have ready now —” Savonarola rose and turned to his desk as he spoke, taking out a sealed letter addressed in his own tiny, exquisite handwriting, the same script that still survives covering the margins of his annotated Bibles. He tucked it into a larger sheet of heavy official paper, folded it, and sealed the outer wrap.

“Pardon me, Father,” Tito cut in, before he could speak. “Unless it is your absolute wish, I would rather not take the responsibility of carrying that packet. Messer Domenico will no doubt return in time, and if he does not, Fra Niccolò can bring it to me at the second hour this evening, when I place the rest of the despatches in the courier’s hands.”

“For the moment,” Savonarola said, brushing that protest aside, “I would ask you to write the outer address to our ambassador in your own handwriting. It will look far less suspicious than a note from my secretary.”

Tito sat to write the address while the Prior stood behind him, arms folded, the flush rising high on his cheeks, his lip finally trembling with suppressed, unnameable excitement. Tito rose to leave, when Savonarola spoke abruptly: “Take it, my son. There is no use waiting. I will not have Fra Niccolò trudge all the way to the Palazzo on an unnecessary errand.”

As Tito slipped the heavy sealed packet under his velvet mantle, Savonarola stood frozen in thrumming, suppressed excitement, unable to speak another word. A strange, heavy silence hung in the air, the kind that radiates unconsciously from passionate, overwrought minds when their thoughts are pulled far away from the world around them. Tito bowed deep, and slipped out of the monastery.

The letter was duly handed over to the official government courier, and carried out past Florence’s city gates. But long before that, a second messenger Tito had hired privately had already slipped a note in cipher out of the city, carried by a string of mounted relays to the armed agents of Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, who were waiting on the Milanese border for the explicit purpose of intercepting Savonarola’s secret despatches.

Barely a week later, on the seventh of April, Florence’s great Piazza della Signoria played host to a stranger spectacle even than the famous Bonfire of Vanities. A greater throng had crammed into its wide expanse than had ever gathered there, even for the feast day of San Giovanni.
It was nearing midday, and since the first light of dawn the people had swarmed to claim every possible vantage point—good or ill—clinging to the facades and roofs of palaces, and staking out every free patch of pavement. Men perched on sharp iron brackets angled out from rising walls, wrapped arms and legs around slim marble pillars, straddled the necks of rough stone statuary that crested grand entranceways, balanced on a palm’s breadth of architrave, and braced their feet on the jagged projections of rustic masonry, clinging tight to the heavy iron rings and staples driven into the walls beside them. They had come to see a miracle. Cramped limbs and scraped skin felt like trivial inconveniences with that prospect so close at hand. Most of the world only hears tales of miracles, and believes them in some small measure; but the Florentines were about to witness one for themselves. At the very worst, they would see half a miracle: if the monk did not walk unharmed from the flames, they would at least watch him step into them, and know he was consumed at the heart of the fire.
No reasonable soul could doubt the pyre would be lit, and the monks would enter it. There, before their eyes, stretched a platform eight feet broad and twenty yards long, heaped high with fuel in a terrible grove of flame: great dry oak boughs formed the foundation, layered with crackling thorns, and topped with well-anointed tow and rags known to burn bright and fierce in Florentine festival illuminations. The platform began at the corner of the marble terrace before the Old Palace, close to Marzocco, the ancient stone lion whose weathered, frowning face looked down the line of fuel stretching diagonally across the piazza.
