Three large armed formations were deployed across the open space: five hundred hired Signoria soldiers stationed before the palace, five hundred of Dolfo Spini’s Compagnacci far across on the opposite side of the piazza, and three hundred armed loyalist citizens under Marco Salviati, Savonarola’s friend, posted in front of Orgagna’s Loggia, where the Franciscans and Dominicans would gather with their two champions. The event had cost vast sums of money and labor, and drawn the attention of the city’s highest dignitaries. There could be no doubt something monumental was about to unfold. Even if both monks were simply burned to ash, that would be a great thing in its own right: God would have spoken plain and clear, that Fra Girolamo was no prophet.
The waiting was almost over. Half the monks had already taken their posts, the half of the Loggia facing the palace filled with their grey cowled mantles, while the far half, partitioned off by rough planks, held nothing but a small altar. The Franciscans had entered and taken their places in utter silence.
Then, from the far side of the piazza, two hundred voices rose in loud, rolling chanting, and the crowd stirred with general satisfaction if not at the hymn itself, then at the proof that the Dominicans had arrived. That thunderous repetition of the prayer *“Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered”* struck many impartial onlookers as an unseemly boast of confidence, a deliberate attempt to strike dread in their rivals. So too did the flame-colored velvet cope worn by Fra Domenico, who led the procession with a cross in his hand, his simple heart truly exalted in faith, fully sincere in his intention to walk through the flames for the glory of God and Fra Girolamo. Behind him walked Savonarola in a white priest’s vestment, carrying a vessel holding the consecrated Host. He too chanted loudly, his bearing firm and unshakable, and as every eye turned to him—some in anxious hope, some in sharp curiosity, some in raw malice—the second he stepped into the piazza and mounted the Loggia steps to set the Sacrament on the small altar, his face glowed with a bright, unyielding energy that answered every stare.
Few of us escape this quirk of human nature: the false show of confidence that once made us shrink in lonely private dread, when we imagined we would have to perform it, will seize our muscles and move our lips as if it were the easiest thing in the world, once we stand under the pressure of hundreds of expectant eyes and ears. The force of that stimulus on Savonarola can hardly be measured by the experience of ordinary men. Few men ever wield great influence over their fellows without an innate hunger to dominate, a hunger that grows all the more imperious when the tangled compromises of public life twist self-interest into something that looks, even to the man himself, like an unselfish cause. On this day of the Trial by Fire, that doubleness—the core temptation of every public career, for priest, orator, or statesman—burned sharper in Savonarola’s consciousness than it ever had before. He was not fighting impending martyrdom: he was fighting impending ruin.
Beneath his unshakable public front, foreboding pressed on his heart like lead, not only for the uncertain outcome of the trial, but for a secret disaster already done. Two evenings prior, news had arrived that the Florentine courier carrying the Ten’s despatches had been arrested and robbed of all his mail. Savonarola’s sensitive letters were already in the Duke of Milan’s hands, and would soon reach the Pope’s, stoking their rage and giving them full justification to move against him with every extreme measure. That fact was the source of soft, unmalicious satisfaction for Tito Melema, leaning from a window of the Old Palace and looking down at the worn, passion-driven prophet far below. He felt no spite, only the quiet self-congratulation of a man who has just won a game of clever strategy, no effort of brawn or heated temper required. To a brilliant, worldly scholar who understood all the turns of power, the tonsured Fra Girolamo—high-nosed, heavy-lipped, half prophet half opportunist—was no tragic figure. He was an immensely clever friar, who mixed absurd superstitions and deliberate fabrications with strikingly radical ideas about government, a man who never babbled secrets. Tito bore him no more ill will than he bore Saint Dominic. On the contrary, Fra Girolamo’s rise had been a perfect ladder for Tito’s own ascent, one he was about to step off of onto a smooth, stable new footing that suited him perfectly. All his preparations were almost done: one more sun would rise and set, and Tito planned to leave Florence for good. He had worked so hard for months that he felt he had earned the leisure to amuse himself with today’s farce—one the thick-headed Dolfo Spini never could have arranged, not without his quiet scheming.
The chanting did not fade, it swelled to a deafening roar, taken up across every corner of the piazza by the Piagnoni, their red cross badges pinned to their chests, most of them chanting the prayer for the confusion of God’s enemies fully expecting a public, miraculous answer through Fra Domenico. The good friar in his flame-colored cope knelt before the small altar holding the Sacrament, waiting for the call to step toward the pyre.
On the Franciscan side of the Loggia there was no chanting, no bright flame color—only silence, and a sea of grey cowls. They had one advantage: they fielded two champions, Fra Giuliano to face Fra Domenico, while their original challenger, Fra Francesco, reserved his demand for Savonarola himself.
The crowd perched uneasily on their brackets and pillars thought all must be ready now. The chanting could stop, and they would get a clear view of the friars moving toward the platform. But the friars did not move. Pale Franciscan faces peered uneasily over the plank partition at that bright flame-colored cope. It looked evil. It might be enchanted, woven with devil’s magic to produce a false miracle. Their monk might walk out of the fire unharmed, and it would be no work of God at all.
Men began passing back and forth between the Loggia and the Palazzo’s marble terrace, the roar of chanting softened a little as everyone in the far corners of the piazza leaned in to watch. It soon became clear the new movement was not the start of the trial, but a new obstacle to it. The appointed moderators, leading Florentine dignitaries picked from both factions, went in and out of the palace, locked in long debate with the Franciscans. At last, the bright figure of Fra Domenico, conspicuous in his flame-colored robes, was led away toward the palace. The crowd assumed the pyre must already be lit, hidden from their view, and the miracle was about to begin.
They were wrong. The flame-colored cope vanished inside the palace, then another Dominican was fetched away. For a long while, everything dragged on as before: the tiresome, non-miraculous chanting, and Fra Girolamo standing in his white vestment, not moving an inch. At last Fra Domenico emerged again, and returned to his brethren. He had swapped every last piece of clothing with another Dominican, and two Franciscans walked flanking him on either side, to keep him from straying near Savonarola and being bewitched again.
The distant spectators, half forgetting their cramped limbs and empty stomachs, exhaled in anticipation. Fra Domenico would not be entering the fire. It was Fra Girolamo who would offer himself, after all. They would see him move any moment, and if he walked out unharmed, they would have a perfect view of the miracle.
But Fra Girolamo did not step toward the pyre. He only spoke, bold and firm, his tone echoing Elijah’s rebuke to the priests of Baal, demanding the trivial delays cease. Speech, though, is the most infuriating kind of argument for a crowd that cannot hear a word of it, cramped in their posts and empty in their bellies. What need was there for words? If the miracle never started, it must be Fra Girolamo’s fault, who could end all their quarrels by walking into the ready pyre himself, as confidently as he had boasted he would when there was no fuel stacked in sight.
More back and forth, more unheard debate. The afternoon slipped away faster as dark clouds gathered, dimming the light over everything, sending a cold chill through the spectators, hungry in body and restless in spirit. Now the Franciscans declared Fra Domenico could not carry a crucifix into the fire, that he would profane the holy symbol by exposing it to the flames. Savonarola conceded the point after short resistance, scoring one more point for reasonableness, but immediately placed the vessel holding the consecrated Host in Fra Domenico’s hands. The thought that the divine Mystery of the Eucharist might, at the last, avert the fire’s ordinary effects hovered in his mind as a faint possibility, but he counted on a far more certain outcome. As he set the Host in the friar’s palms, he spoke quietly, as if the arrangement had been agreed on from the first:
“Since they will not suffer you to enter with the crucifix, my brother, you will enter carrying the Sacrament alone.”
Fresh horror swept the Franciscan ranks, and fresh unyielding firmness from Savonarola. It was impious presumption, they protested, to carry the Sacrament into fire. If it burned, the weak and unlearned would be scandalized beyond repair.
“Not at all,” Savonarola countered. “Even if it burns, only the outward accidents of bread will be consumed. The divine Substance will remain untouched.”
It was an argument that could be argued until sunset and still remain as elastic as ever. No one could propose settling it by proceeding with the trial, for it was a purely preliminary quarrel. All the two sides had to do was stay firm: the Franciscans refuse to let the Host enter the fire, and Fra Domenico refuse to enter without it.
The clouds grew darker, the air colder. The crowd missed the distraction of the chanting now that it had given way to silent, unseen debate, and the confused murmur of talk rippling across the piazza showed that expectation was fraying fast, curdling into a prickling irritation that nothing decisive would ever happen. Individual shouts broke out, then more frequent, rising in a crescendo of scorn.
“Light the fire and drive them in!”
“Let’s get a smell of roast friar—we want our dinner!”
“Come on, Prophet! Show us something before the twenty-four hours are over!”
“Tell us your last vision, eh? I bet you’ve got a dozen little ones stuffed inside your tunic, small change for a miracle!”
“Hey, Frate, where’d you go? Don’t waste all that good fuel!”
The same back and forth movement between the Loggia and the palace, the same slow debate as unintelligible to the crowd as ants touching antennae for no visible purpose but to wander back and forth. The crowd had no trouble guessing what the unheard arguments meant: it was Fra Girolamo who was holding the trial back, hanging back while everyone else begged him to step forward.
The shouts blurred into a great uproar, mixed with the clash of metal and the thunder of trampling feet. The crowd’s taunts had stoked old ambitions in Dolfo Spini and his Compagnacci. This was a perfect chance, they thought, to end all Florence’s troubles once and for all by seizing the arch-hypocrite himself. The band of armed men charged hard for the Loggia, shoving ordinary citizens aside or driving them back against the line of Signoria soldiers posted in front of the palace.
The monks and flustered magistrates froze, all other business forgotten, waiting to see how the clash would end. But Salviati’s loyal citizen guards held the Loggia, and the Signoria soldiers rushed to help repulse the charge, driving the Compagnacci back toward the Tetto de’ Pisani. In that same moment of utter chaos, the blackness of the sky deepened, and the scattered raindrops that had been spattering down moments earlier burst into a torrential downpour, dousing the stacked fuel on the platform, sending streams of water running off its planks, soaking every last weary, hungry spectator through to the skin, driving their disgust and rage inwards to ferment in the damp chill.
Everyone knew at once the Trial by Fire would never happen. The Signoria seized on the rain as a perfect, obvious excuse, no flimsy pretext needed, to declare both factions free to go home. It was the exact outcome Savonarola had planned for, the one he had counted on—but no one could call him glad. As the rain splashed at the edge of the Loggia, sending spray over the altar, over every cowl and face, the friar knew the demand that he enter the fire was gone for good. But he knew it with a certainty as unshakable as the damp chill that had settled in his bones: his enemies had won. His honor was not saved. He would have to make his way back to San Marco through a crowd raging against him, and many of the friends who would once have defended him with their lives would now turn away.
He had said he was willing to die for the work of his life, and he had not lied. But to die in dishonor, to be held up to scorn as a hypocrite and a false prophet? O God, that is no martyrdom. That is blotting out a whole life spent in protest against wrongdoing. Let him die for the good he has done, not for his failure.
The rain slowed and stopped, and light broke through the clouds to fall on Savonarola as he left the Loggia surrounded by his requested Signoria guard, still walking as he had come, the Sacrament in his hands. But there was no glory in that light, no smile of heaven. It was the plain, impartial light that shines on all things patiently, revealing every turn of slow ripening history without judging, only showing what is. He heard no blessings, no tones of pity, only taunts and threats. He knew this was only a foretaste of the bitterness to come, but his courage rose against the storm of moral attack, and he showed no sign of dismay.
“Very well parried, Frate,” Tito murmured, watching him descend the Loggia steps from his palace window. “But I fear your time in Florence is at an end. What say you, my Niccolò?”
Machiavelli shrugged, a faint melancholy on his face. “It is a pity his falsehoods were not all wise ones. The times were so far on his side on matters of Church reform, he might have done something truly great.”

It was Palm Sunday—known across the olive-cloaked hills of Valdarno far more often as Olive Sunday—and the morning sun blazed with a rare, honeyed clarity, washed clean by the rain that had fallen the day before. Once again Girolamo Savonarola climbed the worn stone steps of San Marco’s pulpit, and looked out over a throng whose faith in him had not yet wavered. That morning, he spoke not with fiery rhetoric, but with quiet, sad sincerity: he told the crowd he was ready to die, that he could see his own doom standing clear before him, sharper than any prophetic vision. He spoke the final benediction, and looked out over the faces of the men and women who lifted their gazes to him, bright with reverent, loving devotion. Then he stepped down from the pulpit, and turned away from that sight for the last time.

Before the sun dipped below Florence’s red-tiled roofs, the whole city was in uproar. Passions that had flared the day before had smouldered low through the soft quiet morning, and now they burst forth with a brutal fury that was no accident: it had been deliberately stoked, and carried the quiet connivance of the city’s officials. The riot first broke out at the Duomo, when a band of Compagnacci, the young aristocratic street thugs, moved to break up the evening sermon the Piagnoni, Savonarola’s followers, had gathered to hear. No sooner had blood been drawn and the shoving turned to a full brawl, than the cry rose up, sharp and ragged, from a hundred throats: “To San Marco! Burn San Marco to the ground!”

Long before the last of the daylight faded, the great church and its attached Dominican convent were under siege from a swelling, screaming mob, numbering thousands. The defenders did not yield easily. The monks, who had long known the hostility outside their walls was growing, had hidden arms stowed away, and many fought just as fiercely in their flowing white tunics as any Knight Templar. Even Savonarola’s command to lay down their arms could not still the rush to self-defence in bodies hard and muscular beneath the rough Dominican serge. Lay supporters who had refused to flee fought alongside them: muskets blazed from the high altar, feet from the great carved crucifix, stones and scalding hot embers were poured down on the assailants from the convent roof, and swords clashed in vicious, close-quarters fighting through the cloisters. For all the weight of the mob’s numbers, the attack dragged on deep into the night.

The city government’s sympathies lay entirely with the besiegers. Early in the fighting, guards were dispatched not to disperse the rioters, but to order every person inside the convent to lay down their weapons, all lay supporters to leave, and for Savonarola himself to quit Florentine territory within twelve hours. Had Savonarola stepped outside the convent walls then, he would almost certainly have been torn to pieces by the waiting crowd. He was willing to surrender, but his friends held him back. Even for high-ranking Piagnoni nobles, slipping out through the unguarded garden wall was a terrible risk, but Francesco Valori, the fiery former statesman, made the attempt, hoping to raise a rescue party in the city.

By the time midnight had fallen, the defenders could not hold out much longer. The Compagnacci were moments away from breaking through to the library, where Savonarola knelt in prayer with the brothers who had refused to take up arms, or laid them down at his order, when a second detachment of guards arrived, sent by the ruling Signoria to demand the surrender of Fra Girolamo, and his two closest coadjutors, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

A roar of triumphant, hateful jeers went up from the crowd when the lantern light glinted off the monks’ white habits as Savonarola stepped through the convent doors, surrounded by guards who offered no promise of safety beyond the walls of the city prison. The crowd surged to press as close as they could, shoving and fighting for a spot in the narrow street leading to the Palazzo Vecchio, eager to see the Prophet dragged back in ignominy to the very public square where he had defied the city’s authorities only days before. They shouted insults at him, spat, and the armed Compagnacci, allowed to mix freely with the guards, struck him with their fists and kicked at his legs.

As Savonarola was dragged and jostled through that hooting throng, as lanterns were held up to his face to show him grinning, mocking faces, as the insults and blows rained down on him, he thought he had reached the bitterest point of suffering a man could know. If the city had judged him guilty and was determined to take his life, only death lay ahead of him. But the bitterest drop of all is never forced into a man’s mouth from outside: the lowest depth of true resignation is not found in martyrdom, but in the quiet, private moment when you wrap your cloak around your head in silence, and think *I am not worthy to be a martyr. The Truth will outlive me, but it will not triumph through my hands.*

That brief, vicious triumph of jeering at the captured monk, before he was hurried through the doors of the Old Palace to the cells beneath, only whetted the mob’s appetite the way a taste of blood rouses a tiger. Were not the houses of the hypocrite’s closest friends ripe for sacking? Half the armed crowd, too far back to take part in the siege of San Marco, had already set to the more profitable work of raiding the homes of wealthy Piagnoni. This was no random, chaotic plunder: the rioters targeted the houses of Savonarola’s most prominent supporters with sharp, deliberate care, proof that the riot was carefully orchestrated, the rabble armed with clubs and staves well led by sword-carrying Compagnacci.

Next after the Frate himself, there was the ambitious Francesco Valori, whom the rioters accused of plotting to make himself lifelong Doge or Gonfaloniere with Savonarola’s backing. The grey-haired noble, who only eight months before had thundered for the execution of five of his fellow citizens, escaped San Marco only to face what his enemies called justice: he found his house surrounded by the angry, greedy mob, saw his wife shot dead through the doorway by an archer’s arrow, and was himself hacked to death on the road to the Palazzo, where he had been summoned to answer false charges, by the swords of two noblemen, Ridolfi and Tornabuoni.

And so that grim masquerade of the Furies, the riot, raged across Florence through the dark hours of the night and the first pale light of dawn. The man who directed it all never showed his face in the streets: he had good reason to issue his orders from a hidden private lodging, his name far too high-profile to have his red-plumed cap seen bobbing above the crowd while all this bloody work played out before sunrise.

That lodging was the very same quiet house, in the street between Santa Croce and San Marco, where we once saw Tito Melema pay a secret late-night visit to Dolfo Spini. There the Captain of the Compagnacci sat through that long, historic night, receiving messengers who came and went in a constant stream. Some were armed Compagnacci, others dressed in plain, unremarkable clothes with no visible weapons. A huge cask of wine stood on the table, with cups set out for every new arrival. Spini was careful not to drink so much he lost his wits, but he sipped steadily enough to stoke the wild excitement of the constant, fresh reports being brought to him.

Of all the plainly dressed visitors, Ser Ceccone the notary was one of the most frequent, and as the dark hours wore on towards dawn, he stayed as Spini’s constant companion, alongside Francesco Cei, the disgraced poet who was hiding out in Florence, waiting for Savonarola’s fall to have his banishment revoked. The tallow candles had burned down to lumpy, misshapen stubs, and the faint grey pre-dawn light was just beginning to show through the cracks in the shuttered windows, when Spini, who had been pacing up and down, face flushed dark with rage after a heated conversation with his two non-military companions, burst out in a snarl.

“Devil take the Greek! He’ll pay for this, I swear. Ha! We’ll have the claws sunk in him when he least expects it. All this time he pretended to toss every scrap of profit my way, as if I was some blind beggar, and all the while he’s winking and shoving every florin into his own purse. I’d like to wrap him in rags hung with raw meat and set my hunting hounds on him! And he’s got that fine ruby of mine, the one I was fool enough to give him the day before yesterday. Curse him! He’s been laughing at me behind my back for two whole years, ruining the best laid plan I ever came up with. What a fool I was to trust a rogue who twists his schemes into knots no one but himself can ever untangle.”

“A Greek, no less, who washed up in Florence with his pockets stuffed full of stolen gems,” said Francesco Cei, a faint amused smile on his face as he watched Spini fume. “You did not pick your confidant very wisely, my Dolfo.”

“He’s a damned sight cleverer than you, Francesco, and handsomer too!” Spini snapped, rounding on his associate, in the surly mood that made him lash out at anyone handy.

“If I may say so humbly,” Ser Ceccone put in, rubbing his palms together in his usual obsequious way, “Messer Francesco’s poetic genius far outweighs—”

“Ugh, stop rubbing your hands like you’re about to forge a fake will! I hate that notary trick of yours,” Spini interrupted. His idea of patronage was mostly this rough, frank bullying. “Wait, that’s Taddeo at the door! Now’s the time. What news?” he called out, as two Compagnacci burst into the room, faces flushed and sweating.

“Bad news,” one of them said. “The mob was dead set on sacking Soderini’s house, and now they’ve been blocked from doing it, they’re going to turn on us next if we don’t give them something new to chew on. I swear there are Medicean agents mixed in with them, stirring up trouble. They’ll be marching on your palace across the bridge before long, if we don’t find them a new bait to chase.”

“I’ve got it!” Spini shouted, grabbing Taddeo by the belt and yanking him aside to whisper new orders in his ear, while the other messenger explained to Cei that the Signoria had stepped in at the last minute to block the sacking of Soderini’s home.

“Done!” Spini exclaimed a minute later, giving Taddeo a hard shove towards the door. “Go. Make it fast, no delays.”

The grey, biting twilight had just settled over Florence when the pair of Compagnacci set out on their errand, and on the far opposite bank of the Arno, another ragged figure slipped out of hiding to wander down to the river’s edge. His purpose seemed entirely unconnected to the armed men’s: he made his way to a stretch of shore tucked within the city walls, overlooked by no cottage windows, only bordered by tall warehouses and granaries that turned their blank, forbidding backs to the water a short distance inland. Sloping down to the current was a wide stretch of long, dank grass and rushes, made all the soggier by open gutters that emptied their refuse steadily into the river.

That loneliness and those trickling gutters were exactly what drew Baldassarre to hunker down in the reeds, bending to watch the thin runlets rush down the sloped bank beside him. More than once before, those friendly streams had carried him a full loaf of discarded bread, a raw carrot, even scraps of apple parings. It was worth waiting for such unlooked-for charity where no one could see him—he often snuck out here long before dawn, when restlessness would not let him lie still, to save himself from the humiliating silent begging that meant sitting motionless on a church step just outside the Porta San Frediano.

Baldassarre hated begging so fiercely he would likely have chosen death over that quiet appeal, if not for one single reason that made him cling to life. It was no longer a clear, defined hope, only that stubborn, unshakable possibility that clings to any idea that has entirely possessed a man’s mind: the same irrational certainty that makes a widow stand for years on a headland watching for a ship all her neighbors know sank decades before.

After the San Miniato monks had nursed him back from the edge of death in their convent hospital, after he had searched in vain for Romola and convinced himself she must have perished in the great pestilence that had blotted out all memory of the night he and Tito had parted, he had been unable to find any way to exact the sacred vengeance that burned at the core of him. His knife was long gone, his body too broken and weak to earn another through work, his mind too fogged and bewildered even to plot a course if he had possessed a blade. He was a shattered, lonely old man, and yet he could not bear to die. He waited for something he could not name, something dim and formless that stirred a wild, sharp thrum in his chest, the same unaccountable restlessness that rouses us from deep sleep even when no sound or touch has disturbed us. So he crept out in the grey half-light, settled in the long grass, and watched the water, faint as a half-remembered promise.

Meanwhile, the Compagnacci went about their work. The great armed bands the Signoria was too fearful (if not openly complicit) to restrain had split into two groups, both making their way toward the Arno by separate routes. The smaller band headed for the Ponte Rubaconte, the larger for the Ponte Vecchio, the same whispered signal passing from mouth to mouth through every throng: every last man knew they were bound for the Via de’ Bardi to sack a house there. What better reason could a mob demand? The leaders of both factions had made perfect plans to converge on a palazzo near the eastern end of the street, where the master of the house would still be fast asleep, caught unawares at dawn.

But that master, Tito Melema, was neither abed nor asleep. He had not rested a wink all night. The chaos of the previous day had spurred his desperate urge to flee Florence; he knew official investigations would follow that might summon him for questioning and delay his departure, and in every moment of delay he sensed a cold, sharp danger. His web of lies had grown thick and strong over the years, but it had spawned an equal, twin life: Fear. He no longer wore armor, no longer cowered from Baldassarre, but the corpse of that old fear had birthed an undying habit of dread. He knew he would not be truly safe until he was far away from this fierce, roiling city, and every last detail of his escape was arranged. Maso was to hand the palazzo over to its new tenant that very day; his horses and mules waited stabled outside the San Gallo gate; Tessa and their children had been lodged overnight in the Borgo just beyond the walls, already dressed and ready to mount their mules and ride away with him. He stepped down the stone steps into his courtyard, out through the great oak doorway, nearly as bright and easy in his bearing as he had been the day he first walked into that house and made the foolhardy choice to marry Romola. That old mistake was done now. His old life was cast off, soon to vanish far behind him.

He turned toward the Piazza dei Mozzi, meaning to cross the quieter Ponte Rubaconte—until a roar of distant sound drifted to his ears, and he spun on his heel to hurry the opposite way. The mob was pouring into the Oltrarno district, it seemed. A nuisance. He would have to take the busier Ponte Vecchio instead. He pulled his mantle tight around his shoulders and broke into a run, thankful no one was abroad to spot him in the dim twilight. But before he had reached the far end of the Via de’ Bardi, those same sounds hit his ears again, far louder and much closer. Could he have misheard before? The mob must be coming across the Ponte Vecchio, too. A surge of blind, unreasoning fear made him turn again—only to see the throng was already entering the street from the end he had just fled from.

He decided not to run back to his house. Surely they would not attack *him*. But he carried valuables on his person, and reason and order never hold sway over a mob. Necessity lent him the illusion of courage, and he pushed onward toward the Ponte Vecchio, the roar of trampling feet and shouting voices swelling so loud ahead that he stopped hearing any sound at his back. The crowd pouring off the bridge met him at the street’s corner and hemmed him in entirely.

He had no time to wonder at a sudden scream before armed Compagnacci surrounded him. His cap was knocked from his head, and he was shoved violently forward into the rabble, jostled down the narrow passageway of the bridge. The shouts rang clear a second later: “Piagnone! Medicean traitor! Piagnone! Throw him over the rail!” They tore at his mantle, the pressure near throttling him until the iron fibula clasping the fabric snapped clean in two. A hand snatched at his scarsella, the leather pouch at his belt, but the crowd dragged him onward before they could tear it free. Half-moteless, rabid execration thundered in his ears, spreading even to men at the back of the throng who had not even seen him, who only knew there was a man to revile. Tito’s most horrifying fear was that he would be beaten or trampled to death before they could hurl him over the bridge arches. His only flickering hope was that they would throw him over before they could break his strength, and his entire soul narrowed to that single desperate wish, and the terror of the opposite.

They were at the central arches now. In that moment of utmost extremity, a spark of sharp self-preservation flared in his brain. With a wild, heaving effort, he ripped the clasp of his belt clean open, and hurled the heavy belt and its bulging scarsella out toward the only empty patch of space against the bridge parapet, screaming at the top of his voice: “Diamonds! Gold! There is treasure here!”

The hands clutching him slackened instantly, as the whole crowd surged forward to grab for the flying pouch. Tito threw himself at the parapet in a desperate leap, and the next second he had plunged, hitting the dark Arno far below with a great echoing splash.

It was his chance, and it was a good one. His powerful swimming had saved his life once before, when he escaped the galley years earlier, and as he broke the surface after a long dive, he felt a surge of deliverance. He struck out with every ounce of the strength of his prime, the current sweeping him swiftly along. If he could only swim past the Ponte alla Carrara, he could land in a remote, unguarded stretch of the city shore and still make his escape to San Gallo. Life was still his for the taking. The screaming, bellowing idiots on the bridge would think he had drowned.

They did. They leaned over the parapet, staring down into the dark, rippling water, but they could not spot the moving black blur of his floating hair, or the heavy velvet of his tunic sleeves bobbing below the surface. Only from the riverbank could his pale olive face be seen, bleached white above the dark current, its square forehead, long low arch of eyebrows, and lustrous agate eyes unmistakeable even to a stranger. His nostrils flared and quivered, the blue veins on his temples standing out as he was carried onward. He passed the Ponte Santa Trinita. Should he risk landing now, instead of pushing his exhausted strength further? No. He thought he could still hear the yells of the mob pursuing him. He feared his fellow men far more than the unknown risks of the river, so he swam on, panting and straining. He was far less fresh than he would have been if he had slept the night through. He passed the last bridge, his muddled mind only barely registering that he had cleared it, that he might soon be safe to land. But he did not know where the current was carrying him. Exhaustion was creeping over him in the hazy, dreamlike daze that precedes unconsciousness.

But there were eyes watching him, aged eyes sharp to spot small things at a distance. Baldassarre, who had been staring blankly at his trickling runlet after finding no scraps in it that morning, lifted his head and spotted a pale white bobbing shape far down the broader stream. Could this be the lucky chance he had been waiting for? He stared and stared until the shape resolved, and he lurched forward so hard the reeds rustled around him, a wild new light flooding his eyes. He did not move, only watched. Something was being brought to him.

The next instant, the current hurled Tito’s limp body hard onto the grass barely two yards from where he sat. Baldassarre launched himself forward like a starved panther, clutching the shoulder of Tito’s velvet tunic as he fell on top of him, his eyes flashing down into the man’s face.

Dead? Was he dead? Tito’s eyes were rigid and glazed. No, that could not be. Justice would not cheat him like that. Men often looked dead when they had only fainted, and life would return to them. In that moment, Baldassarre felt no trace of his old feebleness. He knew exactly what he must do. He slipped his large, calloused fingers inside the neck of Tito’s tunic, held fast, and knelt on one knee beside the body, never taking his eyes from his face. A fierce, trembling hope blazed in his chest; every last spark of the slow-burning life left in him had leaped into flame.

Rigid. Still rigid. Those half-lidded eyes were locked shut against his vengeance. Could he really be dead? There was no way to mark the passage of time, and it stretched on so long that hope began to freeze into despair. At last, the eyelids quivered. The eyes were no longer glassy. A flicker of living light danced in them, and they flew wide open.

“Ah! Yes. You see me. You know me.”

Tito recognized him instantly. He could not tell if this was life or death, if the chill gloom surrounding him was some hell where the face of the hideous past hung over him for all eternity. But Baldassarre had no fear now but one: that the strong young man beneath him would somehow slip away. He pressed his knuckles hard against Tito’s round throat, driving all the weight of his aged frame down onto the younger man’s chest. Death could come now, and welcome.

He never took his eyes off Tito’s face. Even when the eyes went rigid and glazed a second time, he did not trust the stillness. He would never loosen his grip until someone came to find them, a witness for justice. Then he would tell the world that he had killed the traitor he had once raised like a son. They would believe him now, at last. Then he would be content, let justice work its work on earth, and die still clutching the body, to follow the traitor straight to hell and hold fast to him there.

So he knelt, and so he pressed his knuckles to Tito’s throat, never trusting the apparent stillness, until the sun climbed high and he could kneel no more. Then he sat on the body, his grip still tight on the neck of the tunic. The hours dragged by, and no witness came. No one spotted the two figures half hidden in the tall riverside grass. Florence was busy with far greater affairs that day, and the preparation of a far deeper tragedy. Not long after the two bodies lay still in the reeds, Savonarola was strapped to the rack in the Bargello, screaming in his agony: “I will confess!”

It was not until the sun hung low in the western sky that a peasant leading a gentle grey ox wandered to the grassy bank, there to gather the heap of paving stones he had left ready to cart away. Leaning down to scoop the stones up, he spotted the strange shape half hidden in the rushes. The old man had fallen forward, his dead hands still locked fast around the other man’s tunic. It was impossible to pry them apart. The peasant decided it would be better to load both bodies onto his waggon as they were, and carry them into the main Piazza to alert the Eight.

As the waggon trundled through the crowded streets, a growing crowd gathered to follow it, drawn by the strange, silent burden. No one recognized the bodies for a long while, for the old man’s face had fallen forward, half hiding the younger man’s features. But before they had been carried far, they were unmistakeable.

“I know that old man,” Piero di Cosimo testified, pushing through the crowd. “I painted his likeness once. He is the prisoner who clutched Melema on the steps of the Duomo all those years ago.”

“It is likely the same old man who turned up uninvited at supper in my gardens once,” said Bernardo Rucellai, one of the Eight. “I had forgotten him. I thought he died in prison long ago. There is no way to untangle the full truth now.”

Who can point a finger to any single act of men and say, “There—there is perfect, plain justice, made real for all to see”? Justice is like the Kingdom of God. It does not exist out in the world as a neat, tangible fact we can lay hands on. It lives within us, as a great, burning, unquenchable yearning.

Rocked gently by the soft swell of the Mediterranean all night long, Romola drifted out of fevered dreams of the Bargello’s flickering tapers into a deep, untroubled slumber, then back again to half-waking visions where dark, anguished scenes dissolved in an ever-brightening glow. When her eyes fluttered open at last, that glow was no taper’s flame: it was the full golden light of early morning.

Her small boat rested motionless in a sheltered, hidden creek. To her right stretched the sea, a flawless sapphire so bright it seemed to hum under the rising sun. To her left lay one of those timeless, gentle coastal landscapes that loop along the Italian shore like a half-remembered lullaby: a deep curve of fertile green land cupped between soft, tree-draped mountain slopes that rose slow and easy toward craggy, sunlit peaks. Winding pathways glinted through the oak and olive boughs, leading up to a huddle of stone buildings that had clambered haphazardly up the mountainside, its tall, slim belfry raised high to be visible to every cottage and farmstead scattered across the valley below. Only the far, western edge of this crescent nook lay bathed in direct sun; every other corner was wrapped in dewy, cool shadow. No sound stirred across the stillness. Even the waves lapped so softly at the shingle it seemed the sea itself had curved into this cove just to rest.

The warm sun fell across Romola’s face and limbs like a tender caress, and for a long, perfect moment she lay motionless, not thinking, not watching, simply steeped in that quiet peace. Youth brings such moments sometimes, in the first blur of waking: passive existence feels like its own healing, a bliss untethered to memory, unburdened by desire. The agonies of the past months, the despair that had driven her to push off from Florence’s shore the evening before, all slipped away like the dark Bargello dream, and for a breath it felt as if the soft, idle daydreams of her girlhood had risen unbroken to wrap around her again. She did not even dare to hope she could stay here forever — she only felt, at last, that she was resting.

Then memory seeped back. This was the same small boat that had carried her across the dark waters all night, the boat she had half-expected would be her bier. Instead, it had lulled her into a new life. The despair of the previous evening melted: she was glad to see the sun rise again, glad to be alive in familiar light rather than lost in the cold unknown of death. Could she stay here, hidden? No echo of Florence’s feuds, no trace of the betrayals that had broken her, would ever reach this quiet cove. For a little while, at least, she could stop fighting, stop resolving, just be. She would go ashore, find bread and milk from the village, and nestle into the green quiet until the jagged edges of her grief softened.

She had turned back to gaze at the sun-dappled valley, to hold fast to that soothing peace, when a piercing, unceasing cry cut through the stillness. It was the wail of a small child, in pain, left untended. Romola started to her feet, one foot already over the boat’s gunwale, before she paused to listen, half-expecting a mother to appear and hush the sound. But the cry did not stop. It grew louder, more piteous, the sharper for the utter silence that had reigned only moments before, and it drew her irresistibly ashore. She scrambled over a low broken stone fence, pushing through weedy unharvested crops and patches of overripe, neglected corn, toward the crumpled half-ruined hovel she had spotted a dozen yards inland. The air grew thick and oppressive the further she got from the sea breeze, the green luxuriance around her suddenly feeling cloying, almost dangerous.

The moment she stepped across the hovel’s shadowed threshold, she froze. On the straw-strewn floor lay three dead bodies: a tall broad-shouldered man, a thin little girl no more than eight years old, and a young woman whose long black hair was being tugged at, over and over, by a tiny living toddler — the source of the desperate crying.

Years of tending the sick through Florence’s repeated plague outbreaks made Romola’s hands and mind move fast. She lifted the wailing little boy into her arms, pressing him to her chest to soothe his sobs, even as her eyes took in their distinct Sephardic features, their unfamiliar woolen garb. She guessed at once: they were Spanish or Portuguese Jews, refugees fleeing the Inquisition, set ashore by greedy sailors who had stolen all their possessions and left them to die. It was a cruelty that had grown far too common on these shores: greed chased them from the sea, and superstitious fear chased them back again. She reached to feel if the young mother still had a pulse, lifting the edge of the yellow wool drape across her bosom — and spotted the dark purple plague spots blistering her skin.

Romola’s blood ran cold. If the villagers knew a plague party had washed ashore here, they would slam their doors on her and the child rather than risk contact. But she had coin to offer, and so she set off at once toward the clustered cottages higher up the valley, trying to hush the little one by feeding him small bits of wild fig she had plucked from a wayside tree. The further she walked, the eerier the scene grew: fields of corn left rotting uncut, figs and apples browning on the ground, no cows, no goats, no villagers out tending to their work at this late morning hour. The first door she peered through stood open to utter empty darkness. The second held a man dead on his earthen floor, his head fallen across the handle of his spade, an earthen pitcher still clutched in his hand as if he had dropped mid-step, overtaken by the fever in an instant. The whole village was a place of the unburied dead.

The child wailed again, just as she spotted a figure crawling slowly out of a nearby doorway, collapsing against the outer wall. It was a young woman, burning with fever, a pitcher loose in her grip. “Water,” she moaned, barely noticing Romola standing over her. “Get me water.” Romola took the pitcher from her limp hand, asked her to point the way to the well, and set off at a run, the child now quiet against her shoulder as he sucked on the sweet fig pulp.

As she neared the well, her eyes drifted to the grassy patch high on the slope below the village church, where she had spotted a cow and two goats grazing. She was so busy planning her path to that patch to beg milk for the child, that she did not notice a fifteen-year-old altar boy, coming the other way to draw water, who froze at the sight of her. Draped in her long, plain grey pilgrim’s gown, her golden hair streaked with sunlight, the dark-eyed olive child tucked in her arm, the pitcher in her hand to tend the sick, the boy was convinced he was looking at no mortal woman. He was certain the Holy Mother had appeared, come down to save their plague-stricken village. Terrified that she would be angered at the villagers for hoarding water while the sick suffered, he dropped his own clay vessel and fled as fast as his legs could carry him up the slope.

Romola, confused but not stopping to wonder at his flight, drew the water quickly and hurried back to give the fevered woman a drink. When the woman had drunk her fill, she told Romola the whole grim story: weeks earlier, three families of Jewish refugees, twenty souls in all, had been set ashore sick with the plague. The villagers had shut them out in that distant hovel, and when the first of them died, they had tried to throw the bodies into the sea — but a storm washed them right back. After they buried the corpses, the plague had spread through every cottage. Most of the villagers had fled over the mountain, driving all their livestock ahead of them. Only she, the parish priest, and the young altar boy Jacopo were left behind.

Romola wasted no time. She promised the woman she would come back to tend her, then set off up the slope to find the priest. The old man had been hiding behind the olive boughs for ten minutes already, his bucket in hand, half-convinced Jacopo’s tale of a Madonna with a glowing halo tending the sick was true, half-terrified that the plague would take him if he dared step down into the village. When Romola called out to him gently, asking for milk for the hungry child, he crept out from behind the trees, too fearful to disobey. He poured her a brimming cup of warm goat’s milk, and as he watched Romola feed the little boy and then drink deeply herself, the last of his superstitious awe melted into a quiet, humble certainty that God had sent a woman over the sea to save them.

That morning, the three of them walked back down into the valley together: the priest, young Jacopo, and Romola. They found the handful of survivors still clinging to life, fed them, changed their linens, eased their fevers, and carried all the unburied dead to consecrated ground to lay them to rest.

Days turned into weeks, then months. Slowly, the remaining villagers who had fled the plague straggled back. The men took up their spades to dig and sow the fields again, the women carried their vases to the well and smiled as they passed Romola on the lane, and the little Jewish toddler, named Benedetto, was baptized in the small mountain church, his tiny feet already tottering unsteadily across the straw floor of the cottage Romola had made her home. When the last of the sick had recovered, the long months of unbroken labor caught up with her, and she grew weak and faint, happy to rest through the long daylight hours tended by a widow who had lost her whole family to the plague.

Every day, the priest, Jacopo, and all the surviving villagers climbed the small hill to her cottage, bearing offerings of their finest honey, warm polenta, fresh eggs, and honey cakes. For the rest of their lives, they would tell their children of the sainted lady who had come to them over the sea, her face soft and fair, her brown eyes holding a blessing for everyone she met, who had lifted them out of despair when all hope was gone. For generations after, the valley held legends of the blessed woman from the sea — not a myth, but the memory of the quiet, fierce kindness of one woman who chose love over despair, and saved a whole forgotten village from ruin.

The wintry hours in the plague-stricken valley where Romola had laboured for weeks stretched soft and still as frost settling on cypress boughs, as she lay resting her worn limbs. Her mind drifted back over all the roads that had led her there, and peered out across the unwritten stretch of the future, and every familiar shape of the past looked new, viewed from this quiet, unburdened vantage ground.

The experience that had begun the moment she woke adrift in a small boat on the Arno had sealed itself into her soul as deeply as fresh wax holds the stamp of a seal. Not long before, she had felt all bonds cut, no purpose left to hold her to the world, sinking into bitter, selfish grief that life had nothing left to give her. She had thought it no sin to whisper, “I am tired of life. I want to die.” That very thought had throbbed in her throat as she fell asleep lulled by the river’s current — but the second she woke to the cry of a drowning man, the call of unmet need, she had not paused to reason, as she once would have in Florence, that she was glad to live only to lighten other people’s sorrow. She had simply lived, moved by an urgent, unthinking impulse to share the lives of the people around her, to answer every call for help, to do the work that shouted out to be done until there was no room left for arguments over why she should keep breathing, keep enduring, keep labouring.

It was a new baptism for Romola. Back in Florence, the simple, sacred ties between one human being and another had been tangled up for her with the complicated weights of a false marriage, the fickle demands of the city’s state, and the fervid tensions of discipleship to Fra Girolamo. When all those ties had broken one by one and betrayed her trust, the shock had knocked her loose from the whole of life, and numbed her sympathies. Now, she thought with sharp, burning shame: it was nothing but base cowardice in me to wish for death. If every other thing I once believed in is doubtful, this suffering I can ease is certain. If the glory of the cross that Fra Girolamo preached is an illusion, the sorrow of these people is only the truer. While my arm still has strength to lift, I will stretch it out to the fainting. While my eyes can still see, they will search out the forsaken.

Then the past rose up to her with a new, soft, unignorable call. Her work in the green valley was done, and all the love and care that had been tied up in the villagers’ lives rushed back to fill the old, deep, familiar channels of duty and affection she had thought she abandoned forever. That rare, clear moment of self-reflection that comes when you are cut completely loose from every old habit and old company let her judge herself as she never had before. The compulsion of her sympathetic heart, always quick to imagine the pain of others, stirred in her stronger every hour. She questioned every conclusion she had drawn, every choice she had made back then: she had been rash, arrogant, forever bitter that no one around her was good enough to meet her standards, while she herself had betrayed the very best her soul had once known to be right. She began to condemn her flight from Florence. It had been nothing but cowardly self-preservation, after all. The reasons Fra Girolamo had once dragged her back from running away had been truer, deeper than the reasons she had found to flee a second time. If she could feel the urgent needs of these valley strangers so keenly, how could she ignore the needs of the people who were nearest to her in the whole world?

Then the tide turned, and she pushed back against that sharp self-reproach. The memory of her years with Tito rose up, all those impossible conditions that kept any real union between them out of reach, even while their public marriage bound her to a thousand false “duties” that amounted to nothing more than hiding and sanctioning the very sins her soul revolted against. That flight, she told herself, had been her only possible way out. No person who feels things deeply, who has not been dulled by thick insensibility, can avoid this constant back-and-forth conflict when the tangled, twisted conditions of life have made it impossible to keep a bond you once swore to honour. There is no replacing those old ties. The new good you do never erases the fact that the old one broke, that life has been maimed, that a perfection you once hoped for is gone forever. Until the scars over those old wounds have healed completely, conscience will keep casting those doubtful, longing glances backward.

Romola shrank in dread at the thought of being close to Tito again — but she was ill at ease, too, that she had deliberately put herself out of reach of ever knowing his fate. She was tormented by the thought that one day soon he might be lost in terrible misery, crying out for help, and she would not be there. That thin, stinging thread of pain still wove through her heart, bearing out Fra Girolamo’s old words: she could not cease to be a wife. Nothing that had once mingled itself with the current of her heart’s blood could ever be completely erased for her.

Florence, and every small detail of her old life there, called to her like hunger. Her feelings did not wander after vague, impossible dreams: every living part of them was fed on the memory of familiar things — the smell of her blind father’s old manuscript library, the boom of the Duomo’s bells across the Arno, the narrow stone alleys where the vendors called out their wares at dawn. The thought that she had left all of that behind forever grew more and more insistent every hour she was not busy tending to the villagers. What if Fra Girolamo was not perfect? What if the whole life of Florence was nothing but a tangled web of inconsistencies? Had she been so high and mighty that she could shake the dust of the city off her feet and declare, “This world is not good enough for me”? If she had truly been that much better than the people she left behind, she would never have lost all her trust so easily. The bitter grief she had carried for her godfather no longer burned hot and consuming, and her debt of gratitude to Savonarola rose up to take its place. Nothing that had happened, or could ever happen, could erase the fact that the great fire of inspiration in his preaching had woken a whole new life in her. No one else she had ever met deserved that same gratitude from her. His mistakes, his flaws — might they not bring terrible calamities down on the city?

She could not rest. She hardly knew if it was her strength returning with the first budding leaves on the fig trees that made her restless, or if it was her eager, gnawing longing to get closer to Florence. She did not dare imagine walking through the city gates just yet, but the urge to get near enough to learn what was happening to the people she loved swelled so strong in her that it pushed all other plans out of her head.

One bright March morning, all the villagers gathered in the square to see their blessed lady depart. Sturdy Jacopo, the mountain man who had fished her half-dead from the river, had fetched a sure-footed mule for her, and would guide her over the slopes of the hill of Bogoli — ground we have seen on earlier journeys through these hills — to the nearest market town. The old Padre would walk with them too, to help her find the safest path onward to Pistoja. The small store of trinkets and gold she had brought with her to the valley had never been touched, and was more than enough to cover her needs for the journey.

If Romola had not been so pulled by the sharp, hungry longing drawing her onward, that last walk down the village street would have torn her heart in two. The Padre and Jacopo waited by the well with the mule, but her steps were slowed by the wailing villagers, who knelt in the mud to kiss her hands, clung to the hem of her grey wool gown to kiss its worn folds, crying, “Ah, why do you leave us now, when the good warm season is coming, and the crops will grow fat and plentiful? Why do you go?”

“Do not grieve,” Romola told them, her voice thick. “You are all well now, and I will never forget you. I must go and see if my own people are in need of me.”

“Ah, yes! If they have the pestilence, too!” one old woman called out.
“Come back to see us again, Madonna!” a little girl shouted.
“Yes, yes, we will look after little Benedetto, we promise!” the whole crowd chorused.

At last Romola mounted the mule — but little Benedetto, the orphan boy she had nursed back to health, set up such a vigorous scream of protest when he saw her turn away from him that the whole crowd followed along, insisting he must ride perched on the mule’s neck all the way down to the foot of the slope. The final parting could not be put off forever, but Romola turned again and again, long after the path curved upward, to wave her hand. Long after she could hear their voices no more, she could still see that small, loving flock of villagers lingering on the green valley floor, waiting to catch the last flash of her hand, before she turned her face fully toward the distant smoke of Florence, over the familiar Bogoli heights.

On the fourteenth of April, Romola stepped once more beneath the weathered stone gates of Florence.

She had been unable to rest even at Pistoja, where contradictory, wild rumors of the disastrous Trial by Fire at last pulled her further on to Prato. Every hour that passed, she felt herself being drawn inevitably toward the city she had fled, even as dread coiled tight in her chest—until she chanced upon the Augustinian monk of San Spirito who had once served as her godfather’s confessor. From him, she learned the full, terrible truth: Savonarola had been arrested, and her husband Tito was dead. The friar had been caught up in the jeering, surging crowd that followed the cart bearing Savonarola through the piazza, and repeated the tale that ran through every Florentine street: Tito had fled an angry mob by leaping straight into the Arno, only to be dragged to the bank and murdered there by an old man who had nursed a bitter grudge against him for years. But Romola understood the full weight of the catastrophe in a way no other Florentine could. Of Savonarola, the Black Brother spoke with the sneering, dismissive prejudice his order held for the preacher who wore white beneath his black habit, claiming the friar had confessed under torture that he had lied to and deceived the people.

Romola did not linger a moment longer. That same evening, she was home in Florence, sitting in shaken, heavy silence as Monna Brigida poured out a gush of joy, wailing lamentation, and breathless gossip. Brigida had slipped back into wearing a bright new false hairpiece during Romola’s absence, but she tore it off the second her cousin walked through the door, declaring she did not care if her hair turned fully grey, as long as her dear child stayed by her side.

Romola was far too stunned by the two shattering truths she had learned before returning to the city to care for the trivial, gossiping details Brigida rambled out. The tragedy of Tito’s violent death, and the news that Fra Girolamo had broken and confessed to fraud under torture, left her almost no capacity to process smaller, lighter news. All the strength of will she could summon beneath that crushing weight of awe-stricken grief was bent on two goals that outweighed all others: to find a way to see Savonarola in his prison cell, and to learn what had become of Tessa and her two small children.

“Cousin,” she said abruptly, when Brigida’s chatter had drifted far from trouble into endless plans for the two of them living together, “has anyone seen or spoken of a young woman with two little children, in the days since Tito died?”

Brigida started, her eyes rounding, and lifted both hands to the air. “Cristo! No. What! Was he so wicked as that, my poor dear? Ah, that’s why you ran off all those weeks ago, leaving me only a note saying you’d gone of your own free will! I said to myself, never telling a soul, ‘What was she running off from her husband for, just because they cut poor Bernardo’s head off? She’s got her father Bardo’s stubborn temper, that’s what it is.’ But don’t scold me, child—you can’t deny Bardo was fierce. If you’d only told me there was a young girl and children, I’d have understood it all. Seen her? No, and the less said the better. People speak enough ill of Tito without dragging that poor girl into it.”

“No, dearest cousin,” Romola interrupted her gently, earnestly. “Do not speak of her that way. More than anything, I want to find that young woman and her children, and care for them. They are completely helpless. Do not argue with it—it is the first thing I will do.”

“Well,” Brigida huffed, shrugging her shoulders and lowering her voice, bewildered, “if this is what being a Piagnone is about, I’ve been mistaking peas for paternosters! Fra Girolamo said widows ought not remarry, right? So if a girl slips in at the door it’s a sin and a scandal, but if she comes down the chimney she’s welcome? Two whole children—Santiddio!”

“The poor thing never meant any harm,” Romola told her. “She knows nothing of the world. I will explain it all to you, but not now.”

Early the next morning, Romola set out for the little cottage past San Ambrogio where she had once found Tessa. But it was exactly as she feared: Tessa was gone. Romola guessed Tito had sent her away beforehand to some safe spot, planning to join her later—she could not bring herself to believe he would abandon his two children of his own will. The neighbors had no news to give her, save that old deaf Lisa had left a week prior with all their belongings, and no one knew where they had gone. For days, Romola wandered Florence in a haze of anxious waiting. Every time she caught sight of a pair of small brown bare legs, or a short woman dressed in simple contadina linen, her heart would leap into her throat. She never once thought of her mission as great heroism or lofty charity: she only needed a soft, specific, precious thing to care for, after all the grief she had borne. She yearned to clasp the two little children in her arms, to make them love her. It was the one sweet, worthy thing that could come out of all her past pain.

She knew Tito had amassed a great deal of property that legally belonged to her, but she mistrusted the tainted, dishonest origins of most of that fortune, and decided she would sign nearly all of it over to the state—keeping only enough to buy back her late father’s beloved library, and set aside what was needed to support Tessa and the children in quiet comfort. Brigida fussed and fumed that Romola ought to move in with her for good, and that if Romola planned to raise other people’s children, she would help raise them too—only, they had to find them first.

At last, a new path for her search opened. She learned Tito had stashed horses and mules waiting for him at the San Gallo gate, meaning he had planned to flee Florence that way. She wrapped herself in her long widow’s veil and walked along the Via San Gallo, scanning every face, when she spotted the peddler Bratti haggling with a customer over his wares. He was a roving man, who saw far more of the countryside than most Florentines—he might know something. As she lifted her veil to cross the street toward him, her eyes landed on something hanging from the edge of his wicker basket that made her heart soar.

“Bratti, my friend,” she said, breathless, “where did you get that little shell necklace?”

“Your servant, madonna,” Bratti said, turning to her slow, never one to be surprised by anything. “It’s worth good silver, but I won’t make much profit off it. My heart’s too soft for a proper trader. I’m holding it in pledge for a poor soul.”

“Tell me where you got it—from a small young woman named Tessa, isn’t it?” Romola pressed.

“Ah! If you know her,” Bratti said, “and would redeem it from me for a small profit, and give it back to her, you’d be doing a great charity. She cried so hard when she had to hand it over you’d have thought she was about to throw herself in a brook. I’ll let you have it for one florin.”

Romola pressed the coin into his palm and took the necklace, her hands shaking with joy. “Where is she?”

“Outside that gate there, at the far end of the Borgo, at old Sibilla Manetti’s cottage. Anyone will show you the way.”

Romola’s feet carried her there as if they had wings, her mind going back to the Carnival feast, when she had memorized every little detail of that necklace around Tessa’s neck. She found the cottage in minutes. The landlady told her Tessa and the children had been waiting more than two weeks to be fetched, penniless, with nothing but their own clothes to pay the widow for their bread and shelter. Romola did not wait to hear more. She pushed open the door to the inner room.

Tessa was sitting on the low straw bed, her tears dried to silent, hollow sobs, staring blankly at the two little children playing in the far corner: Lillo had pulled his tunic over his own head, roaring as loud as he could to scare little Ninna, before peeking out again to see if she was crying. Tessa had not turned at the sound of the door, expecting it was only the old landlady—hope had long since burned out of her. Romola set down her veil, held up the little necklace so Tessa could see it, and spoke in that soft, clear voice that had once cheered her father through his long, blind days:

“Tessa.”

Tessa jumped to her feet, spinning around. Romola stepped forward, and clasped the familiar necklace back around her neck. “God has sent me to find you again.”

The poor girl screamed and sobbed, clinging tight to Romola’s arms, too overwhelmed to speak. Lillo and Ninna crept out of their corner, clutching at their mother’s skirts, staring up at Romola with wide, unblinking eyes.

That very day, all four of them walked home to Monna Brigida’s house in the Borgo degli Albizzi. Romola broke the news to Tessa gently, bit by bit, that Naldo would never come back to her—he had not run away to be cruel, he was dead. “But do not grieve alone, my Tessa,” she told her. “I am here to take care of you, always. And we have Lillo and Ninna with us.”

Monna Brigida’s mouth twitched, fighting between her awe of Romola and her urge to speak her unruly mind. She resolved that someday soon, she would tell this little contadina girl exactly who Romola was, so she remembered her place in the house—though by supper, she was sneaking the children handfuls of honeyed figs and almond sweets, and by the time she went to bed, she confessed to Romola that it would be a sin not to dote on such perfect little cherubs.

“But you have to give me a little say over what they eat, and all those little things,” she grumbled, half laughing. “You’ve never raised a baby. I had twins once, you know. They died soon after they were born, but I know exactly how to care for little ones.”

When Romola brought Tessa and the two children home to her villa in the last fading days of April, the banks of the Arno were already losing their pale spring blossom, and a second, heavier anxiety hung over her alongside the quiet work of sheltering the little family. That anxiety had swelled to a fever pitch with the official print publication of Fra Girolamo Savonarola’s purported trial record: the set of confessions the sixteen Florentine citizens commissioned to interrogate him had drawn from his broken frame.

The document, issued under the Signoria’s direct order, sparked such immediate, furious public suspicion and protest that the ruling council rushed out decrees to seize and burn every circulating copy. But inevitably, some volumes were mislaid or hidden away, and an unauthorized, pirated second edition was soon being passed between eager hands in apothecary shops, monastic cells, and back street taverns, every Florentine literate enough to read hungrily devouring its pages. Romola, who had long since despaired of ever being granted a chance to speak to the imprisoned Fra Girolamo, read and re-read the printed text, desperate to judge its truth for herself, rather than being tossed between the contradictory, loud assertions of his fierce partisans and his bitter enemies.

The most devout of Savonarola’s followers were struck dumb by his public disavowal of his prophetic claims, his apparent admission that he had lied of being sent by God to reform Florence and the corrupt Church. But that initial shock soon hardened into certainty: every line that ran counter to their faith in him was a forgery, penned by Ser Ceccone, the disreputable, famously hostile notary who had compiled the interrogation digest. It was impossible, they argued, to trust the document at all. Half of the sixteen appointed examiners were his most vicious foes. The brutal gang leader Dolfo Spini, head of the anti-Savonarola Compagnacci, had been prematurely seated on the new governing Eight specifically to stack the deck against a man whose ruin the ruling faction had already preordained. This was no trial: it was a slow, formally staged murder being played out inside the walls of the Palazzo Vecchio. The Signoria had struck a bargain to hand the Frate over to the Pope and the Duke of Milan in exchange for the right to tax Church lands, rid themselves of the man who hounded corrupt local citizens, greedy foreign tyrants, and debased clergy alike. The only remaining point of negotiation was whether Florence would deliver him alive to Rome, or claim the small, vainglorious privilege of hanging and burning their own prophet in their own main piazza. Who under such circumstances would believe a word of this so-called confession? The denial of his prophetic gift, they insisted, had been wrenched from his frail, sensitive body by the agony of the rope and pulley torture—far more rounds than the examiners’ lie of three sessions in a single day, torture enough to break any man’s mind to raving.

More moderate supporters of Savonarola, less ardent in their partisan faith, conceded that the core of the confession was almost certainly genuine, even if Ceccone had twisted phrasing, added malicious flourishes, and reordered lines to paint him in the worst possible light. But they raged that no content in the record justified a death sentence, or even heavy punishment. No charge of political treason had ever been proven against him. No stain of personal vice had ever been found on his character: even his brother monks, including a former secretary of several years who had long criticized Savonarola’s strict rule as Prior, testified even after the shock of his retraction that his life was marked by unimpeachable purity and consistent devotion, which had commanded their unforced veneration. Even the Pope himself had never managed to raise a formal heresy charge against the Frate, only citing him for disobeying a summons to Rome and ignoring the sentence of excommunication. Grave, respected Florentine men felt a deep moral rebellion against the corrupt Roman Curia, softening the scandal of Savonarola’s disobedience: they saw clearly that the triumph of his enemies was nothing less than the triumph of unbridled, gross licence. Sharp, pragmatic statesmen like Soderini and Piero Guicciardini smiled bitterly at the farce of the trial, noting acidly that if the Frate had faked his prophecies to serve powerful men rather than oppose them, no one would have called it fraud—it would have been praised as clever lubrication for the wheels of Church and state. Even these shrewd men, however, were forced to concede that Pope Alexander VI had little choice but to try to eliminate Savonarola: the Frate had openly called for a General Council of the Church to depose the Borgia pontiff, a direct, dangerous act of rebellion that threatened the very foundation of Rome’s power. Savonarola had told Florence openly, and everyone understood his meaning, that he would never obey the devil. The fight between the Frate and the Pope was inevitably a life-and-death struggle—but Florence never had to volunteer to be the Pope’s executioner.

Romola’s ears hummed with these two competing narratives: the ardent, wounded faith of the piagnoni, and the cold, worldly pragmatism of men who judged all actions by the moderate standard of common human frailty. She could not rest satisfied with either. As she pored over the printed pages, she called up half-conscious memories of small, unremarked inconsistencies she had noted through the years of her discipleship, whispering a quiet premonition that Savonarola’s retraction of his prophetic claims was not merely a spasmodic, desperate effort to escape torture. But her soul cried out for an explanation of his lapses that would let her still believe the core of his life’s striving was pure and grand. She remembered the bitter, blighting selfish despair that had overwhelmed her when she first lost her faith in the man who had once been the very embodiment of the highest moral purpose to her, and a new, stubborn faith rose in her out of the ruins of that despair. It could not be true, she told herself, that the disbelieving thoughts that had turned her soul arid and empty were rooted in reality. The spirit that had breathed through the Frate’s sermons, that had lit a new life in her, had been a living thing, not a hollow pretence. Whatever falsity lay in his claims, it had been a fall, not a premeditated plan: a gradual tangle he had wandered into and struggled to escape, not a fraud he had designed for profit from the start.

Many lines on the printed page bore the obvious stamp of clumsy, alien fabrication: overblown, repetitious self-accusation of a kind no man but the crudest hypocrite would ever utter to his fellows. That these forgeries stood out so sharply against the rest of the text only strengthened Romola’s impression that the bulk of the record was true to words the Frate had actually spoken. Not a single line dishonoured him save for the section disavowing his prophetic claims. He never wavered in stating that the ends he had pursued had always been the moral good of Florence, the reform of the Church, and the betterment of all humanity. Even through the notary’s malicious edits, Fra Girolamo shone through as a man who had indeed lusted for glory, but for glory earned by labouring for the highest possible end: to turn abstract moral belief into tangible action that shaped every detail of ordinary people’s lives. One famous, memorable passage, even through its possible erasures and interpolations, echoed all too clearly in Romola’s memory:
“Everything that I have done, I did with the design of being forever famous in this age and ages to come; that I might win credit in Florence, so that no great matter of import should be done in the city without my sanction. Once I had secured that standing, I meant to do great things across Italy and beyond its borders, through the great rulers I had befriended and consulted on high matters, including the summoning of that General Council. As my first efforts bore fruit, I would have moved to further, greater works. Above all, once the General Council was assembled, I intended to rouse the princes of Christendom, and especially those beyond the Alps, to subdue the infidels. It was never much in my thoughts to make myself a Cardinal or Pope, for when I had achieved the work I set out to do, I would have been the first man in the world in authority and reverence, without ever taking the papal crown. If the office had been offered to me I would not have refused it, but it seemed to me that leading that great work was a far greater thing than being Pope—for any man, even a vicious man, may be made Pope, but such a work as I contemplated demands a man of excellent, unyielding virtue.”

That blending of burning ambition and absolute certainty that he was the man chosen to fulfil God’s grand design was no new revelation to Romola. It was the habitual tone of Savonarola’s mind, the voice she had heard ring through the rafters of the Duomo a hundred times. In moments of private ecstatic contemplation, his sense of self melted away into awe of the Unspeakable, and in that space lay the roots of genuine, unfeigned humility. But standing before the crowds who looked to him for leadership, pre-eminence felt to him like a necessary condition of his life’s purpose. It was no wonder, Romola thought, that under the terrible pressure of his new circumstances, his old certainty could shatter. When instead of adoring faces and kneeling crowds, instead of a great work rising to success that stamped him as a chosen instrument of God, he was met with jeers, spittle, curses, the hard faces of his enemies sitting as his judges, then the agony of torture pulling his limbs apart until the irrepressible cry burst out of him: “It is true, whatever you say. Let me go. Do not torture me again. Yes, yes, I am guilty. O God, thy stroke has reached me.”

The thought of that agony, and the deeper, sharper sorrow that must have followed in the silence of his cell after the confession was wrung from him, pressed on Romola’s own heart until slow, bitter tears tracked down her cheeks. Every shallow, self-satisfied fool in Florence was glibly passing judgement on the man’s supposed sins, while he endured a depth of sorrow only known to souls that have strived for the most perfect good, and then seen themselves fallen.

As the days passed, no new transcripts of further interrogations circulated; no changes appeared in his recorded confessions. At last, Savonarola was left alone in his prison cell, and granted pen and ink, to write with his bruised, overstrained right arm if he wished. What he wrote was no defence of his innocence, no protest against the injustice of his trial. It was a long, tender colloquy with the divine purity he longed to be reunited with, an outpouring of unreserved self-abasement, one long, desperate cry for inner renewal. None of his old fiery self-assertion remained, no thunderous proclamations that his enemies were the children of the devil.
